In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah alone. May blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave—said:

Section: The Names of the Quran

The Quran; the Criterion; the Book; the Guidance; the Light; the Healing; the Clarification; the Admonition; the Mercy; Insights; the Message; the Noble; the Glorious; the Mighty; the Blessed; the Revelation; that which is sent down; the Straight Path; the Rope of Allah; the Remembrance; the Reminder; a Reminder:

“And indeed, it is a reminder for the righteous.”

“Indeed, it is a reminder.”

“So whoever wills may take heed of it.”

“Confirming what came before it,” and “a confirmation of what came before it”; the Guardian over it;

“And a detailed explanation of all things.”

“As a clarification of all things.”

The Mutually Consistent; the Oft-Repeated; the Wise: “These are the verses of the Book

---

## Page 7065

of wisdom.” The Perfected; the Detailed:

“It is He who sent down to you the Book, explained in detail.”

The Proof:

“There has come to you a proof from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a clear light.”

This is according to one of the two interpretations. The Truth:

“The truth has come to you from your Lord.”

Arabic; Clear; the Best of Discourse; the Best of Narratives, according to one interpretation; the Speech of Allah:

“Then grant him protection so that he may hear the speech of Allah.”

Knowledge:

“Then whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you.”

The Exalted; the Wise:

“Indeed, in the Mother of the Book with Us, it is exalted, full of wisdom.”

The Upright:

“Reciting purified scrolls.”

“Containing upright writings.”

“He sent down the Book to His servant and placed no crookedness in it.”

“Upright.”

Revelation, in His words:

“It is only a revelation sent down.”

Wisdom, in His words:

“There have already come to them accounts containing a deterrent.”

“Profound wisdom.”

A Judgment, in His words:

“We sent it down as a judgment in Arabic.”

And News, according to one interpretation of His words:

“About the momentous news.”

And a Warner, according to one interpretation:

“This is a warner like the warners of old.”

In the hadith of Abū Mūsā, it is described as an intercessor whose intercession is accepted and a witness that confirms the truth. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called it “a proof for you or against you.” In the hadith narrated by al-Hārith from ‘Alī, it is “a safeguard for whoever holds fast to it.”

As for its being described as recounting, speaking, judging, giving rulings, bringing glad tidings, and guiding, He said:

“Indeed, this Quran recounts to the Children of Israel.”

“This is Our record, speaking against you.”

“Say: Allah gives you a ruling concerning them, as does what is recited to you in the Book.”

That is, what is recited also gives you a ruling.

“Indeed, this Quran guides to what is most upright and gives glad tidings to the believers who act.”

## Page 7067

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about certain hadiths: whether they were authentic and whether any recognized authorities had transmitted them with an authentic chain of transmission, and so forth. He replied:

Section

As for the hadith concerning the Opening of the Book, it is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I have divided the prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves: half is for Me and half is for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.

‘When the servant says, “All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds,” Allah says, “My servant has praised Me.” When he says, “The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,” Allah says, “My servant has extolled Me.” When he says, “Master of the Day of Judgment,” Allah says, “My servant has glorified Me.”

‘When he says, “You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,” He says, “This verse is between Myself and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.”

‘When he says, “Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your anger, nor those who have gone astray,” He says, “These verses are for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.”’”

---

## Page 7068

It is also established in Sahīh Muslim that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“While Gabriel was sitting with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he heard a creaking sound above him. He raised his head and said, ‘This is a gate in heaven that has been opened today; it has never been opened before today.’ An angel descended through it, and Gabriel said, ‘This is an angel who has come down to earth; he has never descended before today.’ The angel greeted him and said, ‘Rejoice in two lights you have been given, which no prophet before you was given: the Opening of the Book and the concluding verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah. You will not recite a single phrase from them without being granted it.’”

Some hadiths state:

“He was given the Opening of the Book from a treasure beneath the Throne.”

Section

Allah Most High says in the Mother of the Quran, the Seven Oft-Repeated Verses, and the Magnificent Quran:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

This surah is the Mother of the Quran, the Opening of the Book, the Seven Oft-Repeated Verses, and the Magnificent Quran. It is the Healing Surah, and its recitation is obligatory in the prayers: no prayer is valid without it. It is also the Sufficient Surah: it suffices in place of other passages, but no other passage suffices in its place.

Prayer is the best of deeds. It consists of good words and righteous action. The best and most obligatory of its good words is the Quran, and the best and most obligatory of its righteous actions is prostration. Allah brought these two together in the first surah He revealed to His Messenger, which He began

---

## Page 7069

with His words, Most High:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created,”

and ended with His words:

“Prostrate and draw near.”

Prayer was therefore structured accordingly: it begins with recitation and ends with prostration. Hence His words, glorified is He, concerning the prayer of fear:

“When they have prostrated, let them take their positions behind you.”

Here, prostration means the unit of prayer they perform on their own after separating from the imam. The proclamation of Allah’s greatness, the opening supplication, and the seeking of refuge that precede recitation mark entry into the sanctity of prayer and prepare for what follows; these initial acts serve as an introduction. What follows prostration—sitting, reciting the testimony of faith with its salutations to Allah and prayers for peace upon His righteous servants, supplicating, and greeting those present with peace—marks the conclusion of prayer and serves as an epilogue to what precedes it. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The key to prayer is purification. One enters its sanctity by proclaiming Allah’s greatness and leaves it with the salutation of peace.”

Thus, when scholars differed over whether frequent bowing and prostration or prolonged standing was better, or whether the two were equal—with three views reported from Ahmad and others—the correct view was that they are equal. Standing includes the best form of remembrance, while prostration is the best deed; thus, the two are balanced.

For this reason, the prayer of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was balanced: he made its pillars approximately equal in duration. When he greatly prolonged the standing—as he would in night prayer and the eclipse prayer—he also prolonged the bowing and prostration. When he kept the standing moderate, he likewise kept the bowing and prostration moderate.

Just as the Mother of the Book is the obligatory recitation, it is also the best surah in the Quran. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said

---

## Page 7070

in an authentic hadith:

“Nothing like it has been revealed in the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, or the Quran. It is the seven oft-repeated verses and the glorious Quran that I have been given.”

Its virtues are very numerous. It has been related from al-Hasan al-Basrī, in a report transmitted by Ibn Mājah and others, that Allah revealed one hundred and four books, gathered their knowledge into the four, gathered the knowledge of the four into the Quran, gathered the knowledge of the Quran into the al-Mufassal section, gathered the knowledge of al-Mufassal into the Mother of the Quran, and gathered the knowledge of the Mother of the Quran into these two comprehensive phrases:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

Thus, the knowledge contained in the books revealed from heaven is gathered into these two comprehensive phrases.

Hence, it is established in the authentic hadith:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I have divided prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves: half is for Me and half is for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’

When he says, ‘All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds,’ Allah says, ‘My servant has praised Me.’ When he says, ‘The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,’ Allah says, ‘My servant has extolled Me.’ When he says, ‘Master of the Day of Judgment,’ Allah, Almighty and Majestic, says, ‘My servant has glorified Me’; and in another narration, ‘My servant has entrusted his affairs to Me.’ When he says, ‘You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,’ He says, ‘This verse is divided into two halves between Myself and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’ When he says, ‘Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your anger, nor those who have gone astray,’ He says, ‘These verses are for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’”

---

## Page 7071

This text thus establishes that this surah is divided between Allah and His servant, and that these two phrases mark its division: “You alone we worship,” together with what precedes it, is for Allah; “and You alone we ask for help,” together with what follows it, is for the servant, who shall have what he asks for.

Hence some of the early generations said: “Half of it is praise, and half of it is petition.” Both worship and seeking help are forms of supplication. Since Allah has made it obligatory for us to commune with Him and call upon Him with these two phrases in every prayer, this clearly entails that we are obliged to worship Him and seek His help. Requiring an utterance that constitutes an acknowledgment and avowal, a supplication and request, means requiring what it signifies—not merely requiring words devoid of meaning, for that cannot be the case. Indeed, requiring this goes further than merely requiring worship and seeking help, since their essential reality may be fulfilled through the heart alone, or through the heart and body. Rather, He has required us to call upon Allah, Almighty and Majestic, to commune with Him, speak to Him, and address Him in these terms, so that what is required may be complete in both form and meaning, through the heart and the rest of the body.

He has brought these two comprehensive principles together in several places, both by way of obligation and otherwise, as in His words at the end of Surah Hūd:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And the words of the righteous servant Shuʿayb:

“My success comes only through Allah. In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

And the words of Abraham and those with him:

“Our Lord, in You we put our trust, to You we turn, and to You is the final return.”

And His words—glory be to Him—when He commanded His Messenger to say:

“Thus We have sent you to a community before which other communities have passed away, so that you may recite to them what We have revealed to you, while they

---

## Page 7072

disbelieve in the Most Merciful. Say, ‘He is my Lord; there is no god but Him. In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.’”

He thus commanded His Prophet to say:

“In the Most Merciful I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

Likewise, He commanded him to do both in His words:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

A command addressed to him is also a command to his community. Allah commanded his community to do this in the Mother of the Quran and elsewhere so that their doing so would constitute obedience to Allah and compliance with His command, and so that they would not put themselves ahead of Allah and His Messenger. Thus, our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the wholly sincere members of his community generally perform their supplications, acts of worship, and other deeds only at Allah’s command. This contrasts with those who do what they have not been commanded to do, even if it is good or carries no blame. This is one reason for his superiority and that of his community over others, and for the superiority of those of his community whose devotion is pure over those who mix what he brought with other things, such as those who deviate from the straight path.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would direct his worship, remembrance, and intimate supplications toward these two principles. For example, when offering the sacrifice, he would say:

“O Allah, this is from You and for You.”

His words “from You” express the meaning of putting one’s trust in Allah and seeking His help, while “for You” expresses the meaning of worship. Another example is what he would say during his night prayers:

“To You I submit, in You I believe, in You I put my trust, and to You I turn. By Your aid I argue, and to You I refer judgment. I seek refuge in Your might—there is no god but You—from Your leading me astray. You are the Ever-Living who never dies, whereas jinn and humankind die.”

There are many other examples of this.

---

## Page 7073

Once this principle is established, a person’s position regarding these two obligations must fall into one of four categories, which exhaust all the possibilities: fulfilling both, worshipping alone, seeking help alone, or abandoning both. People therefore fall into these four categories. Indeed, adherents of religions fall into these categories, and it is they who are the subject of this discussion.

One group is predominantly concerned with devotion to Allah, observing His commands and prohibitions, sincere devotion to Allah Most High, and following the Sharia through submission to His commands, His prohibitions, and His words governing creation. Yet such a person is deficient in seeking Allah’s help and putting his trust in Him. He is therefore either powerless or negligent, and is overcome by either his inward enemy or his outward enemy. He may often become distraught over what befalls him and grieve over what he misses. This is the condition of many who know Allah’s law and command and believe that they are following the Sharia and worshipping as it prescribes, yet do not understand His decree and predestination. Such a person is well-intentioned and seeks the truth, but does not know the way that leads to it or the path by which it is reached.

Another group is predominantly concerned with seeking Allah’s help, putting their trust in Him, displaying their poverty and need before Him, and submitting to His decree, His predestination, and His words governing creation. Yet such a person is deficient in worship and in devoting his religion sincerely to Allah, so his aim is not

---

## Page 7074

that all religion should be devoted to Allah. Even if that is his aim, he does not follow the Sharia of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and His prescribed way. Rather, he seeks some form of authority in the world: either authority through power and influence, or authority through unveiling and disclosing hidden matters. Or he seeks to obtain what he desires and ward off what he dislikes by whatever means, or aims at some form of worship and devotion, whatever its manner. His energies are devoted to seeking help and placing his trust in Allah insofar as these help him achieve his aim. Thus, he is either ignorant or unjust, neglecting some of what Allah has commanded him and committing some of what Allah has forbidden.

This is the condition of many who engage in devotion, practise Sufism and spiritual poverty, and perceive Allah’s predestination and decree but not His commands and prohibitions. They perceive that created things subsist through Allah, that they need Him, and that He sustains them; yet they do not perceive what He has commanded and forbidden, which of their actions Allah loves and approves, and which He hates and finds displeasing.

Hence, many among them experience unveiling, exert influence, and perform extraordinary feats, while casting off some aspects of the Sharia and contravening some of its commands. If one of them goes further, he falls into permissiveness and the abandonment of religious restraints. He may even proceed to corrupt his belief in Allah’s oneness, lapsing into union and restricted indwelling, as has happened to many shaykhs. Statements leading to this can be found in the words of the author of “Manāzil al-Sā’irīn” (The Stations of the Wayfarers) and others.

Some of them may embrace “absolute union and the doctrine of the unity of existence,” believing that Allah is absolute existence, as the author of “al-Futūhāt al-Makkiyya” (The Meccan Openings) says at its beginning:

---

## Page 7075

“The Lord is real, and the servant is real—
If only I knew who bears the duty!
If you say, ‘The servant,’ he is dead;
Or if you say, ‘The Lord,’ how can He be charged with duty?”

A third category consists of those who turn away both from worshipping Allah and from seeking His help. They comprise two groups: the worldly and the religious. The religious among them follow a corrupt religion: they worship others besides Allah and seek help from others besides Him, following their conjectures and desires.

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, although guidance has already come to them from their Lord.”

The worldly among them seek what they desire of this fleeting life through whatever they regard as means to that end. Know that a distinction must be made between someone who may turn away from worshipping Allah and seeking His help, and someone who worships another and seeks help from someone other than Him.

Section

Allah, Mighty and Majestic, says at the beginning of the surah:

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

He thus began with these two names: Allah and the Lord. “Allah” is the God who is worshipped, so this name is most fitting in worship. Hence we say: “Allah is the Greatest,” “All praise is for Allah,” “Glory be to Allah,”

---

## Page 7076

and “There is no god but Allah.” “The Lord” is the One who nurtures, creates, provides, grants help, and guides. This name is most fitting for seeking help and making requests. Hence we say:

“My Lord, forgive me and my parents.”

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will surely be among the losers.”

“My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me.”

“Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our conduct.”

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

Thus, most prescribed supplications and requests for help use the name “Lord.” The first name encompasses the servant’s ultimate aim, destination, and final end: what he was created for and wherein his well-being and perfection lie—namely, worshipping Allah. The second name encompasses the servant’s creation and beginning: Allah nurtures him and takes him into His care. Yet the second is included in the first, just as lordship is included in divinity; lordship also necessarily entails divinity. The name “the Most Merciful” encompasses the perfection of both relationships; with both states expressed in it, the servant’s happiness in this world and the Hereafter is complete.

Hence Allah Most High says:

“Yet they disbelieve in the Most Merciful. Say, ‘He is my Lord; there is no god but Him. In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn in repentance.’”

Here He mentions the three names: “the Most Merciful,” “my Lord,” and “God.” He says, “In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn in repentance,” just as He mentions the three names in the Mother of the Quran. There, however, He begins with the name “Allah.” Hence, in that surah, He begins with “You alone we worship,” placing this name and the worship associated with it first, because

---

## Page 7077

that surah is the Opening of the Book and the Mother of the Quran. He therefore places first in it the intended end, which is the final cause, for it is an efficient cause of the final cause. I have explained this meaning at length in several places: at the beginning of the “Quran Commentary,” in “A Treatise on Love and Will,” and elsewhere.

Section

People recognize their need for and dependence upon the Lord before recognizing their need for and dependence upon God as the One to be worshipped, and they seek to meet their immediate needs before their future ones. Thus, their acknowledgment of Allah’s lordship precedes their acknowledgment of His divinity. They supplicate to Him, seek His help, and put their trust in Him more often than they worship Him and turn to Him in repentance. This is precisely why He sent the messengers to call them to worship Allah alone, without any partner: this is the intended end, which necessarily entails acknowledging His lordship. He has told us concerning them:

“If you ask them who created them, they will surely say, ‘Allah.’”

He also tells us that when adversity befalls them, those they call upon vanish from their thoughts—all but Him. And He says:

“When waves like canopies engulf them, they call upon Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him.”

Thus, He states that they acknowledge His lordship and devote their religion sincerely to Him when they are afflicted by

---

## Page 7078

adversity, calling upon Him and seeking His help, but then turn away from worshipping Him once their aims have been attained. Many speculative theologians establish Allah’s oneness only in terms of His lordship, whereas the messengers called people to His oneness in worship. Likewise, many Sufi devotees and those who experience spiritual states turn to Allah only in terms of His lordship, because of the inward states He supplies them with, through which they exercise influence. Such people are of the same kind as kings, and Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has frequently censured this class in the Quran. Reflect on this, for it reveals the condition of people who speak about realities and act upon them. By my life, they are concerned with a category of realities pertaining to creation, divine decree, and lordship—not realities pertaining to religion, revealed law, and divinity. I have discussed this meaning in several places. It is a fundamental principle deserving great attention. Allah, Glorified is He, knows best.

Section

This is because human beings—indeed, all created beings—are servants of Allah Most High, dependent upon Him and owned by Him. He is their Lord, Sovereign, and God; there is no god but Him. A created being possesses absolutely nothing of its own accord. Rather, its very self, attributes, actions, whatever benefits it or is its due, and everything else are all part of Allah’s creation. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is the Lord of

---

## Page 7079

all of that, as well as its Sovereign, Originator, Creator, and Fashioner.

When we say that a created being has nothing of itself but nonexistence, nonexistence is not something that requires an existing agent. Rather, nonexistence is not a thing at all. Its persistence is conditional upon the agent’s not acting; this does not mean that the agent’s absence necessitates and brings it about in the way that an agent necessitates and brings about an existing effect. The absence of an effect may indeed be attributed to the absence of its cause, but these two cases differ. An existing effect is created and brought into being by an agent, whereas what does not exist is not brought into being by the agent’s absence, for that would lead to infinite regress and circularity. Moreover, there is no greater justification for one absence to necessitate the other than the reverse: neither absence possesses a distinctive reality by virtue of which it could qualify as an agent.

Although it is intelligible that the absence of a productive cause should account for the absence of its effect rather than the reverse, this is because the existence of the productive cause brings about the existence of what it produces. The mind therefore attributes the effect’s absence to the cause’s absence as a relation of necessary implication. For a thing’s nonexistence is due either to the absence of its productive cause or to the presence of an impediment. Once the productive cause is in place, nonexistence can only be conceived as arising from one of these two circumstances or states. A thing whose cause of existence has been established may be obstructed and prevented by an incompatible impediment—which is something existent—while at other times its cause has not been established. Its nonexistence is therefore attributed sometimes to the absence of its productive cause and sometimes to the presence of an impediment incompatible with it.

This is the meaning of the Muslims’ saying:

“Whatever Allah wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass”; for

---

## Page 7080

His will alone, and nothing else, necessitates a thing’s existence; its absence therefore entails the thing’s absence. Nothing comes to be unless He wills it; under no circumstances can anything come to be without His will. There is, then, no cause necessitating a thing’s existence such that His will would prevent it from existing. Rather, His will is the complete cause: when it is present, there is no impediment, and when it is absent, there is no productive cause.

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release thereafter.”

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, can they remove His harm? Or if He intends mercy for me, can they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him those who trust place their trust.’”

And once it is understood that the servant has no good whatsoever of himself—rather, whatever blessing we have is from Allah, and when harm touches us, it is to Him that we cry out, and all goodness is in His hands—as He says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

And He says:

“When a calamity befell you, although you had inflicted twice as much, did you say, ‘How has this happened?’ Say, ‘It is from your own selves.’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the foremost supplication for forgiveness, recorded in Sahīh al-Bukhārī:

“O Allah, You are my Lord; none has the right to be worshipped but You. You created me, and I am Your servant. I abide by Your covenant and promise as best I can. I seek refuge in You from the evil of what I have done. I acknowledge Your blessing upon me, and I acknowledge my sin. So forgive me, for none forgives sins but You.”

And he said in the opening supplication of the prayer, recorded in Sahīh Muslim:

---

## Page 7081

“I am at Your service and ever ready to obey You. All good is in Your hands, and evil is not ascribed to You. Blessed and exalted are You, our Lord.”

This is because evil may consist either in something existent or in an absence. The latter may be the absence of an entity, of one of its attributes of perfection, or of one of its acts. Examples include the absence of life, knowledge, hearing, sight, speech, reason, or righteous deeds in their various forms: knowing Allah, loving Him, worshipping Him, trusting in Him, turning to Him, hoping in Him, fearing Him, obeying His commands, avoiding His prohibitions, and other praiseworthy inward and outward words and deeds. All these are good things, whereas their absence constitutes evils and ills.

Yet this absence is not a thing at all, such that it should have a maker and an agent and thus be attributed to Allah. Rather, it is inseparable from the soul—which is the very essence of the human being—both before and after its creation. Before its creation, the soul is nonexistent, which necessarily entails this absence. After its creation—having been created weak and deficient—it has deficiency, weakness, and incapacity, all of which consist in absence. This absence is therefore attributed to the soul in the same way that the absence of an effect is attributed to the absence of its cause and of that which would bring it about. In another respect, it may also be attributed to the presence of something that precludes it, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

The crux of the matter is that these evils and ills, which consist in absence, are not existent things such that Allah should be their Creator; for Allah is the Creator of all things.

---

## Page 7082

Nonexistent things are sometimes attributed to the absence of their maker, and sometimes to the presence of something that prevents them. These evils that consist in absence cannot be attributed to Him in either of these two ways.

As for the “first,” He is the manifest Truth, so it cannot be said that they are absent because their maker and that which would bring them about are absent. As for the “second”—the presence of an impediment—an impediment is only needed when a cause conducive to the outcome exists. Had He willed to bring them about, no impediment could have prevented Him. He—glorified is He—does not prevent Himself from doing what He wills. Rather, He does whatever He wills.

Allah may, however, create something as a cause, a factor conducive to an outcome, or an impediment. If He makes the cause complete, nothing prevents it; if He does not make it complete, an impediment obstructs it because of the cause’s weakness and the absence of Allah’s assistance to it. Thus, nothing fails to exist except because He has not willed it, just as nothing exists except because He wills it.

These ills that consist in absence are attributed to the servant sometimes because the cause is absent on his part, and sometimes because an impediment exists on his part. The absence of the cause is clear: no strength, power, good, or cause of good originates independently from him. If anything did originate from him, it would constitute a cause. Thus, the absence is attributed to him because the cause is absent. Moreover, he has performed acts for which he was a cause through Allah’s assistance to him; whatever has not proceeded from him is therefore due to the absence of the cause.

---

## Page 7083

As for the presence of an impediment that opposes and precludes it, this is because he himself may be too limited and weak to combine acts that are possible in themselves but mutually incompatible for him. If he is occupied with hearing or seeing something, speaking about it, considering it, or willing it—or if his limbs are occupied with extensive activity—this diverts him from another activity, even if it is good, because of his limited capacity and inability. Thus, the presence in him of one attribute or act becomes an impediment and barrier to another. His limited capacity and inability come down to his lack of power, so this too comes down to the absence that is attributable to him. Pure nonexistence is not a thing that could be attributed to Allah Most High.

If, however, the thing exists—as pain and its cause do—one should recognize that the evil that exists is neither evil in an absolute sense nor pure evil. Rather, it is evil for whoever suffers from it. One people’s calamities may be another people’s benefits. Hence the words of the hadith we have transmitted with a recurring feature throughout its chain:

“I believe in divine decree, its good and its evil, its sweetness and its bitterness.”

And in the hadith narrated by Abū Dāwūd:

“If you spent an earthful of gold, He would not accept it from you until you believed in divine decree, its good and its evil, and knew that whatever befell you could never have missed you, and whatever missed you could never have befallen you.”

Good and evil are thus relative to the servant to whom they pertain, exactly like sweetness and bitterness. Whatever causes a person no suffering is not evil for him, while whatever gives him enjoyment is good for him, as in the report: “The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would teach a person whose brother recounted a dream to him to

---

## Page 7084

say:

‘May you encounter good and be spared evil—good for us and evil for our enemies.’”

When evil befalls a servant, his enemy’s heart rejoices; thus it is good for one and evil for the other. For someone who is neither his friend nor his enemy, it is neither good nor evil. Among Allah’s creations, nothing continually causes suffering to all creatures, nor does anything continually cause suffering to most of them. Rather, His creations benefit them, or most of them, most of the time, as do the sun and good health. Thus, among the things Allah has created, nothing is evil in an absolute and universal sense.

It is therefore clear that the evil that exists in creation is limited and particular. It has another aspect in which it is good and excellent, and this is the predominant of its two aspects. As Allah Most High says:

“Who perfected everything He created.”

And Allah Most High says:

“The work of Allah, who perfected all things.”

And Allah Most High says:

“We did not create the heavens and the earth and everything between them except in truth.”

And He says:

“And they reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth: ‘Our Lord, You have not created this in vain.’”

Muslims know that Allah has created nothing without a wise purpose. That wise purpose is the aspect of its goodness and excellence. There is nothing in creation that is pure evil, containing no good or benefit whatsoever.

This clarifies the meaning of his words:

“And evil is not attributable to You.”

It also explains why evil is not attributed to Allah in isolation: rather, it is either included in a general attribution, attributed to its cause, or expressed without naming its agent.

---

## Page 7085

Thus, this particular, limited evil that exists has as its cause either an absence or something existent. An absence may be the lack of a necessary condition or of part of a cause. Its cause cannot be sheer nonexistence, for sheer nonexistence cannot constitute a complete cause of something existent. Rather, the cause of good and pleasure may be in place, yet a necessary condition is not met, and so pain occurs. Examples include failure to perform obligatory duties, which causes blame and punishment; lack of knowledge, which causes the pain of ignorance; lack of hearing, sight, and speech, which causes the suffering of blindness, deafness, and muteness; and lack of health and strength, which causes pain, illness, and weakness.

In these and similar cases, evil is likewise traced to an absence on the servant’s part. This bears out the words of Allah’s close friend:

“And when I am ill, it is He who cures me.”

Although illness is an existing pain, its cause is diminished strength and the loss of existing health. This is an absence originating in man, who has no existence in and of himself. Thus, the words of the True One are borne out:

“Whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

And His words:

“You said, ‘From where has this come?’ Say, ‘It is from yourselves.’”

The same applies to similar statements concerning cases where the cause is a failure to perform an obligatory duty, and likewise to the Companion’s statement: “If it is an error, then it is from me and from Satan.”

This is clarified by the fact that all prohibited acts—disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience—are committed by the servant only because of his ignorance or need. If he knew their harmfulness and had no need of them, he would not commit them. Ignorance is rooted in absence, and need is likewise rooted in absence.

---

## Page 7086

Thus, his commission of evil deeds is rooted in a lack of knowledge and freedom from need. Hence Allah says in the Quran:

“They were unable to hear.”

“Did you not, then, use reason?”

“Indeed, they found their fathers astray.”

“So they hasten in their footsteps.”

And there are other passages conveying similar meanings.

As for something existent that causes a particular existing evil, such as pain, examples include forbidden acts: disbelief consisting in denial or arrogance, wickedness consisting in committing prohibited acts, and the like. These cause blame and punishment. The same applies to consuming harmful foods and to strenuous movements that cause pain. Such existence cannot be complete and unalloyed, for complete, unalloyed existence produces nothing but good, just as we have said that sheer nonexistence cannot give rise to anything existent. Rather, it must be deficient existence, whether the deficiency lies in the cause or in its subject. Thus, denial may be caused by a failure to recognize and acknowledge the truth. The absence of this knowledge and acknowledgment is caused by the absence of their means: thorough reflection and attentive listening to the signs and evidences of the truth.

The absence of reflection and listening is caused either by the absence of an impelling cause—in which case it is sheer nonexistence—or by the presence of an obstacle in the soul, such as pride or envy:

“And Allah does not love anyone who is conceited and boastful.”

This is a false conception, caused by the soul’s lack of sufficiency in the truth, for which it substitutes false imaginings.

---

## Page 7087

And “envy” too is caused by lacking the blessing that would make the envier equal or superior to the person envied. This makes the envier resent the envied person’s being his equal or superior. Likewise, wickedness—such as killing, illicit sexual intercourse, and all other vile deeds—is caused only by the soul’s need to find satisfaction through killing or pleasure through illicit intercourse. Otherwise, someone who achieves his aim without killing or obtains pleasure without illicit intercourse does not commit such acts. Need, in turn, originates in nonexistence.

If one reflects on this, it becomes clear that when existing evil is attributed to either nonexistence or existence, it must be deficient existence. Sometimes it is attributed to a lack of completeness in the cause or the absence of a condition; at other times, it is attributed to something existent. It is also sometimes described in terms of a deficient cause and a deficient subject. This deficiency is caused either by the absence of a condition or by the presence of an obstacle. An obstacle can only obstruct because the impelling cause is weak.

Everything I have mentioned is clear and evident except this point, which contains an obscurity that reflection will dispel. It has two aspects:

“First,” something existent cannot have sheer nonexistence as its cause.

“Second,” something existent cannot be a cause of sheer nonexistence. This is self-evident: existing things proceed only from something real and existent.

---

## Page 7088

For this reason, it is known by the innate disposition that every made thing must have a maker, as Allah Most High says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they the creators?”

That is: Were they created without a creator who created them, or did they create themselves? Some speculative theologians have sought to establish this proposition through analogy and illustrative examples. It can indeed be established by argument, and the proofs for it are numerous. Yet the sound innate disposition affirms it more firmly, finds it more self-evident, and is more compelled to acknowledge it than the example used to establish it by analogy.

Scholars of legal theory have differed concerning the effective cause of a legal ruling: may an affirmative ruling be grounded in the absence of an attribute, while they maintain that a negative ruling may be grounded in an absence? Some allow this, while others reject it. Others distinguish between the two forms of analogy: an absence cannot be a cause of existence in a causal analogy, but may serve as its cause in an evidential analogy, without the effect being attributed to it in the latter.

This settles the matter: in an evidential analogy, an absence may serve as a cause and as part of a cause, because the absence of an attribute may indicate an existent attribute that entails the ruling. In a “causal analogy,” however, an absence cannot constitute a complete cause. It may nevertheless be part of the complete cause and a condition for the impelling cause, which is not complete. We have said, “Part of the complete cause”; this is what it means for it to be a condition for an existent cause to produce its effect.

---

## Page 7089

And this is a verbal dispute that disappears once the meanings are clarified. This explains one of the two sides: that something existent cannot have pure nonexistence as its cause.

As for “the second side”—that something existent cannot cause an existence that entails nonexistence—this is because pure nonexistence does not require an existent cause; the absence of an existent cause suffices. Moreover, when an existent cause produces an effect, it must produce something, whereas pure nonexistence is not a thing. An effect that is pure nonexistence is therefore equivalent to the absence of an effect. Rather, when annihilation is brought about, annihilation is something existent involving nonexistence. Making something existent nonexistent, or something nonexistent existent, is intelligible. Making something nonexistent nonexistent, however, is intelligible only in the sense of leaving it nonexistent, and the absence of an agent suffices for that. There is a clear distinction between the absence of an agent and of a necessitating factor where the cause is absent, on the one hand, and an agent that produces nonexistence, a factor that necessitates nonexistence, and a cause of nonexistence, on the other. Nonexistence does not require the latter; the former suffices.

Thus both sides have become clear: pure nonexistence, unmixed with any existence, cannot be anything existent at all—neither a cause nor an effect, neither an agent nor anything acted upon. Accordingly, pure and complete existence, unmixed with any nonexistence, cannot in any way be a cause of nonexistence, an effect arising from it, an agent producing it, or something produced by it. That it is neither an effect arising from nonexistence nor something produced by it is evident. As for its not being a cause of nonexistence: if it were a cause of pure nonexistence, pure nonexistence would not require an existent cause; and if it were a cause of nonexistence

---

## Page 7090

that involves existence, then that existence must have a cause. If its cause were complete and it were receptive, no nonexistence would enter into it. For when the cause is complete and the recipient is receptive, the effect must exist. Wherever nonexistence occurs in it, this is due to something lacking in the cause or in the recipient; it is therefore not pure existence.

It thus becomes clear that wherever a cause fails to produce its effect, if this is because a condition is missing, that is a nonexistence. If it is because an impediment exists, that impediment only becomes an impediment because of the cause’s weakness—which, again, is the absence of its strength and completeness. Thus, existence is not the cause of pure nonexistence. This also clarifies the fourfold division: pure existence can only be good.

This is further explained by the fact that every evil in the world falls into one of two categories: either pain or a cause of pain. Causes of pain include evil deeds that entail punishment. Pain that actually exists arises only from some kind of nonexistence, as when it is caused by a disruption of continuity. This disruption is the absence of the cohesion and connection between the two, and that is the evil and corruption.

As for the cause of pain, I have established in a “lengthy treatise” that the root of sins is the failure to fulfil obligations, not the commission of prohibited acts, and that prohibited acts are committed only because obligations have not been fulfilled. Thus, the root of sins is the failure to fulfil obligations, and the root of pain

---

## Page 7091

is the absence of health. Hence:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) used to teach them to say in the sermon of need: ‘We seek refuge in Allah from the evil of our souls and from the evils of our deeds.’”

One thus seeks refuge from the evil arising from one’s soul—its sins and transgressions—and from the evils of one’s deeds: the punishments and suffering they entail. For the words “and from the evils of our deeds” may refer either to the evil in the deeds themselves or to their punishments. In Allah’s Book, the word rendered “evils” denotes harm that brings distress to a person, and it may also denote evil deeds. Allah Most High says:

“If good touches you, it distresses them; but if harm befalls you, they rejoice at it.”

And He Most High says:

“But if adversity befalls them because of what their hands have done, then man is indeed ungrateful.”

It is well known that the evil of the soul consists of evil deeds. The evils of those deeds would therefore be the harm and punishments resulting from them. One would thus be seeking refuge from both kinds of evil: evil deeds and their punishments. This is like the supplication for refuge that we are commanded to recite in prayer:

“I seek refuge in You from the punishment of Hell, from the punishment of the grave, from the trials of life and death, and from the trial of the False Messiah.”

He thus commanded us to seek refuge from punishment—the punishment of the Hereafter and that of the intermediate realm—and from the cause of punishment, and from the trials of life and death and the trial of the False Messiah. He mentioned the particular trial—the trial of the False Messiah—after the general trial, for it is the greatest of all trials, as stated in the authentic hadith:

“From the creation of Adam until the coming of the Hour, there is no trial greater than the trial of the False Messiah.”

---

## Page 7092

Section

Once it is clear that the servant—and every created being—is dependent on Allah and in need of Him, not of anyone besides Him, it follows that he can find sufficiency neither in himself nor in anyone other than his Lord; for that other being is also dependent on Allah and in need of Him. Among the sayings transmitted from Abū Yazīd—may Allah have mercy on him—is:

“A created being seeking help from another created being is like a drowning person seeking help from another drowning person.”

Shaykh Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Qurashī is reported to have said:

“A created being seeking help from another created being is like a prisoner seeking help from another prisoner.”

This is only an approximation; in reality, it is like nonexistence seeking help from nonexistence. For unless the True One creates strength and power in the one whose help is sought, that being has nothing of his own. He, glory be to Him, says:

“Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

And He Most High says:

“They intercede only for those with whom He is pleased.”

And He Most High says:

“But they cannot harm anyone through it except by Allah’s permission.”

The term “servant” encompasses two meanings. The first refers to one who serves Him involuntarily, as He says:

“There is no one in the heavens and the earth who will not come to the Most Merciful as a servant.”

And He says:

“To Him all those in the heavens and the earth have submitted, willingly or unwillingly.”

And He says:

“Originator of the heavens and the earth”

“All

---

## Page 7093

are devoutly obedient to Him.” And He says:

“To Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly.”

The “second” sense is that of one who worships willingly: someone who worships Him and seeks His help. This is the meaning intended in His words:

“The servants of the Most Merciful are those who walk upon the earth humbly.”

And His words:

“A spring from which Allah’s servants drink, making it flow abundantly.”

And His words:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants.”

And His words:

“Except Your chosen servants among them.”

And His words:

“O My servants, no fear shall be upon you today, nor shall you grieve.”

And His words:

“Remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.”

And His words:

“Then He revealed to His servant what He revealed.”

And His words:

“What an excellent servant! Indeed, he was ever turning back to Allah.”

And His words:

“Glory be to the One who took His servant on a journey by night.”

And His words:

“And when the servant of Allah stood up calling upon Him.”

A person may at times lack this kind of servitude. The first kind, however, is an inseparable attribute if what is meant by it is his being subject to divine decree and the Creator’s control over him. He Most High says:

“Do they seek a religion other than Allah’s, when to Him all those in the heavens and the earth have submitted, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will be returned?”

The prevailing view among the early generations is that the submission intended here is their submitting to Him in humility and lowliness, not merely the Lord’s control over them, as in His words:

“To Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly.”

This humility and lowliness are likewise inseparable from every servant; he cannot escape them, even though

---

## Page 7094

he may at times turn away from his Lord and act arrogantly. In reality, however, he cannot escape humbling and abasing himself before Him. Yet the believer submits to Him willingly, loving Him and obeying His command, whereas the disbeliever humbles himself before Him only out of desire or fear. Once that passes, he turns away from his Lord, as He says:

“When adversity touches man, he calls upon Us, whether lying on his side, sitting, or standing. But when We remove his adversity, he goes on as though he had never called upon Us concerning the adversity that touched him.”

And He says:

“When adversity touches you at sea, all those you call upon are forgotten except Him. But when He delivers you safely to land, you turn away. Man is ever ungrateful.”

The creature’s need and servitude are intrinsic to it: it cannot exist without them. Dependence is inseparable from all things made and created. By virtue of this, they belong to their Creator and Originator, for they cannot subsist without Him. People differ only in their awareness of this need and utter dependence, or its absence from their hearts.

“Furthermore,” the servant needs Allah as the One he worships and loves with reverence and veneration. He is the ultimate object of his pursuit and desire, and the highest goal of his aspirations. The servant can find no soundness except through this. Love is the source of all movement, and Allah alone deserves to be loved for His own sake. Whoever loves anything alongside Allah associates partners with Him, and his love is corrupt. Sound, beneficial love is only love of Allah and love for Allah’s sake. A person needs Allah both in worshipping Him and in seeking His help, in order to surrender and submit to the One upon whom you depend, who is your Lord and your God.

---

## Page 7095

This knowledge and practice are innate and unavoidable. Souls know their need for their Creator and humble themselves before the One they need. His self-sufficiency is part of His unique quality of being al-Samad, the One upon whom all depend, for:

“Everyone in the heavens and the earth asks of Him.”

This is recognition of His lordship through seeking His help, relying upon Him, supplicating to Him, and asking of Him. Yet this alone does not suffice souls until they know what knowledge and deeds will set them right: worshipping Him and turning to Him in devotion. The servant was created only to worship his Lord. His well-being, perfection, delight, joy, and happiness lie in worshipping his Lord and turning to Him. This goes beyond merely asking of Him and needing Him.

All created things come into being by His will, subsist through His power and His word, need Him, depend upon Him, and submit to Him willingly or unwillingly. When the servant recognizes this, surrenders to Him, and humbles himself before Him, he has believed in His lordship and perceived his own need and dependence upon Him. He then asks of Him, relies upon Him, and seeks His help, whether through his condition or his words, unlike one who arrogantly turns away from Him and refuses to ask of Him.

Now, whoever seeks His help and asks of Him may ask for something he is commanded to seek, something he is forbidden to seek, or something permissible for him. The “first” is the state of the blessed believers, whose condition is expressed in:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

The “second” is the state of the disbelievers, the defiantly disobedient, and the sinners who have some belief in Him, even though they are disbelievers, as He says:

“Most of them do not believe in Allah without associating partners with Him.”

They believe in His lordship but associate partners with Him in worship, as in the following: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Husayn al-Khuzāʿī:

---

## Page 7096

‘O Husayn, how many gods do you worship?’

He replied, ‘Seven gods: six on earth and one in heaven.’

He asked, ‘Which of them do you turn to in hope and fear?’

He replied, ‘The One in heaven.’

He said, ‘Accept Islam, and I will teach you words through which Allah Most High will benefit you.’ So he accepted Islam, and he said, ‘Say: O Allah, inspire me to follow right guidance and protect me from the evil of my own soul.’” Reported by Ahmad and others.

For this reason, He, glorified and exalted is He, says:

“When My servants ask you about Me, I am indeed near. I answer the call of the supplicant when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be rightly guided.”

He, glorified is He, tells us that He is near to His servants and answers the call of the supplicant when he calls upon Him. This is a statement of His lordship over them, His granting their requests, and His answering their supplications. For when they call upon Him, they acknowledge His lordship over them, even if they are also disbelievers in another respect and are defiantly disobedient or sinners. He Most High says:

“When harm befalls you at sea, those you invoke vanish from your thoughts, except Him. But when He delivers you safely to land, you turn away. Man is ever ungrateful.”

He Most High also says:

“When harm touches man, he calls upon Us, lying on his side, sitting, or standing. But when We remove his harm, he goes on as though he had never called upon Us to relieve any harm that had touched him. Thus were the deeds of the transgressors made appealing to them.”

There are many similar passages in the Quran. Then He commands them to do two things, saying:

“So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be rightly guided.”

The “first” is to obey Him in the worship and seeking of His help that He has commanded them. The “second” is to believe in His lordship and His sole right to worship, and that He is their Lord and their God.

For this reason, it has been said: supplications are answered through sound belief and complete obedience;

---

## Page 7097

for He followed the verse concerning supplication with His words:

“So let them respond to Me and believe in Me.”

Obedience and worship serve the servant’s true welfare, bringing him happiness and salvation. As for answering his supplication and granting his request, this may benefit him or harm him. He Most High says:

“Man prays for evil as he prays for good, and man is ever hasty.”

He Most High also says:

“If Allah were to hasten evil for people as they seek to hasten good, their appointed term would have come to an end.”

He Most High says of the polytheists:

“And when they said, ‘O Allah, if this is indeed the truth from You, then rain down stones upon us from heaven, or bring us a painful punishment.’”

He also says:

“If you sought a judgment, judgment has now come to you. But if you desist, that will be better for you.”

And He says:

“Call upon your Lord humbly and secretly. Indeed, He does not love those who transgress.”

And He says:

“Relate to them the story of the one to whom We gave Our signs, but he cast them off. Satan then pursued him, and he became one of those who went astray.”

“Had We willed, We would have raised him through them, but he clung to the earth and followed his desires”—to the end of the verse.

And He says:

“If anyone disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you, say, ‘Come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves; then let us pray earnestly and invoke Allah’s curse upon the liars.’”

When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered upon Jābir’s household, he said:

“Do not invoke anything but good upon yourselves, for the angels say ‘Āmīn’ to what you say.”

---

## Page 7098

Section

Just as the servant is always in need of Allah to help him, answer his supplication, grant his requests, and fulfill his needs, he likewise needs Him in order to know what is good for him and what he should seek and desire. This is what commands, prohibitions, and the revealed Law concern. Otherwise, if the need he sought and desired is fulfilled but is not in his best interests, it harms him, even if it brings him immediate pleasure and benefit. What matters is pure benefit, or benefit that outweighs harm. Allah has made this known to His servants through His messengers and His scriptures.

The messengers taught and purified them, commanded them to do what benefits them, and forbade them from doing what harms them. They explained to them that Allah alone, with no partner, must be the One they seek, the goal they pursue, and the One they worship, just as He is their Lord and Creator. If they abandon His worship or associate others with Him, they will suffer manifest loss and go far astray. Whatever strength, knowledge, standing, wealth, or other things they have been given will then harm them—even though they depend upon Allah for these things, seek His help with them, and acknowledge His Lordship. Theirs will be an evil destination and a wretched abode. This is the concern of the religious command prescribed in the revealed Law and of the religious will

---

## Page 7099

prescribed in the revealed Law, just as the former is the concern of the command and will pertaining to creation and divine decree. Allah, glorified is He, has blessed the believers with help and guidance. He has shown them the way by sending messengers and revealing scriptures, and has helped them follow it in knowledge and practice. Likewise, He has bestowed His favors upon them and the rest of creation by creating them, providing for them, and granting them well-being. He has also favored most of creation by making them aware of His Lordship over them and their need for Him, granting their requests, and answering their supplications. He, the Exalted, says:

“Everyone in the heavens and the earth asks of Him. Every day He is engaged in a matter.”

Thus, all the inhabitants of the heavens and the earth ask of Him. There are therefore four levels:

“One group” neither worshipped Him nor sought His help, although He created them, provided for them, and granted them well-being.

“Another group” sought His help, and He helped them, but they did not worship Him.

“Another group” sought to worship and obey Him, but did not seek His help or put their trust in Him.

“The fourth category” consists of those who worshipped Him and sought His help, so He helped them worship and obey Him. These are the ones who believe and do righteous deeds. He, glorified is He, has explained the favor He has bestowed specifically upon the believers in His words:

“He has endeared faith to you and made it beautiful in your hearts, and has made disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience hateful to you. Those are the rightly guided.”

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad, the best of the messengers, and all his family and Companions.

---

## Page 7100

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

The servant is always in dire need of Allah to guide him to the straight path. He therefore desperately needs what this supplication seeks, for there is no escape from punishment and no attainment of happiness except through this guidance. Whoever lacks it is either among those who have incurred Allah’s wrath or among those who have gone astray. This guidance can only be attained through Allah’s guidance, and this verse is among those that demonstrate the falsity of the Qadariyyah’s doctrine.

Both the objection, “He has already guided them, so they have no need to ask,” and the reply, “What is sought is the continuation of that guidance,” are the words of those who do not understand the true nature of causes or what Allah has commanded. For following the straight path means that the servant does, at every moment, what he has been commanded to do at that moment, in knowledge and action, and refrains from what he has been forbidden. This requires him, at every moment, to know and carry out what he has been commanded at that moment

---

## Page 7101

and to know what he has been forbidden to do, and to develop a firm resolve to carry out what is commanded and a firm aversion that leads him to shun what is forbidden. Such detailed knowledge and resolve cannot conceivably be acquired by the servant all at once. Rather, at every moment he needs Allah to instil in his heart the knowledge and resolve by which he can follow that straight path.

Certainly, he has received general guidance: that the Quran is true, the Messenger is true, and the religion of Islam is true. This is indeed true, but such general guidance will not suffice unless he receives detailed guidance concerning every particular thing he does or leaves undone. These particulars bewilder most people’s minds, and whims and desires overpower their reason, so great is the hold that desires and doubts have over them.

Man was created deeply unjust and ignorant. His starting point is a lack of knowledge and an inclination toward the evil he desires. He therefore constantly needs detailed knowledge to dispel his ignorance, and justice in his love and hatred, pleasure and anger, action and abstention, giving and withholding, eating and drinking, sleeping and waking. In everything he says and does, he needs knowledge that counters his ignorance and justice that counters his wrongdoing. Unless Allah graciously grants him this detailed knowledge and justice in every particular, he will have enough ignorance and injustice within him to lead him away from the straight path.

Allah Most High said to His Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—after the Treaty of al-Hudaybiyyah and the Pledge of Divine Pleasure:

“Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory.”

Up to His words, Exalted is He: “and guide you to a path

---

## Page 7102

that is straight.” If this was his condition at or near the end of his life, what, then, of anyone else?

The straight path has been explained as the Quran, Islam, and the path of servitude to Allah. All these explanations are true: it may be described in these and other ways. The “Quran” contains essential matters, subtleties, prohibitions, reports, narratives, and other things. Unless Allah guides His servant to them, he remains ignorant of them and astray from them. The same applies to “Islam” and the noble qualities, acts of obedience, and praiseworthy traits it encompasses, and likewise to “worship and all that it encompasses.”

The servant’s need to ask for this guidance is indispensable to his happiness, salvation, and success. His need for provision and victory is different. Allah provides for him, and when his provision ceases, he dies—but death is inevitable. If he is among those guided by Allah, he will be happy before death and after it, and death will lead him to eternal happiness. The same applies to victory: if it is decreed that he is overcome and killed, he dies a martyr, and his being killed completes the blessing bestowed upon him.

Thus, the need for guidance is greater than the need for victory and provision; indeed, there is no comparison between them. For when he is guided, he becomes one of the God-fearing:

“And whoever fears Allah, He will make for him a way out”
“and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

He also becomes one of those who support Allah and His Messenger, and whoever supports Allah, Allah will support him. He becomes one of Allah’s soldiers, and it is they who will prevail. This is why this supplication is the one that has been made obligatory.

---

## Page 7103

and it also encompasses provision and victory. For when a person is guided, then instructs and guides others through his words, his deeds, and the very sight of him, such complete guidance is the greatest means of obtaining provision and victory. It is thus clear that this supplication encompasses everything one could seek.

This also makes clear to you that nothing else can take the place of al-Fātihah, and that its superiority over other speech is greater than the superiority of bowing and prostration over all other acts of humble submission. If those particular actions are indispensable, then these particular words have an even stronger claim to be so. And Allah knows best.

May Allah bless His Prophet Muhammad and grant him abundant peace.
