## Page 7104

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

I have discussed in various places how “Surah al-Baqarah” establishes the foundations of knowledge and the principles of religion. Allah Most High begins it by mentioning His Book, which guides the God-fearing. He describes the condition of those who follow guidance, then the disbelievers, then the hypocrites. These are “declarative statements.” He then introduces “directive statements,” calling people to worship Him alone. He then mentions the evidence for this: spreading out the earth, building the heavens, sending down water, and bringing forth fruits as provision for His servants.

He then establishes “messengership” and mentions “the promise of reward and the threat of punishment.” He then mentions the beginning of “prophethood and guidance,” and what He has spread throughout the world in creation and command. He then mentions teaching Adam the names and commanding the angels to prostrate before him because of the knowledge with which He had honoured him. This establishes the same kind of guidance and religion of truth with which Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent. Thus He recounts the message common to the prophets.

He then turns to addressing the Children of Israel and recounting the story of Moses with them. This includes an affirmation of Moses’ prophethood, for he is Muhammad’s counterpart. Thus He mentions Adam, who was the first,

---

## Page 7105

and Moses, who was his counterpart. These were the two who debated one another. Moses killed a person, and Allah forgave him; Adam ate from the tree, and Allah accepted his repentance.

The account of Moses contains a refutation of the Sabians and others who acknowledge prophethood in principle but do not regard following what the prophets brought as obligatory, and who may reinterpret the prophets’ reports. It also contains a refutation of the People of the Book, through its command to believe in what Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought and its affirmation of his prophethood. Allah mentions the condition of those who turned from prophethood to sorcery, and He mentions abrogation, which some of them deny. He also mentions the Christians and states that neither community would be pleased with him until he followed their religion. All of this establishes the foundations of religion: Allah’s oneness and messengership.

Then He, glory be to Him, begins to explain the laws of Islam that accord with the religion of Abraham. He mentions Abraham, who was a leader, and the building of the House, whose veneration distinguishes the people of Islam from others. He mentions facing it in prayer and establishes this practice, for it is the distinguishing sign of the religion, setting its adherents apart from others. Hence the expression “the people of the qiblah,” just as it is said:

“Whoever prays as we pray, faces our direction of prayer, and eats what we slaughter is a Muslim.”

He mentions those “pilgrimage rites” that are specific to the place. Hajj has both a prescribed place and a prescribed time, whereas “umrah” has only a prescribed place. Devotional retreat, bowing, and prostration are prescribed there, but are not restricted to it or to any particular place or time. Prayer, however, is subject to the requirement of facing it. Thus He, glory be to Him, mentions these five forms of worship: devotional retreat

---

## Page 7106

and prayer, circumambulation, umrah, and Hajj. Circumambulation is tied only to a particular place. He then follows this with matters connected to the House: going between the two hills and the affirmation that there is no blame in doing so. This answers the Ansār’s pre-Islamic aversion to going between them because they had dedicated their pilgrimage to Manāt, and also answers those who had refrained from going between them.

The mention of going between the hills comes after the acts of worship connected to the House—indeed, those involving hearts, bodies, and wealth as well—and after the command to seek help through patience and prayer, without which religion cannot be upheld. This served as an introduction to jihad, which is founded upon patience, for it completes the requirements connected to the House. Since the adherents of the various religions do not disagree concerning it, the House can only be upheld through jihad in its defence. He mentions patience in fulfilling what He has prescribed and enduring what He has decreed, and explains His favour to this community in the glad tidings given to the patient. It has been granted what no previous community was granted, making this one of its distinctive qualities and religious hallmarks, like the acts of worship connected to the House.

For this reason, He pairs Hajj with jihad, since both come within Allah’s cause. Jihad is the foremost activity in Allah’s cause, according to explicit textual evidence and scholarly consensus. Hajj likewise comes within it, according to the sounder view, as he said:

“Hajj is part of Allah’s cause.”

By condemning those who conceal knowledge, He makes clear that this is known to the People of the Book. He then mentions that He accepts no religion other than this.

At its beginning, He says:

“So do not set up rivals to Allah.”

And in its midst:

“Yet among people are those who take others besides Allah as rivals.”

The first is a general prohibition, while the second is a specific prohibition. He mentions this after mentioning the House, to deter people from turning to

---

## Page 7107

rivals to Him and His House, such as idols, graves, and the like. Before this, He affirms His oneness, declaring:

“There is no god but Him, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.”

He then mentions the signs that attest to His oneness. Next, He discusses what is lawful and unlawful, granting broad permission regarding food, for the Messenger was sent with the religion of pure monotheism and its emblem, the House. He describes its ease regarding permissible matters and cases of bloodshed, through the retribution He prescribed and the acceptance of blood money.

He then mentions acts of worship connected to time: bequests, which are connected to death, followed by fasting, which is connected to Ramadan, and the devotional retreat associated with it. He mentions this retreat among both place-bound and time-bound acts of worship, since it is restricted to mosques and, in time—whether by recommendation or obligation—to periods of fasting. Earlier, He placed it between circumambulation and prayer, because circumambulation is restricted to the Sacred Mosque, whereas prayer is prescribed throughout the earth, and devotional retreat lies between the two.

He follows this with the prohibition against consuming wealth wrongfully, explaining that forbidden things fall into “two categories”: those forbidden in themselves, such as carrion, and those forbidden because of how they are acquired, such as usury and usurped property. Thus, He follows the discussion of what is fixed with things whose prohibition is fixed and intrinsic, while amid acts of worship tied to changing times, He mentions things whose prohibition is variable.

For this reason, He follows it with His words:

“They ask you about the new moons,”

and the rest of the verse. These mark the times of time-bound acts of worship. He explains that He has made them measures of time for people in their religious and worldly affairs, and for Hajj, since angels and jinn also perform pilgrimage to the House. This too was

---

## Page 7108

concerned with Hajj being tied to a particular time, much as it is tied to the House as a place. For this reason, He next mentions those rulings of Hajj that relate specifically to time, although place is also integral to completing both Hajj and ‘umrah.

He mentions “the pilgrim prevented from completing the rites.” He also mentions placing the financial aspect of release from ihrām—the sacrificial offering—before its bodily aspect—shaving the head. The pilgrim leaves the consecrated state gradually, being released first from the lighter restrictions, then from the next lightest. Thus, sexual intercourse itself is the last thing to become permissible, since it is the gravest prohibited act, and no other prohibited act invalidates the pilgrimage rites.

He mentions “tamattu‘—performing ‘umrah followed by Hajj with release from ihrām in between”—because it relates to both time and place. A pilgrim is not performing tamattu‘ unless he enters ihrām for ‘umrah during the months of Hajj and his family are not residents in the vicinity of the Sacred Mosque. Such a person is a pilgrim from afar: the benefit of tamattu‘ is evident in his case, since he enjoys the ease of being spared one of the two journeys. For a local resident, however, it makes no difference whether he performs tamattu‘ or performs ‘umrah before the months of Hajj.

He then mentions the time of Hajj, explaining that it falls within well-known months. He mentions entering ihrām and standing at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, since these are tied to particular times and places. Thus, He says:

“Whoever commits himself to Hajj during them.”

He does not add “and ‘umrah,” because one may commit oneself to ‘umrah at any time. Undoubtedly, the Sunnah is to commit oneself to Hajj during its months; whoever does so before then has acted contrary to the Sunnah. Either he must fulfil what he has undertaken, as with a vow—since this does not overturn what is prescribed, and is not like offering a prayer before its time—or he must remain bound by

---

## Page 7109

ihrām, while his Hajj lapses and he is regarded as performing ‘umrah. These are two well-known views.

Then He commands that He be remembered upon completing the rites. Their completion—Allah knows best—means removing the traces of dishevelment and leaving ihrām. Hence He says thereafter:

“Remember Allah during the appointed days.”

This too is an act of worship tied to both time and place: remembering Allah Most High when stoning the pillars and in conjunction with the prayers. That it is tied to place is indicated by His words:

“Whoever hastens to leave after two days…”

—and the rest of the verse. Hastening or delaying concerns departure from that place. Hence these days are named after their location—the days of Minā—and after the activity performed during them—the days of Tashrīq. Similarly, one speaks of the night of Jam‘, the night of Muzdalifah, the Day of ‘Arafah, the Day of the Greater Hajj, the day of Eid, and Friday. Times are thus named after activities and the places where those activities occur, since time follows movement, and movement follows place.

Reflect, then, on the harmony of the Quran and the interconnectedness of its passages, and on how He mentions the rulings of Hajj in this surah in two places: alongside the mention of His House, where He discusses matters relating to the place of Hajj; and where He mentions the crescent moons, where He discusses matters relating to its time. He also mentions fighting at the Sacred Mosque and retaliation in kind during the sacred month, because these concern time associated with place. Hence He—glory be to Him—links the crescent moons’ marking of time for people with their marking of time for Hajj. He also explains that “righteousness” does not consist in a man making life hard for himself and doing what has no benefit

---

## Page 7110

, such as remaining exposed to the sky and refusing the shelter of his own roof, so that when he wishes to enter his house, he enters only from the back. He explained that appointing the crescent moon as a marker of time for Hajj was a religious prescription of this kind, and that it entailed only the prescription of piety.

He then discussed the rulings concerning marriage and mothers, as well as wealth, charitable giving, usury, debts, and other matters. He concluded the surah with the great supplication asking for the removal of burdens and shackles, for pardon, forgiveness, and mercy, and for victory over the disbelieving people, who are enemies of the religion He has prescribed in His clear Book.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 7111

Shaykh al-Islām said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, to the point that some works of Quranic commentary contain only erroneous interpretations of them.

Among them are His words:

“Indeed, whoever commits an evil deed and is encompassed by his sin”—to the end of the verse.

He mentioned that the well-known interpretation is that the evil deed means associating partners with Allah. Another interpretation is that it means a major sin in which a person remains until death; this was the view of ʿIkrimah. Mujāhid said: “It refers to sins that surround the heart.”

I say: The correct approach is to mention the views of the early generations, even if some are weak, for the evidence will make their weakness clear. Their views should not be omitted merely because they agree with the position of a group of religious innovators. They themselves report from some of the early generations that the scribe made an error in this verse, as has also been said about other verses. Whoever denies any part of the Quran after its transmission through numerous independent channels has been established is called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is put to death. But before its transmission in this manner has been established for him, he is not called upon to repent; rather, the matter is explained to him. The same applies to opinions contradicted by hadiths, whether in jurisprudence, Sufism, creed, or other matters.

Mujāhid’s statement is correct, as indicated by the authentic hadith: “When a servant commits a sin

---

## Page 7112

, a black mark is made on his heart,” and so on. What covers the heart is called “rust,” “stamping,” “sealing,” “locking,” and the like. This, then, concerns the sin in which he persists. A sin’s “encompassing” him means that it surrounds him so that he cannot escape. This is being held captive by what his soul has earned: it is kept from what would bring it salvation in both worlds. Sins shackle and imprison those who commit them, preventing them from roaming the open expanse of monotheism and gathering the fruits of righteous deeds.

Some who affiliate themselves with the Sunnah say that anyone who commits a major sin will invariably be punished. Most hold otherwise: Allah, glorified is He, weighs good deeds against evil deeds. This is what the Quran and Sunnah indicate, and this is what weighing means. Nevertheless, interpreting “an evil deed” as associating partners with Allah is more evident, because He, glorified is He, distinguishes between what is committed and what encompasses a person. Had they been one and the same, He would not have distinguished between them. A person who associates partners with Allah has other sins besides that association which encompass him, because he has not repented of them.

Moreover, the word sayyi’ah (“an evil deed”) is indefinite, and there is agreement that evil deeds in general are not intended. Furthermore, the word sayyi’ah has been used in several passages to mean associating partners with Allah. Here, “an evil deed” means an evil state, an evil place, or the like, just as in His words:

“Our Lord, give us good in this world.”

That is, a good state encompassing every kind of good. This word functions as an adjective and may be transferred from adjectival to nominal use; it is used intransitively or

---

## Page 7113

transitively. One says, “This matter was bad,” meaning that it was reprehensible; one also says, “This grieved me.”

Regarding His words,

“But those who have earned evil deeds—the recompense of an evil deed is its like,”

Ibn ʿAbbās said: “They associated partners with Allah.” This is because He described them only in these terms; had they believed, they would have had good deeds. Likewise, when He said “earned an evil deed,” He mentioned no good deed. This parallels His words, exalted is He:

“For those who do good is the best reward.”

That is, they did what was good, namely what they were commanded to do. Similarly, “an evil deed” encompasses what is forbidden, and thus includes associating partners with Allah.

---

## Page 7114

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

Allah Most High said:

“We have certainly created above you seven paths, and We have never been heedless of creation.”

And He Most High said:

“We will surely question those to whom messengers were sent, and We will surely question the messengers. Then We will surely recount to them with knowledge, and We were never absent.”

He Most High also said:

“Those who believe in the unseen.”

Some of the early generations said that “the unseen” refers to Allah, or that belief in Allah is included in belief in the unseen. Thus, in one passage He denied that He was absent, while in another He described Himself as unseen.

For this reason, people have differed over this issue. Some speculative theologians, both of our school and others—such as al-Qādī, Ibn ‘Aqīl, and Ibn al-Zāghūnī—advocate reasoning by analogy from the observable to the unobservable, by which they mean Allah. They hold that this analogy is established through definition, cause, evidence, and condition, as they maintain

---

## Page 7115

in matters concerning the divine attributes, when affirming knowledge, awareness, will, and so forth.

Others objected to their position, including Shaykh Abū Muhammad in his epistle to the people of Ra’s al-‘Ayn. He said, “Allah is not to be called absent,” citing what has already been mentioned.

The decisive distinction between the two groups is that the terms ghayb (“the unseen”) and ghā’ib (“absent”) express relative meanings. They may refer to what is absent from us and therefore not perceived by us, or to what is absent from us and therefore does not perceive us. For when one of us is entirely absent from another, neither perceives the other. Allah, glorified be He, however, witnesses His servants, watches over them, and keeps them under His oversight. Not an atom’s weight in the earth or heaven escapes Him. He is therefore not absent. Rather, He is unseen because His servants have not seen Him. This is why He is included in the unseen in which one believes, although He is not absent.

For ghā’ib is an active participle: one says ghāba, yaghību (“he was absent, he is absent”), hence ghā’ib (“one who is absent”). Allah witnesses and is not absent. As for ghayb, it is the verbal noun in the sequence ghāba, yaghību, ghayban. A verbal noun is often used in place of an active participle, as with ‘adl (“justice,” used for a just person), sawm (“fasting,” used for one who fasts), and zawr (“visiting,” used for visitors). It is also used in place of a passive participle, as with khalq (“creation,” meaning what is created), rizq (“provision,” meaning what is provided), and dirham darb al-amīr (“a dirham of the ruler’s minting”).

He therefore pairs ghayb with shahāda (“witnessing”), which is also a verbal noun. Shahāda denotes either what is witnessed or the one who witnesses. Ghayb denotes either the one from whom something is absent—who therefore does not witness, the opposite of shahāda—or the absent one who is beyond our perception and whom we have not witnessed. Thus, referring to Him by the verbal noun draws attention

---

## Page 7116

to its relation to another: that is, He is not absent in Himself; rather, He is absent from another, or another is absent from Him. It may also be said that the terms “the witnessed” and “the unseen” encompass both relations: the witnessed is what witnesses us and what we witness, while the unseen is what is absent from us and from which we are absent, so that we do not witness it.

In any case, describing Him as unseen means that we do not witness Him. This is a sound Quranic designation. Had they said, “reasoning about the unseen (ghayb) by analogy with the witnessed (shahāda),” their wording would have been consistent with this. As for speaking of an analogy concerning “the absent (ghā’ib),” the wording differs on its face, although the meaning agrees. Hence the disagreement over using this expression.

---

## Page 7117

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

The primary meaning of mathal (likeness) is something similar to another. It is of two kinds, since a given proposition may express either a particular resemblance or a general, universal one. Universal propositions that are known and stated correspond to, and resemble, everything that falls within their scope. This is called qiyās (inference) both in the language of the early generations and in the terminology of logicians. Comparing one particular thing with another is also called qiyās in the language of the early generations and in the terminology of jurists. This is what is called analogical inference.

Some later scholars—such as al-Ghazālī and others—claimed that the term qiyās properly applies only to this latter form, whereas calling the combination of universal propositions qiyās is metaphorical. Their reason is that nothing in it is compared to anything else; rather, the universality of the judgment entails that all the individual cases it covers share equally in that judgment. Others took the opposite view, such as Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm, who maintained

---

## Page 7118

that the term qiyās should apply only to those universal propositions, and that this is valid inference.

The correct view is the linguistic usage of the early generations, which accords with the Quran, as I shall explain: both are forms of inference, comparison, and reasoning by correspondence. This is evident in analogical inference. As for inference by taklīl and universal inclusion, each individual case is measured against that universal standard established in knowledge and discourse, which serves as its basis, just as a particular case is measured against a comparable original case. In both, the basis is the likeness, and inference consists in drawing a likeness.

The underlying meaning of this expression—Allah knows best—is determining a measure. Thus, setting out a likeness for something means determining a counterpart for it, just as inference originally means measuring one thing against another. Related usages include striking a dirham, which determines its measure, and imposing the poll tax and land tax, which determines their amounts. A darība is an assessed levy. The expression darb fī al-ard means travelling through the land, because the extent of the walker’s track corresponds to the extent of his movement. Likewise, striking with a stick determines the pain by means of the instrument.

Darb involves gathering, combining, and determining a measure, just as darība denotes money collected together. Darība also means a person’s constitution. Striking a dirham means bringing silver together into a combined, measured form. Imposing the poll tax and land tax means prescribing and assessing them over successive years. Travelling through the land consists of measured movements brought together to reach a definite destination. Another related usage is quilting a padded garment: drawing together its interstices in successive rows.

For this reason, logicians call a syllogistic form a darb, just as a single kind is called a darb because its members belong together and agree with one another. And since drawing a likeness brings together two items of knowledge from which knowledge is sought

---

## Page 7119

as a third, it is comparable to the mating of a male animal, from which offspring is produced. This is why they classify syllogistic forms as productive or sterile, just as a male animal’s mating with a female may be productive or sterile. Each of the two kinds of drawing a likeness—which is inference—sometimes serves to illustrate a meaning and make it understood, and sometimes serves to demonstrate its truth and establish assent to it. Thus, there is inference that conveys a conception and inference that establishes assent. Reflect on this. Often both are intended, for drawing a likeness clarifies both the intended conception and the judgment concerning it.

Drawing likenesses to convey meanings takes two forms, corresponding to the two forms of inference:

The first consists of particular likenesses, in which a derivative case is compared with a specific original case, whether actual or hypothetical. There are forty-odd such likenesses in the Quran, as in His words:

“Their likeness is that of someone who kindled a fire,”

and so on; and His words:

“The likeness of those who spend their wealth in the way of Allah is that of a grain that grows seven ears, in each ear a hundred grains.”

And His words:

“O you who believe, do not nullify your charities with reminders of your generosity and hurtful words, like someone who spends his wealth to be seen by people and does not believe in Allah and the Last Day. His likeness is that of a smooth rock covered with dust,”

and the rest of the verse;

“And the likeness of those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure, with firm conviction in their hearts, is that of a garden on high ground: heavy rain falls upon it, and it yields twice its produce.”

For the comparison between those He describes—the hypocrites and those who spend, both the sincere among them and those who show off—and the likenesses He, glorified be He, sets forth

---

## Page 7120

is a form of analogical reasoning, as when one says: “The case of someone who kills with a fuller’s implement is like that of someone who kills with a sword,” or “The case of a cat falling into oil is like that of a mouse falling into clarified butter,” and so forth. It rests on identifying the common features and differences between the two cases with respect to the attributes relevant to the judgment one seeks to affirm or deny.

His saying, “Its likeness is like that of such-and-such,” compares one mental representation with another, since it is through these that the analogy is drawn. Someone drawing a comparison considers one case, and it becomes represented in his mind; he then considers the other, and it too becomes represented in his mind. He then compares the two representations and finds them alike. Thus he knows that the two cases are alike in themselves because they are alike in his knowledge. He cannot compare the two cases themselves until each is represented in his mind, for judging a thing depends upon first conceiving it. This is why—and Allah knows best—it is said: “The likeness of this is like that of …” (1)

In some passages, He, glorified be He, mentions the original case used for comparison so that the judgment concerning the derivative case may be inferred from it, without explicitly mentioning the derivative case. An example is His saying:

“Would any of you wish to have a garden of palm trees and grapevines, beneath which rivers flow, containing every kind of fruit, and then be overtaken by old age,”

through to His words:

“Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may reflect.”

This requires reflection. For this reason, ʿUmar asked the Companions present with him about this verse, and Ibn ʿAbbās gave him an answer that satisfied him. The recounting of narratives is similar: they are all likenesses that serve as original cases for analogy

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 7121

and analogical inference. It is impossible to enumerate all the cases to which these narratives apply, since everyone has some share in one of the situations they describe. Thus, it is said of them:

“Indeed, in their stories there is a lesson for people of understanding.”

And after recounting them, it is said:

“So take heed, O people of insight.”

And He says:

“There has already been a sign for you in the two forces that met,”

through to His words:

“Indeed, in that is a lesson for people of insight.”

Drawing such inferences is precisely analogical reasoning. Thus, when Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about the compensation payable for fingers, he said: “It is the same for all of them. Compare them in this respect to the teeth.” That is, reason by analogy with the teeth: compensation for teeth is equal despite their differing functions, and the same applies to fingers. One also says, “I assessed the dirhams against the standard weight,” meaning that one measured them by it.

“The Second Type”

Universal likenesses. People have found it difficult to understand why these are called likenesses, just as they have found it difficult to understand why they are called forms of inference. Some have even objected to His saying:

“O mankind, a likeness is presented, so listen to it,”

asking: “Where is the likeness being presented?”

Likewise, when they hear His saying:

“We have certainly presented to mankind in this Quran every kind of likeness,”

they remain bewildered, not knowing what these likenesses are, having counted only forty-odd specific likenesses in it.

These “likenesses” are sometimes descriptions and sometimes syllogisms. When they are syllogisms, they must contain two statements, constituting two propositions and two judgments, one of which must be universal. For statements, which are propositions, are divided into singular, unquantified, universal, and particular, and each of these is further divided into statements of affirmation

---

## Page 7122

and statements of negation. Thus, presenting a likeness in the form of a syllogism must involve a general statement, a universal proposition. This is the likeness established in the mind against which particular instances are measured to determine their status. Without its universality, such inference would be impossible, since the instance whose status is sought might fall outside its scope.

Hence it is said: no syllogism can be formed from two particular propositions; rather, one of them must be universal. Nor can a syllogism be formed from two negative propositions; rather, one of them must be affirmative. Otherwise, neither of the two negations is included within the other. A statement of general application is therefore indispensable.

The total number of combinations used to construct these likenesses is sixteen. The first proposition is either particular or universal, and either affirmative or negative. These are four possibilities; multiplying them by four gives sixteen. Combinations of two particular propositions are excluded, whether both are affirmative, both negative, or one negative and the other affirmative. These account for six of the sixteen. Combinations of two negative propositions are also excluded, whether both are particular, both universal, or one of each. However, the combination of two particular negative propositions has already been included in the first exclusion, leaving two further combinations to be excluded from the sixteen.

Also excluded is the combination of a universal negative minor premise with a particular affirmative major premise. For when the major premise is particular, the negation need not intersect with it. Affirmation is different: two particular affirmations intersect. Likewise, a particular affirmation and a universal negation intersect, because what is affirmed falls within the scope of the general negation.

---

## Page 7123

Six combinations remain out of the sixteen. If one premise is a universal affirmative, the other may take any of the four forms. If it is a universal negative, it may be paired with either affirmative form, though its pairing with the universal affirmative has already been considered; the particular affirmative must be the minor premise. If it is a particular affirmative, it may be paired with either universal form, both of which have already been considered. If it is a particular negative, it may be paired only with a universal affirmative, which has likewise already been considered. Thus, six productive combinations remain, while ten are discarded. Taking both considerations into account, they become eight.

Of these ten combinations, eight depend on universal affirmation. Every combination must involve one of two things: either affirmation and universality, or negation and particularity. Combining two contradictories yields no useful result. Rather, when two contradictories of different kinds are combined—as with a universal negative and a particular affirmative—they yield a result provided that the major premise is the universal one. It is therefore clear that every syllogism requires affirmation and universality, either together in one premise or distributed between the two premises.

It should also be understood that, in most likenesses and syllogisms, only one of the two propositions is obscure; the other is evident and known. The person presenting the likeness or constructing the syllogism therefore needs only to explain the obscure proposition. The intended conclusion thereby becomes known, owing to the negative proposition closely associated with it in the reasoning. The evident proposition is the major premise, which is the more general one.

---

## Page 7124

For the more general a thing is, the more familiar it is to the mind, because its individual instances pass through the mind more frequently. The best speech conveys much in few words. This is why the evident proposition is omitted from the likenesses presented in the Quran: stating it would introduce needless length and clumsy expression. Likewise, stating the intended conclusion after mentioning both premises is considered unnecessarily lengthy.

Consider His words:

“Had there been in them gods other than Allah, both would have fallen into ruin.”

How excellent this demonstration is! Had it been followed by, “But neither has fallen into ruin; therefore, there are no gods in them besides Allah,” this would have been insipid speech, unsuited to the eloquence of revelation. Such explicit formulation merely assembles meanings in the mind, just as words are assembled from letters when spelling and writing. When teaching a child to write, we say, “bā,” “sīn,” “mīm”; together they form (bismi, “in the name of”). Once he understands this, it is no longer appropriate for him to read it by spelling out its letters, thereby stripping the speech of its beauty. Rather, the combination has become firmly established in his mind.

Similarly, once a grammarian knows that “Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah” consists of a subject and predicate, he does not keep saying, “Because it is a subject and predicate,” whenever he gives a similar construction the nominative case. Thus, combining letters into words, in sound and meaning, combining words into statements, and combining statements into likenesses are all processes of the same kind.

This is why those who construct syllogisms first discuss individual expressions and meanings, which constitute terms. They then discuss combining terms into statements, which constitute assertions, accounts, and judgments. They then discuss constructing illustrative likenesses, which constitute “syllogism,” “demonstration,” “evidence,” and “sign”

---

## Page 7125

and “mark.”

This deserves careful attention. Among the Quran’s greatest perfections is that, in the likenesses it presents and the syllogisms it sets forth, it dispenses with stating the clear, evident, familiar premise and then following this with an explicit statement of the conclusion already understood from the outset to be the intended point. Rather, a likeness is presented by mentioning what is worth stating and beneficial to know. That is true exposition and demonstration. To mention what need not be mentioned is an inability to express oneself effectively.

This reveals the error of certain ignorant rhetoricians and misguided logicians. One of the former said, “Among the methods of exposition, the demonstrative method of speculative theology occurs only rarely in the Quran.” One of the latter said, “The Quran contains no complete demonstration.” These people are among the most ignorant of all concerning both wording and meaning. For anyone who understands and reflects, the Quran employs nothing but sound demonstrative reasoning.

“Furthermore,” it should be understood that presenting likenesses and constructing syllogisms revolve around generality and particularity, negation and affirmation. Every statement is either general or particular, and either negative or affirmative. What is individually specified is particular and delimited; a particular proposition is likewise particular, but not delimited. An unrestricted expression is either general or particular in meaning. Anyone wishing to understand this subject should therefore learn the “forms of negation and generality,” for these occur in the Quran in the most eloquent arrangement.

---

## Page 7126

For example, someone judging at first glance may suppose that the “interrogative form” has no place in a syllogism, since only declarative propositions enter into it, whereas an interrogative expresses a request. Upon reflection, however, he realizes that most Quranic questions—or many of them—are rhetorical questions expressing rejection. They convey censure and prohibition when the rejection concerns divine law, or negation and denial when it concerns the existence or occurrence of something. Examples include His words:

“He presents an example for Us, forgetting his own creation. He says, ‘Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled away?’”

“He presents an example for you from among yourselves: do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners in what We have provided for you?”—and the rest of the verse.

Likewise, He says:

“Is Allah better, or what they associate with Him?”

And, when enumerating His signs:

“Is there a god alongside Allah?”

That is: did a god alongside Allah do these things? The meaning is that none but Allah did them. He also says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they themselves the creators?”

The same applies to the accompanying verses.

What we have described as the Quran’s practice is the presentation of likenesses in terms of meaning. In linguistic usage, however, “presenting a likeness” or “the likeness presented” may refer to a kind of verbal expression. Such an expression supplies a way of saying something, just as language does, but, unlike Quranic likenesses, it does not furnish evidence for a judgment. This occurs when someone utters something in verse or prose prompted by a particular occasion, and it becomes so widespread in usage that it comes to express anything resembling that original meaning, even though the wording was not originally coined for those other instances. It is as though customary usage has transferred that proverbial sentence from its particular meaning to

---

## Page 7127

a general one, just as individual words undergo a transfer of meaning. Here, however, the transfer concerns an entire sentence. For example, the saying:

“Your hands tied it shut, and your mouth inflated it”

is equivalent to saying, “You brought this upon yourself.” This proverb was originally addressed to someone whose own tying and blowing had caused his misfortune; it subsequently became a proverb of general application. Likewise, the saying:

“In summer you squandered the milk”

is equivalent to saying, “You were negligent and failed to act prudently; you neglected what you would need while it was still within your reach, until the opportunity was lost.” The original saying referred to a particular situation. Similarly:

“Perhaps al-Ghuwayr will bring calamities.”

That is: “Do you fear that this fair exterior conceals something bad within?”

This is a form of expression belonging to language and discourse. Someone using it is like someone conveying a meaning through an expression that signifies it, whether that meaning is itself true or false, since a proverb may be applied to someone whom it does not actually describe. Seeking such expressions in the Quran is therefore like seeking expressions established by customary usage: it involves examining how wording signifies meaning, not whether that meaning is sound or establishes a judgment. This is not what is intended by His words:

“We have certainly set forth for people in this Quran every kind of example.”

Reflect on this, for it will clear away a confusion concerning both wording and meaning. These linguistic proverbs are of various types, and their general categories occur in the Quran. They attest to the eloquence of its wording and composition and the brilliance of its verbal expression. Those who discuss rhetoric and the Quran’s inimitability discuss matters of this kind. Some people’s words become proverbial as soon as they first utter them, whereas others’ words do not become proverbial

---

## Page 7128

until someone uses them as a proverb, becoming the first to do so, as with the Prophet’s sayings—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“Now the battle rages fiercely!” and “A kindler of war!” and similar expressions.

However, negation expressed through a rhetorical question conveying rejection contains within it the evidence for that negation, so it cannot be countered by a bare denial. For one uses such a question to negate only that for which the grounds of negation are evident, or claimed to be evident. The person employing it is therefore either sound in his inference and analogical reasoning or ignorant, like the one who said:

“Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled to dust?”

Once this is clear, the parables set forth in the Quran include some explicitly called parables and others not called by that name. … (1)

“Their example is like that of one who kindled a fire,” and the parable immediately following it;

“Allah is not ashamed to set forth a parable of a mosquito or anything beyond it”;

“The example of those who disbelieve is like that of one who calls out”;

“While there has not yet come to you the like of what befell those who passed away before you”;

“The example of those who spend their wealth in the way of Allah”;

“Do not nullify your charities by reminders of your generosity and by causing hurt, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by people”—the verse;

“And the example of those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure.”

The passage following this does not contain the word “parable.”

“Like the way of Pharaoh’s people”—in all three occurrences;

“There has already been a sign for you”;

“The example of what they spend in this worldly life”;

And His words:

“Have you considered: if Allah took away your hearing?”
