## Page 7131

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, so much so that a number of books of Quranic commentary contain nothing but erroneous explanations of them.

Among them are the two verses beginning with His words:

“Indeed, those who believe and those who are Jews.”

He, glorified be He, describes those who attain felicity among earlier and later generations. This is what the wording indicates and what makes its meaning clear, without contradiction and in harmony with what precedes and follows it. This is the understanding known among the early generations. It is also supported by the occasion of revelation they related through reliable chains of transmission, from Sufyān, from Ibn Abī Najīh, from Mujāhid:

“Salmān said, ‘I asked the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, about people of a religion whom I had accompanied.’ He mentioned some of their acts of worship, and the verse was revealed.”

This report does not mention that they were among the people of the Fire, as has been reported through weak chains of transmission. This is the correct account, as in Muslim:

“Except for a remnant of the People of the Book.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would not answer on matters of which he had no knowledge. It has also

---

## Page 7132

been established that he praised individuals who died during the interval between messengers, such as Zayd ibn ʿAmr and others. Ibn Abī Hātim reported no disagreement among the early generations. However, he related that Ibn ʿAbbās said: “Then Allah revealed the verse beginning, ‘And whoever seeks a religion other than Islam.’” What he meant was that Allah makes clear that He accepts only Islam from earlier and later generations alike. Many of the early scholars used the term “abrogation” to mean setting aside a meaning mistakenly thought to be indicated by a verse.

It is well known that whoever rejects even one messenger is a disbeliever and is therefore not included in His words, “Whoever believes in Allah,” and what follows.

Some people mistakenly supposed that the verse concerned only those to whom Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, was sent. They then split into mutually contradictory positions.

---

## Page 7133

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

Allah divided those whom He condemned among the People of the Book into those who distort Scripture and those who are unlearned, saying:

“Do you then hope that they will believe you, when a group of them used to hear the words of Allah and then knowingly distort them after understanding them?”

“When they meet those who believe, they say, ‘We believe.’ But when they are alone with one another, they say, ‘Do you tell them what Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it to argue against you before your Lord? Will you not then understand?’”

“Do they not know that Allah knows what they conceal and what they reveal?”

“Among them are unlearned people who know nothing of the Scripture beyond mere recitation, and they do nothing but conjecture.”

“So woe to those who write the Scripture with their own hands and then say, ‘This is from Allah,’ to gain a small price for it! Woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they earn!”

In this is a lesson for those of our community who follow their path; for those who deviate in

---

## Page 7134

their approach to the texts of the Quran and Sunnah—such as those concerning the divine attributes and other reports and commands—fall into the following groups:

“One group” distorts either their wording or their meaning. They reject and negate what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—affirmed, claiming that this is what sound reason requires, with reason sitting in judgment over revelation.

“Another group” does no more than recite the texts, without understanding their meaning. They claim that this is what revelation requires and what the early generations followed, and that Allah did not intend His servants to understand these texts. Thus, they “know nothing of the Scripture beyond mere recitation”—that is, simply reciting it—“and they do nothing but conjecture.”

Others then write works on branches of knowledge, claiming that these are religious sciences, that both the scriptural texts and reason substantiate them, and that they constitute Allah’s religion, despite their contradicting the Book of Allah. These are the ones who write the Scripture with their own hands and then, in one way or another, say that it is from Allah.

Reflect, then, on how these verses encompass all three categories. His words describing those people—

“Do you tell them what Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it to argue against you before your Lord?”—

describe the condition of those who conceal the texts that their opponents use as evidence. Some even prohibit the transmission of hadiths reported from the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. If they could conceal the Quran, they would do so. Instead, they conceal the ways in which it establishes meanings, including the knowledge deduced from it, and offer people in its place what they write with their own hands, claiming that it comes from Allah.

---

## Page 7135

He was asked about the meaning of His words:

“Whatever verse We abrogate or cause to be forgotten,”

given that Allah, glorified is He, is not subject to forgetfulness.

He replied:

As for His words, “Whatever verse We abrogate or cause to be forgotten,” there are two readings. The better-known reading is aw nunsihā (“or We cause it to be forgotten”), meaning, “We cause you to forget it.” That is: if We abrogate what We have sent down, or choose to send down what We wish to reveal, We bring you something better than it or its like.

The second reading is aw nansa’hā (“or We defer it”), with a hamzah, meaning, “We delay it.” No one has recited it as nansāhā (“We forget it”). Whoever thinks that nansa’hā means nansāhā is ignorant of Arabic and Quranic interpretation. Moses, peace be upon him, said:

“Knowledge of them is with my Lord, in a Record. My Lord neither errs nor forgets.”

“Forgetting” is attributed to the servant, as in His words:

“We will make you recite, and you will not forget, except what Allah wills.”

For this reason, some of the Companions recited it as aw tansāhā (“or you forget it”), meaning, “You forget it, O Muhammad.” This is clear and escapes only an ignorant person who does not distinguish between nansa’hā, with a hamzah, and nansāhā, without one. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7136

Abū al-'Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Concerning the words of the Exalted,

“Retribution is prescribed for you in cases of murder,”

and the remainder of the verse, there are two interpretations.

The first is that retribution means retaliatory execution, and it is accepting blood money instead of execution. As reported from Ibn 'Abbās, retribution existed among the Children of Israel, but blood money did not. Allah therefore allowed blood money for this community, saying:

“But whoever is pardoned by his brother in any way…”

Pardoning means accepting blood money in a case of intentional killing.

“That is a concession from your Lord and a mercy,”

compared with what had been imposed upon the Children of Israel. According to this interpretation, the meaning is that a free person is to be executed for a free person, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female.

Qatādah said: “The people of the pre-Islamic era were given to oppression. When a tribe possessed strength in numbers and arms, and their slave killed another people's slave, they would say, ‘None but a free man shall be executed for him,’ asserting their superiority over others. If one of their women killed a woman from another people, they would say, ‘None but a man shall be executed for her.’ Then this verse was revealed.”

This is the view of most jurists, and al-Shāfi'ī and others have mentioned it.

---

## Page 7137

And a group of followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad cite this verse as evidence that a free person is not to be executed for killing a slave, because Allah says:

“A slave for a slave.”

Their argument, however, is countered by the case of a woman, for He also says:

“A female for a female.”

Some Quran commentators have mentioned only this interpretation.

“The second interpretation” is that retribution for those killed applies between two groups fighting out of tribal partisanship and pre-Islamic ignorance, with free persons, slaves and women killed on both sides. Allah Most High commanded that justice be established between the two groups by offsetting the blood money for a free person against that for a free person, that for a woman against that for a woman, and that for a slave against that for a slave. If, after this offsetting, anything remains due to either group, it should seek payment from the other fairly, and the other should pay it graciously. This is the view of al-Sha‘bī and others, and Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī and others have mentioned it.

On this interpretation, taking it as the apparent meaning of the verse gives rise to difficulties. Yet the second meaning is what the verse signifies and entails, and it presents no difficulty, whereas the first interpretation is inferred from the verse, as we shall point out, if Allah Most High wills. What we have stated becomes clear in several ways.

The first is that He said:

“Retribution is prescribed for you in cases of murder.”

The word qisās (retribution) is a verbal noun of qāssahu, yuqāssuhu, whose verbal-noun forms are muqāssah and qisās. An example is offsetting two debts against each other. Retribution concerning those killed applies only when all those concerned have been killed, as al-Sha‘bī explained: those killed on one side are offset against those killed on the other. But if someone kills

---

## Page 7138

a man, the victim is dead. Here, there is no offsetting of the victim; rather, retribution consists in allowing the killer—and no one else—to be killed. Jurists hold two views on whether equivalence of status is required. One view requires it: a Muslim is not executed for killing a non-Muslim under Muslim protection, nor a free person for killing a slave. This is the majority view, held by Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. The other view does not require equivalence of status, as maintained by Abū Hanīfah. Equivalence of status, however, is not called retribution.

Furthermore, He said:

“Retribution is prescribed for you.”

If “retribution” means equivalence of status, that has not been prescribed. If it means exacting retaliatory execution, that is permissible for the victim’s next of kin: they may exact retribution if they wish, or refrain if they wish. Exacting retribution has therefore not been prescribed as an obligation upon them.

Some have raised this objection and answered: “The killer is obliged to surrender himself.” The response is that Allah Most High said:

“Retribution is prescribed for you in cases of murder.”

This is not addressed to the killer alone. Rather, it addresses the victim’s next of kin, as indicated by His words:

“If anyone is pardoned anything by his brother, then payment should be sought fairly and made to him graciously.”

Moreover, one does not say to the killer, “Retribution concerning the victim is prescribed for you,” for no retribution can be exacted upon the victim. Furthermore, the killer’s mere submission to the victim’s next of kin is not itself retribution. Rather, they may exact retribution or refrain from doing so. This is called qawd (retaliatory execution) only because the victim’s next of kin lead the killer away. It is comparable to handing merchandise over to its buyer.

Then Allah Most High said:

“A free person for a free person.”

How, then, can it be said that such words are intended to address the killer? Rather, this is an address to the Muslim community

---

## Page 7139

concerning offsetting and equivalence in cases of killing.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, “The ruling in Allah’s Book is retribution,” only when al-Rubayyi‘ broke a girl’s tooth and her people refused to accept compensation. Anas ibn al-Nadr said, “No! By Him who sent you with the truth, al-Rubayyi‘’s front tooth will not be broken!” The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, replied, “O Anas, the ruling in Allah’s Book is retribution.” The people then agreed to accept compensation, whereupon the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, “Among Allah’s servants are those whose oaths Allah would fulfil if they swore by Him.”

This accords with His words, Most High:

“Wounds are subject to equal retribution.”

“The ruling in Allah’s Book” means that a bodily part is taken for its counterpart. This is retribution because it establishes equality. Accordingly, the scholars unanimously agree that equivalence must be observed in bodily parts and wounds. If it is said, “Retribution means killing the victim’s killer, and no one else; it is therefore the opposite of transgression,” the answer is: Yes, but this is retribution among the living, not among the slain.

Second, He said:

“In cases of murder: a free person for a free person, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female.”

It is well established, by Muslim consensus, that a slave is executed for killing either a slave or a free person, and a female is executed for killing either a female or a male. A free man is executed for killing another free man and, according to the great majority of scholars, for killing a female as well. Some hold that his full blood money must be made up. That being so, His words:

“A free person for a free person, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female”

indicate only that one free person is offset against another, treated as equivalent to him, and set against him. The same applies to one slave against another and one female against another. This can only be when they have been killed: each is set against the other to determine whether they are equivalent or whether one exceeds the other in value. As for those killed, however, the Muslims unanimously agree that these pairings are not exclusive.

Third, He said:

“If anyone is pardoned anything by his brother.”

The word ‘ufiya (“is pardoned”)

---

## Page 7140

is used here transitively. He says ‘ufiya … shay’un (“something was pardoned”), not ‘afā … shay’an (“he pardoned something”). This usage applies only to an act, as in His words, Most High:

“They ask you what they should spend. Say, ‘Whatever is surplus.’”

As for waiving execution, the expression used is, “I pardoned the killer.” The slain person’s heir has two options, including waiving execution and taking blood money. Nothing has then been pardoned for him; rather, he himself has waived execution. If he waives it, there are two views as to whether he becomes entitled to blood money by that act itself, or without the killer’s consent.

Some have interpreted “from his brother” to mean “from his brother’s blood”: that is, he spared him execution and accepted blood money. The person intended is the killer, meaning that the killer was pardoned something of the blood of his slain brother—that is, he was spared execution. The implied wording would therefore be that the heir pardoned something for the killer from the slain person’s blood. Yet this is not a recognized expression. One does not say, “I pardoned something for you,” nor, “I pardoned something from the killer’s blood.” Rather, one says that he pardoned the killer. How can these expressions be equated?

According to the first interpretation, however, when the two parties offsetting their claims count their slain against one another, “whoever is left with something in his favour” means whoever has a further claim remaining after offsetting his brother’s claim. That is, a surplus remains in his favour. It is like asking, “Does any balance remain due to him from his brother?”

“Then there should be a claim made fairly.”

The person entitled to this surplus should pursue his claim against the other party fairly, and the latter should pay him graciously.

“That is a concession and a mercy from your Lord.”

That is, relief from each party having to pay compensation for all those slain on the other side, which would impose a very heavy burden upon it.

“And in retribution there is life for you.”

For they

---

## Page 7141

—when they count their slain, offset their claims, and reach an equal settlement—no longer have any claim against one another. Thus, lives are preserved on both sides. If, however, they do not offset their claims, they fight one another, and strife erupts between them in which multitudes perish, as is well known from the conflicts of both pre-Islamic times and the Islamic era. Such strife arises only because the two parties fail to deal equitably and fairly with one another. When there is an equitable and fair settlement acceptable to people of understanding, no cause for strife remains.

His words:

“But whoever transgresses after that”

refer to someone who demands money or men from the other party, or harms them on account of the bloodshed between them:

“will suffer a painful punishment.”

This is like His words:

“If two groups of believers fight one another, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, then reconcile them justly and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

“The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your two brothers.”

“Brotherhood” here has the same meaning as brotherhood in the earlier passage. This concerns those slain in civil strife.

As for a man killing another outside such strife, they already knew that the killer was to be put to death. Yet the stronger group would demand the execution of someone other than the killer, someone of higher standing than the killer, or two men for one. If the killer belonged to their own group, they would not execute him for someone of lower standing, as reportedly happened between Qurayzah and al-Nadīr. However, this did not give rise to civil strife; rather, it involved the stronger group oppressing the weaker. Nor

---

## Page 7142

had anyone among the nations maintained that a wrongful, transgressing killer should never be put to death. No human being held this view; rather, all humanity agrees that, as a general rule, a killer is put to death. Yet powerful oppressors distinguish between one slain person and another.

Some interpret His words:

“In legal retribution there is life for you”

as meaning that, when a would-be killer knows he will be put to death, he refrains, thereby preserving both his own life and that of his intended victim. The response to this is: this meaning is sound, but everyone knows it, and it is ingrained in human nature. No human being permits someone to be killed without his killer being put to death. Rather, everyone allows the killer to be put to death when the two are equal. Nor can it be imagined that people … (1) if anyone who could overpower another killed him without himself being put to death, would settle for money.

Since this principle is among the first things human beings recognize, and they know that they cannot live without it, it is comparable to their need for food, drink, and shelter. The Quran is too exalted for its purpose to be merely to acquaint people with such self-evident matters. Rather, this is included within its meaning: when He prescribed legal retribution for those slain, whereby one free person is offset against another, one slave against another, and one female against another, He made the blood-money for one equal to that for the other, and the blood of one equivalent to that of the other. This entails their equality in matters of blood and blood-money. Through this offsetting of claims, their lives are preserved from the strife that would bring about their destruction, as is well known.

This meaning can be derived from this verse. Thus, it is understood that the blood and blood-money of one free person are equivalent to those of another free person, so one is put to death for killing the other. And once it is understood that claims are offset because the amounts of blood-money are equal, it is understood that blood-money is due for the slain person.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 7143

And the term “legal retribution” denotes equivalence and equality, indicating that Allah has enjoined justice and fairness concerning those slain. Whoever kills anyone other than the killer of the person whose death he is avenging acts unjustly. Likewise, if the slain person and his heirs refuse to give the heirs of the other slain person their due, they are unjust. The former depart from the justice Allah has enjoined, and so do the latter.

He, glorified be He, expressed this meaning in His words:

“If anyone is killed unjustly, We have given his heir authority; but let him not exceed the limits in killing, for he is supported.”

Once the verse is understood to indicate justice in retaliation through necessary implication and implicit indication, the difficulty disappears. One will no longer ask: “Why, then, did He not say, ‘a slave for a slave and a free person’?” For the intended meaning was not that the one should be offset against the other among the slain. It is well known that, when claims are offset, a free man is matched with a free man, not a woman; a woman with a woman, not a free man; and a slave with a slave. Thus, the purpose of specifying these categories and matching them against one another in the verse becomes clear.

The verse then indicates that a free person is put to death for killing a free person, a slave for killing a slave, and a female for killing a female, provided the two are equal in respect of blood, the compensation for which is blood-money. This does not exclude putting a slave to death for killing a free person, or a female for killing a male; nor does the verse carry any implied meaning that excludes this. Rather, just as it indicates the former through implicit indication, the import of its wording, and a fortiori reasoning, it likewise indicates the latter. For if a slave is put to death for killing a slave, there is all the more reason to put him to death for killing a free person; and if a woman is put to death for killing a woman, there is all the more reason to put her to death for killing a man.

---

## Page 7144

As for putting a free person to death for killing a slave, or a male for killing a female, the verse neither affirms nor denies this. Nor can either ruling be inferred from it by analogous or contrary implication. For if, when claims are offset, a free person is matched with a free person, a slave with a slave, and a female with a female because their respective blood-money amounts are equal, this indicates that an equal may be put to death for killing an equal, and someone of lower status for killing someone of higher status. There remains the execution of someone of higher status, whose blood-money is greater, for killing someone of lower status, whose blood-money is less. The verse does not address this, for its purpose was not to establish retaliation in the first instance, but to offset claims concerning the slain because their blood-money amounts were equal.

If it is objected:

“The blood-money for one free person equals that for another, and the blood-money for one female equals that for another; but what about slaves, whose values differ?”

The reply is:

Their slaves were of roughly equal value. Moreover, His words,

“A slave for a slave,”

may mean a slave for another of comparable value, just as one says, “A garment for a garment.” Even if one is worth more, that difference is waived. It may also be waived when their values are unknown, as is usually the case. For the slaves killed in these civil conflicts are those who fight alongside their masters, having been raised in their households rather than purchased. Thus, this applies both when their values are known to be equal and when any difference between them is unknown.

For what is unknown is treated as nonexistent. If each of two men destroyed the other’s garment, and neither knew the value of either garment, it would be said, “A garment for a garment.” This is because the difference in value could favour either side: this man’s garment may be worth more

---

## Page 7145

and the other man’s garment may be worth more. There is no stronger reason to favour either possibility over the other. The presumption is that neither party owes anything for a difference in value. Liability cannot be established on the basis of something doubtful even if the uncertainty concerns only one party; how much more so when it concerns both?

Thus, the wisdom of His words becomes clear:

“A slave for a slave.”

This also makes clear that the Quran sets forth what people need to know and practise, whereby their blood is spared and their lives preserved. This includes what others have mentioned concerning justice in retaliation. The verse also indicates that blood-money is payable for those killed, thereby establishing the killer’s liability for blood-money and showing that its amount varies according to the person killed. This is among the favours Allah has bestowed upon the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, by instituting both retaliation and blood-money. As for whether pardon means accepting blood-money in a case of intentional killing, and whether the person granting pardon becomes entitled to it merely by granting pardon, the verse does not address this.

This verse also indicates that groups possessing the power to resist may each hold the other liable for the lives and property it has wrongfully destroyed, because He says:

“From his brother.”

This differs from what Muslims destroy of the unbelievers’ lives and property, and what unbelievers destroy of the Muslims’ lives and property. As for fighting based on a religious interpretation—“such as the fighting at the Battle of the Camel and at Siffīn”—according to the majority of scholars, there is likewise no liability, with even greater reason. For if unbelievers acting on a religious interpretation

---

## Page 7146

are not held liable, then Muslims acting on a religious interpretation have even greater reason not to be held liable.

The verse also indicates that this liability rests upon the group as a whole, with those providing support and those directly responsible equally liable. They are not told, “Find out who killed your companion and demand his blood-money from him.” Rather, they are told, “His blood-money is due from all of you, for you all killed him.” This is because the direct perpetrator was only able to act through the assistance of those supporting him. This is also indicated by His words:

“And if any of your wives have left you for the disbelievers and you subsequently obtain [something], then give those whose wives have left the equivalent of what they had spent.”

Those disbelievers owed the equivalent of the dower of the woman who had gone over to them. If they did not pay it, it could be taken from whatever property of theirs the Muslims could obtain. An example is a woman who comes from among them and whose dower they are entitled to reclaim. The Muslim then pays the husband of that apostate woman the equivalent of her dower out of the dower owed for this Muslim emigrant woman, which the disbelievers are entitled to receive. By embracing Islam and emigrating, she has deprived her husband of his conjugal rights, just as the apostate woman has deprived her husband of his conjugal rights. This applies even if the emigrant woman’s husband is not the man who married the apostate woman, because a group possessing the power to resist, whose members protect one another, is treated as a single person.

Thus, when Khālid killed those whom he killed from Banū Jadhīma, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, paid their blood-money from his own resources. Khālid was his deputy, and he would not allow them to demand payment from Khālid or detain him, since he had acted on a religious interpretation. The same applies to ʿAmr ibn Umayya, for whose blood-money Khālid ibn al-Walīd was liable, because he committed this killing in the course of jihad, not out of personal enmity. Jurists have differed over liability for a ruler’s error: does it fall upon the public treasury or upon him personally? There are two views.

---

## Page 7147

For this reason, the army shares in whatever spoils a detachment captures, and the detachment shares in whatever the army captures, since each gains spoils only through the support of the others. Just as they share liability for losses, they share the gains. Likewise, regarding punishment, the majority of jurists hold that both those who provide support and those who directly perpetrate armed brigandage are liable to execution, just as ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with him, executed the brigands’ lookout. This is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad. Mālik also holds this view regarding execution in retaliation and thieves.

The verse indicates this as follows: when claims concerning those killed are offset—a free person against a free person, a slave against a slave, and a female against a female—the killer of the free person on one side need not be the legal heir of the free person on the other; it may be someone else. Likewise, the killer of the slave on one side need not be the master of the slave on the other; it may be someone else. However, since they acted together and supported one another in fighting and waging war against the other group, anyone killed by some of them is regarded as having been killed by them all, and they are all liable for him. Thus, any outstanding balance due to either group is taken from the property of the other.

If it is asked:

“If it is firmly established in human nature that someone who wrongfully kills his equal deserves to be killed, and no human being says that he should not be killed, what purpose is served by the words of the Most High:

‘And We ordained for them therein’—meaning in the Torah—‘a life for a life, an eye for an eye,’

and the rest of the verse, when all rational people recognize such a law?”

The reply is:

Its purpose is to make clear the equal worth of the blood of the Children of Israel, and that their blood

---

## Page 7148

is of equal worth, with the noble enjoying no privilege over the weak. This is the great benefit brought by the laws of the prophets. Those who depart from the prophets’ laws, however, do not apply this principle without exception; they may refrain from executing a nobleman. If the king is just, he may implement some of it. This, then, is what Allah prescribed in the Torah: their blood is of equal worth, the least of them may grant protection binding upon them all, and they stand united against others. He likewise decreed that the blood of those who believe in Him, of every people, is of equal worth. Thus, by scholarly consensus, a free Muslim is executed in retaliation for killing another free Muslim, regardless of their respective peoples.

This also answers those who invoke the verse concerning the Torah to argue that a Muslim is executed in retaliation for killing a non-Muslim protected subject, citing His words:

“And We ordained for them therein: a life for a life,”

and the principle:

“The revealed law of those before us is binding upon us.”

The answer is that what was prescribed for them was a life from among them for a life from among them. They were all believers, with no unbeliever among them. Their law did not permit an unbeliever to remain among them, whether through payment of jizya (the poll tax) or otherwise. This resembles the law of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which establishes that Muslims’ blood is of equal worth. Neither law states that an unbeliever’s blood is equal in worth to a Muslim’s. Rather, making faith the necessary basis for this equality indicates that it does not apply to an unbeliever—whether a protected subject or someone granted safe-conduct—since he lacks the faith required for that equality.

Its general wording may, however, be invoked in the case of a slave. There are no explicit, authentic texts concerning a slave like those concerning a non-Muslim protected subject. Rather, what has been reported is:

“Whoever kills his slave, we shall execute him in retaliation.”

This is because, if he kills him wrongfully, the ruler becomes the legal guardian of

---

## Page 7149

his blood; for just as a killer does not inherit from his victim when the victim is free, he likewise cannot be the guardian entitled to claim retaliation for his blood when the victim is a slave. Indeed, this is even more compelling: how could he be the guardian of his blood when he himself is the killer? He cannot be its guardian. Instead, there are the heirs of the master who killed him; yet they are his heirs while he is still alive, and no such authority was established for him that could pass to them. Thus, the ruler becomes the slave’s guardian. The ruler may then execute him. Accordingly, whenever someone kills his own slave, the ruler has the right to execute him.

Furthermore, the Sunnah and transmitted reports establish that if someone mutilates his slave, the slave thereby becomes free. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad and others. Killing him is the most extreme form of mutilation, so he dies a free man. His freedom, however, was not established during his lifetime such that his agnatic heirs would inherit from him; rather, it was established as a matter of legal judgment. Once emancipated, his bond of patronage belongs to the Muslims, making the ruler his guardian. The ruler therefore has the right to execute the man who killed his own slave.

This may also be cited as evidence by those who hold that, when someone kills another person’s slave, the slave’s master has the right to have the killer executed. If the hadith establishes this, then this is the stronger position. The opposing position has neither an explicit text nor a sound analogy to support it. Jurists among Ahmad’s followers and others have stated that when someone is killed and has no guardian entitled to claim retaliation, the ruler becomes the guardian of his blood. He may have the killer executed, or pardon him in return for blood money, but not without compensation.

This is further supported by the fact that those who hold that a free person is not executed in retaliation for killing a slave also hold that a free non-Muslim protected subject is not executed in retaliation for killing a Muslim slave. Allah Most High says in His Book:

“A believing slave is better

---

## Page 7150

than a polytheist.” Thus, a believing slave is better than a polytheist who is a protected non-Muslim subject. How, then, can the latter not be executed in retaliation for killing him? A believing slave is equal to free believing women, as this verse indicates. This is the position of the vast majority of earlier and later scholars.

This argument is particularly strong according to Ahmad’s position, since he accepts a slave’s testimony just as he accepts a free person’s, unlike that of a protected non-Muslim subject. Why, then, should a free person not be executed in retaliation for killing a slave, when both are believers? The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The blood of believers is of equal value.”

---

## Page 7151

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

The words of Allah Most High,

“They ask you about the sacred month—about fighting therein,”

contain an apposition of inclusion. The question concerned only fighting during that month. Why, then, was the month mentioned first, when you have said that people mention first what is more important to explain and what concerns them more?

The answer is that they asked only after fighting had occurred during that month and their enemies had denounced them for violating it and profaning its sanctity. Their concern about the month exceeded their concern about the fighting. Indeed, the question arose because of the month’s sanctity, so it was mentioned first. Giving it precedence thus accords with the principle we have stated.

If it is asked: What, then, is the benefit of repeating the explicit word “fighting”? Why not simply use a pronoun and say, “It is a grave matter”? When you say, “I asked him about Zayd: ‘Is he in the house?’” this is more concise than saying, “Is Zayd in the house?”

The answer is that repeating the explicit noun serves an exquisite rhetorical purpose: it attaches the stated judgment to fighting during that month generally. Had a pronoun been used instead—“It is a grave matter”—one might have supposed that the judgment applied exclusively to the particular fighting about which the question was asked. But that is not the case;

---

## Page 7152

rather, it applies generally to all fighting that takes place during a sacred month. A similar benefit appears in the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when he was asked about performing ablution with seawater:

“Its water is pure and purifying.”

He repeated the word “water” rather than merely saying, “Yes, perform ablution with it,” lest anyone suppose that the ruling applied exclusively to those asking, owing to some particular circumstance. Thus, instead of saying, “Yes, perform ablution,” he gave a general answer that attaches the ruling and the purifying quality to seawater itself, as such. This conveys that the ruling remains in force at all times and applies to the entire Muslim community, dispelling any supposition that it is restricted to the circumstances that prompted the question. Reflect on this, for it is exquisite.

Likewise, in the verse, when He said:

“Fighting therein is a grave matter,”

He made “a grave matter” the predicate of “fighting therein,” so that the ruling applies to it generally. A pronoun would not convey this. Similar to this are the words of Allah Most High:

“As for those who hold fast to the Book and establish prayer—We do not let the reward of those who set things right go to waste.”

He did not say “their reward,” thereby attaching this ruling to their defining quality: that they set things right. The pronoun contains nothing indicating that quality. Similar to this, but subtler in meaning, are the words of Allah Most High:

“They ask you about menstruation

---

## Page 7153

. Say, ‘It is harm, so keep away from women during menstruation.’”

He did not say “during it,” thereby linking the ruling requiring abstention to menstruation itself and indicating that it is the reason for abstention. He said:

“Say, ‘It is harm,’”

rather than “Menstruation is harm,” because using the pronoun here follows the normal pattern of expression. Moreover, repeating the noun would have made the wording cumbersome, since it would have occurred three times. Using the explicit noun in the command to abstain was preferable to using a pronoun, as it conveys that the ruling is linked to its being menstruation. This differs from His words:

“Say, ‘It is harm,’”

which state a fact: those addressed know that what makes it harmful is precisely its being menstruation. By contrast, linking the ruling to it is something that can be known only through revealed law. Reflect on this.

---

## Page 7154

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

Concerning the words of Allah Most High, “Do not marry polytheistic women”: scholars have permitted marriage to Christian and Jewish women. Are they polytheists or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

Marriage to a woman of the People of the Book is permissible on the basis of the verse in Sūrat al-Mā’idah. Allah Most High says:

“The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them. And [lawful for you are] chaste believing women and chaste women from among those who were given the Scripture before you.”

This is the position of the vast majority of earlier and later scholars, including the four imams and others. It has been reported that Ibn ‘Umar disapproved of marrying a Christian woman, saying: “I know of no greater polytheism than that of a woman who says that her Lord is Jesus, son of Mary.” This is today the position of a group of religious innovators. They have cited as evidence the verse in Sūrat al-Baqarah and His words:

“Do not maintain marriage bonds with disbelieving women.”

The verse in al-Baqarah may be answered in three ways.

(First: the People of the Book are not included among the polytheists. He distinguishes the People of the Book from the polytheists, as shown by His words:

---

## Page 7155

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and those who associate partners with Allah.”

If it is objected that He has described them as associating partners with Him in His words:

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no deity except Him. Exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

The answer is that the religion of the People of the Book contained no polytheism in its original form. Allah sent the messengers only with monotheism, so the religion of all those who believed in the messengers and the Scriptures originally contained no polytheism. The Christians, however, introduced polytheism into their religion, as He says:

“Glorified and exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

Thus, wherever He describes them as having associated partners with Him, it is because of the polytheism they introduced, which Allah did not command. Wherever He distinguishes them from the polytheists, it is because their religion was originally based on following the revealed Scriptures, which taught monotheism, not polytheism.

Thus, if it is said that the People of the Book were not polytheists in this respect, this is because the Scripture with which they are associated contains no polytheism. This is like saying that the Muslims—the community of Muhammad—have, in this respect, neither the doctrine that Creator and creation are one, nor Rāfidī doctrines, nor denial of divine decree, nor any other such innovations, even though some who belong to the community have introduced these innovations. Yet the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, does not agree unanimously upon misguidance. There will therefore always remain within it those who follow the law of monotheism, unlike the People of the Book.

Moreover, Allah, Mighty and Majestic, did not describe the People of the Book using the noun “polytheists.” Rather, He used a verb in the phrase “above what they associate with Him.” In the verse in al-Baqarah, He says:

---

## Page 7156

“Male polytheists” and “female polytheists,” using nouns; and a noun is more emphatic than a verb.

The second approach is to say:

If the term “polytheists” in Surah al-Baqarah includes them, just as He described them as associating partners with Him, this can be understood by distinguishing between the term’s meaning when used alone and when mentioned alongside another term. When “polytheists” is used alone, it includes the People of the Book; when mentioned alongside the People of the Book, it does not include them. A similar distinction has been made regarding the terms “the poor” and “the needy,” and other such terms. On this understanding, the verse in al-Baqarah is general, whereas the other verse is specific, and the specific takes precedence over the general.

(The third approach is to say: The verse in al-Mā’idah abrogates the verse in al-Baqarah, because al-Mā’idah was revealed after al-Baqarah, according to the consensus of the scholars. It is reported in the hadith: “Al-Mā’idah is among . . . (1)

---

## Page 7157

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

When He—glory be to Him—mentioned what nullifies charity—reminders of one’s generosity, hurtful conduct, and showing off—He likened it to dust on a smooth rock struck by rain. For this reason He said:

“and does not believe in Allah and the Last Day”

For belief in just one of the two is of no benefit here. This differs from His saying in Surah al-Nisā’:

“Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is arrogant and boastful,”

through to His saying:

“and those who spend their wealth to be seen by people, and believe neither in Allah nor in the Last Day.”

Here He is speaking in condemnation, so He mentions its utmost extent. He also mentions the opposite: those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure and out of firmness within themselves. The first is sincerity. “Making firm” means being firm, as in His saying:

“If they had done what they were instructed to do, it would have been better for them and would have made them firmer.”

Similarly, He says:

“And devote yourself wholly to Him.”

It seems—and Allah knows best—that this is akin to qaddim and taqaddam (“put forward” and “go forward”), as in His saying:

“Do not put yourselves forward

---

## Page 7158

before Allah and His Messenger.”

Thus tabattal (“devote yourself wholly”) and tathabbat (“stand firm”) are intransitive, the latter meaning thabata (“be firm”). Firmness means strength and ability; its opposite is being shaken and trembling. Giving charity is akin to fighting: the coward trembles, while the brave person stands firm. Hence the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“As for the proud bearing that Allah loves, it is a man’s proud bearing in battle and his proud bearing when giving charity.”

This is because such a situation calls for firmness and strength, so a proud bearing is fitting. What Allah does not love is the arrogant boaster who is miserly and urges others to be miserly. But He loves the person who bears himself proudly when giving or fighting.

His words, “from within themselves,” mean that the source of a person’s strength is not external—as with someone who stands firm in battle because his companions hold him in place. This is like His saying:

“And when they are angry, they forgive.”

Rather, his firmness and forgiveness come from within himself.

Allah—glory be to Him—has mentioned in Surahs al-Baqarah and al-Nisā’ the four categories of people with respect to giving. A person either does not give, in which case he is the miser condemned in al-Nisā’; or gives reluctantly, with reminders of his generosity and hurtful conduct, so that his giving is not grounded in firmness—this is the person condemned in al-Baqarah; or gives to show off, in which case he is condemned in both surahs. This leaves the fourth category: seeking Allah’s pleasure and out of firmness within themselves.

---

## Page 7159

Prayer is analogous: a person either does not pray, prays to show off, prays sluggishly, or prays sincerely. The first three categories are blameworthy. The same applies to zakat. Migration and jihad are likewise analogous, for people fall into four categories with respect to them. The same applies to steadfastness and remembrance in His words:

“When you encounter an enemy force, stand firm and remember Allah often.”

Likewise, regarding patience and mercy in His words:

“And urge one another to patience and urge one another to mercy.”

People fall into four categories with respect to patience and mercy. The same applies to His words:

“Seek help through patience and prayer.”

Thus, they … (1) with respect to patience and prayer.

Generally, these pairings in the Quran consist of either two deeds or two qualities within a deed, and people fall into four categories with respect to them. If they are two separate deeds, such as prayer and patience, prayer and zakat, and similar pairings, performing either one is beneficial even if the other is neglected. But if they are two conditions of a deed, such as sincerity and firmness, neither alone is beneficial. Reminders of one’s generosity and hurtful conduct nullify the reward, just as showing off nullifies it, as the Quran indicates.

Such pairings include fear of Allah and good character—for Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good—as well as righteousness and piety, and truth and patience. The finest faith is generosity and patience.

This differs from pairings of blameworthy qualities, such as falsehood and sin, arrogance and boasting, miserliness and cowardice, and sin and transgression; for blame attaches to either one on its own

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Some words here are unclear.

---

## Page 7160

and when paired with the other. This is because good is something whose existence is sought for its benefit, and that benefit may only be attained when it is complete. Evil, however, is something whose absence is sought because of its harm, and even a portion of what is harmful generally causes some harm. For this reason, in interpreting terms, a distinction is made between commands and prohibitions, and between affirmation and negation. A command to do something requires doing it completely, whereas a prohibition encompasses every part of it.

Thus, wherever Allah requires marriage—as in the case of a woman divorced three times, “until she marries a husband other than him,” and in establishing married status—it must be complete through both the marriage contract and consummation. Wherever He prohibits it, as with women within the prohibited degrees of kinship, the prohibition applies to each of these separately. This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad. It is explicitly reported from Ahmad that someone who swears to marry does not fulfil his oath without both the marriage contract and consummation. By contrast, someone who swears not to marry breaks his oath by concluding the marriage contract alone. Likewise, someone who swears not to do something breaks his oath by doing part of it, unlike someone who swears to do it. Whether a term denotes the whole or a part varies according to whether it occurs in a negative or affirmative statement.

For this reason, when Allah commanded purification, prayer, zakat, and Hajj, what was obligatory was their complete performance, as He, Exalted is He, said:

“with commands, which he fulfilled.”

And He said:

“And Abraham, who fulfilled his obligations.”

When He prohibited killing, unlawful sexual intercourse, theft, and drinking intoxicants, He prohibited their partial forms and even the acts leading to them, although the term does not encompass these in an affirmative statement. For this reason, a distinction is made between negation and affirmation with respect to indefinite nouns, and all verbs are

---

## Page 7161

indefinite, and a distinction is made between commands and prohibitions regarding whether repetition is required.

He (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can; and when I forbid you something, avoid it.”

The disagreement concerns only definite nouns in negative constructions, on which two views are reported, as in the statements: “Do not take the dirhams” and “Do not speak to the people.”

---

## Page 7162

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Taqī al-Dīn Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave—also said:

Section

On the words of the Most High:

“Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it. Then He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills. And Allah has power over all things.”

It is established in Sahīh Muslim, through al-ʿAlāʾ ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān, from his father, from Abū Hurayrah, who said:

“When Allah revealed, ‘Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it,’ this weighed heavily upon the Prophet’s Companions. They came to the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), knelt down, and said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, we have been charged with deeds within our capacity: prayer, fasting, jihad, and charity. But this verse has been revealed to you, and we cannot bear what it requires.’

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said: ‘Do you wish to say what the followers of the two Scriptures before you said: “We hear and disobey”? Say: “We hear and obey. Grant us Your forgiveness, our Lord; to You is the final return.”’

“When the people recited these words and their tongues grew accustomed to them, Allah revealed immediately thereafter: ‘The Messenger has believed

---

## Page 7163

in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so have the believers. Each has believed in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers: “We make no distinction between any of His messengers.” And they say, “We hear and obey. Grant us Your forgiveness, our Lord; to You is the final return.”’ When they did so, Allah abrogated it and revealed:

‘Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity. It shall have the good it has earned and bear the evil it has incurred. Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.’ He said: ‘Yes.’

‘Our Lord, do not lay upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us.’ He said: ‘Yes.’

‘Our Lord, do not burden us with what we have no strength to bear.’ He said: ‘Yes.’

‘Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy upon us. You are our Protector, so grant us victory over the disbelieving people.’ He said: ‘Yes.’”

Sa'īd ibn Jubayr narrated a report to the same effect from Ibn 'Abbās, with the words, “I have done so, I have done so,” in place of “Yes.”

For this reason, many of the early and later scholars said that it was abrogated by His words, “Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.” This was reported from Ibn Mas'ūd, Abū Hurayrah, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn 'Abbās—in one report from him—al-Hasan, al-Sha'bī, Ibn Sīrīn, Sa'īd ibn Jubayr, Qatādah, 'Atā' al-Khurāsānī, al-Suddī, Muhammad ibn Ka'b, Muqātil, al-Kalbī, and Ibn Zayd.

Others were reported to have said that it was not abrogated; rather, it remains applicable to accountability in general: He takes to task whom He wills and forgives whom He wills. This was reported from Ibn 'Umar and al-Hasan

---

## Page 7164

and it was adopted by Abū Sulaymān al-Dimashqī and the judge Abū Ya'lā. They said: “This is a statement of fact, and statements of fact are not subject to abrogation.”

The decisive clarification is that the term “abrogation” is ambiguous. The early scholars used it for setting aside what a verse was thought to imply through its generality, unrestricted scope, or otherwise. Thus, some said that His words, “Fear Allah as He should be feared,” and “Strive for Allah with the striving due to Him,” were abrogated by His words, “So fear Allah as much as you are able.” There is no contradiction between the two verses. However, some people might understand “as He should be feared” and “with the striving due to Him” as commanding something beyond a servant’s ability. That person’s understanding is then set aside, just as Allah nullifies what Satan casts and makes His verses firm. Nullifying this does not mean nullifying what He revealed; rather, it means nullifying what Satan has cast, whether through inner thoughts, hearing, or speech. Likewise, Allah removes an understanding that arises in people’s minds, even when the verse does not indicate that meaning, though it may admit such an interpretation. This verse falls into that category.

His words, “Whether you disclose what is within yourselves,” and the rest of the verse indicate only that Allah will call people to account for what is within them, not that He will punish them for everything within them. His words, “whom He wills,” imply that forgiveness and punishment are His to decide, not anyone else’s. They do not imply that He forgives and punishes without wisdom or justice, as some may suppose among

---

## Page 7165

people, who go so far as to consider it possible that He would punish someone for a minor sin despite his many great good deeds; or that, of two men who have both good and evil deeds, He would forgive one despite his many evil deeds and few good deeds, yet punish the other for a single sin despite his many good deeds, and give the former a higher rank in Paradise than the latter.

These people consider it possible that Allah would punish people without their having sinned, charge them with what they cannot bear, and punish them for failing to fulfil it. The Companions recoiled because they feared that the matter might be of this kind. They said: “We cannot bear this; if He charges us with what we cannot bear, He will punish us.” Allah thus set aside this supposition and made clear that He does not charge any soul beyond its capacity. He thereby exposed the falsehood of the claim that He charges a servant with what he cannot bear and punishes him for it. This view is not known to have been held by any of the early generations or the leading scholars. Rather, their statements contradict it. Indeed, Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah was asked about His words:

“Allah does not charge a soul beyond its capacity.”

He replied: “Only what it can bear with ease; He has not charged it to the utmost limit of its ability.”

Al-Baghawī said: “This is a sound interpretation, for capacity is less than the utmost one can bear.”

The former view was advanced only by a group of later scholars when they debated the Mu‘tazilah on questions of divine decree. They followed the path of determinism taken by Jahm and his followers, adopting this view in varying degrees. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Ibn al-Anbārī said concerning His words:

“And do not burden us with what we cannot bear”:

“That is, do not burden us with what would be difficult for us to fulfil, even if we could manage it through strenuous effort

---

## Page 7166

and burdensome hardship.”

He said: “He thus addressed the Arabs in terms they understood. One of them might say to another, ‘I cannot bear to look at you,’ although he is capable of doing so; looking at him is simply burdensome to him.” He said: “The same applies to His words: ‘They were unable to hear.’”

I say: This usage is not peculiar to the Arabs; rather, it is recognized by all rational people. “Ability in the revealed law” means being able to act without incurring overriding harm, as with the ability to fast or stand in prayer. Whenever doing so would aggravate an illness or delay recovery, a person is not considered able, because of the overriding harm involved.

Those people, by contrast, were unable to hear because they hated the truth and found it burdensome, whether out of envy of the one proclaiming it, or because they followed their desires and disbelief and sins had encrusted their hearts. This is no excuse. Had He commanded His servants to do only what they desired, the heavens, the earth and all who inhabit them would have fallen into ruin.

The point is that none of the early generations maintained that a person is able to act only while actually performing the act, and that before acting he was unable to do so. This has never been taught by revelation, supported by language, or indicated by reason. Indeed, reason indicates the opposite, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The Lord Most High knows that a person will not perform an act even though he is able to do so. What is known is that he will not do it and does not will it—not that he lacks the ability to do it. And knowledge corresponds to

---

## Page 7167

what is known. Allah knows that whoever is able to perform Hajj, stand in prayer, and fast possesses that ability, and He knows that this person is able and performs what he is able to do. What is known, then, is that the act does not occur because the servant does not will it, not because he is unable to perform it—just as with things within his power which he knows he will not do because he does not will them, not because he lacks the power to do them. The servant is able to act, although Allah knows that he will not act despite having that ability. This is why He punishes him: He has commanded him only to do what he is able to do, not what he is unable to do. Whoever lacks the ability, He neither commands him nor punishes him for what he cannot do.

If it is said:

“It follows that he must be able to change Allah’s knowledge, because Allah knows that he will not act. Thus, if he is able to act, he is able to change Allah’s knowledge.”

The answer is:

“This is a fallacy. Merely having the ability to act does not entail any change in that knowledge. Those who imagine such a change imagine it only if the act occurs. But if the act were to occur, what was known would have been its occurrence, not its nonoccurrence. It is therefore impossible for the act to occur while Allah knows that it will not occur. Rather, if it occurs, Allah already knew that it would occur; and if it does not occur, Allah already knew that it would not occur. We know what Allah knows only through what becomes manifest, and Allah’s knowledge corresponds to reality. Thus, nothing can occur that would entail a change in His knowledge. Rather, whatever occurs is precisely what was known. The servant who has not acted has done nothing that changes that knowledge; rather, he is able to do what has not occurred

---

## Page 7168

and if it were to occur, Allah would already have known that it would occur, not that it would not occur.”

If it is said:

“Since it does not occur, Allah knows that it does not occur. Thus, if the servant were able to bring it about, he would be able to change that knowledge.”

The answer is:

That is not so. Rather, the servant is able to bring it about, although he has not done so. Had he brought it about, what was known would have been nothing other than its occurrence. Thus, when something within the servant’s power occurs, what was known could only have been its occurrence. If it occurs, Allah already knew that it would occur; and if it does not occur, Allah already knew that it would certainly not occur. If, then, its occurrence is posited while a necessary consequence of its occurrence is denied, this becomes impossible because it affirms something without its necessary consequence. All things, considered in this way, are impossible.

One implication of their position is that no one but the Lord would have the power to do anything. For things fall into two categories: one “kind” which Allah knows will occur, and another “kind” which Allah knows will not occur. The “first” must necessarily occur, while the “second” will certainly not occur. Whatever Allah knows will occur, He knows will occur by His will and power; and whatever He knows will not occur, He knows that He does not will it. Whatever He—glory be to Him—wills occurs, and whatever He does not will does not occur.

---

## Page 7169

As for the Muʿtazilah, they hold that He wills what does not occur, and that what He does not will occurs. Those advocates of compulsion stand at one extreme, and these at the other, while Ahl al-Sunnah occupy the middle ground.

Whatever the servants do by their own choice, He—glory be to Him—knows that they do it through their own power and will. Whatever they refrain from doing despite having the power to do it, He knows that they refrain because they do not will it, not because they lack the power to do it. He—glory be to Him—is the Creator of the servants, their power, their will, and their actions. All of this lies within the Lord’s power. This is not something shared between the powers of two agents. Rather, the created agent himself, his power, and what he brings about all lie within the Creator’s power and are created by Him.

The point here is that His words, exalted is He,

“Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it,”

are true. Abrogation here means removing the understanding of someone who took the verse to mean something it does not indicate. Whoever understood that Allah burdens a soul with what exceeds its capacity has had that understanding and supposition annulled by Allah. Likewise, whoever understood from it that forgiveness and punishment occur without wisdom or justice has had that understanding and supposition annulled. His words,

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity,”

refute the first understanding, while His words,

“To its credit is what it has earned, and against it is what it has incurred,”

refute the second. His words,

“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills,”

are like His words in Āl ʿImrān:

“To Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

And His words:

“Do you not know that to Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth

---

## Page 7170

, and that He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills, and that Allah has power over all things?” And other similar verses.

He has taught us that He does not forgive associating partners with Him, that He does not punish the believers, and that He forgives those who repent. The same applies to His words:

“Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it,”

and the remainder of the verse.

This verse indicates that He—glory be to Him—calls people to account for what is within their souls. ‘Umar said: “Weigh yourselves before you are weighed, and call yourselves to account before you are called to account.” “Calling to account” entails that these things are reckoned and enumerated.

As for “forgiveness and punishment,” the Book and the Sunnah indicate that anyone whose heart harbours unbelief, hatred of the Messenger, and hatred of what he brought is a disbeliever in Allah and His Messenger. Allah has pardoned this community—those who truly believe and harbour no doubt—for their inner thoughts, so long as they do not utter them or act upon them, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections in the reports of Abū Hurayrah and Ibn ‘Abbās. It is also reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever intends a good deed has it recorded for him, while whoever intends an evil deed does not have it recorded against him until he does it,”

provided he is a believer who habitually does good deeds and refrains from evil deeds. If he refrains from an evil deed for Allah’s sake, a good deed is recorded for him. When a servant expresses the evil within himself through words or deeds, it becomes one of the actions for which he deserves blame and punishment.

---

## Page 7171

and if he conceals it, and what he conceals entails abandoning faith in Allah and the Messenger—such as doubting or hating what the Messenger brought—he is subject to punishment for concealing that within himself, because he has abandoned the faith without which there is neither salvation nor happiness. If, however, it is an intrusive thought that the servant detests, then this is pure faith, as expressly stated in the Sahīh.

These “intrusive thoughts” assail the heart without a person’s choosing. If the servant detests and rejects them, his aversion is pure faith. Some of the Companions feared punishment for such thoughts, so Allah Most High said:

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.”

Wus‘ (capacity) is a verbal form used in the sense of its object: that which the soul can accommodate. Allah does not burden it with what is too much for it to accommodate; rather, this means what lies within its power and ability. Some people have said that “capacity” denotes what accommodates a person without constraining him. This is not so. Rather, what accommodates a person is permissible for him, while what he cannot accommodate is not commanded of him. What he can accommodate may be commanded of him; as for what he cannot accommodate, that is what is permissible.

One says, “I have room to do such-and-such,” and, “I have no room to do such-and-such.” What is permissible affords room; the related expression bāhat al-dār means the open courtyard of a house. Thus, what you are permitted to do affords you room, and you do not go beyond it. Hence the saying: “May Allah have mercy on someone who finds sufficient room within the Sunnah and does not go beyond it into innovation”—that is, in what Allah has commanded and what

---

## Page 7172

He has permitted, there is enough to meet the religious and worldly needs of the believer who follows the Sunnah, without his needing to go beyond it into what is forbidden.

As for what you have been charged with, it is what you have been commanded to do. This must be something within your capacity, not something that affords you latitude. One may say, “I am not at liberty to leave it undone”; indeed, leaving it undone is forbidden. Allah Most High has said:

“These are the limits set by Allah, so do not approach them.”

This marks the beginning of what is forbidden. He has also said:

“These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them.”

These mark the outer boundary of what is lawful. He has also said:

“That is because Allah would not change a favor He has bestowed upon a people until they change what is within themselves.”

This change is of two kinds. The first is that they manifest it outwardly, so that it takes the form of words and deeds that incur blame and punishment.

The second is that they replace the faith in their hearts with its opposites—misgivings, doubt, and hatred—and resolve not to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. In this latter case, they deserve punishment for neglecting what is commanded; in the former, for doing what is forbidden.

Likewise, whatever is within the soul that contradicts love for Allah, reliance upon Him, sincere devotion to Him, and gratitude to Him is punishable, because all these are obligatory. If the heart is devoid of them and characterized by their opposites, it deserves punishment for neglecting these obligations.

---

## Page 7173

With this distinction, many doubts are dispelled and the revealed texts are reconciled, for they all agree on this. The hypocrites, who outwardly display the opposite of what they conceal, are punished because their hearts have not believed, but have harbored disbelief. Allah Most High says:

“They say with their tongues what is not in their hearts.”

He also says:

“In their hearts is a disease.”

And He says:

“Those are the ones whose hearts Allah does not intend to purify.”

A hypocrite will inevitably reveal in his words and deeds something that indicates his hypocrisy and what he conceals. As ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān said: “No one conceals anything in his heart without Allah revealing it in his facial expressions and slips of his tongue.” Allah Most High has said concerning the hypocrites:

“If We willed, We could show them to you, and you would recognize them by their marks.”

Then He says:

“And you will surely recognize them by the tenor of their speech.”

This last statement is the clause answering an unstated oath, meaning: “By Allah, you will surely recognize them by the tenor of their speech.” Thus, recognizing the hypocrite by the tenor of his speech is inevitable, whereas recognizing him by his marks is dependent upon Allah’s will.

Now the verse:

“Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it”

is a statement from Allah; it contains no affirmation of faith on the servant’s part, unlike the two verses that follow it. As the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Whoever recites the last two verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah in a night, they will suffice him.”

Reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim. These two verses begin with His words:

“The Messenger has believed in what was revealed to him from his Lord, and so have the believers,”

and continue to the end.

The statements of the early generations agree with what we have explained. Ibn ʿAbbās said: “This verse has not been abrogated. Rather, when Allah gathers all creation, He will say: ‘I will tell you what you concealed within yourselves

---

## Page 7174

of which My angels were unaware.’ As for the believers, He will inform them and forgive them for the thoughts they entertained. This is the meaning of His words:

‘Allah will call you to account for it.’

That is, Allah will inform you of it. As for the people of polytheism and doubt, He will inform them of the denial they concealed. This is the meaning of His words:

‘Then He will forgive whom He wills and punish whom He wills.’”

It has also been reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that this verse was revealed concerning the concealment of testimony. The same was reported from ʿIkrimah and al-Shaʿbī. Concealing testimony constitutes neglecting an obligation, just like concealing a defect that must be disclosed or withholding knowledge that must be made known. Mujāhid said that it concerns doubt and certainty. This too concerns neglecting an obligation, since certainty is obligatory.

It is reported from ʿĀ’ishah: “Whatever you disclose, Allah will call you to account for it. As for what you conceal, its reckoning is in the punishment hastened for you in this world.”

This may be among the things for which a servant is punished with distress. Thus, when Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah was asked about distress whose cause is unknown, he said: “It is a sin you intended in secret but did not commit, so you were recompensed for it with anxiety.” Sins have their punishments: secret sins are punished secretly, and public sins publicly.

A report traced back to the Prophet is also narrated from her. She said:

“I asked the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) about this verse:

‘Whether you disclose what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it.’

He replied: ‘O ʿĀ’ishah, this is Allah’s reproach of His servant through the misfortune and fever that befall him—even a thorn-prick, or an article he puts in his sleeve and then cannot find, becoming alarmed over it, only to discover it in the bosom of his garment—until the believer emerges free of his sins, just as red gold emerges from the furnace.’”

---

## Page 7175

I say:

This report traced back to the Prophet is—and Allah knows best—an explanation of the ways in which the believer is punished in this world. It does not state that he is punished for everything he conceals. Rather, it states that if he is punished for what he conceals, his punishment takes such forms. This is what the authentic hadiths indicate.

Al-Rūyānī narrated in his Musnad, through al-Layth, from Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb, from Saʿīd ibn Sinān, from Anas, that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“When Allah intends good for His servant, He hastens his punishment in this world. When He intends evil for His servant, He withholds punishment for his sin until He requites him for it on the Day of Resurrection.”

Allah Most High has said:

“So He repaid you with distress upon distress, so that you would not grieve over what you had missed or what had befallen you. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do.”

“Then, after the distress, He sent down upon you security in the form of drowsiness, which overcame a group of you, while another group cared only for themselves, entertaining false thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ignorance. They said, ‘Do we have any say in the matter?’ Say, ‘The matter belongs entirely to Allah.’ They conceal within themselves what they do not disclose to you, saying, ‘If we had had any say in the matter, we would not have been killed here.’ Say, ‘Even if you had remained in your homes, those destined to be killed would have gone out to the places where they were to fall.’ This was so that Allah might test what is in your breasts and purify what is in your hearts. And Allah knows well what is within the breasts.”

These people entertained thoughts of ignorance that were incompatible with certainty in divine decree and with belief that Allah would grant His Messenger victory. Thus, their punishment was for abandoning certainty and harboring doubt and thoughts of ignorance. There are many such examples.

---

## Page 7176

This also includes the intentions behind deeds, for deeds are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended. “Intention” is something a person conceals within himself. If his purpose is to seek the Face of his Lord, the Most High, he deserves reward; but if his purpose is to show off before people, he deserves punishment. As Allah Most High says:

“So woe to those who pray, yet are heedless of their prayer—those who show off.”

And He says:

“When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off before people.”

The authentic hadith narrated by Abū Hurayrah concerning the three with whom the Fire will first be kindled mentions the man who learned and taught so that he would be called a scholar and a reciter; the man who fought so that he would be called bold and courageous; and the man who gave charity so that he would be called generous and noble. Their sole purpose was to gain people’s praise and esteem and to seek status among them. They did not seek the Face of Allah through these deeds, even though their deeds were outwardly good. When such people are brought to account, they will be among those who deserve punishment, as stated in the hadith:

“Whoever seeks knowledge to vie with scholars, argue with fools, or draw people’s attention to himself will have the Fire as the recompense for his deed.”

And in another hadith:

“Whoever seeks knowledge by which the Face of Allah should be sought, seeking it only to obtain some worldly gain, will not smell the fragrance of Paradise, although its fragrance can be detected from a distance of five hundred years’ travel.”

In short, the heart is the foundation, as Abū Hurayrah said: “The heart is the king of the limbs, and the limbs are its troops. If the king is good, its troops are good; and if

---

## Page 7177

the king is corrupt, its troops are corrupt.” This accords with the hadith of al-Nu‘mān ibn Bashīr, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, there is a piece of flesh in the body: when it is sound, the whole body is sound, and when it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Truly, it is the heart.”

The heart’s soundness or corruption necessarily entails the soundness or corruption of the body. This therefore belongs to what a person manifests, not what he conceals. Everything Allah has made obligatory upon His servants necessarily places an obligation upon the heart, for it is the foundation, even when other faculties bear that obligation by extension. It is the heart of the servant subject to commands and prohibitions that knows those commands and prohibitions, and it is the heart that is addressed by the requirement to obey and comply. Knowledge of what is commanded and compliance precede the performance of the commanded act, such as prayer, zakat, and fasting. When the servant turns away from knowing the command and from intending to comply, disobedience begins in the heart; indeed, the heart itself is disobedient, and the rest follows it in this.

Hence He says of the wretched:

“He neither believed nor prayed, but denied and turned away,”

and the verses that follow. He says of the blessed, in numerous places:
