“Indeed, those who believe and do righteous deeds.”

What is commanded is of two kinds. One kind consists of outward deeds performed by the limbs. These can occur only with the heart’s knowledge and will, so the heart is their foundation. Examples include ablution and ritual bathing; the movements of prayer, such as standing, bowing, and prostrating; and the rites of Hajj, such as standing and circumambulation.

---

## Page 7178

and if they consist of spoken words, the heart is more directly involved in them: it must be conscious of the words being uttered, or aware of what it is saying, and intend it.

For this reason, utterances are recognized under Islamic law only when made by a person of sound mind who knows what he is saying and intends it. As for an insane person or a child who has not attained discernment, all their utterances are legally without effect. No declaration of faith or disbelief, no contract, and no other utterance of theirs has legal validity, by agreement of the Muslims. Likewise, everything a sleeping person says in his sleep is legally without effect, whether the insane person or the sleeper utters words of divorce, disbelief, or anything else. This is unlike the case of a child, for if an insane person or a sleeping person destroys property, he is liable for it; and if he kills someone, blood money becomes due, just as it does for accidental killing.

The scholars differed concerning an intoxicated person, although they agreed that his prayer is invalid, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Command them to pray at seven, strike them for neglecting it at ten, and separate them in their beds.”

This hadith is well known in the Sunan collections. They differed concerning an intoxicated person’s legal acts, such as divorce, and his prohibited actions, such as killing and unlawful sexual intercourse: is he to be treated as a person of sound mind or as an insane person? Or should distinctions be made between his words and actions, or between some of these and others? There are several well-known opinions on this.

What the scriptural texts, foundational principles, and statements of the Companions indicate is that his utterances have no legal effect, like those of an insane person: they effect neither divorce nor anything else. For Allah Most High has said:

---

## Page 7179

“Until you know what you are saying.”

This indicates that he does not know what he is saying. The heart is the sovereign from which words and deeds proceed. If he does not know what he is saying, his words do not proceed from his heart; rather, they are treated as unintentional speech. The Lawgiver has made accountability depend only on what the heart earns through outward words and deeds, as He says:

“But He holds you accountable for what your hearts have earned.”

He does not hold people accountable for words and deeds of which their hearts were unaware and which they did not intend. Likewise, a person is not held accountable for his inner thoughts unless he speaks or acts upon them.

Some have said that Allah has affirmed that the heart earns deeds, saying:

“For what your hearts have earned.”

According to them, no servant of Allah conceals or openly performs any deed—whether a movement of his limbs or a thought within his heart—without Allah informing him of it and calling him to account for it. Then He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills. They cited as evidence His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, the hearing, the sight and the heart—each of these will be questioned.”

This view is weak and anomalous. His words:

“He holds you accountable for what your hearts have earned”

were mentioned only to explain that accountability for deeds depends on what the heart has earned. He does not hold people accountable for unintentional oaths, as He says:

“For the oaths you have deliberately sworn.”

Thus, accountability arises only when what the heart earns is combined with an action of the limbs. As for what occurs within the soul, Allah has pardoned it so long as a person neither speaks of it nor acts upon it. Nor does He hold a person accountable for any utterance or movement made without the heart’s intention and awareness.

Furthermore, if an intoxicated person’s divorce is invalid, whereas a discerning child can validly

---

## Page 7180

perform his prayer, yet his divorce does not take effect, then an intoxicated person’s divorce is all the more deserving of being deemed invalid.

“When Māʿiz confessed to an offence carrying a prescribed punishment, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) asked him, ‘Are you insane?’ He replied, ‘No.’ He then ordered that his breath be smelled to make sure he was not intoxicated.”

This indicates that an intoxicated person’s confession is invalid. The incident involving Māʿiz occurred later, after wine had been prohibited, for wine was prohibited in the year 3 AH, after Uhud, by common agreement. It is authentically reported from ʿUthmān and other Companions, such as ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, that an intoxicated person’s divorce does not take effect. No contrary position has been authentically established from any Companion.

Those who hold that his divorce takes effect have offered only a weak argument. Their principal contention is that he sinned by depriving himself of his reason. This is true and requires that he be punished for the sin of drinking, so the prescribed punishment is inflicted upon him for that. Divorce, however, is not a punishment imposed upon a Muslim for a sin. Were that so, the wife of everyone who drank wine or became intoxicated would be divorced. Yet those who hold this position say only that she is divorced if he pronounces divorce. Thus, they have given effect to his words, not his sin.

Moreover, while intoxicated, he might emancipate a slave, and emancipation is an act of devotion. If they uphold his emancipation, the distinction collapses; if they invalidate it, then there is even greater reason to invalidate his divorce, for Allah loves emancipation but does not love divorce.

Furthermore, anyone who bases this ruling on the sin committed must consistently apply it to someone who loses his reason through something other than an intoxicant, such as banj (a narcotic). This is the position of those among al-Shāfiʿī’s followers and others who agree with them, such as Abū al-Khattāb, who treat those affected by banj and those who are intoxicated alike. Most scholars, however, distinguish between the two, and this is explicitly stated by

---

## Page 7181

Ahmad, Abū Hanīfa, and others. This is because wine is something the soul craves, and drinking it incurs a prescribed punishment, unlike banj, which incurs no prescribed punishment but rather a discretionary one, because it is not craved. It is like carrion, blood, and pork: consuming these incurs a discretionary punishment. Scholars generally hold that no prescribed punishment applies to them, apart from an opinion reported from al-Hasan. This concerns someone who has lost his reason.

As for someone who understands what he is saying, if he acts voluntarily and intends what he says, his words have legal effect. If, however, he is wrongfully coerced, then, according to the majority of scholars, all his utterances are without legal effect, including statements of unbelief, professions of faith, pronouncements of divorce, and the like. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others.

Abū Hanīfa and a group of scholars distinguish between what admits of annulment and what does not. They say:

Whatever admits of annulment, such as a sale, is not binding upon someone who is coerced; rather, it remains contingent upon his ratification. Whatever does not admit of annulment, such as marriage, divorce, and oaths, is binding upon someone who is coerced.

The majority dispute this distinction on two grounds: whether the stated characteristic actually applies, and whether the ruling depends upon it. They say:

Marriage and similar transactions admit of annulment. Likewise, emancipation admits of annulment according to al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two positions in Ahmad’s school. Indeed, they may rule that a slave under a contract to purchase his freedom has become free, then annul that emancipation and return him to slavery. Binding oaths, too, admit of release, as Allah Most High says:

“Allah has already ordained for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

---

## Page 7182

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

“The point here” is that the heart is the foundation of all deeds and words. The outward deeds Allah has commanded necessarily require the heart’s knowledge and intention. The same applies to the words He has commanded and everything discussed above. Punishment for prohibited words and deeds is incurred only when they proceed from the heart’s intention. As for the applicability of certain rulings—such as liability for loss of life or destruction of property caused by someone who is insane, asleep, mistaken, or forgetful—this concerns justice in upholding people’s rights, not punishment.

Thus, as we have mentioned, what is commanded is of “two kinds”: outward acts performed by the limbs and inward acts within the heart.

“The second kind” comprises what takes place inwardly in the heart, such as sincerity, love for Allah and His Messenger, reliance upon Him, fear of Him, and the heart’s faith itself and its affirmation of what the Messenger has conveyed. The connection of this kind to the heart is evident, for the heart is its seat. This kind is the foundation of the first and has a greater impact, for good or evil, than the first.

No outwardly commanded act is complete without the heart’s faith, its love and reverence for Allah, its fear and hope, its reliance upon Him, and its sincere devotion to Him. Indeed, if someone performed outward deeds without these, he would be a hypocrite. These inward qualities themselves necessarily give rise to corresponding outward deeds in the person who possesses them, and they are nobler than the deeds that branch from them, as Allah Most High says:

---

## Page 7183

“Neither their meat nor their blood reaches Allah, but it is your piety that reaches Him.”

Likewise, denying the Messenger in one’s heart, hating him, envying him, and arrogantly refusing to follow him are graver sins than outward deeds unaccompanied by these inward states, such as murder, unlawful sexual intercourse, drinking alcohol, and theft. Outward deeds that constitute disbelief—such as prostrating to idols, reviling the Messenger, and the like—do so only because they necessarily entail inward disbelief. Otherwise, if, hypothetically, someone prostrated before an idol without intending in his heart to prostrate to it, but instead intended to prostrate to Allah, that would not constitute disbelief. This may be permissible when someone is among polytheists and fears for his life, so he conforms to their outward actions while intending in his heart to prostrate to Allah. It is related that some Muslim scholars and scholars among the People of the Book did something similar with a group of polytheists, eventually calling them to Islam, whereupon they embraced it through their efforts. They had not openly opposed them at the outset.

Here there are “fundamental questions” over which people have disagreed. One is whether affirmation or denial can exist in the heart without anything of it ever appearing in speech or bodily actions, while only its opposite appears, despite the absence of fear.

The position of the early generations, the leading scholars, and the majority of people is that what this inward state entails must manifest itself in bodily actions. Thus, anyone who says that he believes the Messenger, loves him, and reveres him in his heart, yet has never professed Islam or performed any of its obligations, despite having no fear, is not inwardly a believer; rather, he is a disbeliever.

---

## Page 7184

Yet Jahm and those who agreed with him claimed that such a person is inwardly a believer … (1) and that mere knowledge and affirmation in the heart constitute faith that warrants reward on the Day of Resurrection, without any spoken profession or outward action. This is false according to both revelation and reason, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Scholars of the early generations, such as Wakīʿ, Ahmad, and others, declared those who held this view to be disbelievers. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, in the body is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the whole body is sound; and if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Indeed, it is the heart.”

He thus explained that soundness of the heart necessarily entails soundness of the body. If the body is not sound, this indicates that the heart is not sound. Since a believing heart is sound, it follows that someone who professes faith but does not act upon it does not have a believing heart. Even someone under coercion, when it comes to expressing faith, must speak of it to himself and privately with someone he trusts. It must also show in his facial expressions and unguarded words, as ʿUthmān said. But if no trace of it ever appears in either his words or his deeds, this indicates that there is no faith in his heart.

This is because the body follows the heart. Nothing becomes firmly established in the heart without its necessary effects and implications appearing on the body in some way, even if an impediment prevents all its effects from appearing. The cause that brings about those effects remains present, whereas the impediment does not accompany a person as inseparably as his heart does. Its manifestation is impossible only in certain circumstances, when

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 7185

he conceals what is in his heart, as did the believer from Pharaoh’s household. Yet his call to faith revealed something of the faith in his heart that is not revealed by someone who openly professes his faith among those who share it. This concerns the heart’s knowledge and affirmation.

Another aspect is the heart’s intention and resolve. If a person intends an act and resolves to perform it, while able to do what he intends, is it possible that nothing of what he intends and resolves upon should occur?

There are two views on this. The sounder view is that when firm intention is combined with ability, the intended act necessarily follows. Whenever a person does not do what he is able to do, this indicates that there is no firm intention. A firm intention may exist despite an inability to perform the intended act, but it will be accompanied by preliminary steps toward that act. The other view holds that complete resolve may exist without any outward manifestation.

This parallels the view of those who say the same about knowledge and affirmation. Both are among the views held by Jahm’s followers who defended his position on faith, such as the judge Abū Bakr and others like him. They defended his position and opposed the early generations, the leading scholars, and the Muslim groups at large.

This resolves the dispute over “holding a person accountable for an intention.” Some say that he is held accountable for it if it amounts to resolve, while others say that he is not held accountable for it. The correct understanding is that once an intention becomes resolve, it must be accompanied by speech or

---

## Page 7186

action; for intention, together with ability, necessarily brings about the intended act.

Those who say that a person is held accountable for it cite his saying:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire.”

They cite this hadith, but it provides no proof for their position. He said this concerning two men who fought, each intending to kill the other. This is not mere resolve, but resolve accompanied by doing what lies within one’s power, while being unable to complete what one intends. Such a person is held accountable by the agreement of the Muslims.

Thus, someone who makes every effort to drink wine, pursuing it through his words and deeds, but ultimately cannot do so, is sinful by the agreement of the Muslims. He is like one who drinks, even though he has not actually drunk. Likewise, someone who strives to commit unlawful sexual intercourse, theft, or the like through his words and deeds, but ultimately cannot do so, is sinful like the person who commits the act. The same applies to killing a person and other acts.

Similarly, the one who calls to good is assigned the same reward and burden as the person he calls, because he intended that person’s action and intended to do whatever lay within his own power. Thus, with firm intention accompanied by doing what lies within his power in that regard, he receives the same reward and burden as the person who performs the act.

Allah Most High says:

“Those believers who remain behind—other than those with disabilities—are not equal to those who strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives.”

The verse continues. The decisive explanation of this verse is that “those with disabilities” fall into two categories. One category consists of those firmly resolved to strive in Allah’s cause: had they been able, they would neither have remained behind nor stayed away. It was only their excuse that kept them from going. They are as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said: “

---

## Page 7187

There are men in Medina who have been with you on every journey you have made and in every valley you have crossed.” They asked, “Even though they are in Medina?” He replied, “Even though they are in Medina; a valid excuse has kept them back.”

They are also as he described in the hadith of Abū Kabshah al-Anmārī:

“They are equal in reward.”

Likewise, in the hadith of Abū Mūsā:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, he is credited with the deeds he used to perform while healthy and at home.”

He thus affirmed that such a person is credited with the equivalent of those deeds, because his resolve is complete and only a valid excuse has prevented him.

The second category of “those with disabilities” consists of those who have no resolve to go forth. Those who go forth and strive in Allah’s cause, as well as those with disabilities who are firmly resolved to go forth, are superior to them.

Allah Most High’s words, “other than those with disabilities,” whether construed as an exception or a qualifying description, indicate that they are not included with those who remain behind in the denial of equality. Once a distinction is made among them between those who are resolved and those who are not, the verse retains its apparent meaning.

Were His words, “Allah has preferred those who strive with their wealth and their lives over those who remain behind by a degree,” taken as applying generally to those with disabilities and others alike, that would contradict His words, “other than those with disabilities.” For His words, “Those who remain behind and those who strive are not equal,” merely deny equality. If all those with disabilities were likewise unequal, His words, “other than those with disabilities,” would be rendered meaningless. It would also follow that no one who remains behind could equal those who strive, even if he had a disability. This is contrary to the intended meaning of the verse.

Moreover, if those who remain behind are not among those with disabilities, and striving in Allah’s cause

---

## Page 7188

is not an individual obligation, the collective duty having been fulfilled by others, then there is no blame on them for remaining behind. Indeed, they are promised the finest reward, just like those with disabilities. This resembles His words:

“Not equal among you are those who spent and fought before the conquest,” and the rest of the verse.

Thus, the promise of the finest reward includes both those with disabilities and others.

If it is said: In the first statement, He described their superiority as “a degree,” then described their superiority as:

“Degrees from Him, and forgiveness and mercy.”

Similarly, He said:

“Do you consider providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque equal to the deeds of one who believes in Allah and the Last Day and strives in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight. And Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

“Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives are greater in rank with Allah. It is they who are the successful.”

“Their Lord gives them glad tidings of mercy from Him, His good pleasure, and gardens in which they will enjoy lasting bliss.”

His words, “greater in rank,” resemble what He said concerning those who were foremost: “greater in rank.” Here, “rank” is in the accusative as a specification; that is, their rank is greater. This conveys superiority in general terms, as when one says, “This person’s standing is greater and higher.” The same applies to His words:

“And Allah has favoured those who strive over those who remain behind with a tremendous reward,” and the following verses.

This does not mean that they were favoured over them by only one degree. For in the authentic hadith narrated by Abū Saʿīd and Abū Hurayrah:

“In Paradise there are one hundred degrees which Allah has prepared for those who strive in His cause. The distance between every two degrees is like the distance between heaven and earth,” and the rest of the hadith. And in

---

## Page 7189

the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd:

“Whoever is pleased with Allah as his Lord, Islam as his religion, and Muhammad as his Prophet is assured Paradise.” Abū Sa‘īd marvelled at this, and the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said: “And there is another deed by which Allah raises His servant one hundred degrees in Paradise, the distance between every two degrees being like the distance between heaven and earth.” He asked: “What is it, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied: “Striving in Allah’s cause.”

This authentic hadith makes clear that the one who strives is favoured by one hundred degrees over someone who remains behind without a disability and has been promised the finest reward. It refutes those who say that the promise of the finest reward and the mention of superiority by a degree relate exclusively to those with disabilities. That view contradicts the Quran and Sunnah.

It may also be said that “a degree” is in the accusative as a specification, as in His words, “greater in rank”; that is, their rank surpasses that of the others. Similarly, one may say: “This person is favoured over that person in station and standing.” Moreover, “a degree” may refer to degrees in general—that is, station and abode—rather than a single numerical degree.

In His words:

“And Allah has favoured those who strive over those who remain behind with a tremendous reward,”

the word “degrees” is in the accusative, governed by “has favoured,” because favouring entails granting something additional to the one favoured. The meaning, then, is that He has granted them more than the others: a tremendous reward—degrees from Him, forgiveness, and mercy.

Thus, the disagreement concerns someone firmly resolved to act who has done all that is within his power: is he equal in reward or sin to the person who actually performs the act, or not? As for his deserving reward or sin, there is no disagreement about that.

The Prophet’s words:

“When two Muslims confront each other with their swords,”

entail that each is eager to kill the other and does all that is within his power. Thus, both deserve the Fire.

---

## Page 7190

and the question remains whether the two instances of remaining behind are equal in some other respect.

Such, too, is the condition of Muslims who fight one another in the civil strife that occurs among them: the outcome can only be evil for both victor and vanquished. The vanquished gains neither this world nor the Hereafter. As al-Sha‘bī said:

“We were overtaken by a trial in which we were neither righteous and God-fearing nor wicked and wretched.”

The victor, meanwhile, obtains an immediate worldly share, only to suffer retribution in the Hereafter. Allah may also hasten retribution for him in this world, as happened to most of those who prevailed in civil strife: calamity befell them in this world, as it did those who prevailed at al-Harra and during the turmoil involving Abū Muslim al-Khurāsānī, and in similar instances.

As for those who say that a person is not held accountable for resolve within the heart, they cite the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Allah has pardoned my community for what their souls whisper to them.”

This does not state that He pardons them for their resolve. Rather, it states that He pardons their inward thoughts until a person speaks or acts. It thus indicates that, so long as he neither speaks nor acts, he is not held accountable. Some, however, have supposed that this amounts to resolve, but that is not so. Rather, unless a person speaks or acts, it does not amount to resolve, for resolve must be accompanied by whatever action lies within his power, even if the person resolved does not attain his intended objective.

Someone firmly resolved to kill, commit unlawful sexual intercourse, or do something similar must make some movement—even if only with his head—or walk, take up an implement, utter a word, or say or do something. All of this is something for which he is held accountable, like the fornication of the eye, the tongue, and the foot. He is held accountable for these acts, which are preliminaries to the complete act of unlawful sexual intercourse

---

## Page 7191

involving the private parts. Pardon applies only to what has not found outward expression in word or deed and has never been accompanied by anything outward. This is pardoned for those who fulfil the heart’s obligation to carry out what is commanded, whether the command concerns an act of the heart whose necessary effects appear in the body, or an outward bodily act which the heart knows and intends.

When such people entertain thoughts within themselves, these are pardoned—for example, a persistent inclination unaccompanied by action, or intrusive thoughts they detest. They are rewarded for detesting those thoughts and for abandoning, for Allah Most High and out of fear of Him, what they had contemplated and resolved to do.

---

## Page 7192

The Shaykh—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Know that Allah, glorified and exalted be He, gave His Prophet Muhammad—may Allah’s blessings, peace, and benedictions be upon him—the closing verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah from a treasure beneath the Throne, from which no prophet before him had been given anything. Whoever reflects upon these verses and understands the truths of religion, the five foundations of faith, and the refutation of every proponent of falsehood that they contain, as well as the fullness of Allah Most High’s blessings upon this Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his community, Allah’s love for them, and His favoring them above all others—may he rejoice in that knowledge!

Were we to discuss these verses exhaustively, we would stray beyond the purpose of this book. Nevertheless, a few brief words pointing to some of these matters are necessary. We therefore say:

Since Sūrat al-Baqarah is the pinnacle of the Quran, contains more rulings than any other surah, and most comprehensively encompasses the principles of religion, both its foundations and its branches; and since it describes the “categories of people”—believers, disbelievers, and hypocrites—their characteristics and their deeds, presents the evidence establishing the existence of the Creator—glorified and exalted be He—and His oneness, recounts His blessings, and establishes the prophethood of His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 7193

and affirms the Hereafter, describing Paradise and Hell and the bliss and torment they contain; then recounts the creation of the upper and lower worlds; then describes the creation of Adam—peace be upon him—and Allah’s blessings upon him in teaching him, commanding His angels to prostrate before him, and admitting him to Paradise; then recounts his trial with Iblīs and the favorable outcome for Adam—peace be upon him; then presents the “debate” with the Jews among the People of the Book, rebuking them for their disbelief and obstinacy; then discusses the Christians, refutes them, and affirms that the Messiah is Allah’s servant; then establishes abrogation and the wisdom behind its occurrence; then recounts the building of the Sacred House, affirms the reverence due to it, mentions its builder and praises him; then establishes pure monotheism, the religion of Abraham—blessings and peace be upon him—declares those who turn away from it foolish, and recounts his enjoining it upon his sons; and so proceeds, step by step, to the end of the surah—Allah Most High concluded it with comprehensive verses affirming everything the surah contains. The Most High said:

“To Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will bring you to account for it. He then forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills. And Allah has power over all things.”

The Most High thus declares that whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth belongs to Him alone, with no

---

## Page 7194

partner sharing in it. This entails that true sovereignty belongs to Him alone and that His dominion encompasses everything that exists. It thus entails His oneness in lordship and His exclusive right to worship, ruling out any offspring, consort, or partner. For if whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth belongs to Him and is His creation, then none of them can be His offspring, consort, or partner.

He—glorified be He—has employed this very argument in Surah al-Anʿām and Surah Maryam. The Most High said:

“He is the Originator of the heavens and the earth. How could He have a child when He has no consort, and He created all things?”

The Most High also said in Surah Maryam:

“It is not fitting for the Most Merciful to take a child.”

“There is no one in the heavens and the earth but will come to the Most Merciful as a servant.”

This also entails that all longing, supplication, petition, and dependence must be directed to Him alone, for He owns whatever is in the heavens and the earth.

His dealings with His creation never depart from justice and beneficence, and He acts through His creation and His command. He has declared that whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth belongs to Him; thus, in creating and commanding, He acts only within what truly belongs to Him. Since Surah al-Baqarah contains more concerning command and creation than any other surah, Allah Most High declared that all this proceeded from Him within His own dominion. The Most High said:

“Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will bring you to account for it.”

This affirms the perfection of His knowledge

---

## Page 7195

—glorified and exalted be He—of His servants’ inner secrets and outward states, and affirms that none of this escapes His knowledge, just as no one in the heavens and the earth lies outside His dominion. His knowledge is all-encompassing, and so is His dominion.

Then the Most High states that He will bring them to account for this, meaning that He will make known to them what they revealed or concealed. This thus includes His knowledge of their affairs and His making those affairs known to them. Then He says:

“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills.”

This includes His governance of them with justice and grace: He forgives whom He wills out of grace and punishes whom He wills with justice. This entails reward and punishment, which necessarily imply commands and prohibitions, which in turn necessarily imply messengership and prophethood.

Then the Most High says:

“And Allah has power over all things.”

This means that absolutely nothing lies beyond His power and that everything brought about by His power occurs by His decree. This refutes the dualist Magians, the philosophers, the Magian Qadariyyah, and all who exclude anything subject to His power from His creation and power—and they comprise many groups.

Thus, the verse affirms Allah’s oneness; His knowledge of particulars and universals; revealed laws and prophetic missions; resurrection, reward, and punishment; the Lord’s governance of His creation with justice and grace; and the perfection and all-encompassing scope of His power. This entails that the world in its entirety came into existence, for that which is beginningless can neither be subject to power nor be something brought into being. Furthermore, affirming the perfection of His knowledge and power necessarily entails affirming all His other attributes

---

## Page 7196

, all of them exalted. For each attribute He has a beautiful name, so this also entails affirming His most beautiful names. His perfect power necessarily entails that He does whatever He wills. This, in turn, entails His being free of everything contrary to His perfection, including injustice, which is incompatible with His perfect self-sufficiency and perfect knowledge. Injustice proceeds only from someone who is needy or ignorant. As for Him who needs nothing and knows everything—glorified be He—injustice is impossible for Him, just as He cannot be incapable, for that would contradict His perfect power, or ignorant, for that would contradict His perfect knowledge.

Thus, the verse encompasses all this knowledge in the most concise expression, the most eloquent wording, and the clearest meaning. From this you can understand that the verse does not entail punishment for mere passing thoughts. Rather, it entails only that the Lord will call His servant to account for them. Being called to account is broader than being punished, and the broader does not necessarily entail the narrower. After calling His servants to account for these thoughts, He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills. Understood in this way, the verse remains in force and is not abrogated. Those among the early generations who said, “What follows it abrogated it,” meant that the subsequent passage clarified its meaning and intent. Such clarification was called abrogation in the usage of the early generations, just as they called an exception abrogation.

Then the Most High says:

“The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers.”

This is Allah Most High’s testimony that His Messenger—blessings and peace be upon him—believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord. This entails granting him

---

## Page 7197

the reward of those most perfect in faith—in addition to the reward for messengership and prophethood—for he shared the believers’ faith and attained its highest degrees, while being distinguished from them by messengership and prophethood.

His words, “sent down to him from his Lord,” imply that it is His speech, which He Himself spoke, and that it came down from Him, not from anyone else. As the Most High says:

“Say: The Holy Spirit has brought it down from your Lord.”

And He says:

“A revelation from the Lord of all worlds.”

This is one of the arguments the people of the Sunnah advanced against the Mu'tazilah, who say that Allah did not speak the Quran. They argued: “Had it been the speech of anyone other than Allah, it would have been sent down from that locus, not from Allah, for the Quran is an attribute that does not subsist independently. This differs from His words:

‘And He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth—all from Him.’

Those things are independently existing entities, so they are from Him in the sense that He created them. Speech, however, is an attribute subsisting in the speaker. Since it is from Him, it is His speech, for it is impossible for it to be from Him without His having spoken it.”

Then the Most High testified that the believers believed in what their Messenger believed in. He then testified that all of them believed in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. This testimony thus encompasses their belief in the five foundations of faith, without which no one can be a believer: belief in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day. The Most High has mentioned these five foundations at the beginning of the surah and in its middle

---

## Page 7198

and at its end. At its beginning, He says:

“And who believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you, and are certain of the Hereafter.”

Belief in what was revealed to him and what was revealed before him includes belief in the Books, the messengers, and the angels. Then He says:

“And are certain of the Hereafter.”

Belief in Allah is included in belief in the unseen and in belief in the Books and the messengers. Thus, these verses encompass belief in the five foundations.

In the middle of the surah, He says:

“Rather, righteousness is to believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Book, and the prophets.”

Then He relates that the people of faith said:

“We make no distinction between any of His messengers.”

That is, we do not believe in some and disbelieve in others, in which case our belief in those whom we accepted would avail us nothing, just as it availed the People of the Book nothing. Rather, we believe in them all, affirm their truthfulness, and make no distinction between them, for the message of their Lord unites them. How, then, could we distinguish between those whom Allah has united, oppose His messengers, and thereby be opposed to Him?

Through this faith, they distinguished themselves from every group of disbelievers, both those who reject the messengers altogether and those who believe in some while rejecting others. Their belief in Allah included belief in His lordship, His attributes of perfection and majesty, His most beautiful names, His all-encompassing power and will, and His perfect knowledge and wisdom. In this, they distinguished themselves from all the people of innovation who deny these matters or any part of them. For complete faith in Allah entails affirming what He has affirmed for Himself and declaring Him free of all that He has declared Himself free

---

## Page 7199

of. Through these two principles, they distinguished themselves from all groups of disbelievers and all misguided sects that deviate concerning Allah’s names and attributes.

Then they said:

“We hear and obey.”

This is their affirmation of the two pillars without which faith cannot stand. The first is hearing that entails acceptance—not merely the perception of sound, which believers and disbelievers share, but hearing with understanding and acceptance. The second is obedience that entails complete submission and compliance with the command. This is the opposite of the words of the nation that incurred Allah’s wrath: “We hear and disobey.”

These words thus encompass the completeness of their faith, acceptance, and submission. Then they said:

“We seek Your forgiveness, our Lord, and to You is the final return.”

They knew that they had not fulfilled the demands of faith, together with the obedience and submission it required of them. They also knew that overpowering natural inclinations and human impulses would inevitably lead them to fall short in some of the obligations of faith, and that nothing but Allah’s forgiveness could remedy these shortcomings. They therefore asked Him for His forgiveness, which is their ultimate happiness and the culmination of their perfection. For the ultimate goal of every believer is forgiveness from Allah Most High. Thus they said:

“We seek Your forgiveness, our Lord.”

Then they acknowledged that their final destination and return were to their true Master, and that they must inevitably return to Him. They said:

“And to You is the final return.”

These words thus encompass their faith in Him and their submission to Him in obedience and servitude

---

## Page 7200

and their acknowledgment of His lordship, their desperate need for His forgiveness, their admission that they have fallen short in fulfilling His rights, and their acknowledgment that they will return to Him.

Then Allah Most High said:

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

He thereby dispelled their assumption that He would punish them for passing thoughts they could not prevent, and that such thoughts fell within the scope of what He held them accountable for. He informed them that He required of them only what was within their capacity. This is the clarification to which Ibn ʿAbbās and others referred when they said: “Allah then abrogated it for them through His words:

‘Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.’”

This also establishes that everything He has required of them, whether through commands or prohibitions, is within their ability and power, and that He has not required of them anything beyond their capacity. This explicitly refutes anyone who claims otherwise.

Allah Most High commanded them to worship Him and guaranteed their provision. He therefore required of them deeds within their capacity and gave them provision sufficient for their needs. What He requires lies within their capacity, and His provision suffices them. Thus, both His provision and His commands allow them ample scope: they can fulfil His commands, and His provision meets their needs. There is a difference between what suffices a servant and what lies within the servant’s capacity. This is what befits His mercy, kindness, beneficence, wisdom, and freedom from all need—not the claim that He has required of them what they have absolutely no power or ability to do, and then punishes them for failing to do it.

Reflect on His words, mighty and majestic is He:

“Only what is within its capacity.”

Consider how they convey that His requirements afford people ease and bounty, rather than constraint, distress, and hardship; for capacity

---

## Page 7201

implies precisely that. The verse thus indicates that what He has required of them is within their ability, without hardship, constraint, or distress. This differs from what a person is merely capable of doing, for something may be within a person’s ability yet entail constraint and distress. By contrast, a person’s capacity—within which there is ease—falls short of the limits of strength and exertion. It leaves ample room and scope, which is incompatible with constraint and distress:

“He has placed no hardship upon you in religion.”

Rather:

“Allah intends ease for you and does not intend hardship for you.”

Commenting on His words, “Except within its capacity,” Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah said: “Only what it can do with ease, not with hardship. He did not require it to exert its full strength; had He done so, it would have been stretched to its limit.”

Such is the understanding of the leading scholars of Islam. How far removed this is from the claim that He has required of them what they are utterly unable to do and have no power to accomplish!

Then the Most High explained that the fruits and ultimate benefit of these obligations return to them, and that He is far above benefiting from the good they earn or being harmed by the evil they commit. Rather, the good they earn and its benefit are theirs, while the evil they commit and its harm fall upon them. He did not command them out of any need for them, but out of mercy, kindness, and generosity. Nor did He forbid them anything out of stinginess toward them, but to shield, preserve, and protect them, and keep them free from harm.

The verse also indicates that no soul is punished for the evil another commits or rewarded for the good another earns. It thus conveys the meaning of His words:

“And that a person will have nothing but what they strive for.”

“No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another.”

---

## Page 7202

It also affirms the soul’s earning of its deeds, which rules out compulsion. It also brings together aspects of wisdom: whether a person earns good or commits evil, the evil he commits does not nullify the good he has earned, contrary to those who maintain that sins nullify good deeds and entail eternal punishment. They say that what he has committed counts against him, while what he has earned is not credited to him. Thus, the verse refutes all these groups.

Consider how, regarding what is credited to the soul, He uses kasb (earning), which can arise from even the slightest involvement, whereas regarding what counts against it, He uses iktisāb (acquiring), which conveys attention, eagerness, and effort. The verb iktasaba is more emphatic than kasaba. This points to the predominance of grace over justice, and of mercy over wrath.

Now, the obligations He has imposed upon them are covenants, injunctions, and commands from Him that must be observed and upheld, without neglecting any part of them. Yet human nature inevitably gives rise to forgetfulness, error, weakness, and shortcomings. Allah Most High therefore guided them to ask Him to pardon them for all of this and relieve them of its consequences, saying:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord, do not lay upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us.”

That is: Do not impose upon us the heavy burdens You imposed upon those before us, for our bodies are weaker and our endurance is less.

Then, knowing that they cannot escape what He ordains and decrees for them, just as they cannot escape His commands and prohibitions, they asked Him for ease in what He ordains and decrees

---

## Page 7203

just as they had asked Him for ease in His commands and prohibitions, saying:

“Our Lord, do not burden us with what we have no strength to bear.”

This concerns divine decree, destiny, and afflictions, whereas their words:

“Our Lord, do not lay upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us”

concern commands, prohibitions, and religious obligations. Thus, they asked Him for ease in both respects.

Then they asked Him for pardon, forgiveness, mercy, and victory over their enemies. Through these four, blessing becomes complete for them. Life in this world and the Hereafter cannot be untroubled without them, and happiness and success depend upon them.

Pardon entails His waiving His claim against them and releasing them from it. Forgiveness entails protecting them from the evil consequences of their sins, turning toward them with favor, and being pleased with them. This differs from pardon alone, for someone may pardon another without turning toward him favorably or being pleased with him. Pardon is simply foregoing a claim, whereas forgiveness involves kindness, grace, and generosity. Mercy encompasses both, together with further kindness, compassion, and goodness. These three thus encompass deliverance from evil and attainment of good.

Victory enables them to proclaim His worship openly, make His religion manifest, exalt His word, overcome His enemies, find relief in their hearts from those enemies, and dispel the anger in their hearts and the resentments in their souls.

In the course of this supplication, they appealed to Him by acknowledging that He is their true Protector, besides whom they have no protector. He is the One who grants them victory, guides them, suffices them, helps them, answers their supplications, and whom they worship.

When these truths became firmly established in their hearts, their hearts submitted and humbled themselves before the might of their Lord and Protector, and their limbs responded accordingly, they were granted everything they requested of these things. They did not ask

---

## Page 7204

anything of Him without Allah Most High saying, “I have done so,” as is established in the authentic report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

These are brief, concise remarks on appreciating the worth of these verses of immense significance and exalted stature, which Allah bestowed exclusively upon His Messenger Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his community, from a treasure beneath the Throne. Yet they contain insights and profound truths of knowledge that human minds cannot fully encompass. We implore Allah not to deprive us of understanding His Book. Indeed, He is Merciful and Loving.

All praise belongs to Allah alone. May Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet, and upon all his family and Companions.

---

## Page 7205

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Chapter: On the supplication mentioned at the end of Surah al-Baqarah

This is His saying:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake,”

through to the end of the surah.

It is established in Sahīh Muslim that Allah said: “I have done so.”

Likewise, Muslim records in his Sahīh a report from Ibn ‘Abbās that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You have been given the Opening of the Book and the concluding verses of Surah al-Baqarah from a treasure beneath the Throne. You will not recite a single letter of them without being granted what it asks.”

Also in his Sahīh, Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was taken on the Night Journey, he was brought to the Lote Tree of the Utmost Boundary, which is in the seventh heaven. Whatever ascends from the earth reaches it and is received there, and whatever descends from above it reaches it and is received there.”

He recited: “When the lote tree was covered by whatever covered it,” and explained: “Moths of gold.”

He continued: “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was then given three things: he was given the five daily prayers, he was given the concluding verses of Surah al-Baqarah, and forgiveness of grave sins was granted to those of his community who die without associating anything with Allah.”

---

## Page 7206

Some people have said: “If this supplication has already been answered, then asking for what it contains is seeking to obtain what has already been secured. There is no benefit in that, so this supplication must be purely an act of worship, not intended as a request.” A group has said the same about all supplication: if what is sought has been decreed, there is no need to ask for it or seek it; and if it has not been decreed, supplication will not help—whether you supplicate or not. Thus, they regarded supplication as purely an act of worship, just as another group said concerning reliance upon Allah.

We have discussed these people at length elsewhere. We also mentioned the view of those who regard it as an indication or a sign, on the premise that no cause exists through which anything is brought about; rather, one event merely accompanies another. This was the view of a group of speculative thinkers among the Qadariyyah. The first person known to have held it was al-Jahm ibn Safwān, followed by those who agreed with him.

We explained that the “third view” is correct: supplication, reliance upon Allah, and righteous deeds are causes for obtaining the good of this world and the Hereafter for which one supplicates; sins, too, are a cause. An outcome tied to a cause may also require the fulfillment of a condition and the absence of impediments. Once these requirements are met, the effect undoubtedly follows.

Our purpose here is to discuss a supplication known to have been answered. Some people have said: “This is purely an act of worship, since what is sought is obtained without our supplication; it therefore no longer serves as either a cause or a sign.” This is a weak view.

---

## Page 7207

First, Allah does not command any deed in which there is no benefit for His servant. This rests upon the position of the early generations:

“Allah neither creates nor commands anything except for a wise purpose, just as He neither creates nor commands anything except for a cause.”

Those who deny causes and wise purposes say, rather, that He commands things that bring His servants absolutely no benefit, even when they obey Him and carry out His commands. This has been discussed at length elsewhere. The point is that everything Allah commands, He commands for a wise purpose, and everything He forbids, He forbids for a wise purpose. This is the position of all the leading jurists, the early generations of this Ummah, its imams, and its people generally. Thus, purely devotional prescriptions, in the sense of prescriptions devoid of any wisdom, have never occurred.

Certainly, the wisdom may lie in the commanded act, in the command itself, or in both. Some commanded acts would benefit a servant even if he performed them without having been commanded to do so, such as justice, kindness to others, maintaining ties of kinship, and so forth. Once such an act is commanded, it embodies “two aspects of wisdom”: wisdom in the act itself and wisdom in the command. It is therefore good both in itself and by virtue of the Lawgiver’s command. This is true of most of the revealed Law. As for what the revealed Law prescribed after it had not previously been prescribed, its wise purpose applied when it was commanded. Likewise, when a prescription was abrogated, its wise purpose ceased to apply and came to reside in its replacement, as with the direction of prayer.

If it is supposed that an act contains no wisdom whatsoever, does the command itself give it a wise purpose—namely, obedience? This is possible according to those who affirm purely devotional prescriptions, even if they do not maintain

---

## Page 7208

that anything whatsoever may be commanded. Rather, such a command is regarded as a test and trial: by carrying it out, the servant becomes obedient—as when they were forbidden to drink, except for those who scooped up a single handful.

The correct understanding is that when a command serves as a test and trial, urging an act that contains no benefit in itself, its purpose is achieved once the servant acknowledges the command and resolves to comply, even if he does not perform the act. Such was the case with Abraham when he was commanded to sacrifice his son. Likewise, in the hadith about a bald man, a leper, and a blind man, they were asked to give to a stranded traveller. The leper and the bald man refused and were stripped of their blessings. The blind man, however, offered what was requested, whereupon he was told: “Keep your wealth. You were only being tested. Allah is pleased with you and displeased with your two companions.”

This is the wisdom arising from the command or prohibition itself, rather than from the act itself. A servant may be commanded or forbidden to do something, and the wisdom may lie in his obedience to the command, his submission to it, and his offering what is asked of him. Thus, what was required of Abraham was to place his love for Allah above his love for his son, so that his intimate friendship with Him would be perfected before sacrificing this beloved son for Allah. When he proceeded and his resolve grew firm through his willingness to do so, his love for Allah above his child and all else became a reality. Nothing beloved remained in his heart that rivalled his love for Allah.

Likewise, Saul’s companions were tested by being required to abstain from drinking, so that they might attain the faith and obedience through which compliance is achieved. Here, the test took the form of a prohibition, not a command.

As for stoning the pillars and going between al-Safā and al-Marwah, the act itself is intended because of the remembrance of Allah it contains.

---

## Page 7209

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) made this clear in a hadith recorded in the Sunan collections:

“Going between al-Safā and al-Marwah and stoning the pillars were instituted only to establish the remembrance of Allah.”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, and others. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) thus explained that these acts have a wise purpose. How, then, can it be said that they have no wise purpose, but are merely acts of worship and a pure test? As for an act prescribed by Islamic law which believers perform, yet which contains no good, benefit, or wisdom beyond mere obedience, I know of no such act. Rather, commands of this kind were abrogated after the resolve to comply had been formed, just as the obligation of fifty prayers was reduced to five.

The “Muʿtazilah” deny the wisdom arising from the command itself. For this reason, they did not allow that a command could be abrogated before compliance became possible. A group of Ahmad’s followers and others, such as Abū al-Hasan al-Tamīmī, agreed with them on this. They based this on their principle that a command reveals the goodness already inherent in an act rather than conferring goodness upon it, and that only something good can be commanded.

They were mistaken in both premises. Even if a command reveals an act’s goodness, the act acquires through that command an additional goodness beyond its original goodness. Moreover, if the one issuing the command intends to test obedience, he may command something that is not intrinsically good and then revoke the command before compliance becomes possible, once its purpose has been achieved through the obedience, resolve, and submission of the person commanded. This occurs both in Allah’s commands and in the commands people issue to one another.

The Jahmiyyah deny that there is any wisdom whatsoever in an act, whether in the act itself or in the very

---

## Page 7210

command itself, based on their principles that He does not command for a wise purpose and that all actions are alike in relation to Him, with none being good and others evil. The Ashʿariyyah agreed with them on both principles, as did those jurists who followed them, including followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik, Ahmad, and others. Both principles are religious innovations. The position of the early generations and the imams is that Allah creates for a wise purpose and commands for a wise purpose. Their position is also that Allah loves faith and righteous deeds and is pleased with them, but does not love disbelief, wickedness, or disobedience, even though He has willed their existence. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Allah Most High says:

“Enter the gate bowing humbly and say, ‘Forgive us.’”

Prostration itself is an act of humble submission to Allah. If someone were to prostrate to Allah without knowing that He had commanded it, he would benefit from doing so, as did the magicians who prostrated before being commanded to prostrate.

Likewise, when a servant says, “Relieve us of our sins,” this is a supplication to Allah and an act of humble submission. Allah Most High says:

“When My servants ask you about Me, I am indeed near. I answer the call of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.”

These actions requested in the supplications at the end of al-Baqarah are things Allah’s servants seek. Another answer has also been given: when Allah Most High decrees something, He also decrees its causes, and supplication is among those causes. Thus, when He decreed victory on the day of Badr, and the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) told his Companions beforehand of the victory and the places where their enemies would fall, the Prophet’s appeals for help and supplications were among the causes of that victory. Likewise,

---

## Page 7211

in the case of al-wasīlah, which his Lord promised him and decreed for him, he commanded his community to ask for it on his behalf. Allah, glorified is He, decreed it through causes, including the supplications that would be made. On this understanding, the supplications actually offered in obedience to this command form part of the cause—and Allah knows best. He therefore rewards the supplicant for his supplication by making it complete the cause. On this explanation, the supplication does not cause the supplicant alone to receive any of what is sought; rather, it contributes to its attainment by the community as a whole. Nevertheless, he is rewarded for supplicating because his supplication is among its causes. This is because the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“No servant calls upon Allah with a supplication involving neither sin nor the severing of family ties without Allah granting him, through it, one of three things: either He hastens the fulfilment of his supplication, stores up an equivalent amount of good for him, expiates an equivalent amount of his sins, or wards off an equivalent amount of affliction. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, then we shall supplicate abundantly!’ He replied, ‘Allah has still more to give.’” (1)

Thus, someone who offers this supplication is like someone who asks for al-wasīlah: he receives a reward particular to himself, just as someone who supplicates for the community or for his absent brother does. His supplication is among the causes of good through which mercy reaches the community. Similarly, someone who asks that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) be granted al-wasīlah is rewarded by becoming entitled to his intercession on the Day of Resurrection.

There is also a “third answer”: everyone who offers this supplication obtains something of what he asks and seeks that he would not obtain without offering the supplication called for. Supplicating in this way is therefore like supplicating for his other needs, such as forgiveness and mercy. It is not like the supplication of

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The original manuscript and the printed edition include an additional item: “Or an equivalent amount of his sins is expiated for him.” This would make four items, contradicting the hadith’s opening words, “Three things.” The correct reading is the one we have adopted from Ahmad, Ibn Abī Shayba, and al-Tabarānī.

---

## Page 7212

one person for another in his absence; for in that case the angel says, “And may you have the same,” thus asking for him the same as he requested for the absent person. Here, however, he is supplicating for himself and for the believers.

To explain: although the revealed law was finalized with the death of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), and it has been stated that Allah has pardoned his community for mistakes and forgetfulness, and that the Messenger relieves his community of their burden and the shackles that bound them, and although he asked his Lord not to give an outside enemy power to destroy his community, and was granted this request—nevertheless, individual members of the community may only enjoy these protections through obedience to Allah and His Messenger. If someone disobeys Allah, he is punished for that disobedience by being deprived of this blessing, even though the revealed law has not been abrogated.

This is illustrated by the fact that this supplication includes asking Allah for pardon, forgiveness, mercy, and victory over the disbelievers. It is well known that not every individual member of the community receives all this. Rather, some enter the Fire, some are overcome by the disbelievers, and some are deprived of provision because they have neglected obedience to Allah and His Messenger. They are deprived of these blessings in proportion to their neglect or shortcomings.

As for Allah’s words, “I have done so,” two explanations may be offered.

The first is that He has done this for the believers mentioned in the verse, and faith without qualification entails obedience to Allah and His Messenger. Whoever is not like this falls short in

---

## Page 7213

the faith required of him. He therefore deserves to be deprived of these blessings in proportion to that deficiency. Allah hinders his enjoyment of them, and he does not merit the reward merited by one who fulfils the requirements of obligatory faith.

The second explanation is that this supplication was answered for the community as a whole, which does not necessarily mean that it was answered for every individual. Both explanations are correct, for what was requested has indeed been granted to the community as a whole. Otherwise, they would have been destroyed by a punishment of total annihilation, as earlier communities were destroyed.

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said in an authentic hadith:

“I asked my Lord for three things for my community. He granted me two and withheld one. I asked Him not to destroy my community through a general famine, and He granted me that. I asked Him not to give an enemy from outside their ranks power over them to wipe them out, and He granted me that. I asked Him not to let them fight one another, but He withheld that from me. He said, ‘O Muhammad, when I issue a decree, it cannot be reversed.’”

Likewise, the two Sahīh collections relate:

“When the words of the Most High were revealed, ‘Say: He has the power to send punishment upon you from above you,’ the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘I seek refuge in Your Face.’ At the words, ‘or from beneath your feet,’ he said, ‘I seek refuge in Your Face.’ At the words, ‘or divide you into factions and make some of you taste the violence of others,’ he said, ‘These two are less severe.’”

This is because sins will inevitably occur within this community, and its members will inevitably differ. These are inherent features of human nature; the children of Adam cannot be otherwise.

---

## Page 7214

And for this reason, the disagreements, fighting, and sins that have occurred within this community are not evidence of its inferiority. Rather, it is the best of communities. What has occurred among its members is inherent in human nature, and occurs more frequently and more severely in other communities. Other communities have less goodness; this community has more goodness and less evil. Thus, every good found in another community is greater in this one, and every evil found in this community is greater in others.

As for individual members of this community receiving what was sought in the supplication, it does not follow that every sinner must receive it, since he has not fulfilled his obligations. Nevertheless, a sinner may receive some of it in proportion to his obedience to Allah Most High. That forgiveness, pardon, and mercy are granted in proportion to faith and obedience is clear, since these are matters of divine decree governing creation, belonging to the same category as the promise of reward and the threat of punishment. They vary according to faith and righteous deeds. However, exemption from accountability for mistakes and forgetfulness, and the lifting of burdens, may present a difficulty, since these concern the rulings of religious law—the rulings of command and prohibition.

It may therefore be said:

The exemption granted to the community for mistakes and forgetfulness extends to its sinful members, for a sinner incurs no sin through mistakes or forgetfulness. If he eats forgetfully, he continues his fast, whether he is otherwise obedient or sinful. This is precisely where the difficulty lies, and there are two answers to it. (The first is that sins and acts of disobedience may cause a lack of knowledge of the upright monotheistic faith

---

## Page 7215

with its ease and tolerance. A person may do something forgetfully or mistakenly, yet, because he has fallen short in obeying Allah in knowledge and practice, he may not know that he is exempt from accountability for it—either because of his ignorance or because no one is available to explain to him the concession afforded by this tolerant monotheistic faith.

Scholars have disagreed over many questions concerning mistakes and forgetfulness. Many have held that these invalidate acts of worship, or some of them. Some, for example, regard forgetfulness as invalidating a fast, while others regard mistakes as doing so. Similar disagreements concern ihrām, speaking during prayer, and doing an act covered by an oath forgetfully or mistakenly. Although Allah, glorified be He, has exempted people from accountability for mistakes and forgetfulness, many Muslim scholars have remained unaware of this in numerous cases. This becomes a punishment for someone who finds himself unable to trust anyone but these scholars: they give him rulings that entail holding him accountable for mistakes and forgetfulness. Thus, what this supplication seeks is not realized for him because of a lack of knowledge, not because the religious law has been abrogated.

Among the ways Allah, glorified be He, punishes people for sins is by depriving them of guidance and beneficial knowledge, as He says:

“And for their saying, ‘Our hearts are wrapped.’ Rather, Allah has sealed them because of their disbelief.”

He also says:

“And they say, ‘Our hearts are wrapped.’ Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief.”

And He says:

“And what will make you realize that even if it came, they would not believe?”

“And We will turn their hearts and their eyes away, just as they refused to believe in it the first time.”

And He says:

“In their hearts is a disease, so Allah has increased their disease.”

And He says:

“So when they deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate.”

---

## Page 7216

And this is akin to His forbidding the Children of Israel wholesome things previously permitted to them because of their wrongdoing and transgression. Muhammad’s religious law is not subject to abrogation, nor is his entire community punished in this way. Its wrongdoers, however, may be punished by being deprived of wholesome things or having them forbidden to them.

This may take the form of deprivation through the divine decree: their rain may fail, their crops may perish, and their supplies of provisions may be cut off. Or they may no longer find the pleasure they once enjoyed in food, drink, marital intimacy, clothing, and the like. They may be overwhelmed by sorrows and things that spoil and obstruct their enjoyment, and made to swallow the bitterness associated with wealth, children, and family. As Allah Most High says:

“So let neither their wealth nor their children impress you. Allah only intends to punish them through these in the life of this world.”

And He says:

“Do they think that, by providing them with wealth and sons, We are hastening to grant them good things? Rather, they do not perceive.”

And He says:

“Your wealth and your children are only a trial.”

This is like the trial of the Sabbath-breakers through the fish.

Alternatively, they may be punished by coming to believe that something wholesome and lawful is forbidden, because they are unaware that Allah and His Messenger have permitted it. Many in this community have done precisely this: they have believed certain things to be forbidden, and this belief has gained currency among them through the oaths and divorces in which they become entangled, even though Allah and His Messenger have not forbidden those things. Yet, because they thought these things were forbidden to them, they were punished by being deprived of the knowledge through which they would recognize their lawfulness. Thus, these things became forbidden to them through the divine decree and, outwardly, under religious law. For a scholar exercising independent judgment must state the conclusion to which his reasoning leads him. If his reasoning leads him only to prohibit these wholesome things because he is unable to discern

---

## Page 7217

the evidence indicating their lawfulness, his inability becomes a cause of their being forbidden to those who fall short in obedience to Allah. Likewise, they have believed many transactions they need—such as leasing orchards, partnerships, and others—to be forbidden, because the evidence of religious law was obscure to them. Thus, these transactions became forbidden for them on the strength of what they took to be evidence.

This is like a person being punished by being kept unaware of wholesome food and drink that are available and within his reach, if only he knew of them; yet, as a punishment, he remains unaware of them. Indeed, a servant is deprived of provision because of a sin he commits. Allah Most High has said:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him”

“and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

He, glorified be He, has guaranteed that things will take their proper and sound course only for those who fear Him, just as He has guaranteed this to those who fear Him.

It thus becomes clear that members of this community who fall short in obedience to Him may be held accountable for mistakes and forgetfulness, without any abrogation having occurred after the Messenger. This is because they do not know of the ease the Messenger brought, nor do the scholars among them know of it. Hence, many who do not shorten their prayers while travelling also consider it forbidden not to fast while travelling. They therefore fast despite the great hardship this causes them. This is a punishment for falling short in obedience; nevertheless, it is also a means by which Allah expiates some of their sins, just as He expiates the believers’ sins through the other afflictions of this world.

---

## Page 7218

Likewise, some of them believe that offering the full four units of prayer while travelling is obligatory, so they do so and are afflicted with this burden because of their shortcomings in obedience. Others believe that many permissible things are forbidden. Some of these are permissible by consensus, while others are disputed; nevertheless, the Messenger did not forbid them. Thus, those who believe obligatory what Allah and His Messenger have not made obligatory, and forbidden what He has not forbidden, have had a burden laid upon them. Not all burdens and shackles have been removed from them, even though the Messenger has removed them, because they remain unaware of this.

They may also be afflicted with someone in authority who imposes these requirements upon them. Thus, burdens and shackles come upon them through someone they obey, such as a judge, a mufti, an administrator of a charitable endowment, or a ruler. These requirements are attributed to the revealed Law because of his mistaken belief that they belong to it. Their authorities’ ignorance of the ease Allah has granted them becomes a punishment for their sins. This is like someone leading them along a harmful road, turning them away from a route with water and pasture out of ignorance rather than any deliberate intention to harm them; or keeping them in a town where prices are high when they could live elsewhere.

For just as people may be afflicted with someone in authority who wrongs them deliberately, they may also be afflicted with someone in authority who is ignorant of their religious and worldly welfare. The ignorance of the latter becomes a cause of their punishment, just as the injustice of the former becomes a cause of their suffering. These people have not had their burdens and shackles removed because of their sins and disobedience, even though the Messenger’s law contains no such burdens or shackles. For this reason, unjust and oppressive rulers are given power over them, and there are brought

---

## Page 7219

enemies against them, led in chains of compulsion and divine decree. These are among the burdens and shackles that have not been removed from them, along with countless other punishments. This is because obedience is weak in their hearts, while sins and the love of desires have taken firm hold there. Thus, when they say,

“Our Lord, lay not upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us,”

this too is included.

As for His words,

“And do not burden us with what we cannot bear,”

there are two interpretations.

One is that this refers to burdens imposed through divine decree, not obligations prescribed by the revealed Law. That is: do not afflict us with calamities we cannot bear, as a person may be afflicted with unbearable poverty, illness, misfortune, fear, love or infatuation, with his sins being the cause. This is among the things that demonstrate that sins invariably have blameworthy consequences.

His words, “Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,” and “So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,” and “And whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it,” are true. The Most High also said concerning the people of Lot:

“And We left therein a sign for those who fear the painful punishment.”

No one is afflicted with engaging in deeds of their kind without suffering some painful punishment. Even deliberately looking gives rise to an attachment in the heart that torments a person. If it grows stronger until it becomes consuming love and infatuation, the painful torment increases, whether he is presumed capable of attaining

---

## Page 7220

the beloved or unable to do so. If he is unable, he suffers the painful torment of sorrow, anxiety and distress. If he is able, he suffers the painful torment of fearing separation, striving to win the beloved’s affection and seeking ways to secure his approval. If death befalls him or he becomes poor, his torment is multiplied. If his beloved turns to someone else, either replacing him or sharing his affections, his torment intensifies. This kind of attachment brings suffering that does not occur in infatuation with prostitutes, nor does anything comparable occur in lawful relationships. Even if some suffering occurs within a lawful relationship, it is lighter than its counterpart, and that becomes a cause of further sins.

When a person offers this supplication, including both himself specifically and the Muslims generally, he receives the greatest share of its benefit. How could it be otherwise, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one recites the last two verses of Sūrat al-Baqarah in a night without their sufficing him.”

And how could they fail to suffice him, when he has received, of what he prayed for, only what the other believers who did not recite them received? For the person who offers this supplication receives a share of its benefit particular to him, as with other supplications.

This is further clarified by the fact that the Companions had this supplication answered only when they committed themselves to unconditional obedience to Allah, saying:

“We hear and we obey.”

Then this supplication was revealed; they offered it, and it was answered. Thus, during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they lived according to the upright, accommodating religion

---

## Page 7221

and their adherence to it during the time of Abū Bakr was better than during the time of Umar. During Umar’s time, some of them committed sins that required the ruler, exercising his judgment, to impose certain stricter measures upon them: preventing them from combining umrah and Hajj in one journey (tamattu), counting a triple divorce pronounced in a single statement as three divorces, and increasing the punishment for drinking wine. Umar would defer to those most obedient to Allah and most detached from worldly life, such as Abū Ubaydah, as he would not defer to others.
