Some questions of inheritance law and other matters remained unclear to them, leading them to disagree, yet they remained united and affectionate towards one another, each allowing the others to follow their own independent legal judgment.

Towards the end of Uthmān’s caliphate, change and indulgence in worldly comforts increased, and various practices appeared that had not existed during Umar’s time. Estrangement arose between some hearts, until Uthmān was killed and they fell into great turmoil. Allah Most High has said:

“And beware of a trial that will not afflict only the wrongdoers among you.”

That is, this trial does not afflict the wrongdoer alone; it afflicts both the wrongdoer and whoever remains silent instead of forbidding his wrongdoing. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When people see wrongdoing and do not change it, they are on the verge of being overtaken, all of them, by a punishment from Allah.”

This became a cause of their being deprived of many good things. They began disputing over tamattu and similar matters about which there had been no contention during Umar’s time. One group, such as Ibn al-Zubayr, prohibited tamattu altogether. Another group, including the Umayyads and most people, prohibited converting an intended Hajj into umrah to be followed by Hajj, and they began punishing those who performed tamattu. Yet another group considered tamattu obligatory, and none of them

---

## Page 7222

intended to oppose the Messenger; rather, the knowledge was hidden from them because of the sins that had occurred. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I came out to tell you when the Night of Decree would be, but two men quarrelled, so knowledge of it was taken away. Perhaps that will be better for you.”

That is, concealing it may be better for you, so that you strive in worship throughout all ten nights. Concealing certain matters may indeed be a mercy for some people. Disagreement over rulings may also be a mercy, provided that the ruling’s obscurity does not lead to great harm. Thus, when a man compiled a book and called it The Book of Disagreement, Ahmad told him, “Call it The Book of Latitude.”

The truth is, in reality, one; yet Allah’s mercy towards some people may entail its remaining hidden, because making it known would impose hardship on them. This falls under His words, Most High:

“Do not ask about things which, if made known to you, would distress you.”

Likewise, food and clothing found in the markets may actually have been wrongfully seized. If a person does not know this, it is all lawful for him, and he incurs no sin whatsoever on that account—unlike when he knows. Thus, remaining unaware of what entails stricter obligations may be a mercy, just as remaining unaware of what permits a concession may be a punishment. Likewise, the removal of doubt may be a mercy or a punishment.

A concession is a mercy, yet what a person dislikes may be more beneficial, as in jihad:

“But perhaps

---

## Page 7223

you dislike something which is good for you, and perhaps you love something which is bad for you.”

The point here is that some sins cause beneficial knowledge, or part of it, to remain hidden. Indeed, they may cause a person to forget what he has learned and to confuse truth with falsehood, giving rise to turmoil.

Allah, glorified is He, had settled Adam and his wife in Paradise and said to them:

“Eat freely from it wherever you wish, but do not approach this tree, lest you become wrongdoers.”

“But Satan caused them to slip and brought them out of the state they had enjoyed. We said, ‘Go down, as enemies to one another.’”

Every instance of enmity, affliction, and suffering among their descendants until the Hour is established, and in the Fire on the Day of Resurrection, is caused by sins and disobedience to the Lord, Most High.

When a person remains steadfast in obeying Allah, inwardly and outwardly, he enjoys the bliss of faith. Knowledge comes to him from every direction, and he lives in the paradise of this world, as in the hadith:

“When you pass by the gardens of Paradise, graze in them.”

It was asked, “What are the gardens of Paradise?”

He replied, “Gatherings of remembrance.”

He also said:

“Between my house and my pulpit is a garden from the gardens of Paradise.”

For here he would be amid the gardens of knowledge and faith. Whenever a person’s heart is engaged in loving Allah, remembering Him, and obeying Him, it is attached to the highest realm

---

## Page 7224

and it remains elevated as long as this continues. But when he sins, his heart descends, and it continues to sink as long as he remains in that state. Enmity then arises between him and others like him. If Allah wills good for him, he turns back and strives while his heart is still low, until he becomes upright and his heart ascends. Allah, Most High, says:

“Neither their meat nor their blood reaches Allah, but your piety reaches Him.”

It is the piety of hearts that reaches Allah, as He says:

“To Him ascends good speech, and righteous deeds raise it.”

As for things separate from us, such as meat and blood, they do not reach Allah.

The Bātiniyyah (esotericists), who deny the creation of the world in six days and bodily resurrection, and interpret the Quran to accord with their own doctrine, hold that there is no “Paradise” other than the pleasure arising from the knowledge and praiseworthy character possessed by the soul. Nor, according to them, is there any “Fire” other than the pain arising from the ignorance and evil, blameworthy character possessed by the soul. Thus, the fire of souls is the pain within them, such as their anguish over failing to attain knowledge or losing the worldly things they love. Their sins alone veil them.

Such views are among those Abū Hāmid mentions in al-Madnūn bihi ‘alā ghayr ahlihi (What Is Withheld from Those Unfit for It). He may, however, say that this is not the punishment of the grave mentioned in connection with bodies; rather, that is a separate matter explained by the people of the Sunnah. According to them, bliss consists only in such states within the soul. Thus, they acknowledge neither bliss nor punishment separate from the soul.

---

## Page 7225

This view is among the most untenable, both according to revelation and reason. People in this world are rewarded and punished through things external to themselves; how much more so in the abode of recompense! Some of what they affirm concerning these two matters is true, but their falsehood lies in denying what Allah and His Messenger have revealed.

These people hold that Adam was only ever in a paradise of knowledge, and that his descent was a decline in his degree of knowledge. This is false. Nevertheless, whatever truth they affirm remains true, and Adam’s story points to it by way of drawing an analogous lesson—what the Sufis call “allusion”—not because this is the intended meaning of the verse. Rather, other verses establish it: they indicate that whoever rejects the truth is punished by having his heart sealed, so that he cannot grasp knowledge or understand its intended meaning, and that his enemy is given power over him and he suffers humiliation. As the Most High says concerning the Jews:

“Humiliation and destitution were imposed upon them.”

“That was because they disobeyed and used to transgress.”

There is no doubt that the pleasure of knowledge is the greatest of pleasures. The “pleasure” that endures after death and benefits one in the Hereafter is the pleasure of knowing Allah and acting for His sake; this is faith in Him. Yet they equate this with absolute existence.

Furthermore, mere knowledge of Him, if unaccompanied by love and worship of Him, but accompanied instead by love for someone else, whoever that may be, may bring a person some of the greatest torment in this world and the Hereafter. Yet they locate perfect pleasure in knowledge alone.

---

## Page 7226

Moreover, their restriction of pleasure to intellectual pleasure is mistaken. The Christians went further, adding hearing and smell, saying that people will enjoy alluring fragrances and delightful melodies. Yet neither they nor the Jews affirmed eating, drinking, or sexual intercourse—the pleasure of touch. Muslims, however, affirmed every kind of pleasure—hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch—for both soul and body. This is true perfection, not what is affirmed by the People of the Book or by those worse than them among the esoteric philosophers.

The greatest pleasure of the Hereafter is the pleasure of looking upon Allah, glorified be He, as stated in the authentic hadith:

“He will have given them nothing dearer to them than looking upon Him.”

This is the fruit of knowing and worshipping Him in this world. The sweetest thing in this world is knowing Him, and the sweetest thing in the Hereafter is looking upon Him, glorified be He. Thus, His manifestation on Fridays in the Hereafter corresponds to one’s Friday prayer in this world.

Abū Hāmid and others like him mention “vision” in their books, describing it as the highest form of bliss. He speaks of the removal of the veils and of people seeing Allah’s Face. Yet by all this he means what the Jahmiyyah and the philosophers maintain, for “vision,” according to them, is nothing but knowledge. Just as a person may see something with his own eyes, while his imagination may represent it to him when it is absent, so too, they hold, is knowledge: in this world there is only a representation of knowledge, like a mental image in calculation, whereas in the Hereafter they will know Him without a representation. According to them, He is “an existence neither within the world nor outside it,” and “the lifting of the veil”

---

## Page 7227

means, in their view, removing the impediment within a person that prevents vision. This is a privation, so in reality it amounts to making the servant know. All of this is maintained by the philosophers and the esotericists. They urge renunciation of this world only so that the soul’s attachment to it will cease when the soul departs, leaving it separated from nothing it loves. Abū Hāmid, however, never permits anything forbidden by religious law. Rather, he says that killing one of these men is better than killing a great many unbelievers. These others, by contrast, may permit someone who has attained the knowledge they seek to engage in things forbidden by the revealed laws—even sexual immorality, drinking wine, and other things—if they are among those who believe wine to be forbidden in the first place. Otherwise, most of them do not regard Islamic law as binding at all; rather, they permit adopting Judaism or Christianity. Anyone among these people who has attained their knowledge has, in their view, attained felicity.

The same is maintained by those among them who profess union, such as Ibn Sabʿīn, Ibn Hūd, al-Tilimsānī, and others like them. They enter churches with the Christians, pray with them facing east, and drink wine with them and with the Jews. They incline more towards the Christian religion than towards the religion of the Muslims because it permits forbidden things, because Christians are closer to union and indwelling, and because they are more ignorant, and thus more receptive to what these men say than to what Muslims say. Christian scholars are ignorant; if there is someone among them who dabbles in philosophy,

---

## Page 7228

they hold him in high esteem—and these men dabble in philosophy. One of them rejoices when he is told, “You are not a Muslim.” He recounts of himself—as Ahmad al-Mārdīnī, one of Ibn ʿArabī’s associates, used to recount of himself—that he entered a Christian monastery to obtain food for himself and his companion. One of the Christians began speaking about the Muslims, saying, “They say such-and-such.” Another told him, “Do not speak against the Muslims, for this man is one of them.” The speaker replied, “Does this man have the face of a Muslim?”—meaning that he was not a Muslim. Al-Mārdīnī would recount how the Christian had said of him, “This man is not a Muslim,” rejoicing at the Christian’s words and affirming what he said: namely, that he was not a Muslim.

Those who dabble in philosophy state this explicitly. They say, “We say such-and-such, while the Muslims say such-and-such.” Sometimes they say, “We say this, while the adherents of religious faiths say that”—meaning the Muslims, Jews, and Christians. Their books are full of this. When among the adherents of a religious faith, each of them must follow their religion. Yet their participation in this is like their involvement in the political affairs of kings, as when they were with the unbelieving Turks, with Hülegü, king of the unbelieving Mongols, and with the Khan, who ranked above him, the successor of Genghis Khan in the lands of Khitā. For one of them there, affiliation with Islam means affiliation with a form of Islam acceptable to that

---

## Page 7229

king, as suited his purposes—just as al-Nasīr al-Tūsī and his like behaved with Hülegü, king of the unbelievers. It was he who advised them to kill the caliph in Baghdad upon its capture. He seized people’s books, both privately owned and held as charitable endowments, took those that served his purposes, and ruined the rest. He built an observatory and placed the books there. He gave funds from Muslim endowments to the scholars of the polytheists—the Bakhshiyya and the Tawīniyya—and at his observatory he paid the philosopher, astrologer, and physician many times what he paid the jurist. He and his companions drank wine during the month of Ramadan and did not pray.

Likewise, in Syria and Egypt there was a group whose members, for all their Sufism, devotion, and asceticism, would drink wine during the daytime in Ramadan. Sometimes they prayed, and sometimes they did not. They did not believe themselves bound by Islam’s obligations and prohibitions. Rather, they said, “These are for the common people and the prophets. People like us have no need of the prophets.”

They relate that one of the philosophers was told, “A prophet has been sent,” and he replied, “If everyone were like me, they would have no need of a prophet.” Such a story is recounted even by someone who is chief physician, yet does not recognize heresy or understand what this statement entails, owing to his ignorance of prophethood. Their foremost leader in the time of Moses was asked, “Why do you not go to him and learn from him?” He replied, “We are a rightly guided people, so we have no need of anyone to guide us.”

As for what they mention of pleasure in the heart and bliss through faith in Allah

---

## Page 7230

and knowledge of Him—that is true, and it is a means of entering Paradise. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the month of Ramadan begins, the gates of Paradise are opened, the gates of the Fire are closed, and the devils are shackled.”

This is only because, during Ramadan, hearts are stirred toward goodness and righteous deeds, through which and on account of which the gates of Paradise are opened. People refrain from the evils through which the gates of the Fire are opened, and the devils are shackled, so they cannot do what they do when people are not fasting. To be shackled is to be bound. The devils gain power over the children of Adam only through their desires; when people restrain their desires, the devils are shackled.

The Paradise and Fire whose gates are opened and closed are distinct from what is in the hearts. Yet what is in the hearts is a cause leading to them, evidence of them, and one of their effects. Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, those who unjustly consume the property of orphans consume nothing but fire into their bellies.”

And the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever drinks from vessels of gold and silver is only gulping the fire of Hell into his belly.”

One explanation is that they eat and drink what will turn into fire; another is that doing so is a cause of entering the Fire. And Allah, Glorified and Exalted, knows best.

---

## Page 7231

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-‘Abbās Taqī al-Dīn Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave—said:

Section

Concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity but Him—as do the angels and those endowed with knowledge—while He upholds justice. There is no deity but Him, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

“Indeed, the religion with Allah is Islam.”

The Quranic commentators have explained the expression “bears witness” in various ways. One group, including Mujāhid, al-Farrā’ and Abū ‘Ubaydah, said: “It means He has judged and decreed.” Another group, including Tha‘lab and al-Zajjāj, said: “It means He has made clear.” Another group said: “It means He has made known.”

Likewise, some said that Allah’s testimony means His declaring and making known, whereas the testimony of the angels and the believers means their acknowledgment. It is reported from Ibn ‘Abbās that Allah Himself bore witness concerning Himself before He created creation, when He existed and there was neither heaven nor earth, neither land nor sea. He said:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity but Him.”

All these explanations, and others to the same effect, are correct. This is because testimony

---

## Page 7232

includes the witness’s speech, statements, and report of what he bears witness to. This may occur when the witness himself speaks of it, utters it, and mentions it, even without making it known to anyone else or informing another of it. This is the first level of testimony. He may then inform someone else of it and make it known to him, so that his testimony conveys knowledge and information to another. Whoever informs another of something has thereby testified to it, whether or not he uses the word “testimony,” as in the words of Allah Most High:

“And they have made the angels, who are servants of the Most Merciful, female. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned.”

And His words:

“We testify only to what we know”—to the end of the verse.

In both passages, they merely made a statement. He has also said:

“And avoid false speech,”

“Being upright in devotion to Allah, associating nothing with Him.”

In the two Sahīhs, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“False testimony has been equated with associating partners with Allah.” He said this twice or three times, then recited this verse: “And avoid false speech.”

This encompasses every false statement, whatever its wording and whatever form it takes. Thus, a servant must neither utter it, nor be present where it is spoken, nor listen to someone else saying it. “Falsehood” is that which is baseless and has deviated—that is, turned away—from truth and uprightness. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called it “false testimony.” Concerning those who declare their wives as forbidden to them as their mothers, Allah has said:

“They are certainly saying something reprehensible and false.”

---

## Page 7233

And in the two Sahīhs, Ibn ‘Abbās is reported to have said:

“Men whom I trusted—and the most trusted of them in my eyes was ‘Umar—testified before me that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited prayer after the dawn prayer until the sun rises, and after the afternoon prayer until the sun sets.”

These men told him that the Prophet had prohibited this; they did not say, “We testify before you.” The Companions did not invariably use this wording when relating hadith, although one of them might sometimes use it. Another example is their statement concerning Mā‘iz: “When he had testified against himself four times, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had him stoned.” What he said was a confession; he did not say, “I testify.” Another example is the saying of Allah Most High:

“Stand firmly for justice as witnesses for Allah, even against yourselves.”

A person’s testimony against himself is his admission, and the scholars agree that this does not require the word “testimony.” They differed only over whether testimony before a judge requires the words “I testify.” There are two views within Ahmad’s school. Ahmad’s statements indicate that this is not required, which is also Mālik’s position. The second view is that it is required, as is reported to be the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī.

Our concern here, however, is the verse. Testimony thus comprises two levels: the first is the witness’s speaking, stating, and mentioning what he has inwardly borne witness to; the second is his informing others and making known to them what he has borne witness to. Thus, whoever says:

---

## Page 7234

“He has ruled and decreed,” is referring to what testimony entails, for ruling and decreeing involve imposing an obligation and issuing a command.

There is no doubt that Allah has made monotheism obligatory upon His creation, commanded them to uphold it, and decreed and ruled accordingly. He says:

“And your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him.”

He also says:

“Warn that there is no deity except Me, so fear Me.”

And:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods’”—to the end of the verse.

Allah Most High also says:

“Allah has said, ‘Do not take two gods. He is only One God, so fear Me alone.’”

And:

“They were commanded only to worship one God. There is no deity except Him. Exalted is He above whatever they associate with Him.”

“They were commanded only to worship Allah with sincere devotion to Him, inclining to the truth.”

Such passages are numerous in the Quran: He obliges His servants to worship Him alone and forbids them to worship anything besides Him. He has thus ruled and decreed that there is no deity except Him. The question, however, concerns whether the word “testimony” conveys this meaning.

When He testifies that there is no deity except Him, He has declared, explained, and made known that nothing besides Him is a deity and therefore must not be worshipped, and that He alone is the deity who deserves worship. This entails commanding that He be worshipped and forbidding the worship of anything besides Him. For negation and affirmation in such a context entail a command and a prohibition. For example, if someone asks another person for a religious ruling, and someone says to him, “This man is not a mufti; that man is the mufti,” this entails forbidding him to seek a ruling from the former, and commanding and directing him to seek one from the latter.

---

## Page 7235

Similarly, if someone seeks judgment from a person who is not a judge, or requests something from someone who holds no authority, and is told, “This man is not a judge, nor is that man a ruler; this is the judge, and that is the ruler,” this negation and affirmation entail a command and a prohibition. A person seeking something turns only to someone who has what he wants and seeks. If he supposes that a particular person has it, and is told, “What you want is not with him; rather, it is with this person,” this amounts to directing him to seek what he wants from the latter rather than the former.

Those who worship intend only to worship a deity who deserves worship. Thus, when they are told, “Nothing besides Allah is a deity; Allah alone is the deity,” this forbids them to worship anything besides Him and commands them to worship Him.

Moreover, even if there were no one seeking to worship, the word “deity” itself entails being worthy of worship. Thus, when He declares that He alone deserves worship, to the exclusion of everything else, this constitutes a command to render Him the worship He deserves. What is meant here by “deity” is not something that someone worships without its deserving worship, for there are many such deities. Rather, calling them deities, describing them as such, and taking them as objects of worship are false. As Allah Most High says:

“They are nothing but names you and your forefathers have named, for which Allah has sent down no authority.”

And He says:

“That is because Allah is the Truth, and what they invoke besides Him is falsehood.”

---

## Page 7236

Thus, the deities that their worshippers have taken as gods to worship are numerous. Yet they do not deserve worship and are therefore not truly deities. This is like someone appointing another person as a witness, judge, mufti, or ruler when that person is competent in none of these roles. Every person must have a deity whom he adores and worships:

“Wretched is the slave of the dinar and the slave of the dirham!”

Some people have indeed made such wealth their deity through love, submission, and veneration, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Thus, when Allah testifies that there is no deity but Him, He has thereby ruled and decreed that none but Him should be worshipped.

Moreover, the terms “judgment” and “decree” are also used of declarative statements. A declarative statement is called a “proposition,” and one may say that it judges a particular meaning to be affirmed and another to be negated. Every witness and every person reporting something makes a judgment in this sense: he judges what he affirms to be true and what he denies to be false. This is a declarative judgment that may entail a prescriptive judgment.

Section

The Lord’s testimony, clarification, and communication are sometimes through His words and sometimes through His actions. His words are what He sent His messengers with, sent down in His Books, and revealed to His servants

---

## Page 7237

as He says:

“He sends down the angels with revelation by His command upon whom He wills of His servants: ‘Warn that there is no deity but Me, so fear Me.’”

There are other verses to this effect. It is established through mass transmission and known with necessary certainty that all the messengers reported from Allah that He has borne witness, and continues to bear witness, through His words and speech, that there is no deity but Him. This is known through everyone who conveyed His speech on His behalf. Hence, the Most High says:

“Or have they taken gods besides Him? Say, ‘Produce your proof. This is the reminder for those with me and the reminder for those before me.’”

As for His testimony through His actions, it consists of the evidence He has established indicating His oneness, whose significance can be understood through reason even without any communication from Allah. The terms “testimony,” “indication,” and “guidance” are used in this connection, for evidence clarifies and reveals what it indicates. It thus serves like someone who reports something and bears witness to it. As has been said: “Ask the earth who caused its rivers to flow, planted its trees, brought forth its fruits, gave life to its vegetation, darkened its night, and brightened its day. If it does not answer you in words, it answers you through reflection.”

He, glorified be He, has borne witness to that which He made these things indicate, for their indicative force comes only from His creating them. Thus, since created things indicate that there is no deity but Him, and He, glorified be He, is the One who made them indicate this—for their indicative force comes only from His creating them, and He has made this clear—He is the One who bears witness and makes clear through them that there is no deity but Him. A number of scholars have mentioned this testimony through action.

Ibn Kaysān said:

“Allah bears witness” through His wondrous governance and His works

---

## Page 7238

of perfect precision, before His creation, that there is no deity but Him.

Section

His words, “upholding justice,” are in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier. There are two interpretations. According to one, they qualify “bears witness,” meaning that He bears witness while upholding justice. According to the other, they qualify “Him,” meaning: “There is no deity but Him, upholding justice,” just as one says: “There is no deity but Him alone.” Both meanings are correct.

The phrase “upholding justice” may be governed by both grammatical elements, according to the Kufan position that a single dependent expression can have two governing elements. They offer the same analysis of His words:

“Take and read my record!”

“Bring me molten copper to pour over it.”

“On the right and on the left sits an attendant.”

And other similar passages. Sībawayh and his followers, however, assign a separate dependent expression to each governing element. They maintain that the dependent expression of one has been omitted because the other indicates it. The Kufan position is stronger, as I have explained at length elsewhere.

On either grammatical analysis, His words “with justice” can accordingly be understood as referring either to His bearing witness while upholding justice—for one who upholds justice acts justly—as in His words:

---

## Page 7239

“Stand firm for justice.”

Upholding justice applies both to speech—that is, speaking justly—and to action. Thus, when it is said that He bears witness “while upholding justice,” meaning that He speaks, declares, and commands what is just, this affirms that His testimony is one of justice and equity. It is more just than any other testimony, just as associating partners with Allah is more unjust than any other injustice. This testimony is the greatest of all testimonies.

Accounts given of the occasion of this verse’s revelation accord with this meaning. Ibn al-Sā’ib related:

“Two Jewish scholars from Syria came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When they saw Medina, one said to the other, ‘How closely this city resembles the description of the city of the Prophet who will appear at the end of time!’ When they entered the Prophet’s presence, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they recognized him from the description. They asked, ‘Are you Muhammad?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ They asked, ‘And Ahmad?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ They said, ‘We shall ask you about a testimony. If you tell us what it is, we shall believe in you.’ He said, ‘Ask me.’ They said, ‘Tell us of the greatest testimony in Allah’s Book.’ Then this verse was revealed.”

The expression “upholding justice” encompasses action just as it encompasses speech. Thus, the meaning is: He bears witness while speaking and acting with justice, not injustice. For this testimony encompasses both speech and action: it affirms that He alone deserves worship and is therefore to be worshipped; that none besides Him deserves worship; that those who worship Him alone are the successful and blessed; and that those who associate partners with Him will be in the Fire. Thus, when He bears witness while upholding justice, which entails rewarding His devoted servants with Paradise and requiting

---

## Page 7240

those who associate partners with Him with the Fire, this constitutes the full realization of what this testimony entails. His words,

“Upholding justice,”

thus draw attention to the recompense of His devoted servants and of those who associate partners with Him, as in His words:

“Is He who watches over every soul and what it earns…?”

A group of commentators, including al-Baghawī, said: “The verse is construed as: ‘Allah bears witness while upholding justice.’ The meaning of ‘upholding justice’ concerns His governance of creation. Thus, one says, ‘So-and-so attends to another’s affairs,’ meaning that he manages them and attends to what they require; and ‘He fulfils another’s due,’ meaning that he recompenses him. Allah Most High therefore governs, provides, and recompenses deeds.”

If upholding justice is understood in relation to His divinity, the meaning is:

“There is no god but Him, upholding justice.”

That is, He alone is God, upholding justice. He alone therefore deserves worship, while also upholding justice. This is like saying: “I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, one God, uniquely One, the Self-Sufficient.”

This interpretation is more compelling, for it entails that the angels and those endowed with knowledge bear witness both that there is no god but Him and that He upholds justice. The first interpretation does not indicate this. For His upholding justice carries greater force when it is part of what is attested than when it merely describes His state as the witness. His upholding justice entails that He speaks the truth and acts justly, as He says:

“The word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice.”

And Hūd said:

“Indeed, my Lord is on a straight path.”

He thus declared that Allah is on a straight path: justice in which there is no deviation.

---

## Page 7241

And He said:

“Is he equal to one who commands justice and is on a straight path?”

This is a parable Allah presents concerning Himself and the idols associated with Him, as also expressed in His words:

“Say, ‘Do any of those you associate with Allah guide to the truth?’ Say, ‘Allah guides to the truth’”—to the end of the verse.

And He said:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create?”

And the following verses, up to His words:

“And they do not perceive when they will be resurrected.”

He thus declares that He creates, bestows blessings, and knows, whereas those they invoke besides Him create nothing, bestow no blessings, and know nothing. He also declares that they are lifeless. Can the two be equal? How, then, can they worship them besides Allah despite this distinction, greater than which there is none? That is why this is the gravest injustice and falsehood. Another example of this is His saying, Most High:

“Say, ‘Praise be to Allah, and peace be upon His servants whom He has chosen. Is Allah better, or those they associate with Him?’”

Thus, the two passages in which He says:

“Allah presents a parable: a slave owned by another, powerless to do anything, and one to whom We have given good provision from Us, who spends from it secretly and openly. Are they equal? Praise be to Allah! But most of them do not know.”

“Allah also presents a parable of two men: one of them is mute, powerless to do anything, and a burden on his master. Wherever he directs him, he brings no good. Is he equal to one who commands justice and is on a straight path?”

are both parables in which Allah makes clear that He and those they associate with Him are not equal. Similar examples are mentioned elsewhere. This distinction is self-evident to everyone; yet the polytheists, despite acknowledging that

---

## Page 7242

their gods are created and owned by Him, equate Him with them in love, supplication, worship, and the like.

And “the point here” is that the Lord, glorified be He, is on a straight path. This is equivalent to His saying:

“Upholding justice.”

For uprightness and equity are inseparable: whoever is just in word and deed is upright, and whoever is upright in word and deed upholds justice. Hence Allah, glorified be He, has commanded us to ask Him to guide us to the straight path—the path of those He has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. Their path is that of justice and the balance, so that people may uphold justice. The straight path consists in obeying Him and refraining from disobeying Him. All sins are wrongdoing, contrary to justice and incompatible with upholding fairness and equity. Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

Section

Then He, Most High, said:

“There is no deity except Him, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

It is reported that Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad said: “The first describes Him and affirms His oneness; the second gives direction and instruction”—meaning His words:

“There is no deity except Him, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

This means that, in the first occurrence, He

---

## Page 7243

stated that Allah bore witness to it, saying:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity except Him.”

The person reciting the Quran merely states that Allah, the angels, and those endowed with knowledge have borne witness to it; this does not constitute testimony to it by the reciter himself. Allah therefore stated it separately so that the reciter would pronounce it and thus himself bear witness that there is no deity except Him. The first occurrence reports Allah’s affirmation of His own oneness through His testimony about Himself, whereas the latter declares Allah’s oneness.

He concluded it with His words:

“The Almighty, the All-Wise.”

Might encompasses power, strength, invulnerability, and supremacy. The Arabs say: ʿazza–yaʿazzu, with an a vowel on the ʿayn, meaning to become hard; ʿazza–yaʿizzu, with an i vowel, meaning to be invulnerable; and ʿazza–yaʿuzzu, with a u vowel, meaning to prevail. Thus He, glorified be He, is strong and firm in Himself; He is invulnerable and cannot be assailed; and He prevails and cannot be overcome. “The All-Wise” encompasses His judgment, knowledge, and wisdom in what He says and does. Whenever He commands something, it is good; whenever He states something, it is true; and whenever He wills to create something, it is right. He is therefore wise in His intentions, actions, and words.

Section

This verse encompasses three fundamental principles: the testimony that there is no deity except Allah, that He upholds justice, and that He is the Almighty, the All-Wise. It thus affirms His oneness, which excludes

---

## Page 7244

any association of partners with Him. It also affirms His justice, which excludes injustice, and His might and wisdom, which exclude abasement and folly. It declares Him free of partners, injustice, and folly. Thus it affirms His oneness, justice, wisdom, and power.

The Muʿtazilah may invoke this verse to support what they call oneness, justice, and wisdom, but it provides no proof for them. Rather, it is proof against both them and their opponents, the Jabriyyah, the followers of al-Jahm ibn Safwān, who say, “Whatever He can do is just,” and deny wisdom, saying, “He acts without any wise purpose.”

The verse provides them with no proof, for He has declared that there is no deity except Him, and this does not entail denying His attributes, although they call the denial of His attributes “oneness.” Rather, the deity is the One who deserves worship, and worship necessarily involves love for the one worshipped. The polytheists set up rivals to Allah whom they love as they love Allah, whereas those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah. This indicates that the believers love Allah more than the polytheists love their rivals to Him. It is therefore clear that Allah is loved for His own sake. Whoever does not affirm this has not truly borne witness that there is no deity except Him.

The Jahmiyyah and the Muʿtazilah say that His very Self is not loved. Thus, in reality, they deny His divinity. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 7245

His upholding of justice is coupled with the affirmation that there is no deity except Him. He mentions this while affirming that no one resembles Him in any aspect of His affairs. The Muʿtazilah, however, regard His justice as being like the justice of created beings: whatever is just on the part of created beings is just on the part of the Creator. They thereby equate the Creator with His creation, undermining the affirmation that there is no deity except Him.

According to the Jahmiyyah, anything that can possibly occur is just. His statement,

“Upholding justice,”

would therefore convey neither any meaningful point nor any praise. For if everything He has the power to do is just, the meaning would simply be that He carries out what He does—in other words, that He does what He does. This contains no praise, nor is it what is understood by His upholding justice. Rather, it means that He upholds justice, not injustice, despite His power to commit injustice. Yet He—glorified be He—is holy and exalted above wronging anyone, as He says:

“And your Lord wrongs no one.”

He has commanded His servants to be steadfast in upholding justice, and He says:

“Is He, then, who stands over every soul for what it has earned…?”

Thus He stands over each soul according to its own deeds, not the deeds of another. This is part of His upholding justice.

He also says:

“And We shall set up the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection, so that no soul will be wronged in the least,”

and the rest of the verse.

Another aspect of His upholding justice and standing over every soul according to what it has earned is that He does not wrong anyone by even an atom’s weight, as He says:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,”

and so on to the end.

---

## Page 7246

The Mu'tazilah hold that numerous great good deeds are nullified by a single major sin, and that a person’s faith and affirmation of Allah’s oneness are nullified by sins less than that. This is among the injustices peculiar to their doctrine from which Allah has declared Himself free. Thus, they attribute injustice to Allah, not justice. And Allah knows best.

Section

His statement:

“He is the Almighty, the All-Wise,”

affirms His might and wisdom, and refutes both groups: the Jabriyyah and the Qadariyyah. For according to the Jabriyyah—the followers of Jahm—He does not truly possess wisdom. Hence, when the Ash'arīs sought to explain His wisdom, they interpreted it as either power, knowledge, or will.

It is well known that none of these interpretations affirms His wisdom. Someone who possesses power, knowledge, and will may or may not be wise; wisdom is something additional to these. They say that Allah does not act for a wise purpose. They also say: “Acting to achieve a purpose is possible only for one who can benefit, suffer harm, experience pain, and feel pleasure; all of this is ruled out with regard to Allah.”

The Mu'tazilah, however, affirmed that He acts for a wise purpose. They called this a “purpose”—both they and a group

---

## Page 7247

among the affirmationists. They maintained, however: “Wisdom is something separate from Him and does not subsist in Him,” just as they said of His speech and will. The Jabriyyah seized upon this to argue against them, saying: “A wise person acts for a wise purpose that pertains to himself. If it does not pertain to himself, he is not wise but foolish.”

The Jabriyyah may be answered as follows: The very argument by which you deny wisdom is the argument used by those philosophers and others who deny will. They say: “Will can belong only to one who can benefit, suffer harm, experience pain, and feel pleasure. Affirming will without these things is inconceivable.” Yet you say: “We agree with the early generations and all the other adherents of the Sunnah in affirming will.” Whatever answer you give to this objection is also the answer that the other adherents of the Sunnah can give you when you affirm a will without a wise purpose for which the act is willed. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where the ambiguous terms in the wording of this argument have also been explained. And Allah knows best.

Section

Affirming the testimony of those endowed with knowledge entails that others besides Him—created beings, both angels and humans—bear witness to His oneness. This is agreed upon: they bear witness that there is no god but Allah, and they testify to what He has testified concerning Himself.

---

## Page 7248

A group of the unionists claimed that no one affirms Allah’s oneness, and recited:

“No one has affirmed the oneness of the One,
For whoever affirms His oneness denies Him.”

The substance of their doctrine resembles what the Christians say about the Messiah. They claim that truly affirming Allah’s oneness means that the one affirming it is himself the One whose oneness is affirmed. Thus, it is the Real who speaks through the servant’s tongue, and Allah—not the servant—who affirms His own oneness. This, they allege, is the secret belief held by al-Hallāj and the doctrine of the elite among those who know Allah, though they do not state it openly.

In reality, they believe concerning the righteous in general what the Christians believe concerning the Messiah. Yet they could not proclaim this openly, since the religion of Islam plainly contradicts it. They therefore resorted to alluding to it, saying: “It belongs to the concealed secret and the knowledge of unseen mysteries, and so it cannot be divulged.”

Yet this is nothing but a heretical doctrine, worse than that of the Christians, for the Christians said this only of the Messiah, not of all the righteous. This has been discussed at length in several other places. The purpose here is to draw attention to the foundations of faith and the affirmation of Allah’s oneness contained in this verse, and to refute the doctrine of the religious innovators.

---

## Page 7249

Section

Since Allah’s testimony entails His explaining to His servants, guiding them, and acquainting them with what He has testified concerning Himself, He must make known to them that He has borne witness, for this testimony is the greatest of all testimonies. Otherwise, if He bore witness in a way they had no means of knowing, they would derive no benefit from it, nor would that testimony establish any proof against them. Likewise, if a created being possesses testimony but does not disclose it, concealing it instead, no one benefits from it, nor does it establish any proof.

For this reason, He, glorified be He, condemns those who conceal the knowledge He has revealed and the testimony it contains. As He, Most High, says:

“And who is more unjust than one who conceals a testimony he has from Allah?”

That is, he possesses testimony from Allah but conceals it. This is the knowledge Allah has made clear, for it is a report from Allah and His testimony to what it contains.

He has condemned those who conceal it, just as some of the People of the Book concealed the reports and testimony they possessed concerning Abraham and his household. They concealed their submission to Allah, along with the reports in their possession corresponding to what Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conveyed, descriptions of him, and other matters. He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, those who conceal what We sent down of clear proofs and guidance after We made it clear for the people in the Scripture—those are cursed by Allah and cursed by those who curse.”

And He says:

---

## Page 7250

Most High:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture recognize him as they recognize their own sons. Yet a group of them knowingly conceal the truth.”

Testimony requires the witness to have knowledge, speak truthfully, and make his testimony clear. Its purpose cannot be fulfilled without these things. For this reason, He condemns those who conceal and distort the truth, saying, Most High:

“O you who believe, stand firmly for justice as witnesses for Allah, even against yourselves, your parents, or your close relatives. Whether someone is rich or poor, Allah has a greater claim over both. So do not follow your desires and depart from justice. If you distort your testimony or refuse to give it, Allah is indeed fully aware of what you do.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Hakīm ibn Hizām reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The buyer and seller each have the option to cancel the transaction until they part. If they are truthful and make things clear, their transaction will be blessed. But if they lie and conceal things, the blessing of their transaction will be erased.”

Section

Since His testimony must be made clear to His servants so that they know He has borne witness, He has made it clear through both hearing and sight. Those who can hear listen to Allah’s revealed signs as they are recited, and those who can see behold His signs in what He has created and done. For His testimony entails

---

## Page 7251

His making it clear to His servants, guiding them to it, and enabling them to know it. This is accomplished through His signs, for His signs are His indications and proofs through which His servants come to know what He declares and bears witness to, just as He has made His commands and prohibitions known to them through these signs. He is All-Knowing, All-Wise: what He communicates includes His commands and prohibitions, while His actions reveal His wisdom. Thus, when the prophets convey His words, His testimony and His spoken signs become known through them. He must also make known that the prophets are truthful in what they report from Him.

He has made this known through the signs with which He supported the prophets and demonstrated their truthfulness. He never sent a prophet without a sign establishing his truthfulness, for it is not valid to affirm his truthfulness on the basis of something that does not demonstrate it. As He said:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs.”

That is, with clear signs.

He also said:

“We sent before you only men to whom We revealed—so ask the people of knowledge if you do not know—with clear proofs and scriptures. And We have sent down to you the Reminder so that you may explain to people what has been revealed to them, and so that they may reflect.”

He also said:

“Say: Messengers came to you before me with clear proofs and with what you have demanded.”

And He said:

“Messengers before you were also denied, though they came with clear proofs, scriptures, and the enlightening Book.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There was no prophet who was not given signs of a kind that led people to believe. What I have been given, however, is revelation that Allah has revealed to me

---

## Page 7252

so I hope to have more followers than any of them on the Day of Resurrection.”

The signs and proofs with which the messengers were sent are Allah’s indications of their truthfulness, through which He guided His servants. They constitute Allah’s testimony that the messengers were truthful in what they conveyed from Him, and what they conveyed includes His testimony concerning Himself in what He has declared.

For this reason, some thinkers have said that a miracle confirms the messenger’s truthfulness and serves as confirmation from the one who sent him. It is thus confirmation through action, equivalent to verbal confirmation, since people do not hear the speech of Allah, who sent him. His confirmation declares the messenger truthful, testifies to his truthfulness, testifies that He sent him, and testifies that everything the messenger conveys from Him is His speech.

One of His names, glorified is He, is al-Mu’min, which, according to one of its two interpretations, means the Confirmer: the One who confirms His prophets’ truthfulness in what they report from Him, through the proofs He provides to establish their truthfulness.

As for the way of direct observation, it consists in His servants seeing signs in the world around them and within themselves that make clear to them that the revelation the messengers conveyed from Allah is true. As the Most High says:

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. Is it not sufficient that your Lord is a Witness over all things?”

That is: is His testimony, which communicates what He knows, not sufficient? This is the revelation He communicated to the Messenger. For Allah is a Witness over all things and knows them. When He reports something and bears witness to it, that is sufficient, even if one has not seen

---

## Page 7253

what He has testified to. His testimony is known through the signs by which He demonstrated the Messenger’s truthfulness. Whoever gains knowledge through this approach does not need to examine the observable signs that indicate that the Quran is true. Rather, he may know this through the evidence by which he came to know that the Messenger is truthful in what he reports concerning Allah’s testimony and speech.

Likewise, He mentioned the revealed Book, saying:

“And do not argue with the People of the Book except in the best manner, except with those among them who act unjustly,”

and the subsequent verses, through His words:

“except the wrongdoers.”

He thus made clear that the Quran consists of clear signs within the hearts of those who have been given knowledge. It is among the greatest clear signs demonstrating the truthfulness of the one who brought it. It brings together signs that are not found together in anything else: it is the call and its proof, the evidence and what that evidence establishes, and the judgment. It is the claim and the evidence for that claim, the witness and what is testified to.

As for His words:

“within the hearts of those who have been given knowledge,”

whether this means that it is clear within their hearts, that it is preserved within their hearts, or both—which is the correct understanding—it is indeed preserved within the hearts of the learned and clear within their hearts: they know that it is true. As He says:

“Those who have been given knowledge see that what has been sent down to you from your Lord is the truth.”

And He says:

“Is one who knows that what has been sent down to you from your Lord is the truth like one who is blind?”

“And so that those who have been given knowledge may know that it is the truth from your Lord

---

## Page 7254

and thus believe in it, and their hearts humbly submit to it. Indeed, Allah guides those who believe to a straight path.”

And He, Most High, says:

“They say, ‘Why have signs not been sent down to him from his Lord?’ Say, ‘The signs are only with Allah, and I am only a clear warner.’

“Is it not enough for them that We have sent down to you the Book, which is recited to them? Surely in this is mercy and a reminder for people who believe.

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you. He knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth. Those who believe in falsehood and disbelieve in Allah—it is they who are the losers.’”

These verses explain what brings happiness to the believers and saves them from punishment.

Then He says:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you. He knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth.’”

Since He knows all things, His testimony is based on knowledge. He has made His testimony clear through the signs demonstrating the Messenger’s truthfulness, among them the Quran. And Allah knows best.

Section

As for His being truthful—glorified be He—this is self-evident to everyone through their innate nature. Lying is among the qualities most detested by humankind, and He—glorified be He—is free of

---

## Page 7255

that, and every praiseworthy person keeps himself above it. Everyone condemns lying, so it is an unconditionally blameworthy attribute. A person’s lack of knowledge of certain things, however, is inherent in being a created being. None but Allah encompasses all things in knowledge. Thus, people do not regard a lack of knowledge as a deficiency comparable to lying. For this reason, the Lord makes clear His knowledge of what He testifies to, and that He is more truthful in speech than anyone, better in judgment, and more truthful in His words. For He—glorified be He—is more entitled than anyone to the attributes of perfection:

“To Him belongs the highest description in the heavens and the earth.”

He speaks the truth and guides to the right way. He—glorified be He—speaks by His will and power.

As for “and those who possess knowledge of the Scripture,” they are the People of the Book. They testify to what the prophets before Muhammad brought. They testify that those prophets brought teachings like those he brought, such as the command to worship Allah alone, the prohibition of associating partners with Him, accounts of the Day of Resurrection, and overarching religious laws. They also testify to what their scriptures contain concerning his attributes, his message, and his Book.

These are the two ways by which the prophethood of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is established: signs and proofs demonstrating his truthfulness, or testimony to his prophethood from another prophet whose truthfulness is already known.

He mentioned these two kinds of evidence in His words:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you

---

## Page 7256

and those who possess knowledge of the Scripture.’” Through the former, his truthfulness is known by rational reflection on his signs and proofs; through the latter, it is known through transmitted reports from the prophets before him.

Likewise, He says:

“Say, ‘Whose testimony is greatest?’ Say, ‘Allah is a witness between me and you.’”

The words “Say, ‘Allah’” admit two grammatical analyses. According to one, “Allah” answers the questioner, while “a witness” is the predicate of an implied subject—that is, “He is a witness.” According to the other, “Allah” is the subject and “a witness” its predicate, making a separate answer to the question unnecessary. The first analysis corresponds to the recitation of those who pause after “Say, ‘Allah’”; the second corresponds to the recitation of those who do not pause there. Both are correct, but the second is better and more complete.

Everyone knows that Allah’s testimony is greatest. Thus, when He says:

“Say, ‘Whose testimony is greatest?’”

it is understood that Allah’s testimony is greater than that of anything else. He therefore instructs him:

“Say, ‘Allah is a witness between me and you.’”

Saying “Allah is a witness between me and you” makes it unnecessary to state that Allah’s testimony is greatest. For the fact that Allah’s testimony is greatest is already known; it is not established merely by his saying “greatest in testimony”

---

## Page 7257

—unlike His being a witness between him and them, which is known through scriptural text and reasoned examination. One therefore considers: Has Allah testified to his truthfulness and to their falsehood in rejecting him, or has He testified to his falsehood and to their truthfulness in rejecting him?

Upon examining this, one realizes that Allah has testified to his truthfulness and their falsehood through both kinds of signs: through His words that He sent down, and through the evidence by which He made clear that he is a truthful messenger. Hence He followed this with His words:

“And this Quran has been revealed to me so that I may warn you with it and whoever it reaches.”

For this Quran contains a warning and is itself a sign through which He has testified that he is truthful. He has also testified through the signs He manifests on the horizons and within themselves, until it becomes clear to them that the Quran is the truth.

His words in this verse are:

“Say, ‘Allah is a witness between me and you.’”

Likewise, He says:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you.’”

And likewise:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient between me and you as a witness.’”

And likewise:

“He knows best what you say concerning it. He is sufficient as a witness between me and you.”

Thus He, glorified be He, stated that He is a witness between him and them. He did not say, “a witness against us” or “a witness for me,” because He included judgment in the meaning of testimony. He is therefore a witness who judges by His testimony between me and you. Judgment entails more than mere testimony: a witness may simply give testimony, whereas a judge rules justly in favor of the party in the right against the party in the wrong, secures the former’s rights from the latter, and treats both the party in the right and the party in the wrong as each deserves.

---

## Page 7258

And such is Allah’s testimony between the Messenger and his followers, on the one hand, and those who reject him, on the other. It includes Allah’s judgment in favor of the Messenger and his followers. Through the signs He manifests attesting to the Messenger’s truthfulness, He judges that this is the truth. These signs are of many kinds. He also decrees for him deliverance, victory, support, and happiness in this world and the Hereafter, while decreeing for those who reject him destruction, punishment, and misery in this world and the Hereafter. As the Most High says:

“It is He who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion.”

He makes it prevail through proofs and signs that establish knowledge and make clear that it is true. He also makes it prevail by granting it victory and support against its opponents, so that it is triumphant. As the Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance, so that people may uphold justice. And We sent down iron, in which there is great might.”

This, then, is testimony that entails judgment, as we explained earlier concerning His words:

“Allah bears witness.”

Mujāhid, al-Farrāʾ, and Abū ʿUbaydah said that “Allah bears witness” means that He judges and decrees. Yet the sense of judgment is more apparent in His words, “between me and you.”

A person may say to another, “So-and-so is a witness between me and you,” meaning that he witnesses what passes between us. Allah thus bears witness through what He has revealed and what He says. This is like witnessing the deeds of His servants. However, those who rejected the Messenger did not deny their rejection, nor did they suspect the Messenger of denying his claim to messengership. Thus, understanding “witness” as including judgment is more firmly established and more consonant with the Quran. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7259

Section

The same applies to His words:

“But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you; He has sent it down with His knowledge. The angels too bear witness, and Allah suffices as a witness.”

His testimony concerning what He sent down to the Messenger is His testimony that Allah sent it down from Himself and that He sent it down with His knowledge. Whatever information it contains comes from Allah’s knowledge, not from anyone beneath Him. This is like His words:

“If they do not respond to you, then know that it has been sent down only with Allah’s knowledge.”

The meaning is not merely that what He sent down is known to Him, for all things are known to Him, and that alone does not establish that they are true. Rather, the meaning is that He sent it down containing His knowledge, just as one says that someone speaks with knowledge or makes statements with knowledge. Thus He, glorified be He, sent it down with His knowledge, as He says:

“Say: It was sent down by Him who knows the secrets of the heavens and the earth.”

He did not say, “He spoke it with His knowledge,” for that would not convey that it had been sent down to earth. Thus, when He says:

“He has sent it down with His knowledge,”

this entails that the Quran sent down to earth contains Allah’s knowledge, as He says:

“Whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you…”

This entails that it is the speech of Allah Himself: it came down from Him, not from anyone else. For none other than Allah knows the knowledge within Allah Himself—and His Self is His essence

---

## Page 7260

, which is holy—unless Allah teaches him this, as the Messiah, peace be upon him, said:

“You know what is within myself, and I do not know what is within Yourself. Indeed, You alone are the Knower of all unseen things.”

The angels said:

“We have no knowledge except what You have taught us.”

And He said:

“They encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills.”

And He said:

“He does not disclose His unseen to anyone except a messenger whom He approves.”

Thus, He does not disclose the unseen that He has reserved for Himself to anyone except a messenger whom He approves. The angels do not know the unseen that the Lord has reserved for Himself. As for what He has disclosed to His servants, He teaches it to whomever He wills. The devils may overhear some of what the angels discuss. But this is not part of His unseen or the knowledge within Himself that He reserves exclusively for Himself. Rather, He has disclosed it to whomever He wills among His creatures. He, glorified be He, said:

“But Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you; He has sent it down with His knowledge.”

He thus bore witness that He sent it down with His knowledge through the signs and proofs demonstrating that it is His speech and that the Messenger is truthful.

Likewise, He said in Surah Hūd:

“Then produce ten fabricated surahs like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.”

Having challenged them to produce its like in His words:

“Then let them produce a discourse like it,”

He then challenged them to produce ten surahs like it. They were unable to meet either challenge. Then He challenged them to produce a single surah like it, and they were unable to do so. Created beings cannot produce its like, nor even a single surah like it; and if

---

## Page 7261

all created beings are incapable of producing a single surah like it, and Muhammad is one of them, it is known that it was sent down from Allah. He sent it down with His knowledge, not with the knowledge of any created being. Thus, whatever information it contains comes from Allah’s knowledge.

And His words:

“Say: It was sent down by the One who knows every secret in the heavens and the earth.”

This is because it contains secrets known only to Allah, demonstrating that Allah sent it down. His mention of this is sometimes used as evidence that it is true and sent down from Allah. Yet it contains accounts of the secrets of the heavens and the earth, of this world, of earlier and later generations, and of the hidden unseen, which none but Allah knows. Thus, from our knowledge that its accounts are true, we infer that it is from Allah.

Once it is established that He, exalted be He, sent it down with His knowledge, we infer from this that its accounts are true. Since these accounts convey Allah’s knowledge, the information it contains serves as evidence—whether concerning the prophets and their peoples or the Day of Resurrection and what it holds. For an account to serve as evidence, we must know its truth independently. Examples include its foretelling future events that then occurred just as it foretold; its accounts of past nations that agree with what the People of the Book possess, without any learning from them; and its disclosure of matters kept secret by those concerned, as He said:

“And when the Prophet confided something to one of his wives,”

up to His words:

“The All-Knowing, the All-Aware informed me.”

Thus, His words:

“Say: It was sent down by the One who knows every secret in the heavens and the earth,”

present its accounts as evidence. This is why He mentioned this to refute those who said that it was “a fabrication he has invented, and he was helped

---

## Page 7262

by other people.” And His words, “He sent it down,” serve as evidence that it is true and that the reports it conveys from Allah are true. This is why He mentioned this after the challenge had been established and the inability of created beings to produce anything like it had become evident.

Section

His testimony also includes the knowledge He places in people’s hearts and what their tongues express of that knowledge, as related in the Sahīh:

“A funeral procession passed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the people spoke well of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured! It is assured!’ Another funeral procession passed by, and they spoke ill of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured! It is assured!’ They asked, ‘Messenger of Allah, what do you mean by saying, “It is assured! It is assured!”?’ He replied, ‘You spoke well of the first deceased person, so I said that Paradise was assured for them. You spoke ill of the second deceased person, so I said that the Fire was assured for them. You are Allah’s witnesses on earth.’”

By saying “Allah’s witnesses,” he ascribed them to Allah Most High. Testimony is sometimes ascribed to the one on whose behalf it is given, and sometimes to the one before whom it is given and who accepts it. Thus people speak of “the judge’s witnesses,” “the ruler’s witnesses,” and similar expressions referring to those whose testimony is accepted. This may also include the person concerning whom a witness testifies, on the basis of what the witness has undertaken to bear

---

## Page 7263

as testimony, so that he may give it before others, as in the case of people whose contracts or acknowledgments are witnessed by others. Allah’s witnesses are those who testify for Him to what He has brought about and done, and convey testimony from Him. When they see someone whom Allah has made righteous and pious, they testify that Allah has made him so, conveying testimony from Him. Thus they are Allah’s witnesses on earth. It is He, glorified be He, who has made them witnesses by enabling them to know and express what they testify to. His making this known to them is itself testimony from Him to that effect. This, too, is therefore part of His testimony.

Allah Most High says:

“For them are glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained these glad tidings as good dreams, and also as people’s commendation and praise. Glad tidings are news of something pleasing, and a report is testimony to those glad tidings, forming part of Allah Most High’s testimony. And Allah, glorified be He, knows best.

---

## Page 7264

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Concerning the statement of Allah Most High,

“Whoever enters it shall be safe,”

is this referring to safety from disbelief at the time of death, when the religions are presented to a person? Or does it mean that if someone commits an offence, no retribution is exacted from him as long as he remains within the Sanctuary?

He replied:

The well-known interpretation is that Allah made the Sanctuary a place of safety, both by His decree and by His law. During the pre-Islamic era, people would shed one another’s blood outside the Sanctuary. Yet when they entered the Sanctuary, or a man encountered his father’s killer there, they would not violate its sanctity. In Islam, this sanctity likewise applies, and is even greater.

However, if a man commits an offence outside the Sanctuary that incurs a prescribed punishment, then seeks refuge within it, is he safe there, so that the punishment is not carried out upon him within its bounds, or not? There is disagreement concerning this. Most of the early generations held that he is safe, as reported from Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, and others. This is also the position of Abū Hanīfah, Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others. They cited this verse as evidence, along with the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “Indeed, Allah

---

## Page 7265

made Mecca sacred on the day He created the heavens and the earth. Fighting there was not lawful for anyone before me, nor will it be lawful for anyone after me. It was permitted to me only for a brief period during one day, and its sanctity has now been restored.”

If anyone claims a concession on the grounds that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fought there, say: “Allah permitted it to His Messenger, but He has not permitted it to you.” It is well known that the Messenger was permitted to shed blood there only when doing so would have been lawful outside the Sanctuary. He clearly stated that this permission was granted to him alone, to the exclusion of everyone else. The words “Whoever enters it” refer to the entire Sanctuary.

As for religions being presented to a person at the time of death, some people undergo this trial while others do not. It is feared that someone who has not performed Hajj may die outside Islam, as stated in the hadith: “Whoever possesses provisions and a mount that can take him to the House of Allah, yet does not perform Hajj, let him die, if he wishes, as a Jew or a Christian.” And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7266

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—also wrote concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“It is only Satan who makes you fear his allies. So do not fear them, but fear Me, if you are believers.”

This is the correct interpretation, held by the majority of Quran commentators, including Ibn 'Abbās, Sa'īd ibn Jubayr, 'Ikrimah and al-Nakha'ī, and by linguists such as al-Farrā, Ibn Qutaybah, al-Zajjāj and Ibn al-Anbārī. Al-Farrā phrases it as “He frightens you by means of his allies,” just as Allah says, “to warn of a severe punishment from Him,” meaning “of a severe punishment”; and His words, “to warn of the Day of Meeting.” Al-Zajjāj phrases it as “He makes you afraid of his allies.”

Ibn al-Anbārī said: “Our preferred interpretation of the verse is: ‘He makes you fear his allies.’ The Arabs say, ‘I gave the wealth,’ meaning, ‘I gave the people the wealth.’ They omit the first object and mention only the second.”

This is because Satan makes people fear his allies in a general sense; he has no particular need to frighten certain people by means of others. Thus, omitting the first object is not an end in itself. This is called “omission for brevity,” as when one says, “So-and-so gives wealth and dirhams.”

Some commentators have said that it means: “He frightens his allies, the hypocrites.” This interpretation has been transmitted

---

## Page 7267

from al-Hasan and al-Suddī. This interpretation has a valid basis, which we shall explain. The first, however, is more evident, because the verse was revealed in connection with attempts to make the believers fear the disbelievers, as Allah says immediately before it:

“Those to whom people said, ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them,’ but this only increased them in faith,”

and in the subsequent verses. He then says:

“So do not fear them, but fear Me, if you are believers.”

Thus, it was revealed concerning those who made the believers afraid of other people. He says, “He makes you fear his allies,” followed by, “So do not fear them.” The pronoun refers to Satan’s allies, concerning whom He had previously said, “So fear them.”

As for the other interpretation, its proponents explained the verse in terms of its meaning: Satan makes his allies afraid of the believers, since his power over his allies operates through fear. He continually instils fears in them, so that fears descend upon them and pervade their speech, even if they possess standing, numbers and equipment. So do not fear them. The believers, however, put their trust in Allah, and Satan does not make them fear the disbelievers.

Or perhaps they had the first object in mind, meaning: “He frightens the hypocrites, his allies.” Otherwise, he frightens the disbelievers just as he frightens the hypocrites. If “He frightens his allies” were intended to mean that he makes them afraid, the pronoun in His words, “So do not fear them,” would have no antecedent.

Furthermore, this interpretation is questionable, for Satan makes promises to his allies and fills them with false hopes, as He says:

---

## Page 7268

exalted is He:

“And when Satan made their deeds seem fair to them and said, ‘No one among mankind can overcome you today, and I am your protector.’”

And He, the Most High, says:

“He makes them promises and fills them with false hopes, but Satan promises them nothing but delusion.”

However, it is Allah who casts fear of the believers into the hearts of the disbelievers; Satan does not choose this. The Most High says:

“You indeed inspire greater fear in their hearts than Allah.”

And He says:

“When your Lord revealed to the angels, ‘I am with you, so strengthen those who believe. I will cast terror into the hearts of those who disbelieve.’”

And He says:

“We will cast terror into the hearts of those who disbelieve because they have associated partners with Allah.”

In the report of Qurtubah, Gabriel said:

“I am going to them and will make their fortress quake around them.”

Thus, it is Allah who instils fear and terror in the disbelievers and hypocrites, in support of the believers.

However, those among the early generations who expressed that interpretation meant that Satan frightens those who outwardly profess Islam, so they ally themselves with the enemy and thereby become hypocrites. It is only the hypocrites who fear the disbelievers because Satan frightens them, as the Most High says:

“They swear by Allah that they are indeed of you, but they are not of you; rather, they are a people who are afraid.”

And the Most High says:

“But when fear comes, you see them looking at you, their eyes rolling like someone overcome by death,”

and the subsequent verses, up to His words:

“They wish they were out in the desert among the Bedouins, asking about your news.”

Both interpretations are therefore correct in meaning. However, the expression “his allies” refers to those whom Satan makes objects of fear, not those whom he makes afraid, as indicated by the context of

---

## Page 7269

the verse and its wording. And Allah knows best.

When Satan makes them objects of fear, only those whom he frightens with them become afraid of them. Thus, the verse indicates that Satan makes his allies objects of fear and makes some people afraid of them. It also indicates that the believer must not fear Satan’s allies or fear people.

As the Most High says:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me.”

Rather, the believer must fear Allah. He has commanded fear of Allah and forbidden fear of Satan and his allies.

The Most High also says:

“So that people may have no argument against you, except those among them who do wrong. So do not fear them, but fear Me.”

He thus forbade fearing the wrongdoer and commanded fear of Himself. And those who convey Allah’s messages fear Him and fear no one but Allah.

He also says:

“And fear Me alone.”

Some people say, “My Lord, I fear You, and I fear those who do not fear You.” This is a baseless and impermissible statement. Rather, a servant must fear Allah alone and fear no one else—neither those who fear Allah nor those who do not. For anyone who does not fear Allah is too base and abject to be feared: he is a wrongdoer and one of Satan’s allies, and Allah has forbidden fearing him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7270

Commenting on the words of the Most High,

“While those who follow their desires wish you to go far astray,”

Shaykh al-Islām discussed human desires and the various things people crave, including women and beardless youths. He said:

A servant who falls into any of these things must struggle against his own self and desires, and his struggle must be for Allah Most High alone.

He then said:

Attraction to women is part of the natural disposition common to all human beings. Many may also be tried with attraction to males, such as beardless youths. If a person does not commit the gravest sexual indecency, this may take the form of lesser acts of physical intimacy; if not that, then looking. The effects this has upon the soul are well known. People have related accounts of passionate lovers that would take a long time to recount.

When a Muslim is tried with any of this, he must struggle against his own self in obedience to Allah Most High. He is commanded to undertake this struggle. What he must refrain from is not something he has forbidden himself, such that he would be obeying his own self and desires.
