Rather, it is something Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, and there is no evading that. Thus, the struggle against his own self is undertaken in obedience to Allah and His Messenger.

---

## Page 7271

In a report transmitted by Abū Yahyā al-Qattāt from Mujāhid, from Ibn ʿAbbās, and attributed to the Prophet:

“Whoever falls passionately in love, remains chaste, conceals it, and patiently endures, then dies, is a martyr.”

Abū Yahyā’s narrations are open to question. However, the meaning expressed in this report is supported by the Quran and Sunnah, for Allah has commanded a person to fear Him and be patient. Fearing Allah includes refraining from everything He has forbidden, whether a glance with the eyes, words spoken with the tongue, or movements of the hands and feet. Patience means refraining from complaining of one’s condition to anyone other than Allah, for this is gracious patience.

As for concealment, two things are meant by it:

The first is to conceal one’s distress and pain, and not complain to anyone other than Allah. Whenever a person complains to someone other than Allah, his patience is diminished. This is the higher of the two forms of concealment. Not everyone, however, can patiently sustain it; indeed, many people complain of what they are suffering. This takes two forms. If he complains to a physician versed in treating souls, seeking to treat his soul with the remedy of faith, he is like someone seeking a religious ruling, and this is good. If he complains to someone who will help him do what is forbidden, this is unlawful. If he complains to someone else for the relief that complaining brings—just as an afflicted person complains to others of his misfortune—without intending either to learn what will benefit him or to seek help in committing a sin, this diminishes his patience. It does not, however, necessarily make him sinful, unless accompanied by something forbidden, as when an afflicted person expresses resentful discontent.

The second is to conceal it by not speaking about it with people, because doing so involves

---

## Page 7272

publicizing evil and indecency. When souls hear such things, they are stirred to desire, yearning, and infatuation. Whenever a person sees, hears of, or imagines someone doing what he himself desires, this prompts him to do it. When women see male animals mounting females, they feel drawn toward sexual intimacy and intercourse. Likewise, when a man hears of someone engaging in sexual acts with beardless youths or women, sees this, or imagines it, this urges him to do the same.

When a person thinks of food, he craves it and feels drawn to it. If something he desires—clothing, a woman, a dwelling, or anything else—is described to him, his soul inclines toward it. A person far from his homeland longs for it whenever he is reminded of it. Thus, whenever a person pictures something he loves, his love and desire for that beloved, sought-after object are stirred, whether through its description or through seeing it. Both produce a mental image. Such an image may arise through hearing, seeing, or thinking about things connected with it. When the soul pictures those associated things, it passes to another image, stirring the impulse of love, whether that love is praiseworthy or blameworthy.

This is why mentioning the Hijāz stirs in souls the desire to perform Hajj. They are likewise stirred by mention of al-Abraq, al-Ajra‘, al-‘Aliyy, and similar places: a person saw those stopping places while journeying to the beloved, so mentioning them brings the beloved to mind. Likewise, mentioning the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brings him to mind and stirs love for him.

---

## Page 7273

Thus, when someone afflicted with sexual immorality and infatuation tells others of his condition, their souls are stirred toward such things, for souls are naturally disposed to love beautiful forms. When they picture such things, they are drawn toward the object of their desire. This is why Allah has forbidden publicizing indecency.

---

## Page 7274

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the words of the Most High:

“As for those wives whose defiance you fear, admonish them, forsake them in bed, and strike them.”

And His words:

“When you are told to rise, then rise,”

through to His words:

“Allah is All-Aware of what you do.”

“Would our shaykh explain to us how this nushūz differs from that?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

Nushūz in His words, “Whose defiance you fear, admonish them and forsake them in bed,” means that a woman turns away from her husband and shuns him, refusing to obey him when he calls her to bed, leaving his home without his permission, or otherwise withholding the obedience she owes him.

As for nushūz in His words, “When you are told to rise, then rise,” it means getting up, standing, and rising. The basic meaning of this root is elevation and roughness. Hence nashz refers to elevated, rugged ground. From the same root are His words:

“And look at the bones—how We raise them,”

meaning that We raise them up to join one another. Those who read it as nanshuruhā intend the meaning “We bring them to life.” Thus, a disobedient woman is called nāshiz because of her harshness and her holding herself above obedience to her husband. Getting up is called nushūz because the seated person rises from the ground. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7275

He also said:

Section

The words of Allah Most High in Sūrat al-Nisā’:

“Indeed, Allah does not love those who are arrogant and boastful—those who are miserly and urge others to be miserly.”

And in Sūrat al-Hadīd:

“He does not love anyone arrogant and boastful—those who are miserly and urge others to be miserly.”

These passages have been interpreted as referring to miserliness with wealth and withholding it, and to withholding knowledge and the like. They encompass withholding anything beneficial in religious or worldly affairs, whether knowledge, wealth, or anything else. Likewise, His words:

“And spend out of what We have provided for them,”

have been interpreted as encompassing giving both wealth and knowledge.

Muʿādh said concerning knowledge:

“For someone who does not know it, learning it is charity.”

Abū al-Dardā’ said:

“No man gives any charity better than an exhortation he delivers to a gathering, whose members then disperse having benefited from it through Allah”—or words to that effect.

A report states:

“What an excellent gift and an excellent present is a piece of news that a man hears and then offers as a gift to a brother of his”—or words to that effect.

This is the charity of the prophets and their heirs, the scholars. That is why Allah bestows blessings upon those who teach people what is good, and His angels, the fish in the sea, and the birds in the sky pray for them, just as

---

## Page 7276

Allah curses those who conceal knowledge, as do all who pronounce curses. There is much to be said about the virtue of making knowledge known and the blameworthiness of concealing it.

The point here is that Allah hates the arrogant, boastful person who is miserly with knowledge. Such miserliness consists in withholding it. An arrogant person, meanwhile, may be too proud to seek or accept knowledge, or may look down on certain people and therefore refuse to share it with them. This often occurs among some people: a person withholds the knowledge he possesses and becomes arrogant because of it, while also being too proud to draw from others. The opposite is humility in seeking and sharing knowledge, and generosity in doing so.

---

## Page 7277

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

We have discussed elsewhere how Allah Most High links arrogance and boastfulness with miserliness, as in His words in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ and Sūrat al-Hadīd:

“Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is arrogant and boastful.”

“Those who are miserly and urge others to be miserly.”

Their opposites are giving and fear of Allah, which entails humility, as He says:

“As for the one who gives and fears Allah.”

He also says:

“Indeed, Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good.”

These two principles encompass religion as a whole, as expressed in the saying: “Reverence for Allah’s command and mercy toward Allah’s servants.” Reverence for Allah’s command is expressed through reverent humility and humbleness, which form the foundation of fearing Allah; mercy toward Allah’s servants is expressed through kindness to them.

These two principles are the very essence of prayer and zakat. Prayer entails reverent humility before Allah, servitude to Him, humbleness before Him, and lowliness before Him—all of which stand opposed to arrogance, boastfulness, and pride. Zakat entails benefiting people and treating them kindly, which stands opposed to miserliness.

---

## Page 7278

For this and other reasons, prayer and zakat are so frequently paired in Allah’s Book.

We have already mentioned that prayer, in its general sense, encompasses everything that constitutes remembrance of Allah or supplication to Him. As ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd said:

“As long as you remember Allah, you are in prayer, even if you are in the marketplace.”

This meaning—supplicating to Allah, that is, seeking Him and turning toward Him, which entails remembering Him with reverent humility and submission—is the essence of prayer present in every instance to which the term applies. This includes the prayer of someone standing, sitting, or lying down; of a reciter or an unlettered person; and of someone able to speak or someone mute. Although its movements and utterances vary, the term “prayer” applies univocally to all these instances, excluding homonymy and metaphor. This has been discussed at length elsewhere. Some have claimed that the term is homonymous, while others have claimed that its use is metaphorical, on the grounds that it has been transferred from its linguistic meaning, that its meaning has been expanded, or on some other basis. But this is not the case.

Rather, when a general, unrestricted generic term with a univocal meaning denotes a particular kind or individual—as when you say, “this human being” or “this animal,” or, “Bring the livestock you have,” when that livestock consists of sheep—the expression indicates two things: the shared meaning present in all its instances, and what is distinctive about this particular kind or individual. The word common to all these forms of usage denotes the shared element, while whatever accompanies it, such as the definite article or something else, indicates specificity and identifies the particular referent. And just as an unrestricted universal meaning has no existence in

---

## Page 7279

external reality, so too no unrestricted expression is found in actual usage entirely detached from everything that makes it determinate. Speech conveys meaning only when its elements are joined into a construction, and this entails qualification and specification, as when you say, “Honor the human being,” or “The human being is better than the horse.” The same applies to His saying:

“Establish prayer,”

and similar expressions. Here many people have erred concerning universal meanings, imagining that they exist in external reality stripped of all qualifications, and concerning univocal terms, imagining that they are used without qualifications.

The precise position is that an unrestricted universal meaning exists in external reality only in a determinate, qualified form, and the expression denoting it occurs in usage only with qualification and specification. If the meaning is conceived in abstraction, its locus is the mind. An abstract expression is then conceived for it, although that expression does not occur in actual usage in such an abstract form.

The “point here” is that the term “prayer” has a general, unrestricted meaning, but is used only in conjunction with a qualification specific to some of its instances—as in our prayer, the prayer of the angels, and prayer as attributed to Allah, glorified and exalted is He. People err in such matters when they suppose that the prayer of one kind of being is like that of another, despite knowing that the one is not like the other. If one is not like the other, its prayer need not be like the other’s, even if the two share an element of resemblance. We have established this in detail in our refutation of the Ittihādiyya, the Jahmiyya, the philosophizers, and others like them.

---

## Page 7280

This same principle applies to Allah’s names and attributes, with resembling names and descriptions being applied to His servants, such as the Living, the Knowing, the Powerful, and the like.

Likewise, the term “zakat” has a general meaning, as illustrated by the Prophet’s saying, reported in the two Sahīh collections (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Every good deed is charity.”

Thus, it is also established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Every Muslim owes an act of charity.”

Obligatory zakat on wealth, however, is due only from some Muslims at certain times. The zakat paired with prayer shares its universal scope, in that every Muslim owes an act of charity, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) explained:

“They asked, ‘What if he has nothing to give?’ He replied, ‘He should work with his own hands, benefiting himself and giving charity.’ They asked, ‘What if he cannot?’ He replied, ‘He should help a craftsman or do work for someone who lacks the skill.’ They asked, ‘What if he cannot?’ He replied, ‘He should refrain from evil.’”

As for his saying in the authentic hadith narrated by Abū Dharr and others:

“Charity is due for every bone in each of you. Every declaration of Allah’s glory is charity; every declaration of His greatness is charity; every declaration that there is no deity but Allah is charity; enjoining what is right is charity; and forbidding what is wrong is charity.”

This, Allah willing, is comparable to the way these deeds bring benefit to created beings, for through deeds such as these, provision, victory, and guidance are obtained. They thus constitute charity toward creation. Moreover, these deeds are a form of prayer, and the form of prayer that

---

## Page 7281

benefits others encompasses both meanings: prayer and charity. Do you not see that the funeral prayer is both prayer and charity?

The same applies to every supplication and request for forgiveness on another’s behalf. Moreover, supplicating for another is also supplicating for oneself, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said in an authentic hadith:

“No man offers a supplication for his brother in his absence without Allah appointing an angel for him. Whenever he supplicates for his brother, the angel appointed for him says, ‘Amen, and may you have the same.’”

---

## Page 7282

He also said:

Section

Regarding people’s saying, “Man is domineering yet weak,” or “So-and-so is domineering yet weak”: his weakness stems from the weakness of his faculties of knowledge and power. His domineering nature, however, stems from his beliefs and desires.

As for his beliefs, he imagines himself to possess a greatness beyond his actual stature, when this is not the case. This is conceit, arrogance, and self-importance: imagining something about oneself that has no basis in reality. Among its causes are false praise of him, in verse and prose, and his seeking such false praise, for this breeds conceit.

As for desire, it is the desire for self-exaltation and aggrandizement: seeking superiority in the land and boasting over others. He desires a degree of superiority that it is not fitting for him to seek—leadership and authority—until he goes so far as to lay rival claim to lordship, as Pharaoh did, and to prophethood. This is found among scholars, devout worshippers, rulers, and others.

---

## Page 7283

Each of these—belief and desire—necessarily entails something of the other. Whoever imagines himself to be great desires what befits that imagined greatness; and whoever desires superiority in the land must imagine himself great and belittle others in order to pursue it. In desire, he imagines that greatness as something to attain; in belief, he imagines it as already existing and pursues the desires that follow from it.

Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is conceited and boastful.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Arrogance is rejecting the truth and looking down on people.”

Boastfulness resembles looking down on people, since both involve exalting oneself over others. Rejecting the truth—meaning denying and repudiating it—resembles unfounded conceit, for a person imagines the truth to be false by denying and repudiating it.

There are two ways of understanding this:

The first is to place conceit and rejection of the truth in the category of beliefs: treating truth as falsehood and falsehood as truth in matters concerning one’s own greatness and elevated standing. A person thus denies the truth that conflicts with his desires and superiority, and imagines falsehood that accords with them. Boastfulness and looking down on people are then placed in the category of desires, for the boastful person wishes to elevate himself and abase others, as does the person who looks down on people. This is supported by what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from ʿIyād ibn Himār al-Mujāshiʿī

---

## Page 7284

who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It has been revealed to me that you should be humble, so that no one boasts over another or transgresses against another.”

He thus explained that the humility commanded is the opposite of transgression and boastfulness. Concerning the conceit that Allah hates, he said: “Conceit expressed in boastfulness and transgression.” … (1)

This indicates that asserting superiority over people, if done without right, is transgression, since transgression means overstepping the bounds. If done on rightful grounds, it is boastfulness.

Yet an objection may be raised here: transgression concerns the will, so it cannot be placed in the category of belief while its counterpart is placed in the category of will. Rather, transgression seems to concern deeds, and boastfulness words. Alternatively, one may say that transgression is rejecting the truth, while boastfulness is looking down on people.

The second way of understanding this is that both concern belief and will. However, conceit—disdain for the truth—concerns truth in itself, which is Allah’s right, even when no human right is involved. Boastfulness and looking down on people, by contrast, concern the rights of human beings. The distinction would thus serve to differentiate human rights from those rights belonging to Allah that are unrelated [to] (2) human beings. This is unlike desire in cases of unlawful sexual intercourse and consuming another person’s wealth.

Thus, when He, glorified be He, said:

“Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is conceited and boastful,”

“those who are stingy and urge others to be stingy,”

He qualified the former by the latter, stinginess being the withholding of what benefits others. In earlier notes, I have written about humility and doing good, and about arrogance and stinginess.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.
> (2) I, Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’, note: The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition and has been supplied from Harf Company’s electronic edition.

---

## Page 7285

Shaykh al-Islam said:

His words,

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah,”

and the rest of the verse, follow His words:

“All is from Allah.”

Had He mentioned only their common source, the sinner would turn away from blaming himself, repenting of sin, and seeking refuge from its evil. Iblīs’s argument would take root in his heart, only causing him to be driven further away, just as it increased the polytheists in misguidance when they said:

“If Allah had willed, we would not have associated partners with Him.”

Had He mentioned only the distinction between them, they would lose sight of Allah’s oneness, belief in divine decree, and turning to Allah for guidance. Thus, in his sermon, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“All praise is due to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help, and ask His forgiveness.”

One thus thanks Him, seeks His help in obeying Him, asks His forgiveness for disobeying Him, and praises Him for His goodness.

Then he said:

“And we seek refuge in Allah from the evils of our own souls,”

and so on. Having sought forgiveness for sins, he sought refuge in Him from sins not yet committed.

Then he said:

“And from the evils of our deeds,”

meaning, from their punishments. Then he said:

“Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray,”

and so on. This is a testimony that He governs His creation. It affirms the divine decree, which holds belief in Allah’s oneness together. All of this is a prelude to the two testimonies of faith, for they can only be truly realized through praising Allah, His help, seeking His forgiveness, and turning to Him,

---

## Page 7286

and believing in His decrees. This sermon thus holds the whole framework of Islam and faith together.

He also said: There are several ways in which good things are from Allah and bad things are from oneself:

First: Blessings come without being earned.

Second: Doing good deeds is itself part of Allah’s kindness to His servant. He created life, sent the messengers, and made faith dear to His servants. When you reflect on this, you thank Allah, and He grants you more. When you recognize that evil comes only from yourself, you repent, and it ceases.

Third: A good deed is rewarded many times over.

Fourth: He loves good and is pleased with it: He loves to bestow blessings and loves to be obeyed. Thus, those who know Allah observe proper reverence by attributing blessings to Him and evil to where it occurs, as the leader of the pure monotheists said:

“Who created me, and He guides me,”

continuing to his words:

“And when I am ill, it is He who cures me.”

Fifth: Good is attributed to Him because, in every respect, His bestowal of it is an act of kindness. As for evil, He decreed it only for a wise purpose.

Sixth: Good deeds have actual existence and are connected with mercy and wisdom;

---

## Page 7287

because they consist either of doing what is commanded or refraining from what is forbidden. Refraining itself has positive existence: a person’s abstaining from something upon recognizing that it is sinful, disliking it, and restraining himself from it are all things that actually exist. It is only for refraining in this manner that one is rewarded. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, counted hatred for Allah’s sake among the firmest bonds of faith, and this is the basis of refraining. He also counted withholding for Allah’s sake as part of complete faith, and this too is the basis of refraining. Likewise, the disavowal by Allah’s intimate friend of his polytheistic people and their objects of worship was not mere abstention; rather, it sprang from hatred and enmity.

As for evil deeds, they arise from wrongdoing and ignorance. In truth, they all ultimately stem from ignorance; if a person had full knowledge of them, he would not commit them, for this is the distinctive function of reason. Yet overpowering desire may make him heedless of all this. Heedlessness and desire are the root of evil, as Allah Most High says:

“And do not obey one whose heart We have made heedless of Our remembrance and who follows his own desires”—to the end of the verse.

Seventh: Allah’s trying a person with sins is a punishment for his failure to do what he was created for and innately disposed to do.

Eighth: The causes of the good and blessings that reach a person through Allah’s favor are countless. He therefore turns to Allah for them and places his hope in Him alone. Allah deserves the fullest gratitude, which none besides Him deserves. Whatever thanks another person deserves is in return for what Allah has made possible through him. Yet this does not extend to thanking him by disobeying Allah, for Allah bestows blessings that no created being can grant, and the blessings bestowed by created beings

---

## Page 7288

also come from Him, and no one can match His recompense for gratitude and ingratitude. When a person understands that

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release thereafter,”

he places his trust and hope in Allah alone. When he recognizes the gratitude He deserves—the gratitude rightfully due to Him—it becomes His … (1)

As for evil, its cause is confined to the self. He thus knows where the harm comes from, repents, and seeks Allah’s help. As one of the early generations said: “Let no servant hope in anyone but his Lord, nor fear anything but his sin.”

The statement of Ibn ‘Abbās and others among the early generations has already been mentioned: everything that befell them on the day of Uhud was because of their sins, without anyone being excepted. This is one benefit of addressing the individual specifically: it prevents the statement from being understood as a general one subject to exceptions.

Ninth: If an evil deed comes from the soul—and evil deeds are impure, as Allah Most High says:

“Impure things are for impure people”—to the end of the verse.

The majority of the early generations explained this as: “Impure words are for impure people.” He also says:

“And the example of an impure word,”

and:

“To Him ascends good speech.”

—then, since words and deeds are attributes of the one who speaks and acts, if the soul is characterized by impurity, its proper abode is a place suited to it. Anyone who sought to make snakes live among people like cats would not succeed. Rather, if there is impurity in the soul, it is purified until

> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 7289

it is fit for Paradise, as in the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd recorded in the Sahīh, which includes:

“Until, when they have been refined and purified, they are permitted to enter Paradise.”

When a person knows that evil comes from his own self, he does not expect perfect happiness while evil remains within him. Rather, he understands that His words will be fulfilled:

“Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it.”

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,”

and so on. He knows that the Lord is All-Knowing, All-Wise, Merciful and Just, and that His actions follow the rule of justice and beneficence, as stated in the Sahīh:

“Allah’s right Hand is full,”

up to the words:

“And justice is in His other Hand.”

He also recognizes the falsehood of the Jahmiyyah’s claim that reward and punishment involve neither wisdom nor justice.

He went on to say:

Whoever follows their path, even when he reveres the commands and prohibitions, can go no further than saying—as is related from al-Shādhilī—“Unity is witnessed in your heart, while distinction is expressed on your tongue.” His words and those of others contain statements and supplications that entail nullifying the commands and prohibitions. This entails accepting the possibility that He might treat those who believe and do righteous deeds like those who spread corruption on earth. They also make supplications that overstep the bounds, as in al-Shādhilī’s litany.

Others among their common followers consider it possible for Allah to honor an evildoer or an unbeliever with the miraculous gifts bestowed upon His allies. They say, “This is a gift,” imagining such things to be miraculous favors, when they are actually satanic states like those experienced by sorcerers and soothsayers. As Allah Most High says:

“And when a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them,”

up to His words:

“Hārūt and Mārūt.”

The following saying of the Prophet is also authentically reported:

---

## Page 7290

“You will surely follow the ways of those who came before you.”

Thus, many who profess Islam have turned aside, casting the Quran behind their backs and following what the devils recite. They neither revere the Quran’s commands and prohibitions, nor ally themselves with those whom it commands them to support, nor oppose those whom it commands them to oppose. Instead, they revere anyone who performs extraordinary feats. Some know that such things come from devils, yet glorify them because of their own desires and prefer them to the way of the Quran. These are unbelievers, concerning whom Allah Most High says:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false objects of worship?”

And so on.

He said:

Among the lessons in His words, “It is from your own self,” is that a servant should not become complacent about himself or occupy himself with blaming and disparaging others. Rather, he should ask Allah to help him obey Him. This is why the supplication in al-Fātihah is the most beneficial and greatest supplication. He needs guidance at every moment, and this encompasses more kinds of needs than can be counted.

This is made clear by the fact that Allah, glorified be He, has not related any account to us in the Quran except for us to learn from it. We can only draw such lessons by comparing later cases with earlier ones. Were it not that our souls contain what was in the souls of those who denied the messengers, we would have no need to learn from people whom we never resemble. Rather, the matter is as Allah Most High says:

“Nothing is said to you except what was said to the messengers before you.”

And His words:

“Did they bequeath this to one another?”

And His words:

“Their hearts are alike.”

For this reason,

---

## Page 7291

it is stated in the hadith:

“You will surely follow the ways of those who came before you.”

The Quran has made clear that evil deeds come from one’s own self. The gravest are denying the Creator, associating partners with Him, and seeking to be a partner to Him. Both of these have occurred.

Some have said, “There is no soul that does not contain what was in Pharaoh’s soul.” For when a person reflects and examines people’s circumstances, he sees how someone may hate his counterpart and that counterpart’s followers out of envy. This is what the Jews did when Allah sent someone calling to the same message to which Moses had called. For this reason, what Allah related about them resembles what He related about Pharaoh.

---

## Page 7292

The shaykh, imam, learned and erudite scholar, Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn ‘Abd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah Most High envelop him in His mercy—also said:

All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help, guidance, and forgiveness, and seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Section

On the words of Allah Most High:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

And on some of the profound wisdom this verse contains.

Allah mentions this verse in the context of commanding jihad and condemning those who turn away from it,

---

## Page 7293

as Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, take your precautions, then go forth in groups or go forth all together.”

The verses continue down to the mention of the prayer in times of fear. Earlier, He had mentioned obedience to Allah and obedience to the Messenger, seeking judgment from Allah and the Messenger, and referring people’s disputes to Allah and the Messenger. He condemned those who seek judgment elsewhere and refer their disputes to others besides Allah and the Messenger.

Those verses thus explain faith in Allah and the Messenger. Hence His words within them:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in their disputes, then find within themselves no resistance to your judgment and submit wholeheartedly.”

This is striving in defense of what the Messenger brought. Allah Most High also says:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then never doubt, and strive with their wealth and their lives in Allah’s cause.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Say: If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your spouses, your relatives, the wealth you have acquired, the trade whose decline you fear, and the homes you cherish are dearer to you than Allah, His Messenger, and striving in His cause, then wait until Allah brings His command to pass. Allah does not guide the defiantly disobedient.”

He also says:

“Do you consider providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque equal to the deeds of one who believes in Allah and the Last Day and strives in Allah’s cause? They are not equal in Allah’s sight. Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

“Those who believe, emigrate, and strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives

---

## Page 7294

are greater in rank in Allah’s sight, and it is they who will triumph.”

“Their Lord gives them good news of mercy from Him, His pleasure, and Gardens”—the verse continues.

Allah Most High also says:

“O you who believe, shall I guide you to a bargain that will save you from a painful punishment?”

“Believe in Allah and His Messenger and strive in Allah’s cause with your wealth and your lives. That is better for you, if only you knew.”

“He will forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens beneath which rivers flow, and pleasant dwellings in Gardens of everlasting residence. That is the supreme triumph.”

“And another blessing you love: help from Allah and an imminent victory. Give good news to the believers.”

“O you who believe, be supporters of Allah, as Jesus, son of Mary, said to the disciples, ‘Who will support me in Allah’s cause?’ The disciples said, ‘We are supporters of Allah.’ A group of the Children of Israel believed, while another group disbelieved. We then supported those who believed against their enemy, and they prevailed.”

After the verses on striving, He mentioned the revelation of the Book to Allah’s Messenger so that he might judge between people according to what Allah had shown him, together with His prohibition against acting otherwise. He reminded him of Allah’s grace and mercy toward him in protecting him and safeguarding him against people leading him astray, and in teaching him what he did not know. He condemned those who oppose the Messenger and follow a path other than that of the believers. He stressed the gravity and grave danger of associating partners with Allah, and that Allah does not forgive it, but forgives lesser sins for whomever He wills—before explaining that the best religion is that of one who worships Allah alone, associating nothing with Him, provided that his worship consists in performing the good deeds He has prescribed

---

## Page 7295

rather than through religious innovations and whims. They are the followers of Abraham’s faith, who followed his faith in pure devotion to Allah.

“Allah took Abraham as a close friend.”

Thus, the command to obey the Messenger and strive to uphold that obedience entailed adhering to monotheism and the faith of Abraham. This means devoting religion sincerely to Allah and worshipping Him through the good deeds He commanded through His messengers.

Within the verses on striving, Allah Most High condemned those who fear the enemy and cling to life. He explained that abandoning striving would not keep death away from them. Rather, wherever they might be, death would overtake them, even in lofty, fortified towers. They therefore gain no benefit by abandoning striving; they gain nothing but loss in this world and the Hereafter. Allah Most High said:

“Have you not seen those who were told, ‘Restrain your hands, establish prayer, and give zakah’? Yet when fighting was prescribed for them, a group of them feared people as they feared Allah, or even more. They said, ‘Our Lord, why have You prescribed fighting for us? If only You had granted us respite for a little while!’ Say, ‘The enjoyment of this world is brief, and the Hereafter is better for those who fear Allah. You will not be wronged even by as much as the thread of a date stone.’”

It has been said that this group were hypocrites. It has also been said that they became hypocrites when fighting was prescribed for them. Another view is that they merely displayed cowardice and lost heart, so there was sickness in their hearts. As Allah Most High said:

“But when a decisive surah is revealed and fighting is mentioned in it,

---

## Page 7296

you see those in whose hearts is sickness looking at you like one overcome by death. So woe to them!”

“Obedience and proper speech”—and the rest of the verse. Allah Most High also said:

“And when the hypocrites and those in whose hearts is sickness said, ‘Allah and His Messenger promised us nothing but delusion.’”

The meaning encompasses both groups, as well as everyone in this condition.

Then He said:

“Wherever you may be, death will overtake you, even if you are in lofty, fortified towers. If good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah.’ But if evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’ Say, ‘All is from Allah.’ What is the matter with these people that they can hardly understand any statement?”

The pronoun in His words, “If good befalls them,” refers back to those He mentioned, namely those who “fear people.” Alternatively, it refers to an understood referent, even though it has not been mentioned explicitly, as occurs in many passages.

It has been said that these people were disbelievers among the Jews. It has also been said that they were hypocrites, or that they included people from both groups. The meaning encompasses everyone who fits this description.

However, it applies more fittingly to those who profess Islam and enjoin striving. If the censure encompasses these people, then it applies all the more to the disbelievers who do not profess Islam

---

## Page 7297

and more deservedly so.

The view held by most Quran commentators is that “good” and “evil” here mean blessings and calamities. They do not refer merely to a person’s freely chosen actions considered as good or evil deeds.

Section

In Allah’s Book, the terms “good” and “evil” encompass both meanings. Allah Most High said concerning the hypocrites:

“If good touches you, it distresses them; but if evil befalls you, they rejoice at it. Yet if you are patient and fear Allah, their plotting will not harm you at all.”

He Most High also said:

“If good befalls you, it distresses them; but if a calamity befalls you, they say, ‘We took our precautions beforehand,’ and turn away rejoicing.”

He Most High also said:

“We tested them with good things and bad, so that they might return.”

He Most High also said:

“Indeed, when We let man taste mercy from Us, he rejoices in it; but if evil befalls them because of what their hands have sent forth, then man is indeed ungrateful.”

He Most High said concerning the disbelievers who regarded Moses and those with him as an evil omen:

“When good came to them, they said, ‘This is our due.’ But if evil befell them, they attributed it to the ill omen of Moses and those with him.”

He mentioned this after saying:

“We afflicted Pharaoh’s people with years of drought and a shortage of crops, so that they might take heed.”

As for deeds that are commanded or forbidden, these terms occur in such statements as His words, Most High:

“Whoever

---

## Page 7298

comes with a good deed will have better than it; but whoever comes with an evil deed—those who commit evil deeds will be recompensed only for what they used to do.”

And His words, Most High:

“Indeed, good deeds do away with evil deeds. That is a reminder for those who take heed.”

And His words, Most High:

“For those, Allah will replace their evil deeds with good deeds. And Allah is Ever-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

Here, however, He said:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

He did not say, “Whatever you do” or “Whatever you earn.” Similarly, He said:

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your hands have earned.”

He Most High also said:

“Know that Allah only intends to afflict them for some of their sins.”

He Most High also said:

“Say, ‘Do you await anything for us but one of the two good outcomes? We, however, await Allah’s afflicting you with punishment from Himself or at our hands.’”

He Most High also said:

“Those who disbelieve will continue to be struck by calamity because of what they have done, or it will settle close to their homes.”

He Most High also said:

“And the calamity of death befalls you.”

He Most High also said:

“And give glad tidings to the patient, who, when calamity befalls them, say, ‘Indeed, we belong to Allah, and to Him we shall return.’”

Thus, the expressions “Whatever good befalls you” and “whatever evil” encompass the blessings that come to a person and bring him joy, and the calamities that befall him and cause him distress. The verse unquestionably encompasses this meaning. This is also what most Quran commentators have said.

Abū al-‘Āliyah said:

“If good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah.’”

---

## Page 7299

He said: “This refers to prosperity.” Concerning “But if evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you,’” he said: “This refers to adversity.”

Al-Suddī said:

“‘If good befalls them’—good being abundance, whereby their horses, cattle, and other livestock reproduce, their circumstances improve, and their women bear sons—‘they say, “This is from Allah.” But if evil befalls them’—evil being harm to their property—they regard Muhammad as an ill omen and say: ‘This is from you.’ They mean: ‘This affliction has befallen us because we abandoned our religion and followed Muhammad.’ Allah then revealed: ‘Say, “All is from Allah”’—both good and evil—‘So what is wrong with these people that they can hardly understand any speech?’” He said that “speech” meant the Quran.

Al-Wālibī reported from Ibn ‘Abbās concerning “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah”: “This means the victory Allah granted you on the day of Badr.” Al-Dahhāk said likewise.

Al-Wālibī also reported from Ibn ‘Abbās concerning “Whatever good”: “Whatever spoils and victory he obtained were from Allah.” He said: “‘Evil’ refers to what befell him on the day of Uhud, when his face was wounded and his lateral incisor was broken.”

He also said: “As for ‘good,’ Allah bestowed it upon you as a blessing; as for ‘evil,’ Allah tested you with it.”

---

## Page 7300

It was also reported through Hajjāj, from ‘Atiyya, from Ibn ‘Abbās, concerning “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah,” that he said: “This refers to the day of Badr.” Concerning “And whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,” he said: “This refers to the day of Uhud.” He explains: “Whatever misfortune befalls you is because of your sin, and I have decreed it for you.”

Likewise, Ibn ‘Uyayna narrated from Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid, from Abū Sālih, concerning “is from yourself,” that he said: “It is because of your sin, and I have decreed it for you.” Ibn Abī Hātim and others narrated these reports.

It was also reported from Mutarrif ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Shikhkhīr that he said:

“What do you want with divine decree? Is this verse in Sūrat al-Nisā’ not enough for you: ‘If good befalls them, they say, “This is from Allah.” But if evil befalls them, they say, “This is from you”’—that is, from yourself? By Allah, they have not entrusted their affairs to divine decree, although they have been commanded to do so, and to it they will ultimately come.”

Likewise, in Abū Sālih’s commentary transmitted from Ibn ‘Abbās, “If good befalls them” means abundance and rain, while “If evil befalls them” means drought and affliction.

Ibn Qutayba said concerning “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself”: “Good means a blessing, and evil means an affliction.”

---

## Page 7301

Abū al-Faraj mentioned three interpretations of His words, “Whatever good befalls you” and “whatever evil [befalls you].”

“The first is that ‘good’ refers to the victory Allah granted them at Badr, and ‘evil’ to what befell them at Uhud.” He said: “Ibn Abī Talha—namely, al-Wālibī—reported this from Ibn ‘Abbās.”

He said: “The second is that ‘good’ means obedience, and ‘evil’ means disobedience. This was stated by Abū al-‘Āliya. The third is that ‘good’ means a blessing, and ‘evil’ means an affliction. This was stated by Ibn Munabbih.” He said: “A similar interpretation is reported from Abū al-‘Āliya, and this is the most correct.”

I say: This is the view known through a chain of transmission from Abū al-‘Āliya, as previously cited from his well-known commentary, which he and others transmit from him through Abū Ja‘far al-Dārī, from al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, from him, and through comparable routes.

As for the second interpretation, he did not mention its chain of transmission. Rather, he quotes from the works of commentators who cite the statements of the early generations without chains of transmission. Many of these reports are weak—indeed, fabricated—and cannot be established as statements of those to whom they are attributed. Later commentators, too, generally interpret the verse along the lines of the statements of the early generations, while a group of them understands ‘good’ and ‘evil’ as referring to obedience and disobedience.

---

## Page 7302

As for the first category, the verse unquestionably encompasses it, as indicated by its wording, context, meaning, and the statements of the early generations. As for the second meaning, it is certainly not intended to the exclusion of the first. It may, however, be said that it is intended alongside the first, insofar as the obedience to which Allah guides a person is a blessing from Allah that has come to him. Whatever disobedience he commits is an evil that has befallen him, and it is his own self that committed the evil deed.

If the recompense stems from his own self, then the deed that incurred that recompense must all the more stem from his own self. Thus, there is no contradiction in both the evil deed and the evil suffered in recompense stemming from his own self, even though all of it is decreed, as explained earlier.

It has been reported through Mujāhid from Ibn ‘Abbās that he used to recite:

“Then it is from yourself, and I have decreed it for you.”

Section

A second act of disobedience may be a punishment for the first. It would thus be an evil suffered in recompense while also being an evil deed. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said—in the hadith whose authenticity is agreed upon—

---

## Page 7303

as narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd (may Allah be pleased with him) from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Adhere to truthfulness, for truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to Paradise. A man continues to speak the truth and earnestly pursue truthfulness until he is recorded with Allah as a man of truth. Beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire. A man continues to lie and deliberately pursue falsehood until he is recorded with Allah as a habitual liar.”

Several passages in the Quran make clear that a second good deed may be part of the reward for the first. Likewise, a second evil deed may be part of the punishment for the first. Allah Most High says:

“If they had done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them and would have strengthened them in steadfastness. Then We would have granted them a great reward from Us, and We would have guided them to a straight path.”

Allah Most High also says:

“As for those who strive for Our sake, We will surely guide them to Our ways.”

And Allah Most High says:

“As for those who are killed in the way of Allah, He will never let their deeds go to waste. He will guide them and set their affairs right, and admit them to Paradise, which He has made known to them.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Then the outcome of those who did evil was the worst.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And a clear Book, through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace.”

And Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and believe in His Messenger. He will grant you a double share of His mercy, provide you with a light by which to walk, and forgive you.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And in their inscription is guidance and mercy for those who fear their Lord.”

And Allah Most High says:

“This is a clear explanation for mankind, and guidance and admonition for the Allah-fearing.”

## Page 7304

And Allah Most High says:

“Say: For those who believe, it is guidance and healing. But those who do not believe have deafness in their ears, and it is blindness for them.”

“But their brothers draw them further into error, never relenting.”

“Thus We turned evil and indecency away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

Abū ‘Uthmān al-Naysābūrī said:

## Page 7305

I say:

He also says at the end of the surah:

and strike every fingertip of theirs.’”

## Page 7307

on earth and sever your ties of kinship?”

“Those are the ones whom Allah has cursed, so He made them deaf and blinded their sight.”

“Then do they not reflect upon the Quran, or are there locks upon their hearts?”

“Indeed, those who turned back after guidance had become clear to them—Satan enticed them and filled them with false hopes.”

“That is because they said to those who hated what Allah revealed, ‘We will obey you in some matters.’ And Allah knows their secrets.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And among them are those who pledged to Allah, ‘If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give charity and be among the righteous.’”

“But when He gave them of His bounty, they withheld it and turned away in aversion.”

—up to His words—

“And if those who disbelieve had fought you, they would have turned their backs; then they would have found neither protector nor helper.”

“This is Allah’s established way, which has prevailed before, and you will never find any change in Allah’s established way.”

Their turning their backs is not something they were forbidden to do; rather, it is part of the recompense for their deeds. This is a vast subject.

---

## Page 7308

Section

Since the evil deeds a person commits may be part of the recompense for earlier sins—and are themselves harmful—it is valid to say that they are among the evils that have befallen him because of earlier sins. In any case, the sins he commits are from his own self, even though they have been decreed for him. For if the recompense resulting from those sins is from his own self, then his deed, which itself constitutes that recompense, is all the more from his own self.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his sermons:

“We seek refuge in Allah from the evil of our own selves and from the evil of our deeds.”

Abū Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, said to him, “Teach me a supplication.” He replied:

“Say: ‘O Allah, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen, Lord and Sovereign of all things! I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped but You. I seek refuge in You from the evil of my own self, from the evil of Satan and his polytheism, and from committing evil against myself or bringing it upon a Muslim.’ Say this in the morning, in the evening, and when you go to bed.”

---

## Page 7309

It has thus been made clear that His words, “it is from yourself,” encompass both the punishments for deeds and the deeds themselves, even though all are decreed by Allah.

Section

The Qadariyyah cannot use this verse as evidence for several reasons.

One is that they say a person’s action—whether good or evil—comes from him, not from Allah. Rather, Allah has given each person the capacity to perform both good and evil deeds. In their view, however, one person originates a will by which he performs good deeds, while another originates a will by which he performs evil deeds. Neither, according to them, is brought into being by the Lord.

The Quran distinguishes between good and evil, whereas they make no distinction between good and evil deeds except in relation to the divine command—not on the basis that Allah creates good deeds within a person but not evil deeds. Rather, in their view, He creates neither.

Some of them, however, say that He brings into being those good and evil deeds that constitute recompense, as Ahl al-Sunnah also say.

---

## Page 7310

But on this view, they do not hold that all good comes from Allah, nor all evil, but only some of each.

Second: He said, “All are from Allah,” thus declaring that good things are from Allah just as evil things are from Allah. They do not affirm this regarding deeds, but only regarding recompense. His subsequent words, “Whatever good befalls you” and “whatever evil,” are like His words, “If good befalls them,” and, “If evil befalls them.”

Third: As explained above, the verse refers to blessings and calamities. Nor can the determinist Qadariyyah invoke this verse to deny that they performed the deeds for which they deserved punishment. For His words, “All are from Allah,” refer to blessings and calamities. Moreover, His words, “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,” are evidence against them. They make clear that a person is the one who commits evil deeds and deserves punishment for them, while Allah bestows good upon him—both the deeds themselves and their reward.

Since whatever good befalls them is from Allah, blessings are from Allah, whether bestowed initially or as recompense. If they are recompense—and they are from Allah—then the righteous deed that occasioned them is also from Allah. Allah has bestowed both upon His servant. Otherwise, if the righteous deed originated from the person himself, just as evil deeds are from himself, then all of that would be from himself. Yet Allah Most High has distinguished between the two kinds in the Quran and the Sunnah.

As in the authentic divine hadith, related from Allah: “O My servants, these are only your deeds

---

## Page 7311

which I record for you and then recompense you for in full. Whoever finds good should praise Allah, and whoever finds otherwise should blame no one but himself.”

Allah Most High said:
“When a calamity befell you, though you had inflicted twice as much, did you say, ‘How has this happened?’ Say, ‘It is from your own selves.’”

And He said:
“But if evil befalls them because of what their own hands have done, they immediately despair.”

And He said:
“Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what people’s hands have earned, so that He may make them taste some of what they have done, that perhaps they may return.”

And He said:
“We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves.”

And He said:
“We did not wrong them, but it was they who were the wrongdoers.”

And He said:
“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

And He said to the believers:
“But Allah has endeared faith to you and made it beautiful in your hearts, and has made disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience hateful to you. Those are the rightly guided.”

They have also been commanded to say in prayer:
“Guide us to the straight path,”
“the path of those You have blessed, not of those who have incurred Your wrath, nor of those who have gone astray.”

Section

Some have supposed that the verse presents an apparent difficulty or contradiction, since He said, “All are from Allah,” then distinguished between good things and evil things, saying, “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

---

## Page 7312

And this stems from their limited understanding and failure to reflect upon the verse. There is no contradiction in it, whether in its apparent or underlying sense, its wording or its meaning. For He spoke of the hypocrites and those with sickness in their hearts who shrank back from jihad, saying:

“Wherever you may be, death will overtake you, even if you are in lofty towers. If good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah’; but if evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’”

They say this to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, meaning: “Because of the religion you commanded us to follow, and because we abandoned our former ways, these evils have befallen us, for you commanded us to do what brought them about.”

The evil things, then, are calamities; and it was he who commanded them to perform the deeds they supposed had caused those calamities. Their statement, “from you,” includes the calamities of jihad that lead to defeat, because he commanded them to undertake jihad. It also includes calamities affecting their livelihood, which they superstitiously attributed to him as a bad omen, meaning: “This is a punishment upon us because of your religion.” Pharaoh’s people likewise regarded Moses and those with him as bad omens. Similarly, the people of the town said to the messengers:

“We regard you as a bad omen.”

And the disbelievers of Thamūd said to Sālih and his people:

“We regard you and those with you as bad omens.”

Thus, concerning whatever befell them—warfare, upheaval, wounds, killing, and other things inflicted by the enemy—they would say: “This is from you, because you commanded us to perform the deeds that brought it about.”

They would say of both these and calamities from heaven, “They are from you,” meaning: “Because we obeyed you and followed your religion, we have suffered these

---

## Page 7313

calamities.” As Allah Most High says:

“Among the people is one who worships Allah on the brink: if good befalls him, he is content with it; but if a trial befalls him, he turns back, losing both this world and the Hereafter.”

This includes everyone who regards obedience to the Messenger and acting upon what he was sent with as a cause of some evil that has befallen him, whether from heaven or at human hands. Such people are many. When they said, “This is from you,” they did not mean, “You are the one who brought it about,” for they knew that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had brought about none of those things. Nor were they addressing one another when they said, “from you”; rather, they were addressing the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Whoever understands this will see clearly that His statement:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself”

does not contradict His statement:

“All is from Allah.”

Rather, it confirms it. For they—and those like them until the Day of Resurrection—regard what the Messenger brought, and acting upon it, as a cause of whatever calamities may befall them. The same applies to those who obey him until the Day of Resurrection.

At times, they would disparage what he brought and say: “This is not something Allah has commanded. Had it been something Allah commanded, those who followed it would not have suffered this affliction.”

---

## Page 7314

At other times, they do not attack the underlying principle, but criticize the particular matter at hand. They say: “This is due to the Messenger’s poor planning.” Such was the statement of ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl on the day of Uhud. His view had agreed with that of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that they should not leave Medina. However, some who were eager to fight in Allah’s cause asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to go out. He agreed, entered his house, and put on his armor. Once he had put it on, they regretted their request and said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “You know best. If you wish us not to go out, we will not go out.” He replied:

“It is not fitting for a prophet, once he has put on his armor, to remove it until Allah judges between him and his enemy.”

This means that jihad becomes binding once it has been undertaken, just as Hajj does. One may not abandon what one has begun, except when unable to continue, as when prevented from completing Hajj.

Section

The Quran commentators have mentioned both explanations concerning His words:

“But if evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’”

It is reported from Ibn ‘Abbās, al-Suddī, and others that they say this because they regard his religion as an evil omen. And ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam is reported to have said: “Because of your poor planning”—meaning

---

## Page 7315

as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ubayy and others said on the day of Uhud—they are like those who “said of their brothers, while they themselves stayed behind, ‘Had they obeyed us, they would not have been killed.’”

In either case, their statement, “This is from you,” is an attack on the faith and jihad that Allah and His Messenger commanded. It portrays these as the cause of the calamities that befall obedient believers, such as those that befell them on the day of Uhud. At other times, calamities befall their enemies, and the disbelievers say, “This is because these people bring ill fortune,” just as the people of the town said to the messengers:

“Indeed, we regard you as an evil omen.”

Likewise, Allah Most High said of Pharaoh’s people:

“When good came to them, they said, ‘This is ours.’ But whenever evil befell them, they regarded Moses and those with him as an evil omen. Surely their fate is with Allah, but most of them do not know.”

Allah Most High also said of the people of Sālih:

“They said, ‘We regard you and those with you as an evil omen.’ He said, ‘Your fate is with Allah. Rather, you are a people being tested.’”

And when the people of the town said:

“Indeed, we regard you as an evil omen. If you do not desist, we will surely stone you, and a painful punishment from us will surely afflict you,”

the messengers replied:

“Your ill fortune is with yourselves. Is it because you have been reminded? Rather, you are a people who transgress all bounds.”

Concerning His words, “Surely their fate is with Allah,” al-Dahhāk said: “This means that the matter comes from Allah. Whatever befalls you comes from Allah because of what your hands have earned.”

---

## Page 7316

And Ibn Abī Talhah reported from Ibn ‘Abbās: “Your faults.” Qatādah said: “Your deeds are with Allah.” In a narration transmitted by someone other than ‘Alī: “Your deeds are with Allah.”

“Rather, you are a people being tested”—that is, you are being tested through obedience to Allah and disobedience to Him. Ibn Abī Hātim and others transmitted both reports.

Ibn Ishāq said: “The messengers said, ‘Your ill fortune is with yourselves,’ meaning your deeds.” Thus, they explained “fate” as deeds and their recompense, because those people used to say: “The calamities that have befallen us have only befallen us because of the sins of the messengers and their followers.”

Allah, glorified is He, therefore made clear that their fate—namely, their deeds and their recompense—is with Allah, and is with them. It is with them because their deeds and the recompense decreed for those deeds accompany them, as Allah Most High said:

“And We have bound every person’s fate to his neck.”

It is from Allah because Allah Most High decreed those calamities on account of their deeds. From Him calamities descend upon them as recompense for their deeds, not because of the messengers and their followers. In this connection, it is said that they are recompensed only for their own deeds, not for the deeds of others.

That is why He said in this verse—when the hypocrites, the disbelievers, and those in whose hearts is disease were saying: “What has befallen us is because of what Muhammad has brought—a punishment

---

## Page 7317

of a religious nature that has befallen us”—He, glorified is He, made clear that the calamities that befell them were only because of their sins. This refutes those who turned away from obedience to the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, lest those calamities befall them; those who professed belief in the Messenger but attributed those calamities to putting his teachings into practice; and those who suffered them while disbelieving in the Messenger and attributed them to what he brought.

Section

The point is that what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought is not the cause of any calamity. Obedience to Allah and His Messenger can never be the cause of a calamity. Rather, obedience to Allah and the Messenger entails only that those who obey be rewarded with the good of both this world and the Hereafter. However, calamities may befall those who believe in Allah and His Messenger because of their sins, not because of their obedience to Allah and the Messenger—as happened to them on the day of Uhud because of their sins, not because of their obedience to Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Likewise, the trials they underwent in prosperity, adversity, and upheaval were not caused by their faith and obedience themselves. Rather, they were tested through them so that they might rid themselves of the evil within them

---

## Page 7318

and were tried through them as gold is tried by fire, so that its pure substance may be distinguished from its impurities. Human souls contain evil, and trials purify the believer of the evil within his soul. Allah Most High says:

“Such days We alternate among people, so that Allah may make evident those who believe and take martyrs from among you. And Allah does not love the wrongdoers.”

“And so that Allah may purify those who believe and destroy the disbelievers.”

Allah Most High also says:

“So that Allah may test what is in your breasts and purify what is in your hearts.”

For this reason, Sālih, peace be upon him, said to his people:

“Your fate is with Allah; rather, you are a people being tested.”

Thus, calamities expiate the believers’ sins, and their ranks are raised through patiently enduring them. As for the calamities they suffer at the enemy’s hands during jihad, Allah increases their reward for patiently enduring them. It is reported in al-Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No fighting force goes forth in Allah’s cause, returns unharmed, and gains spoils without having received two-thirds of its reward in advance. But if they suffer harm and gain no spoils, their reward is granted in full.”

As for the hunger, thirst, and exhaustion they suffer, a righteous deed is recorded for them on account of it. As Allah Most High says:

“That is because no thirst, fatigue, or hunger afflicts them in Allah’s cause, nor do they tread any ground that enrages the disbelievers, nor inflict any loss upon an enemy, without a righteous deed being recorded for them on account of it. Indeed, Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

There is much evidence supporting this.

---

## Page 7319

Section

The point concerns His words:

“If good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah’; but if evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’ Say, ‘All is from Allah.’”

They attributed the calamities that befell them to what the Messenger had brought them. They would say, “The blessings we receive are from Allah, but calamity is from Muhammad”—meaning, because of his religion and what he commanded.

The Most High therefore said: Say that both are from Allah, not from Muhammad. Muhammad brings neither blessing nor calamity. For this reason, He then said:

“What is the matter with these people that they can hardly understand what is said?”

Al-Suddī and others said that this refers to the Quran. For if they understood its contents, it would become clear to them that it commands them only to goodness, justice, truthfulness, and worshipping Allah alone. It does not command them to do anything that would cause calamities. If they understood what is in the Quran, they would know that it cannot be a cause of evil at all.

This helps demonstrate that Allah’s command to do something makes known its goodness and benefit, and that it serves His servants’ welfare. It is not as some claim: that Allah may command His servants to do something which, if they do it, brings them no benefit, but instead causes them harm.

---

## Page 7320

For if that were so, those who regard the messengers and their followers as bearers of ill omen might affirm that claim.

This is further clarified by the fact that, after saying:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

He then said:

“We have sent you to mankind as a Messenger, and sufficient is Allah as a witness.”

Allah has testified to his messengership through the signs and miracles He manifested at his hands. When Allah testifies for him, He is sufficient as a witness. Their denial of his messengership does not harm him. The specious arguments they advanced count against them, not in their favour: they sought to make their misfortunes and punishments evidence disproving his messengership.

Yet Allah Most High has testified that He sent him to mankind as a Messenger. Concluding the passage with this therefore refutes their claim that calamities come from the Messenger. For this reason, He then said:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah. But whoever turns away—We have not sent you as a keeper over them.”

Section

What Allah has stated also refutes the claim of the Jahmī advocates of compulsion and others like them, who say that Allah may punish His servants without their having committed any sin, and that He may command them to do what does not benefit them, but instead harms them. Thus, if they do what He commands, they suffer harm; and if they do not, He punishes them.

---

## Page 7321

They say this and similar things, claiming that this is because He does whatever He wills. The Quran refutes them in many ways, just as it refutes those who deny divine decree. Thus, as explained above, the verse refutes both groups, even though both cite it in support of their views. It is evidence against them both.

If those who deny divine decree say:

“He said of good, ‘It is from Allah,’ and of evil, ‘It is from yourself,’ only because He commands the former and forbids the latter, as all Muslims agree.”

They further say:

“We hold that His will is inseparable from His command: whatever He commands, He wills, and whatever He does not command, He does not will. Thus, His will and His command urge obedience, not disobedience. This is why the former is from Him, but not the latter.”

The reply is:

As for the verse, it has already become clear that those who said, “Good is from Allah, and evil is from you,” meant: “From you, O Muhammad—that is, because of your religion.” They thus made the Messenger’s mission the cause of calamities. This is a different issue from that of divine decree.

Even if obedience and disobedience were intended—as has been suggested—then

---

## Page 7322

His words, “All is from Allah,” would be evidence against you, as explained above. His subsequent words,

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

do not contradict this. Rather, Allah has bestowed both the “good” and its reward, while the “evil” originates in the human soul, even though it occurs by His decree and predestination. As He, Most High, says:

“From the evil of what He has created.”

Thus, some created things have evil in them, even though they exist by His decree and predestination.

Yet you say that obedience and disobedience are both brought about by human beings, without Allah causing either person to act, and without Allah singling out the believer for a blessing and mercy through which he obeys Him. This contradicts the Quran.

Section

If it is asked:

“If acts of obedience and disobedience are predestined, and blessings and calamities are predestined, why does He distinguish between good things, meaning blessings, and evil things, meaning calamities, attributing the former to Allah and the latter to the human soul?”

The answer is:

Because there are distinctions between them:

---

## Page 7323

“The first distinction”:

Allah bestows His blessings and kindness upon His servants without any prior cause on their part whatsoever. He grants well-being, provision, victory, and other blessings to those who have never done any good. He also brings into being creatures for Paradise whom He settles in its unoccupied expanses, having created them in the Hereafter without their having done any good deeds. By His mercy, He admits the children of the believers and those among them who are of unsound mind into Paradise without any deeds on their part.

As for punishment, He punishes no one except for their own deeds.

“The second distinction”:

When someone performs good deeds, the very act of performing them is itself part of Allah’s kindness and His favour in granting that person guidance and faith. As the people of Paradise say:

“Praise be to Allah, who has guided us to this. We would never have been guided had Allah not guided us.”

And in the authentic hadith:

“O My servants, it is only your deeds that I record for you and then recompense you for in full. Whoever finds good, let him praise Allah; and whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself.”

The very fact that Allah created them alive and endowed them with hearing, sight, and hearts is itself one of His blessings. His sending the Messenger to them, and the Messenger’s clearly conveying the message through which they were guided, is likewise one of His blessings. His inspiring them with faith, guiding them to it, and singling them out for an additional blessing through which they attained

---

## Page 7324

faith, while the disbelievers did not, is likewise one of His blessings. As Allah Most High says:

“But Allah has endeared faith to you and made it beautiful in your hearts, and has made disbelief, defiance, and disobedience hateful to you. Those are the rightly guided.”

“As a bounty and blessing from Allah.”

All the good that creation enjoys in this world and the Hereafter is purely a blessing from Him, without any prior cause that would give them a right to it. They have no power or strength of their own except through Him. He is the Creator of their very selves, their righteous deeds, and the recompense. Thus, His statement,

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah,”

is true in every respect, outwardly and inwardly, according to the doctrine of Ahl al-Sunnah. As for “misfortune,” it occurs only because of the servant’s sin, and his sin stems from himself. Allah did not say, “I did not decree that or create it.” Rather, He told people what would benefit them.

Section

When the servant reflects, he realizes that the good he enjoys comes from Allah’s bounty, so he thanks Allah. Allah then grants him still more of His bounty: righteous deeds and blessings that He lavishes upon him.

---

## Page 7325

And when he realizes that evil befalls him only through himself, because of his sins, he seeks forgiveness and repents, thereby removing the cause of that evil. Thus, the servant is constantly giving thanks and seeking forgiveness, so that good continues to multiply for him and evil is warded off.

Thus, in his sermons, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would say, “Praise be to Allah,” thanking Allah. Then he would say, “We seek His help and His forgiveness”: we seek His help in obedience and His forgiveness for disobedience. Then he would say, “And we seek refuge in Allah from the evils of our souls and from the evils of our deeds.”

He thereby seeks refuge in Him from the evil within the soul and from the punishment for his deeds. Evil comes only from his own soul and its deeds. He therefore seeks refuge in Allah from the evil of his soul: lest he commit sins as a result of his wrongdoings. Then, if he does act, he seeks refuge in Allah from the evils of his deeds and from the punishments for them. Thus, he seeks His help in obedience and the means that lead to it, and seeks refuge in Him from disobedience and its punishment.

The servant’s knowledge that whatever good befalls him is from Allah, and whatever misfortune befalls him is from himself, calls for both of these responses. Here, Allah, glorified be He, distinguishes between the two after bringing them together in His statement:

“Say, ‘All is from Allah.’”

He thus explained that good and evil—blessings and calamities, and also acts of obedience and disobedience according to those who include them in this statement—are “from Allah.” Then He explained the distinction that benefits them: namely, that this good comes from

---

## Page 7326

Allah’s favor; so thank Him, and He will grant you more. This evil comes from your sins; so seek His forgiveness, and He will ward it off from you. Allah Most High says:

“But Allah would not punish them while you were among them, nor would Allah punish them while they seek forgiveness.”

And He says:

“Alif Lām Rā. A Book whose verses have been perfected, then explained in detail, from One who is All-Wise, All-Aware.”

“Worship none but Allah. Indeed, I am a warner and a bearer of glad tidings to you from Him.”

“And seek your Lord’s forgiveness, then repent to Him. He will grant you a good life until an appointed time and give every person of merit his due reward.”

When a sinner seeks his Lord’s forgiveness for his sin, he follows the example of the blessed among the prophets and believers, such as Adam and others. But if he persists and invokes divine decree as an excuse, he follows the example of the wretched, such as Satan and those who follow him in error.

Thus, His statement that misfortune comes from a person himself because of his sins—after stating that everything is from Allah—draws attention to seeking forgiveness and repenting, and to seeking refuge in Allah from the evil of one’s own soul and deeds. This supplication is to be made morning and evening and at bedtime, as in the report: “The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, the best of this nation, to do this, teaching him to say, ‘O Allah, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen, I seek refuge in You from the evil of my own soul

---

## Page 7327

and from the evil of Satan and the polytheism to which he calls, and from committing evil against myself or bringing it upon a Muslim.’” Thus, he seeks forgiveness for what has passed and refuge from what lies ahead, and so joins the ranks of the blessed. When he knows that good—both the deed and its reward—comes from Allah, he asks Him for help in doing good deeds, saying:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

And:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

And:

“Our Lord, do not let our hearts deviate after You have guided us.”

And other supplications of this kind.

But if it is stated only that everything comes from Allah, without mentioning the distinction, this places the two on an equal footing.
