As for those beings, the same applies to them as to the soul: He created them for a wise purpose. Since the absence of that by which the soul acts and is set right is one of the two causes, and since pure evil, containing no good, is sheer nonexistence—and nonexistence is not attributed to Allah, for it is not a thing, whereas Allah is the Creator of all things—evil deeds are attributable to the soul in that its very nature necessarily entails volitional activity which, in the absence of what would set it right, gives rise to those evil deeds.

When a servant acknowledges and affirms that Allah is the Creator of all his deeds, this may take either of two forms. If he acknowledges this in affirmation that Allah creates everything by His power and that His will unfailingly takes effect; in affirmation of His perfect words, which neither the righteous nor the wicked can overstep; and in acknowledgment of his own poverty and need of Allah—that unless He guides him, he is astray; unless He turns to him in mercy, he persists in sin; and unless He forgives him, he is doomed—then he humbles himself before His might and wisdom. Such is the state of

---

## Page 7380

the believers whom Allah treats with mercy, guides, and enables to obey Him. If, however, he says this to argue against the Lord, to exempt himself from His commands and prohibitions, and to excuse himself, this is a greater sin than the first. Such a person is among Satan’s followers, and this only makes him worse.

We have already explained that the Lord, glorified be He, is praised for who He is and for His kindness to His creation. Likewise, He deserves to be loved for who He is and for His kindness to His servants. He also deserves that His servant be content with His decree, because His judgment is just: He does nothing but what is good and just. Moreover, He decrees nothing for the believer except what is good for him:

“If prosperity comes his way, he is grateful, and that is good for him. If adversity befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him.”

Thus, the believer is content with His decree because the Lord deserves praise and commendation for who He is, and because He is kind to the believer.

Some people raise the following question: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah decrees nothing for the believer except what is good for him.”

Yet He has decreed that the believer commit sins that incur punishment. How, then, can that be good?

There are two answers to this. The first is that people’s deeds are not included in this hadith; it concerns only

---

## Page 7381

the blessings and afflictions that befall a person, as in His words:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

This is why he said:

“If prosperity comes his way, he is grateful, and that is good for him. If adversity befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him.”

Thus, he described the decree as the prosperity and adversity that befall a person. This is the apparent meaning of the hadith’s wording, and on this understanding there is no difficulty.

The second answer is that, even assuming that deeds are included here, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever is pleased by his good deed and distressed by his evil deed is a believer.”

If Allah decrees that he do good, this pleases him, and he thanks Allah for it. If He decrees that he commit an evil deed, it is only a deed for which he deserves punishment if he does not repent of it. If he repents, it is replaced with a good deed, and he thanks Allah for it. If he does not repent, he is tested with afflictions that expiate it, and he bears them patiently; thus, that is good for him.

The Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Allah does not decree for the believer…” The believer is one who does not persist in a sin but repents of it, so that it becomes a good deed, as stated in several verses. A servant may indeed commit a sin and, through what he does thereafter, enter Paradise because of it: he keeps repenting of it until his repentance for it brings him into Paradise. Sin leads the servant to humility and submission, to supplicate to Allah and seek His forgiveness, and to recognize his utter poverty and need of Him, and that none forgives sins but Him.

---

## Page 7382

So, because of sin, the believer gains good deeds that he would not otherwise have gained. This decree is therefore good for him.

With regard to his sins, then, he faces one of two outcomes: either he repents and Allah accepts his repentance, so that he becomes one of those who constantly repent, whom Allah loves; or Allah expiates his sins through afflictions. Adversity befalls him, and he bears it patiently. Through those afflictions, Allah expiates his evil deeds, and through patience in bearing them, his ranks are raised. It is related in some hadiths:

“Allah Most High says: Those who remember Me are those who enjoy My company. Those who thank Me are those to whom I grant increase. Those who obey Me are those whom I honor. Those who disobey Me—I do not make them despair of My mercy. If they repent, I am their habīb”—that is, the One who loves them, for Allah loves those who constantly repent and those who purify themselves—“and if they do not repent, I am their physician: I test them with afflictions to expiate their faults.”

Among the lessons in His words, “is from yourself,” is that the servant should neither rely on himself nor feel secure in himself, for evil comes only from himself. Nor should he occupy himself with blaming or condemning people when they wrong him, for that is among the misfortunes that have befallen him, and they have befallen him only because of his sins. So he turns his attention to his sins, seeks forgiveness for them, and seeks refuge

---

## Page 7383

in Allah from the evil of his own soul and his evil deeds. He asks Allah to help him obey Him. Through this, he obtains every good and is protected from every evil.

This is why the most beneficial, greatest, and wisest supplication is that of al-Fātihah:

“Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your wrath, nor those who have gone astray.”

For when Allah guides him to this path, He helps him obey Him and refrain from disobeying Him, so that no evil befalls him in this world or the Hereafter. Yet sins are inseparable from the human soul.

He needs guidance at every moment; indeed, his need for guidance is greater than his need for food and drink. It is not as some Quran commentators maintain: “Allah has already guided him, so why does he ask for guidance?” They hold that asking for guidance means asking for steadfastness or further guidance. Rather, the servant needs his Lord to teach him what to do in the details of his circumstances and in the particular matters that arise each day, and to inspire him to act accordingly. For knowledge alone is not enough unless Allah makes him willing to act upon his knowledge; otherwise,

---

## Page 7384

his knowledge would be evidence against him, and he would not be rightly guided. The servant also needs Allah to enable him to act upon that righteous intention. For he cannot be guided to the straight path—the path of those whom Allah has blessed among the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous—without this knowledge, these intentions, and the ability to act accordingly. This encompasses more kinds of need than can be counted.

This is why people are commanded to offer this supplication in every prayer: their need for it is so great. There is nothing they need more than this supplication. Only someone who reflects on the condition of his own soul and the souls of humans and jinn, and of those commanded to offer this supplication, can appreciate something of its worth. Seeing the ignorance and wrongdoing within souls that lead to their misery in this world and the Hereafter, he realizes that Allah, through His bounty and mercy, has made this supplication one of the greatest means of bringing about good and warding off evil.

Among the things that make this clear is that Allah Most High has not related anyone’s story to us in the Quran

---

## Page 7385

except so that we may learn lessons from it, for we need these lessons and benefit from them. Such lessons can only be drawn when we compare the later case with the earlier one, and both share the grounds that warrant the same judgment. Were there not within people’s souls something of what was in the souls of those who rejected the messengers—Pharaoh and those before him—we would have no need to learn lessons from people whom we do not resemble at all. Rather, the matter is as Allah Most High says:

“Nothing is said to you except what was said to the messengers before you.”

And as He says:

“Likewise, no messenger came to those before them without their saying, ‘A sorcerer or a madman!’”

And He says:

“Those before them said the same as they say; their hearts are alike.”

And He says:

“They imitate the words of those who disbelieved before them.”

This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you, as precisely as one arrow feather matches another, so that even if they entered a lizard’s hole, you would enter it too.”

They asked, “The Jews and the Christians?”

He replied, “Who else?”

He also said:

“My community will surely follow the course of the nations before it, span for span and cubit for cubit.”

He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, the Persians and the Romans?”

He replied, “Who else?”

Both hadiths are recorded in the two Sahīh collections.

---

## Page 7386

“During the campaign of Hunayn, the polytheists had a tree called Dhāt Anwāt, on which they hung their weapons, fastening them to it, and beneath which they took shade, seeking blessings. Some people said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, appoint for us a Dhāt Anwāt just as they have a Dhāt Anwāt.’ He replied, ‘Allah is Most Great! You have said just what the people of Moses said to Moses: “Make for us a god just as they have gods.” These are the same ways! You will surely follow the ways of those before you.’”

The Quran has made clear that evil deeds come from the soul, even though they occur by Allah’s decree. The greatest evil deeds are denying the Creator, associating partners with Him, and the soul’s desire to become His partner and equal, or to become a god besides Him. Both of these have occurred. Pharaoh sought to become a god worshipped instead of Allah Most High. He said:

“I know of no god for you other than myself.”

He also said:

“I am your lord most high.”

And he said to Moses:

“If you take a god other than me, I will surely have you imprisoned.”

“Thus he made fools of his people, and they obeyed him.”

Iblīs seeks to be worshipped and obeyed instead of Allah. He wants himself to be worshipped and obeyed, and Allah neither to be worshipped nor obeyed. What is found in Pharaoh and Iblīs is the utmost injustice and ignorance. Within the souls of all other humans and jinn there is a share of both. Unless help is given

---

## Page 7387

by Allah to His servant, together with His guidance, the servant will fall into some of what Iblīs and Pharaoh fell into, as far as he is able.

One of those endowed with insight said:

“There is no soul that does not contain what Pharaoh’s soul contained. But Pharaoh had the power, so he revealed it; others lacked the power, so they concealed it.”

The explanation is this: when a person reflects, comes to know himself and others, and hears accounts of them, he sees that each wishes to be obeyed and to rise above others as far as his power allows. The soul is filled with a love of supremacy and leadership to the extent possible. Thus, you find a person befriending those who agree with his desires and opposing those who disagree with them.

What he worships is nothing other than what he desires and wants. Allah Most High says:

“Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god? Would you then be a guardian over him?”

In this respect, he regards people just as the unbelieving, polytheistic kings among the Turks and others regard them. They say, “Yā rubā'ī,” meaning “friend and enemy.” Whoever agrees with their desires is an ally, even if he is an unbeliever and a polytheist. Whoever does not agree with their desires is an enemy, even if he is among the God-fearing friends of Allah. This was precisely Pharaoh’s condition.

Each of these people wants his commands to be obeyed as far as he is able, but he

---

## Page 7388

cannot do what Pharaoh was able to do: claim divinity and deny the Creator. Although these people acknowledge the Creator, when someone comes to call them to worship and obey Him—which entails ceasing to obey them—they may oppose him just as Pharaoh opposed Moses.

Many people who possess some reason and faith do not aspire to such an extreme; rather, each seeks for himself only what accords with his own position. If he is a Muslim who commands obedience, he seeks to be obeyed in pursuing his purposes, even when these involve sin and disobedience to Allah. Someone who obeys him in following his desires becomes dearer to him and more highly esteemed than someone who obeys Allah and goes against his desires. This is one aspect of the condition of Pharaoh and the others who rejected the messengers.

If he is a scholar—or a shaykh—he loves those who venerate him rather than those who venerate his peer, even if both read the same book, such as the Quran, or perform the same acts of worship in the same way, such as the five daily prayers. He loves those who venerate him by accepting his statements and following his example more than he loves others. He may even hate his peer and his followers out of envy and wrongful rivalry, as the Jews did when Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, calling to the same message to which Moses had called. Allah Most High says:

“When they are told, ‘Believe in what Allah has revealed,’ they say, ‘We believe in what was revealed to us,’ yet they disbelieve in what came after it, although it is the truth, confirming what they have.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after

---

## Page 7389

the clear proof had come to them.” And Allah Most High says:

“They did not become divided until after knowledge had come to them, out of wrongful rivalry among themselves.”

For this reason, Allah Most High described them in terms similar to those in which He described Pharaoh, and gave others power over them, through whom He exacted retribution from them. He says of Pharaoh:

“Indeed, Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and divided its people into factions, oppressing one group among them, slaughtering their sons and sparing their women. Indeed, he was one of those who spread corruption.”

And He says of them:

“We declared to the Children of Israel in the Scripture: ‘You will surely spread corruption in the land twice, and you will surely become exceedingly arrogant.’”

For this reason, Allah Most High says:

“That abode of the Hereafter We assign to those who seek neither exaltation in the land nor corruption.”

Allah, glorified and exalted is He, created His creatures solely to worship Him: to remember Him, thank Him, and worship Him. He sent the messengers and revealed the Scriptures so that they would worship Allah alone, so that all religion would be devoted to Allah, and so that Allah’s word would be supreme. Every messenger He sent bore this same message. Allah Most High says:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no god worthy of worship but Me, so worship Me.’”

And Allah Most High says:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: ‘Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?’”

Allah commanded all the messengers to uphold this and not to become divided over it. He says:

“Indeed,

---

## Page 7390

this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so worship Me.” And Allah Most High says:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.

And indeed, this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.

But they divided their religion among themselves into sects, each faction rejoicing in what it has.”

Qatādah said: “That is, your religion is one religion, and your Lord is one Lord, though the prescribed laws differ.” Likewise, al-Dahhāk reported that Ibn ‘Abbās explained “Indeed, this religion of yours is one religion” as meaning: “Your religion is one religion.”

Ibn Abī Hātim said: “Similar explanations have been reported from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, Qatādah, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd.”

Al-Hasan said: “He made clear to them what they should avoid and what they should do. Then He said: ‘Indeed, this way of yours is one way.’” This is also the explanation given by the majority of Quran commentators.

The word ummah means a religion or a way, as Allah Most High says:

“They said, ‘We found our forefathers following a way, and we are guided by their footsteps’”—or, “following in their footsteps.”

Similarly, a path is called an imām (guide), because the traveller follows it; likewise, the traveller heads towards it and seeks it out. Ummah also means a teacher of goodness whom people follow, just as an imām is someone whom people follow. Allah made Abraham, peace be upon him, an imām and stated that he “was an ummah.”

---

## Page 7391

And Allah commanded the messengers to be united in their faith and religion, without division. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We prophets share one religion.”

Allah Most High says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: establish the religion and do not be divided in it.”

Thus, all of Allah’s messengers and prophets affirm one another’s truthfulness and do not disagree, despite the differences in their prescribed laws.

Anyone who is obeyed—whether a scholar, a shaykh, a ruler, or a king—and follows the messengers commands what they commanded, calls to what they called to, and loves anyone who calls to the same thing as he does. For Allah loves that, so he loves what Allah Most High loves. His true purpose is that Allah Most High alone should be worshipped and that all religion should be devoted to Allah.

But whoever dislikes having a peer who calls to the same thing seeks to be the one who is obeyed and worshipped. He therefore shares something of the condition of Pharaoh and his like. Whoever seeks to be obeyed instead of Allah is following Pharaoh’s way. And whoever seeks to be obeyed alongside Allah wants people to take others besides Allah as His rivals

---

## Page 7392

whom they love as they love Allah.

Allah, glorified and exalted be He, has commanded that none be worshipped but Him, that all religion be devoted to Him alone, that loyalty and enmity be for His sake, that reliance be placed in Him alone, and that help be sought from Him alone.

The believer who follows the messengers commands people to do what the messengers commanded them, so that all religion may be devoted to Allah, not to himself. If someone else commands the same, he loves and supports him, and rejoices that what he seeks has been achieved.

When he does good to people, he does so only to seek the Face of his Lord, the Most High. He knows that Allah has favoured him by making him one who does good rather than one who does evil. He therefore recognizes that his deeds are for Allah and are accomplished through Allah’s help. This is mentioned in the Opening of the Book, which, as we have explained, all creation needs more than anything else. For this reason, reciting it, unlike the other surahs, has been made obligatory upon them in every prayer. Nothing like it has been revealed in the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, or the Quran. For it contains:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

Thus the believer recognizes that his deeds are for Allah, because he worships Him alone, and that they are accomplished through Allah’s help, because he

---

## Page 7393

seeks help from Him alone. He therefore seeks neither recompense nor thanks from those to whom he has done good, for whatever he has done for them was done only for Allah, as the righteous say:

“We feed you only for the sake of Allah’s Face. We desire neither recompense nor thanks from you.”

Nor does he remind the recipient of his favour or cause him hurt. He knows that Allah has favoured him by enabling him to do good, and that both he and the recipient owe their blessings to Allah. He must therefore thank Allah for easing his way to goodness, while the recipient must thank Allah for providing him with someone who offers him something beneficial, whether provision, knowledge, support, or anything else.

Some people, however, do good to others so that they can remind them of their favours, or so that the recipients will repay their kindness with obedience, reverence, or some other benefit. Such a person may remind the recipient of his favour, saying, “I did such-and-such for you.” This person has neither worshipped Allah nor sought His help; he has neither acted for Allah nor acted through Allah’s help. He is showing off. Allah has rendered void both the charity of those who remind others of their favours and the charity of those who give to be seen. The Most High says:

“O you who believe, do not invalidate your charity with reminders of your generosity or by causing hurt, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by people and does not believe in Allah and the Last Day. His likeness is that of a smooth rock covered with dust: a downpour strikes it and leaves it bare. They can gain nothing from what they have earned. And Allah does not guide the disbelieving people.”

“And the likeness of those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure, with firm conviction in their hearts, is that of a garden on high ground: a downpour strikes it, and it yields twice its produce. Even if no downpour strikes it, a light rain suffices. And Allah sees all that you do.”

---

## Page 7394

Qatādah said concerning “with firm conviction in their hearts”: “Seeking reward in their hearts.” Al-Shaʿbī said: “With certainty and belief in their hearts.” Al-Kalbī said likewise.

It has been said: They give charity willingly, certain of the reward and believing Allah’s promise. They know that what they have given is better for them than what they have kept.

I say: If the giver seeks reward from Allah and believes His promise to him, he seeks from Allah, not from the recipient, and therefore does not remind the recipient of his favour. This is like a man saying to another, “Give your slaves this food, and I will pay you its price”: the latter would not remind the slaves of his favour. This is especially so when the giver knows that Allah has favoured him by enabling him to give.

Section

The Sixth Distinction

The sins of commission with which a servant is afflicted—even though they are created by Allah—are a punishment for his failure to do what Allah created him for and innately disposed him toward. Allah created him only to worship Him alone, without partner, and guided him to the natural disposition. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every child is born upon the natural disposition.”

And the Most High said:

“So set your face toward the religion, inclining to truth—the natural disposition upon which Allah created mankind. There is no altering Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

---

## Page 7395

Thus, when he failed to do what he was created for, innately disposed toward, and commanded to do—namely, knowing Allah alone and worshipping Him alone—he was punished for that by Satan making his acts of associating partners with Allah and committing sins seem appealing to him. The Most High said to Satan:

“Go! Whoever among them follows you, Hell will surely be your recompense—an ample recompense.”

Then, later in the passage:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants.”

The Most High also said:

“Indeed, he has no authority over those who believe and put their trust in their Lord.”

“His authority is only over those who take him as an ally and those who, through him, associate partners with Allah.”

The Most High also said:

“Indeed, when those who fear Allah are touched by a temptation from Satan, they remember, and at once they see clearly.”

“But their brothers draw them further into error, and they do not desist.”

It has thus become clear that sincere devotion to Allah protects against Satan’s domination and against the allegiance to Satan that brings punishment. As the Most High said:

“Thus it was, so that We might turn evil and immorality away from him. Indeed, he was one of Our chosen servants.”

When a servant devotes his religion sincerely to his Lord, this prevents him from acting contrary to that devotion and prevents Satan from causing him to do so. But when he does not devote his religion sincerely to his Lord and fails to do what he was created for and innately disposed toward, he is punished for that. And part of his punishment was:

---

## Page 7396

Satan’s gaining mastery over him until he makes evil deeds seem appealing to him. His being inspired to wickedness was a punishment for his failure to fear Allah.

His failure to perform good deeds is not something that positively exists, such that one could say, “Allah created it.” Rather, it is an absence. Yet he is punished for it because he has failed to fulfil what he was created for and commanded to do. This entails punishment for an absence, but through the commission of evil deeds—not through punishments such as Hellfire and the like, which he deserves after the proof has been established against him.

As explained earlier, there are two views on whether a person is punished merely for the absence of the commanded act. The majority say that he is not punished for it, because it is pure absence. They say that he is punished only for refraining from the act, which is something that positively exists. Another group—including Abū Hāshim—say that he is indeed punished for this absence, meaning that he is punished for it with Hellfire and the like, just as he is punished for committing sins.

What has been presented in this line of reasoning is a middle position: Allah punishes him for this absence through his commission of evil deeds, rather than by punishing him for those deeds. He does not punish him for them until He sends His Messenger to him. If he disobeys the Messenger, he then deserves the full punishment.

Initially, he was punished only with something whose evil he can escape by repenting of it

---

## Page 7397

or because the proof has not been established against him. He is like a child who occupies himself not with what benefits him, but with what causes him harm. Yet no sin is recorded against him until he reaches puberty. Once he reaches puberty, he is punished.

His habitual commission of evil deeds may then become a cause of his disobedience after puberty, yet he is punished only for his own sin. However, he deserves punishment in the usual sense only after the proof has been established against him. As for his engaging in evil deeds, this is punishment for his failure to perform good deeds.

Accordingly, evil is not attributable to Allah in any respect. Although Allah is the Creator of His servants’ deeds, His creation of acts of obedience is a blessing and a mercy. His creation of evil deeds involves wisdom and mercy and is, moreover, an act of justice on His part. He has not wronged people in the least; rather, people have wronged themselves.

Their wrongdoing against themselves is of two kinds: their failure to perform good deeds, which is not attributed to Him; and their commission of evil deeds, which He created as punishment for their neglect of the good deeds for which He created them and which He commanded them to perform. Thus every blessing from Him is grace, and every punishment from Him is justice.

---

## Page 7398

Whoever reflects on the Quran will see that, in most passages where Allah mentions His creation of disbelief and sins, He presents it as recompense for such conduct. As He, the Exalted, says:

“Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam; and whoever He wills to lead astray, He makes his heart tight and constricted, as though he were climbing into the sky. Thus does Allah place defilement upon those who do not believe.”

And He, the Exalted, says:

“When they deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate.”

And He, the Exalted, says:

“But as for the one who withholds, considers himself self-sufficient, and denies the best reward, We will ease him toward hardship.”

In these and similar cases, they undertook deeds through which He punished them for committing what was forbidden and neglecting what was commanded. Those deeds proceeded from them and were created in them only because they had not done what they were created to do. Yet they must necessarily act and exercise their will.

When they did not engage in good deeds, they were moved to engage in evil deeds, as an act of justice from Allah. He placed this where it belonged, in a receptacle susceptible to it—the heart, which cannot remain inactive. If it does not engage in good, it is put to work doing evil. As the saying goes: “If you do not keep your soul occupied, it will keep you occupied.”

When properly understood, this explanation cuts off at the root the arguments of both the denialist Qadariyyah and the advocates of compulsion: those who say that people’s deeds are not created by Allah and regard His creating those deeds and punishing people for them as injustice; and those who say that He created the disbelievers’ disbelief and disobedience and punished them for it without any cause or wisdom.

---

## Page 7399

Thus, when those people are told:

“He caused them to fall into those sins and sealed their hearts only as punishment for their failure to do what He had commanded them. He did not wrong them; rather, they wronged themselves.”

To say, “I wronged him,” means, “I deprived him of his due.” The Exalted says:

“Both gardens yielded their produce and fell short in nothing.”

Many of those people acknowledge that Allah creates deeds in a person as recompense for an earlier deed of his. They also say that He creates the obedience of the obedient person. Thus, they do not dispute the creation of people’s deeds in itself. Rather, they say: “He did not create any sins in the first instance; He created them only as recompense, lest He be unjust.”

We therefore say: “The first sin a person commits is one he himself brought about; Allah did not bring it about. Then whatever comes as recompense for it is brought about by Allah.” Their dispute concerning the creation of deeds concerns only this point. They agree with what we have mentioned, but say: “Allah did not bring about the first sin; rather, the person brings it about, lest recompense for it be unjust.”

What we have explained, however, entails that Allah is the Creator of all things. Nothing, then, comes into being except

---

## Page 7400

by His will and power.

Yet the first sin that has positive existence is itself created. It is a punishment for a person’s failure to do what he was created for and what he ought to have done. This absence of action cannot be attributed to Allah. Nor is it a thing that would fall within the statement:

“Allah is the Creator of all things.”

Of the sins with positive existence that He brings about, the first is a punishment for this failure to act. Subsequent sins may punish the person for something that has occurred, or for persisting in that failure. As long as he does not devote his deeds sincerely to Allah, he continues to associate partners with Him, and Satan continues to have power over him.

Moreover, His singling out those whom He guides—by employing them from the outset in what they were created for, while not so employing another—is an act of His grace and mercy. Hence Allah says:

“Allah singles out for His mercy whom He wills, and Allah is the Possessor of great bounty.”

There is wisdom and mercy in this, which He knows best. Likewise, He has endowed some bodies with strengths not found in others, and the absence of such strength may give rise to actual illnesses. There are other manifestations of His wisdom as well. A proper understanding of this dispels the specious objections raised concerning this subject. And Allah knows best what is correct.

---

## Page 7401

Section

Among the passages that mention punishment for the absence of faith is the statement of Allah Most High:

“And We turn their hearts and eyes away, just as they did not believe in it the first time, and leave them wandering blindly in their transgression.”

This continues His statement:

“And what will make you realize that, even if it came, they would not believe? And We turn their hearts and eyes away…”

He thus explains that this turning of their hearts occurred only because they did not believe in it the first time. This is the absence of faith.

But it may be said: this occurred only after the Messenger had called them, and they had abandoned faith and denied the Messenger. These are things with positive existence. Yet what warrants punishment is the absence of faith. What has been mentioned is a condition for punishment, comparable to the sending of the Messenger. A person may be distracted from faith by activities of a permissible kind—eating, drinking, trading, travelling, and so forth. He deserves punishment for activities of this kind only because they distract him from the faith obligatory upon him.

Some people say: “The opposite of faith is abandoning it. It is something with positive existence, and has no opposite other than that.”

---

## Page 7402

Section

The Seventh Distinction

The seventh distinction concerning good and evil—which encompass both deeds and recompense—and why evil is attributed to the self and good to Allah is this:

The evils that befall a person—the afflictions of this world and the Hereafter—have no cause other than his sin, which originates in his own self. Their cause is thus confined to his own self.

As for the good and blessings that come to him, their causes are not so limited. They come from Allah’s bounty and kindness, through a person’s deeds or without them. His deeds themselves are among Allah’s blessings upon him. Glorified is He, Allah does not reward him merely in proportion to his deeds, but multiplies his reward. The servant cannot fully account for the causes of these blessings, but he knows that they come from Allah’s bounty and generosity. He therefore turns to Allah for them, placing his hope in Allah alone and relying on Him alone. He knows that all blessings come from Allah and that everything He has created is a blessing, as explained earlier. He therefore deserves the absolute, all-encompassing and complete gratitude that none besides Him deserves.

There is also gratitude offered in return for the good facilitated through a person

---

## Page 7403

—such as gratitude to one’s parents and to others who have shown one kindness. For “Whoever does not thank people does not thank Allah.” Yet no one’s rights or favors warrant thanking or obeying them through disobedience to Allah. Allah is the One who bestows the immense blessings that no created being can provide, and even the favors bestowed by created beings come from Him. The Most High says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

And the Most High says:

“He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all from Him.”

Moreover, no one can give recompense for obedience, disobedience, and disbelief as He, glorified is He, does. Thus, it is not permissible to obey a created being in disobedience to the Creator. As the Most High says:

“We have enjoined upon man kindness to his parents. But if they strive to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them.”

And He says in the other verse:

“But if they strive to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them. Yet keep their company in this world with kindness, and follow the way of those who turn to Me.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“A Muslim must listen and obey in hardship and ease, whether willing or reluctant, unless commanded to commit a sin. If commanded to commit a sin, he must neither listen nor obey.”

Both Sahih collections report that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Obedience is only in what is right.”

He also said:

“Whoever commands you to disobey Allah, do not obey him.”

And he said:

“No created being may be obeyed in disobedience to the Creator.”

---

## Page 7404

This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is that when a person recognizes that all blessings come from Allah and that none but Allah can bring them about—for He alone brings good and removes ill—and that:

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release thereafter,”

he directs his reliance, hope, and supplication to the Creator alone. Likewise, when he knows the gratitude that Allah deserves—which no one else deserves—his recognition that good things come from Allah leads him to sincerity in thanking Allah and relying upon Him.

To say that these good things come from the person himself would be mistaken, for some of them involve no contribution from his actions. Even where his actions do contribute, Allah is the One who bestows that blessing. There is no power or strength except through Allah, and there is neither refuge nor escape from Him except with Him.

He also recognizes that the cause of evil lies solely within his own soul. He thus understands this clearly and knows where he is vulnerable. He asks his Lord’s forgiveness for what he has done and repents, and he seeks Allah’s help and refuge in Him against wrongdoing he has not yet committed. As one of the early generations said:

“Let no servant hope in anyone but his Lord, and let no servant fear anything but his sin.”

---

## Page 7405

This contradicts the view of the Jahmiyyah and their followers, who say that Allah punishes without any sin having been committed, and subjects the children of unbelievers and others to perpetual, everlasting punishment without their having committed any sin.

They maintain that a person should fear Allah unconditionally, whether or not he has committed any sin. They liken fear of Him to fear of a lion or of a domineering king whose actions and use of force are governed by no rule, and who may subjugate and punish subjects who have committed no offence.

But when a servant believes Allah’s words,

“Whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

he recognizes the falsehood of this view and knows that Allah chastises and punishes him only for his sins. Indeed, all the calamities that befall a servant are due to his sins. The statement of the early generations—including Ibn ʿAbbās and others—has already been mentioned: the distress and loss of resolve that afflicted them on the day of Uhud were due only to their sins. No one made any exception to this. This is one benefit of addressing the individual specifically: it prevents the statement from being understood as a general one subject to exceptions.

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is reported to have said:

“No believer is afflicted by illness, fatigue, worry, sorrow, or distress—even the prick of a thorn—without Allah expiating some of his sins through it.”

---

## Page 7406

Section

The Eighth Distinction

This concerns the case in which evil comes from the soul. Evil is foul and blameworthy, and Allah describes it as foul in such verses as:

“Foul things are for foul people, and foul people are for foul things.”

The majority of the early generations said: “Foul words are for foul people.” Some of them said: “Foul words and deeds are for foul people.” Allah Most High also says:

“Allah sets forth the example of a good word,”

and:

“And the example of a foul word.”

Allah also says:

“To Him good words ascend, and righteous deeds raise them up.”

Words and deeds are attributes of the one who speaks and acts. Thus, if the soul is characterized by evil and foulness, the person in whom it resides benefits only from what is suited to it.

Anyone who seeks to have snakes and scorpions live among people like cats will not succeed.

---

## Page 7407

And anyone who seeks to appoint a liar as a witness concerning people will not succeed. Likewise, anyone who seeks to make an ignorant person a teacher and issuer of religious rulings for people, an incapable coward a fighter defending them, or a fool who knows nothing a governor over people or a keeper of animals—such things bring corruption into the world. They may even be impossible, like trying to make stones float on the surface of water like ships or rise into the sky like the wind, and other such things.

Foul souls are therefore unfit to dwell in pure Paradise, where no foulness exists. That would cause corruption or be impossible. Rather, if a soul contains foulness, it is purified and refined until it becomes fit to dwell in Paradise. Thus, in the Sahīh, Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, may Allah be pleased with him, narrates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the believers have escaped the Fire—that is, crossed the Bridge—they will stop on a bridge between Paradise and the Fire. Redress will then be exacted from some on behalf of others for wrongs committed between them in this world. Once they have been refined and purified, they will be permitted to enter Paradise.”

---

## Page 7408

This is among the reports narrated by al-Bukhārī from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The believers will escape the Fire and be held on a bridge between Paradise and the Fire. Redress will then be exacted from some on behalf of others for wrongs committed between them in this world. Once they have been refined and purified, they will be permitted to enter Paradise. By Him in whose Hand is Muhammad’s soul, each of them will know the way to his dwelling in Paradise better than he knew the way to his dwelling in this world.”

Refinement means purification, just as gold is refined to remove its impurities. It is thus clear that the believers enter Paradise only after being refined and purified of their remaining sins. What, then, of someone who has no good deeds by which to cross the Bridge? Furthermore, if its cause persists, so does the recompense. Good, by contrast, comes from the bounty of the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all, the Everlasting, the First and the Last. Its cause is therefore everlasting, and it endures as long as its cause endures.

When a person knows that evil comes from himself, he does not expect perfect happiness while the evil within him remains. Rather, he recognizes the truth of the Most High’s words:

“Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it.”

And His words:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it.”

“And whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

---

## Page 7409

and knows that the Lord is All-Knowing, Forbearing, Merciful and Just, and that His actions follow the principles of justice and kindness. Every blessing from Him is grace, and every punishment from Him is justice. In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah’s right hand is full; no expenditure diminishes it, ever giving night and day. Have you considered how much He has spent since He created the heavens and the earth? It has not diminished what is in His right hand. In His other hand are the scales, which He lowers and raises.”

The falsity of the position of the Jahmiyyah also becomes clear: they regard reward and punishment as having no basis in wisdom or justice, or in putting things in their proper places. Thus they describe the Lord in terms that imply injustice and foolishness. Yet He, glorified be He, has borne witness:

“that there is no deity except Him—as have the angels and those endowed with knowledge—upholding justice. There is no deity except Him, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

For this reason, they say: “We do not know what He will do with those who have committed evil deeds.” Indeed, they consider it possible that He might pardon them all, or punish them all. They also consider it possible that He might punish some and forgive others without weighing their deeds—even pardoning the worst of people while punishing the best for a minor sin and not forgiving it.

They also say that a sin is not erased, whether through repentance, good deeds that erase sins, or anything else. In this regard, they may make no distinction between minor and major sins.

---

## Page 7410

They said: “This is because all of this can be known only through revelation and reports from Allah and His Messenger.”

They also said: “There is nothing in the Quran and Sunnah that clarifies what Allah will do with those who have committed sins other than disbelief.” They interpreted His saying, Most High:

“If you avoid the major sins you are forbidden, We will expiate your lesser sins,”

as allowing that “major sins” may mean disbelief alone, as in His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him.”

These matters were mentioned by Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī and others who hold such views and follow the approach of Jahm ibn Safwān regarding divine decree and the threat of punishment. They intended thereby to oppose the Mu'tazilah on these two matters.

The Mu'tazilah maintained that Allah did not create people’s actions, that He wills things that do not occur, and that things occur which He does not will. In this, they followed the approach of those who deny divine decree. Regarding the threat of punishment, they adopted a position similar to that of the Khawārij. They said: “Whoever enters the Fire will never leave it, whether through intercession or otherwise; rather, his punishment will be everlasting.” Thus, according to them, Allah will never have mercy on someone who commits a major sin, or whose evil deeds outweigh his good deeds; rather, He will cause him to remain in the Fire forever.

In what they said concerning divine decree, they contradicted the Sunnah transmitted through numerous independent chains and the consensus of the Companions. Jahm opposed them on both matters, and these people followed Jahm’s approach, despite professing affiliation with the people of the Sunnah and hadith

---

## Page 7411

and the followers of the early generations. Likewise, concerning faith and the threat of punishment, they followed the approach of the extremist Murji’ah, such as Jahm and his followers.

Jahm became notorious for two kinds of innovation:

One concerned the divine names and attributes. He went to extremes in denying both the names and the attributes. The heretics among the Bātiniyyah, the philosophers, and others like them agreed with him on this. The Mu'tazilah agreed with him in denying the attributes, but not the names. The Kullābiyyah—along with those of the Sālimiyyah who agreed with them, and the jurists, hadith scholars, and Sufis who followed their approach—agreed with him in denying the attributes connected with Allah’s will, without denying the attributes altogether.

The Karrāmiyyah and others like them agreed with him on the underlying principle: that an infinite succession cannot continue indefinitely, and that it is impossible for Allah to have always spoken whenever He wills and done whatever He wills whenever He wills, because a succession of events without a beginning is impossible. On the basis of this principle—namely, denying the existence of anything endless in the future—Jahm held that Paradise and the Fire would cease to exist.

Abū al-Hudhayl, a leading authority of the Mu'tazilah, agreed with him on this, but formulated his position in terms of movements coming to an end.

Thus, concerning the divine attributes, the Mu'tazilah are the effeminate counterparts of the Jahmiyyah.

---

## Page 7412

As for the Kullābiyyah, they affirm the attributes in general. The Ash'arīs do likewise, but—as Shaykh Abū Ismā'īl al-Ansārī said—they are the female Jahmiyyah. They are the effeminate counterparts of the Mu'tazilah.

Some say that the Mu'tazilah are the effeminate counterparts of the philosophers. Al-Ash'arī and others have mentioned this. This is either because those who said it did not know that Jahm had preceded them in adopting this principle, or because they are the philosophers’ effeminate counterparts in certain respects. Otherwise, their differences with the philosophers are very substantial indeed.

According to al-Shahrastānī’s account of their leading scholars, what they adopted came from the philosophers. This is because al-Shahrastānī has in view only the debates of his Ash'arī colleagues with the Mu'tazilah concerning the attributes and similar matters. By contrast, the imams of the Sunnah and hadith debated the Jahmiyyah, who were well known among the early generations and the Muslim community for denying the attributes.

The early generations called those who denied and negated the attributes the Jahmiyyah. This was the distinguishing feature by which they set them apart from all other groups.

As for the Mu'tazilah, they were distinguished by their doctrine of a position between the two positions, which 'Amr ibn 'Ubayd introduced. He and his companions would sit apart from the gathering, so Qatādah and others would say, “Those are the Mu'tazilah.” This was after the death of al-Hasan al-Basrī, in the early second century AH.

---

## Page 7413

After them, the Jahmiyyah emerged.

As for the doctrine concerning divine decree, its adherents had appeared earlier, during the caliphate of ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, after Mu‘āwiyah’s death. This is why Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās—may Allah be pleased with them—and others spoke against them. Ibn ‘Abbās died before Ibn al-Zubayr, while Ibn ‘Umar died shortly after him. Soon afterward, al-Hajjāj assumed authority over Iraq, sometime in the seventies AH.

People continued debating divine decree in the Hijaz, Syria, and Iraq. Most of this took place in Syria and in Basra, in Iraq, while the least occurred in the Hijaz. Then the Mu‘tazilah emerged after al-Hasan’s death, and the position between the two positions came to be discussed. They maintained that the threatened punishment would be carried out, that monotheists would remain eternally in the Fire, and that no one who entered it would leave. This was a harsh stance toward sinners. To this they added their doctrine concerning divine decree, for it completed their harsh stance toward sinners.

At that time, people had not yet introduced any denial of the divine attributes. This remained the case until al-Ja‘d ibn Dirham appeared; he was the first of them. Khālid ibn ‘Abd Allāh al-Qasrī sacrificed him, saying:

“O people, offer your sacrifices. May Allah accept your sacrifices, for I am offering al-Ja‘d ibn Dirham as a sacrifice. He claimed that Allah did not take Abraham as a close friend

---

## Page 7414

and did not speak directly to Moses. Exalted is Allah far above what al-Ja‘d says!” Then he descended and slaughtered him. This took place in Iraq. Later, Jahm ibn Safwān emerged in the East, in Tirmidh, and from there Jahm’s doctrine spread.

For this reason, the scholars of the Sunnah and hadith in the East—such as Ibrāhīm ibn Tahmān, Khārijah ibn Mus‘ab, ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, and their peers—spoke more extensively in refutation of Jahm’s doctrine than those in the Hijaz, Syria, and Iraq. Ibn al-Mājishūn and others also condemned them, as did al-Awzā‘ī, Hammād ibn Zayd, and others.

Their doctrine became widely known only from the time of the ordeal of Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other scholars of the Sunnah. Their strength and numbers had grown under al-Ma’mūn’s rule, for he had spent some time in Khurāsān and met with them. Then, in 218 AH, he issued written orders from Tarsus for the inquisition. He died that same year. Ahmad ibn Hanbal was returned to prison in Baghdad, where he remained until 220 AH. That year witnessed his ordeal under al-Mu‘tasim and his theological debate with them.

When he refuted the arguments they advanced against him, explaining that none of them provided any proof, and that their demand that people agree with them, and their testing people on this basis, constituted ignorance and injustice, al-Mu‘tasim wished to release him. Some, however, advised him that the best course was

---

## Page 7415

to flog him so that the authority of the caliphate would not be undermined time and again. When they flogged him, public condemnation arose against them, and they feared unrest, so they released him.

Ahmad ibn Abī Du’ād had assembled against him people from every faction who denied the divine attributes and held that the Quran was created. Among those he assembled was Abū ‘Īsā Muhammad ibn ‘Īsā Burghūth, a leading figure of the Najjāriyyah, the followers of Husayn al-Najjār. The imams of the Sunnah—such as Ibn al-Mubārak, Ahmad, Ishāq, al-Bukhārī, and others—called all these people Jahmiyyah.

Many later scholars, including followers of Ahmad and others, came to suppose that his opponents were the Mu‘tazilites. They also suppose that Bishr ibn Ghiyāth al-Marīsī—although he had died before Ahmad’s ordeal—Ibn Abī Du’ād, and others like them were Mu‘tazilites. This was not so. Rather, the Mu‘tazilites were only one group among all those who held that the Quran was created.

The Jahmiyyah, followers of Jahm; the Najjāriyyah, followers of Husayn al-Najjār; the Dirāriyyah, followers of Dirār ibn ‘Amr; and the Mu‘tazilites all held that the Quran was created. A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that Jahm became known for two kinds of religious innovation. The first was:

---

## Page 7416

denial of the divine attributes. The second was extremism regarding divine decree and irjāʾ. He reduced faith to mere knowledge in the heart and held that human beings neither act nor possess any power. On both these matters, the Mu‘tazilites went to the opposite extreme in opposing him.

As for al-Ash‘arī, he agreed with the basic premise of Jahm’s position, though he might dispute with him over terminology. Jahm affirmed none of the divine attributes—neither will nor any other. Thus, when he said that Allah loves acts of obedience and hates acts of disobedience, what he meant was reward and punishment.

Al-Ash‘arī, however, affirmed attributes such as will. He therefore had to address whether will is the same as love, and whether Allah loves acts of disobedience. He said that Allah loves acts of disobedience and is pleased with them, just as He wills them.

Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī stated that al-Ash‘arī was the first to say this, and that Ahl al-Sunnah before him used to say that Allah does not love acts of disobedience. Al-Ash‘arī, however, mentioned in al-Mūjaz that a group of people whom he named had said this before him. I am uncertain about some of them.

---

## Page 7417

This view became widespread among many Sufis and masters of gnosis and spiritual realization. They thus came to agree with Jahm on questions of human acts and divine decree, despite declaring him an unbeliever over questions of the divine attributes. One example is Abū Ismā‘īl al-Ansārī al-Harawī, author of Dhamm al-Kalām (Censure of Speculative Theology). He was among the fiercest critics of the Jahmiyya for their denial of the divine attributes, and wrote a book entitled Takfīr al-Jahmiyya (Declaring the Jahmiyya Unbelievers). He also vehemently censured the Ash‘arīs, although they are among the closest of these groups to the Sunnah and hadith. At times, he would even curse them.

Someone once asked him in the presence of Nizām al-Mulk, “Do you curse the Ash‘arīs?” He replied, “I curse anyone who says that there is no God in the heavens, no Quran in the Quranic codex, and no prophet in the grave.” Then he rose and left in anger.

Yet on the question of Allah’s willing all that occurs and creating human acts, he goes even further than the Ash‘arīs. He affirms neither causes nor wisdom. Rather, he says that when the gnostic contemplates the divine decree, he no longer approves of a good deed or disapproves of an evil one.

By “the decree,” he means the divine will. For him, the gnostic who has attained true realization is one who reaches the station of fanā (self-effacement), becoming oblivious to all his own desires through absorption in what the Real wills. Everything that occurs is willed by Him, and this is what he means by “the decree.” A “good deed” and an “evil deed” differ in their consequences for the servant himself: through one he enjoys bliss, and through the other he suffers punishment. Paying attention to this belongs to the self’s own interests, whereas in the station of self-effacement there is nothing but contemplation of what the Real wills.

---

## Page 7418

This question arose in al-Junayd’s time, as has been mentioned elsewhere. Al-Junayd explained to them the second distinction: while contemplating the all-encompassing divine will, they must also recognize the distinction between what Allah commands and what He forbids—that is, between what He loves and what He hates.

Al-Junayd made this clear to them, as in his statement concerning divine oneness:
“It is to distinguish what has come into being from what has always existed.”

Those Sufis and masters of gnosis who follow al-Junayd’s path are rightly guided, attain salvation, and find felicity. But whoever does not follow his path concerning divine decree, and instead treats everything alike, must consequently make no distinction between good deeds and evil deeds, or between prophets and the wicked. He therefore does not say that Allah loves these people and these deeds, nor that He hates those people and those deeds. Rather, Allah loves all that occurs just as He wills it, as al-Ash‘arī maintained.

The only distinction is that some are granted bliss while others are punished. By affirming this distinction with respect to created beings, al-Ash‘arī was more reasonable than they were. For they claim that the gnostic who reaches the station of self-effacement makes no distinction between the two.

---

## Page 7419

They have erred concerning both the servant and the Lord.

As regards the servant, their position requires him to regard all occurrences alike. This is unquestionably impossible. They may experience states in which they lose awareness of most things. But losing awareness of everything is impossible, for every living being must distinguish between what causes it pain and what gives it pleasure. Thus, it distinguishes between bread and dust, and between water and other drinks.

These people have discarded the distinction established by the revealed Law, grounded in faith and ordained by the Most Merciful—the distinction by which Allah differentiates His allies from His enemies. They imagine that they maintain an all-encompassing view of divine decree.

Accordingly, it is inherently impossible for the servant to regard all occurrences alike. Rather, he must make distinctions. If he does not distinguish according to the revealed Law—between what Allah loves and what He hates, and between what pleases Him and what angers Him—then he will distinguish according to his natural inclinations, following his desires and his devil. He will therefore love whatever his soul desires and whatever his devil commands.

Consequently, many of them have fallen into sins, others into flagrant disobedience, and still others into disbelief, to the point of permitting the worship of idols. Many of them then go on to profess the unity of existence. These are the ones who opposed

---

## Page 7420

al-Junayd and the leading scholars of the religion concerning monotheism. They failed to distinguish between the Eternal and the created. These people explicitly professed the worship of everything that exists, as has been discussed at length elsewhere. This is the doctrine of the proponents of the unity of existence, such as Ibn ʿArabī al-Hātimī, Ibn Sabʿīn, al-Qūnawī, al-Tilimsānī, al-Balyānī, Ibn al-Fārid, and their like.

Our purpose here is to discuss those speculative theologians and Sufis who deny judgment, justice, and causation in divine decree, agreeing with Jahm on this principle. This is the second innovation for which he became known, unlike irjā’, which is also attributed to groups other than his.

These people say that the Lord may do anything within His power that is possible, without regard to wisdom, mercy, or justice. They also say that His will is His love.

Consequently, you find that their followers do not revere the commands and prohibitions, or the promises and warnings. Rather, such a follower casts off all or some of the commands of the revealed Law, or forces himself to act upon what he believes or knows.

For what they mean is that everything is alike in relation to the Lord, and that whatever He wills, He loves. They also mean that He brings things into existence without causes through which He creates them, or any wise purpose toward which He directs them. Rather, He does no more than bring decreed events to their appointed times.

---

## Page 7421

For them, no real distinction remains between what is commanded and what is forbidden. Rather, they agree with Jahm and those who adopt his position—such as al-Ashʿarī—that nothing is good or bad in itself. Goodness and badness consist solely in a thing’s being commanded or forbidden. This distinction concerns the servant’s own interests, whereas these people claim to have effaced all concern for personal interests.

Sometimes they say that observing the commands and prohibitions belongs to the station of talbīs (disguise), or something similar, as appears in the writings of Abū Ismāʿīl al-Harawī, author of Manāzil al-sāʾirīn (The Stations of the Wayfarers). At other times they say that one does this for the inmates of the madhouse—meaning ordinary people—as Shaykh al-Maghribī says. There are other forms of this approach that this is not the place to discuss at length.

Even when someone following their path holds the commands and prohibitions in high regard, the most he can say is what is reported from al-Shādhilī:

“Let your heart witness unity, while your tongue expresses distinction.”

Thus, his writings and those of others contain statements, supplications, and devotional litanies that entail setting aside the commands and prohibitions. For example, he may pray that Allah grant him something greater when he disobeys Him than He grants him when he obeys Him, and other such supplications implying that, in his view, it is permissible for Him to make

---

## Page 7422

those who commit evil like those who believe and do righteous deeds—indeed, even better than them. They offer supplications that overstep proper bounds, as found in al-Shādhilī’s litany. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Others among the common folk of these people consider it possible for Allah to honour someone wicked—even an unbeliever—with the miraculous gifts granted to the greatest of His allies. They say, “This is a gift and a bounty that Allah bestows upon whomever He wills. It has nothing to do with prayer or fasting.” They imagine these to be miraculous gifts bestowed upon Allah’s allies, whereas their supposed miracles are satanic states like those experienced by sorcerers and soothsayers. Allah Most High says:

“And when a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them, a group of those who had been given the Scripture cast the Book of Allah behind their backs, as though they did not know.”

“They followed what the devils recited during Solomon’s reign. Solomon did not disbelieve, but the devils disbelieved, teaching people magic and what was revealed to the two angels in Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you, matching them exactly, so that even if they entered a lizard’s hole, you would enter it too.”

As for the Muslims to whom Allah’s Book, the Quran, has come, many of them—people professing Islam whom Satan has led astray—have instead cast the Book of Allah

---

## Page 7423

behind their backs and followed what the devils recite. They neither respect the Quran’s commands and prohibitions, nor ally themselves with those whom the Quran commands them to support, nor oppose those whom it commands them to oppose. Instead, they venerate anyone they see performing extraordinary feats like those performed by sorcerers and soothsayers with the aid of devils. Such feats are accomplished through what the devils recite.

Some of them know that this comes from Satan, yet glorify it to satisfy their desires and prefer it to the way of the Quran, seeking thereby to gain the veneration of the common people. These are unbelievers, like those of whom Allah Most High says:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false deities and say of the unbelievers, ‘These are better guided than the believers’?”

“Those are the ones whom Allah has cursed. And whoever Allah curses—you will never find any helper for him.”

These people resemble the unbelievers of whom Allah Most High says:

“And when a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them, a group of those who had been given the Scripture cast the Book of Allah behind their backs, as though they did not know.”

“They followed what the devils recited during Solomon’s reign. Solomon did not disbelieve, but the devils disbelieved”—to the end of the verse.

Others among them do not know that this comes from the devils. Groups among the practitioners of speculative theology, people of learning, and people of

---

## Page 7424

worship and Sufism may fall into such things. They have even deemed the worship of celestial bodies and idols permissible because of the extraordinary phenomena they have witnessed in connection with them—phenomena that devils help them bring about. Through these, they achieve some of their aims involving wrongdoing and obscenities. So long as they attain these ends, they care nothing about associating partners with Allah or disbelieving in Him and His Book. Nor do they scruple to teach this to people and have them venerate them, for the authority or wealth they stand to gain.

Even if they know that it is disbelief and associating partners with Allah, they practise it and call others to it. Rather, they have developed doubt and misgivings about what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought, or have come to believe that the Messenger addressed the masses with things that have no underlying reality, for the benefit of the masses. Such is the claim made by some philosophizers, heretics, and esotericists. Some from both of the aforementioned groups have adopted these views.

In this they have imitated the Persians, the Romans, and others. The Persians venerated lights and prostrated themselves before the sun and fire. Before Christianity, the Romans were polytheists who worshipped celestial bodies and idols. Those who have thus resembled the Persians and Romans are worse than those who have resembled the Jews and Christians. The latter imitated the People of the Book in matters that had been altered or abrogated, whereas the former imitated those without a revealed scripture: the Magians and polytheists among the Persians and Romans, and those among the Indians and Greeks who followed the same path.

The doctrine of the heretical esotericists is derived from the Magian belief in two primal principles

---

## Page 7425

and from the Greek philosophers’ doctrine of intellects and souls.

The Magian doctrine ultimately rests on identifying the darkness that rivals light with Iblīs, together with the philosophers’ doctrine of the soul. The root of evil, then, is worshipping the self and Satan, making them partners of the Lord and regarding them as His equals. The human soul commits evil at Satan’s command. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught Abū Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, to say—in the morning, in the evening, and when going to bed:

“O Allah, Lord of Gabriel, Michael, and Isrāfīl, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen! You judge between Your servants concerning that over which they differed. Guide me, by Your permission, to the truth concerning which they have differed. Indeed, You guide whom You will to a straight path.”

This is part of fully realizing the meaning of Allah’s words:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

alongside His words:

“You will have no authority over My servants, except those among the wayward who follow you,”

and His words:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

The self’s claim to divinity manifested itself in Pharaoh and others like him who claimed to be gods alongside Allah or apart from Him. It also manifested itself among those who claimed divinity for a human being alongside Allah, such as the Messiah and others.

---

## Page 7426

And polytheism among the children of Adam began with associating revered, righteous people with Allah. When these people died, others kept vigil at their graves, then made statues of them, and finally worshipped them. This was the first polytheism among the children of Adam, and it arose among Noah’s people. He was the first messenger sent to the people of the earth, calling them to worship Allah alone and forbidding them to associate partners with Him. As Allah Most High says:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

“And they have already led many astray.”

These were the names of righteous men among Noah’s people. When they died, people fashioned idols in their likenesses. These idols disappeared when Allah drowned the inhabitants of the earth, then later passed to the Arabs, as Ibn ‘Abbās and others mentioned. If they were not the very same idols, they were their counterparts.

As for associating Satan with Allah, this is widespread. Unless people believe that “there is no god but Allah”—meaning that He alone deserves worship, to the exclusion of everything else; that He loves to be worshipped and has commanded that He be worshipped; and that He is to be worshipped only through what He loves of the obligatory and recommended acts He has prescribed—they will inevitably fall into polytheism and other wrongdoing. Thus, those who regarded all words and deeds as equal in relation to Allah, holding that He does not love

---

## Page 7427

one thing over another—so that He makes no distinction between someone who worships Him alone, associating nothing with Him, and someone who worships other gods alongside Him—and who made everything contingent upon a will devoid of wisdom, mercy, or justice, which makes no distinction between good and evil deeds, became eager to attain whatever they desired without obeying Allah and His Messenger.

Then, when they accepted the possibility of miraculous gifts for anyone claiming righteousness, without requiring righteousness to be grounded in sound knowledge, sincere faith, and piety, but instead made these extraordinary feats the sign of righteousness, admitted such feats without restriction, related accounts of spiritual unveilings in this connection, and made reprehensible statements, some of them said:

“A saint is granted the power to say, ‘Be!’”

Others said: “It is not impossible for a saint to do anything possible, just as it is not impossible for Allah Most High to do what is impossible.” This was said by Ibn ‘Arabī and those who followed him.

They said: “What is intrinsically impossible can be accomplished.” In their view, nothing can be said to lie beyond a saint’s power—not even bringing two contraries together, or anything else.

Ibn ‘Arabī further asserted that nothing possible lies beyond a saint’s power. Yet the One beyond whose power nothing possible lies is Allah alone.

This is an explicit declaration on their part that a saint is like Allah, if not Allah Himself.

---

## Page 7428

Some of them explicitly declared that a saint knows everything Allah knows and can do everything Allah can do. They claimed that the Prophet had possessed this, and that it then passed to al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī, and from al-Hasan to his descendants, one after another, until it reached Abū al-Hasan al-Shādhilī, and then his son. One of their leading adherents told me this directly.

A trustworthy man among their prominent figures told me that they say: “Muhammad is Allah.”

One of the shaykhs who had spiritual training and experience told me that he and Ibn Hūd were in Mecca and entered the Kaaba. Pointing to the middle of the Kaaba, Ibn Hūd said to him: “This is where the first light descended.”

He then asked him: “If the Master of this House were to say to you, ‘I want to make you a god,’ what would you say to Him?”

The shaykh said: “My hair stood on end at these words, and I recoiled”—or words to that effect.

Some people relate that when the Zanj entered Basra, Sahl ibn ‘Abd Allāh was told about it. He said:

“Ah! Were certain people in this city of yours to ask Allah to remove the mountains from their places, He would remove them. And were they to ask Him

---

## Page 7429

not to bring about the Hour, He would not bring it about. But they know what pleases Him, so they ask Him only for what He loves.”

This account is either falsely attributed to Sahl—which is what we would prefer to be the case—or reflects an error on his part. There is no might or power except through Allah.

For whatever Allah has declared will happen must happen. Even if all the inhabitants of the heavens and the earth asked Him that it not happen, He would not grant their request—as when asking Him not to bring about the Hour, or not to fill Hell with jinn and mankind together, and other such things. Indeed, whatever Allah knows will happen, He will not grant anyone’s supplication that it should not happen.

Supplication, however, is a means by which Allah brings about what He knows will occur through that means, just as He brings about through all other means what He knows will occur through them. People far superior to anyone in Basra have asked Allah Most High for less than this, yet their requests were not granted, because the contrary had already been decreed. Thus Abraham, peace and blessings be upon him, asked Him to forgive his father. Likewise, Noah, peace be upon him, asked Him to save his son, but was told:

“O Noah, he is not of your family; indeed, he is of unrighteous conduct. So do not ask Me about that of which you have no knowledge.”

And the best of creation, Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was told concerning his uncle Abū

---

## Page 7430

Tālib:

“It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to seek forgiveness for the polytheists, even if they are close relatives.”

And he was told concerning the hypocrites:

“It is all the same for them whether you ask forgiveness for them or do not ask forgiveness for them; Allah will never forgive them.”

Allah Most High has also said in general terms:

“Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

And He said:

“Intercession will not benefit anyone with Him except those to whom He gives permission.”

Who, then, is this person who, if he asked Allah for whatever he himself wished, would be granted it?

“Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the foremost intercessor on the Day of Resurrection, related that he would prostrate beneath the Throne, praise his Lord and extol Him. He would then be told: ‘O Muhammad, raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.’ He said: ‘He then sets a limit for me, and I admit them to Paradise.’”

Allah Most High has said:

“Call upon your Lord humbly and privately. Indeed, He does not love transgressors.”

What transgression could be greater or more outrageous than for a servant to ask his Lord not to do what He has declared He will certainly do, or to do what He has declared He will not do? Yet He, glorified be He, is as He has described Himself:

“And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the supplication of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.”

And He said:

“And your Lord says, ‘Call upon Me; I will respond to you. Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, humiliated.’”

In the two Sahih collections, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “There is no

---

## Page 7431

supplicant who calls upon Allah with a supplication involving neither wrongdoing nor severing ties of kinship without Allah granting him one of three things in return: either He promptly grants his request, stores up an equivalent good for him, or averts an equivalent evil from him.”

Thus, a supplication involving no transgression brings about what is sought or its equivalent. This is the most complete response. The particular thing requested may be impossible, or it may be harmful to the supplicant or someone else. The supplicant lacks knowledge and does not know what would harm him. Yet the Lord is near and responsive, and He is more merciful to His servants than a mother is to her child.

When the Generous, the Merciful, is asked for something specific and knows that granting it would not be good for the servant, He gives him its equivalent. This is like a father whose child asks him for something that is not his: he gives him its equivalent from his own wealth. And to Allah belongs the highest comparison.

Likewise, when some of the Prophet’s paternal cousins asked him to appoint them to an office that was not appropriate for them, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave them enough from the one-fifth share to leave them without need of that appointment, and arranged their marriages. This is what he did for al-Fadl ibn ‘Abbās and Rabī‘ah ibn al-Hārith ibn ‘Abd al-Muttalib.

It has also been reported in a hadith:

“Nothing is more honourable in Allah’s sight than supplication.”

And this is true.

---

## Page 7432

Section

Since matters are as Allah states in His words:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself,”

it follows that the servant must seek good things—which include every blessing—from Allah alone, and recognize that they come from Allah alone. Allah therefore deserves thanks for them that no one else deserves. The servant must also know that none is worthy of worship but Him. As Allah Most High says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

This obliges the servant to thank Him and worship Him alone. Then He says:
