from bowing, he would say: ‘O Allah, our Lord, to You belongs all praise, filling the heavens, filling the earth, and filling whatever else You will. O You who are worthy of praise and glory! The truest words a servant can say—and we are all Your servants—are: None can withhold what You give, and none can give what You withhold, nor can anyone’s worldly fortune avail him against You.’”

Muslim also related from Abd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā, may Allah be pleased with him, who said: “Whenever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, raised his head from bowing, he would say: ‘Allah hears whoever praises Him. O Allah, our Lord, to You belongs all praise, filling the heavens, filling the earth, and filling whatever else You will. O Allah, purify me with snow, hail, and cold water. O Allah, purify me of sins and misdeeds as a white garment is cleansed of dirt.’”

Muslim also related in his Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say: “O Allah, to You belongs all praise,” and that he said: “And filling the earth, and filling all that lies between them.”

This was not mentioned in some narrations, because “the heavens and the earth” may refer generally to what is above and what is below. This includes the air and other things, since the air is above what lies beneath it and below what lies above it. It may therefore be counted as part of the sky, just as clouds and a roof may be called “sky.” Likewise, He said in the Quran:

“It is He who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then rose above the Throne.”

---

## Page 7480

and He did not say “and all that is between them,” as He does when He says:

“Allah is the One who created the heavens and the earth and all that is between them in six days, then rose above the Throne. You have no protector or intercessor besides Him.”

Thus, He sometimes mentions “and all that is between them” among what He created in six days, and sometimes omits it, although it is still intended. When He mentions it, this serves to clarify and explain; when He does not, it is included in the expression “the heavens and the earth.”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would sometimes say, “filling the heavens and filling the earth,” without saying “and all that is between them,” and sometimes he would say “and all that is between them.” All these narrations include “and filling whatever else You will.” Abū Saʿīd’s narration includes “The truest words a servant can say,” followed by the remainder of the supplication, while Ibn Abī Awfā’s narration includes “the supplication for purification from sins.”

This thus contains praise—the essence of gratitude—and seeking forgiveness. For our Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Appreciative. Praise corresponds to blessings, while seeking forgiveness corresponds to sins. This bears out His words, Most High:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Thus, the foremost supplication for forgiveness includes: “I acknowledge Your favor upon me, and I acknowledge my sin.” The hadith of Abū Saʿīd contains “praise—the essence of gratitude—and the affirmation of Allah’s oneness,” just as He combined the two in

## Page 7481

the Mother of the Quran.

Its beginning is praise, its middle is the affirmation of Allah’s oneness, and its end is supplication. Likewise, He says:

“He is the Ever-Living; there is no god worthy of worship but Him. So call upon Him, devoting your worship sincerely to Him. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

In the hadith in al-Muwattaʾ:

“The best words that I and the prophets before me have spoken are: ‘There is no god worthy of worship but Allah alone, with no partner. His is the dominion, and His is all praise, and He has power over all things.’ Whoever says these words, Allah records for him a thousand good deeds and removes from him a thousand evil deeds. They will be a protection for him against Satan that day. No one will bring anything better than what he has brought, except a man who says the same or more. Whoever says a hundred times in a day, ‘Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Him,’ will have his sins erased, even if they are like the foam of the sea.”

The virtues of these words are mentioned in many hadiths. They contain the affirmation of Allah’s oneness and praise. The words “There is no god worthy of worship but Allah alone, with no partner” affirm His oneness, while “His is the dominion, and His is all praise” express praise. They also contain other noble meanings.

The affirmation of Allah’s oneness, praise, and seeking forgiveness are combined in several passages, such as the hadith concerning the expiation for a gathering:

“Glory and praise be to You, O Allah. I bear witness that there is no god worthy of worship but You. I seek Your forgiveness and repent to You.”

It contains glorification and praise

## Page 7482

and the affirmation of Allah’s oneness, as well as seeking forgiveness.

Whoever says these words in a gathering: if it was a gathering of idle chatter, they serve as an expiation for it; if it was a gathering devoted to remembering Allah, they serve as a seal upon it. Another hadith also states:

In an authentic hadith recorded by Muslim and others, ʿUqbah narrates from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

Several of the early generations are reported to have mentioned words similar to these when explaining the words Adam received from his Lord. Ibn Jarīr narrated from Mujāhid that he said:

“O Allah, there is no god worthy of worship but You. Glory and praise be to You. My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me. You are the best of those who forgive.

“O Allah, there is no god worthy of worship but You. Glory and praise be to You. My Lord, I have wronged myself, so have mercy on me, for You are the best of those who show mercy.

“There is no god worthy of worship but You. Glory and praise be to You. My Lord, I have wronged myself, so accept my repentance. You are indeed the Accepter of Repentance, the Most Merciful.”

These words are thus similar to those recited at the conclusion of ablution.

Glorification, praise, and the affirmation of oneness are directed to Allah, for He alone brings good. Forgiveness is sought for the sins of the soul, from which evil arises. In several places in His Book, Allah joins the affirmation of His oneness with seeking forgiveness, as in His words:

In a hadith narrated by Ibn Abī ʿĀsim and others:

“Satan says, ‘I destroyed people through sins, but they destroyed me by seeking forgiveness and affirming that there is no god worthy of worship but Allah. When I saw that, I spread whims among them. Thus they sin without seeking forgiveness, because they think they are doing good.’”

“There is no god worthy of worship but Allah” entails sincerity and reliance upon Allah, and sincerity entails gratitude. It is therefore the best of all utterances and the highest branch of faith. As is authentically established in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Faith has more than

---

## Page 7484

sixty—or more than seventy—branches. The highest is saying, ‘There is no god worthy of worship but Allah,’ and the lowest is removing harm from the road. Modesty is a branch of faith.”

Thus, “There is no god worthy of worship but Allah” is the central axis of faith, and everything ultimately comes back to it. The revealed scriptures are summed up in His words, Exalted is He:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

This expresses the meaning of “There is no god worthy of worship but Allah.” The meaning of “There is no might nor power except through Allah” is included within it, as are the meanings of “All praise is due to Allah,” “Glory be to Allah,” and “Allah is the Greatest.” These expressions, however, elaborate on what it states in summary.

Section

Some later scholars have supposed that His words, “It is from yourself,” mean, “Is it, then, from yourself?” They take this as a rhetorical question expressing denial. Their interpretation is that good and evil are all from Allah, not from yourself. This view is contrary to the meaning of the verse, for the verse makes clear that evil comes from a person’s own self—that is, because of his sins. Yet these people say that evil does not come from his own self.

---

## Page 7485

Among those who mentioned this was Abū Bakr ibn Fūrak. He said: “Its meaning is: ‘Is it, then, from yourself?’ This is supported by the poet’s words:

Then they said, ‘Do you love her?’ I replied, ‘How greatly!
As countless as the sand, the pebbles, and the dust.’”

I say: The omission of an interrogative particle where the wording indicates it does not justify assuming its omission in a particular statement without any indication. That would contradict the intended meaning. It would also mean that anyone wishing to deny something Allah has stated could do so by positing an omitted interrogative particle in His statement and interpreting it as a rhetorical question expressing denial.

In terms of Arabic usage, this resembles the claim some have made concerning Abraham’s words, peace be upon him, “This is my Lord”: that they mean, “Is this my Lord?” Ibn al-Anbārī said: “This interpretation is irregular, because the interrogative particle cannot be omitted when it distinguishes a statement from a question.”

These people cited His words:

“If you die, will they live forever?”

But this provides no evidence for their position. The interrogative particle has already appeared at the beginning of the sentence, in the conditional construction in the passage:

“We did not grant immortality to any human before you.”

There was therefore no need to repeat it; indeed, repeating it would spoil the wording. Likewise are His words:

“So if he dies or is killed

---

## Page 7486

will you turn back on your heels?” And His words:

“Is it that whenever a messenger came to you with what your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant?”

And His words:

“Is it that whenever they made a covenant, a group of them cast it aside?”

This is eloquent and effective speech.

They also cited the poet’s words:

By your life, I do not know, though I may know—
Was it seven pebbles they cast, or eight?

And another’s words:

Your eye deceived you—or did you see at Wāsit,
In the gloom of night, a phantom of al-Rabāb?

The implied wording is: “Did your eye deceive you?” But this offers no evidence for their position, because the subsequent phrases “or eight” and “or did you see” indicate the omitted interrogative particle in the first verse. As for the second, if “or” connects two alternatives within a single question, the same applies. If it introduces a separate question, the opening statement retains its declarative meaning.

What these people intend is that the self has no influence on the occurrence of misfortunes.

and is not a cause of them.

## Page 7489

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

Allah Most High says:

“And who is better in religion than one who submits himself to Allah, does good, and follows the faith of Abraham, inclining to truth? And Allah took Abraham as a close friend.”

He thus denied that any religion is better than this religion and rebuked anyone who claimed otherwise. This is a rhetorical question expressing disapproval: it prohibits and condemns regarding any religion as better than this one.

Qatādah, al-Dahhāk, and others said:

The Muslims and the People of the Scripture engaged in mutual boasting. The People of the Scripture said, “Our prophet came before your prophet, and our scripture before your scripture. We are closer to Allah than you.”

The Muslims said, “We are closer to Allah Most High than you. Our Prophet is the Seal of the Prophets, and our scripture passes judgment on the scriptures that preceded it.”

So Allah Most High revealed the verse:

“It is not according to your wishes, nor the wishes of the People of the Scripture.”

---

## Page 7490

And Sufyān narrated from al-Aʿmash, from Abū al-Duhā, from Masrūq, who said:

When this verse was revealed:

“It is not according to your wishes, nor the wishes of the People of the Scripture. Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,”

the People of the Scripture said, “We and you are alike,” until the verse was revealed:

“And whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or female, while being a believer…”

The verse beginning “And who is better in religion…” was also revealed concerning them.

It has also been narrated that Mujāhid said: Quraysh said, “We will not be resurrected,” or, “We will not be called to account.” The People of the Scripture said:

“The Fire will never touch us except for a few days.”

Then Allah, Mighty and Majestic, revealed:

“It is not according to your wishes, nor the wishes of the People of the Scripture.”

This would mean that the verse addresses the disbelievers among the unlettered and the People of the Scripture, because they believed that they would not suffer everlasting punishment. The first account, however, is more widely transmitted and more clearly supported by the evidence. There is consensus that the surah was revealed in Medina, so it addresses the believers, as do the other surahs revealed in Medina.

Furthermore, it is widely reported through numerous channels that when the words of Allah Most High were revealed:

“Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,”

this weighed heavily upon the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, until the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained to them that worldly afflictions are part of this recompense, and that through them the believer receives his recompense. It is thus clear that they are addressed by this verse, not merely the disbelievers.

Furthermore, His words after this:

“And whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or

---

## Page 7491

female, while being a believer…”—and the rest of the verse—and His words,

“And who is better in religion…”

indicate that there was a dispute over the relative merits of religions, not merely a denial of punishment after death.

Furthermore, the preceding and following passages address the believers and answer them. Thus, those addressed in this verse are the same as those addressed in the other verses.

If it is objected: “The verse explicitly rules out any religion better than the religion of this Muslim, but how does it establish that no religion is equal to it? There are three possibilities: another religion may be better than it, inferior to it, or equal to it. It has been established that none is better, but where does the verse establish that none is equal to it? A comparable example is His saying:

‘And who is better in speech than one who calls to Allah, does righteous deeds, and says, “Indeed, I am one of the Muslims”?’”

The answer is: Even if we said here that the verse only rules out anything better, this would not undermine the argument. Discourse varies according to its context. Sometimes it establishes the soundness of a religion when the person addressed claims or supposes it to be unsound. In another context, when the dispute concerns relative merit, it establishes that no other religion is better. Then, in a third context, it establishes that this religion is better than others.

Likewise, when discussing the Messenger, in one context we establish his truthfulness and the authenticity of his message. In another, we establish that no one else is better than him. In a third, we establish that he is the foremost of the children of

---

## Page 7492

Adam; this is because discourse varies according to the circumstances of the person addressed.

We further say: Several considerations demonstrate that this religion is superior.

First: Although this construction originally serves to deny that anything is better—since the negation applies to the comparative form—customary usage often implies the superiority of the entity introduced by min (“than”), which in an affirmative statement is the entity surpassed. Thus, when you say, “This religion is better than that one,” the entity introduced by “than” is the one surpassed, while the first is the one preferred. But when you say, “Nothing is better than this,” “What is better than this?”, “There is no one among them better than this person,” “I know no one more learned than Zayd,” “There is no one among the people more truthful than ‘Amr,” or “There is no one among them better than him,” this construction indicates that the person in question is the best, the most learned, or the finest among them.

Indeed, this construction has acquired an established conventional meaning: it denies superiority to the entity described by the comparative and affirms the superiority over all the others of the entity introduced by “than.” It thus entails denying their superiority and affirming his superiority over them. It also incorporates the meaning of exception, as though you had said, “No one among them is superior except this person,” or “No one among them holds precedence except this person.” Similarly, when (in) is prevented from exercising its grammatical function by the negative particle mā, it comes to convey both negation and affirmation.

The same applies to exception. Although its original function is to exclude something from a judgment, it has acquired an established conventional meaning whereby the exception receives the opposite judgment to that applying to the group from which it is excepted. Thus, an exception to a negative statement constitutes an affirmation,

---

## Page 7493

and an exception to an affirmative statement constitutes a negation. Usage can give an expression a meaning other than that conveyed by its original designation. This occurs sometimes in individual nouns, sometimes in the construction of speech, and sometimes in entire expressions transferred to new meanings, such as widely circulated proverbs. Thus, customary usage changes an individual noun from its original sense, whether by broadening, narrowing, or shifting its meaning, as with dābba (“beast”), ghā’it (“excrement”), and ra’s (“head”).

Usage likewise changes a construction from what analogous constructions would ordinarily imply, as with the addition of an extra negative particle in negative clauses and the addition of negation with kāda (“almost”). An entire sentence may also be transferred from its original meaning to another, as with expressions used proverbially, such as:

“Your own hands tied it, and your own mouth inflated it,”

and:

“Perhaps al-Ghuwayr will bring calamities.”

**Second consideration**

If no religion is better than this one, any other religion must either be its equal or be inferior to it. It cannot be its equal, because if one religion resembles another and equals it in every respect, it is that very same religion. Even if there are several others, the kind remains one. Two different religions therefore cannot be identical and equal, for their difference precludes their being identical: difference is the opposite of sameness. How, then, could they be both different and identical?

Their difference, moreover, is one of opposition, not merely diversity. In one religion, certain things are believed to be true and obligatory, while the other declares them false and forbidden. It is therefore impossible for these two beliefs to be equal.

---

## Page 7494

The same applies to their respective orientations: one seeks the object of worship through certain aims and deeds, while the other seeks it through what opposes and contradicts them. This is unlike the diversity of Muslim paths and schools of thought, for their religion is one. Each believes what the other believes and worships Him according to the same religion. Each allows the other to act upon his position in disputed subsidiary matters, so they have not differed in religion.

Indeed, we go further: in those subsidiary matters over which Muslims disagree, one of the two positions must be better in Allah’s sight. This is the position of the majority of jurists, who agree with the early generations of the Muslim community that, in every question, only one position is correct in Allah’s sight. That correct position is therefore better in His sight, even if one party allows the other to maintain his position. Allowing it does not preclude its being inferior and less well-founded; it only precludes its being forbidden.

If this holds for the finer points of subsidiary matters, what, then, of their disagreements over fundamentals? Neither Muslims nor rational people disagree that only one position is correct in reality. They disagree only over whether the person who errs will be forgiven, and whether he may be deemed correct in the sense of having fulfilled his duty and incurred no blame—not in the sense of holding a true belief. For no rational person would say that two beliefs which contradict one another in every respect can both be correct.

In short, the issue here is simply that of preferring one statement and deed over another statement and deed. Among differing statements and deeds, some must necessarily be preferred over others

---

## Page 7495

according to the majority of the Muslim community. Indeed, even someone who holds that every jurist exercising independent judgment is correct may not dispute that one of the two positions is better and more correct, nor claim that they are equal. Even if he claims equality, he does so only concerning the finer points of subsidiary matters, although his position is weak and contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations. As for permissibility, no one has claimed that the various categories are equal in this regard. This differs from mere variation, such as reciting one surah rather than another, or giving one kind of charity rather than another. Such acts may be equal, because the religion in these cases is one in every respect. Our discussion, however, concerns different religions, and there is no disagreement here whatsoever. Once it is established that two different religions cannot be equal, there is no need to deny their equality explicitly, since reason already rules it out.

Similarly, when they heard His words:

“And do not be like the companion of the fish,”

there was reason to fear that they might disparage him. Yet the texts of the Quran and the Sunnah, together with the consensus of the Muslim community, attest that some prophets are superior to others, and some messengers superior to others. They establish the superiority of the messengers of firm resolve and testify that Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the foremost of Adam’s descendants and the most honored of all creation in his Lord’s sight. Nevertheless, the superiority of the true religion is something that must be believed; this is why Allah mentioned it in the verse.

---

## Page 7496

As for declaring individuals superior to others, this may not be needed at all times. Obligatory religious practice, however, must be acknowledged as superior, since superiority is implicit in obligation. If adhering to it rather than its opposite is obligatory, then believing in its superiority is all the more obligatory.

As for recommended religious practice, the conviction that it should be performed may be prescribed only for those for whom performing it is prescribed. Otherwise, some people who follow one of Islam’s paths of peace are harmed by regarding another path as better: they aspire to the better path but cannot follow it, while turning away from the less excellent one. Just as it is not in a person’s interests to learn of a path better than his own if he would abandon his path without following the other, neither is it true that his own path is superior to all others. Rather, his interests lie in following that path which leads him to the mercy of Allah Most High.

Some students of jurisprudence call a man to what they consider better than his own path—and they may be mistaken—so that he ends up following neither the first nor the second. Among some Sufis, meanwhile, a disciple believes that his shaykh is the most accomplished shaykh on earth and that his path is the best of all paths. Both attitudes are deviations.

Rather, every person should be instructed to obey Allah and His Messenger to the best of his ability. He should not be moved away from the obedience to Allah and His Messenger that he practices through his own path, even if it contains some deficiency or error. Its deficiency should be explained to him only if he is moved to a better path. Otherwise, his heart may turn away from the first path altogether, until he abandons the truth that he is not permitted to abandon, without holding fast to anything else.

---

## Page 7497

This is a broad subject that we do not intend to examine exhaustively here. It rests on four principles:

First: Knowing the degrees of truth and falsehood, good deeds and bad deeds, and good and evil, so as to recognize the better of two good things and the worse of two evils.

Second: Knowing which of these things are obligatory and which are not, and which are recommended and which are not.

Third: Knowing the conditions governing obligation and recommendation in relation to ability and inability, and recognizing that obligation and recommendation may depend on whether knowledge and the ability to act are attainable.

Fourth: Knowing the different kinds of people being addressed, as well as the individuals themselves, so that each person is instructed to obey Allah and His Messenger in whatever way benefits him, or benefits him most, and is forbidden whatever he would benefit from being forbidden. He should not be instructed to do something good that can be dispensed with if it would lead him into something worse than the prohibited act.

The point established by this verse—that the religion of one who submits himself to Allah, does good, and follows the faith of Abraham is the best of religions—is agreed upon among Muslims and is necessarily known to be part of the religion of Islam;

---

## Page 7498

rather, whoever follows a religion other than Islam will never have it accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers. But Allah’s Book judges between the people of the earth concerning their disagreements and explains the basis for its judgment.

In this verse, He explains the grounds for superiority through His words, “submits himself to Allah,” and His words, “and does good.” The first clarifies his intention, his purpose, the One he worships and takes as his God; and His words, “and does good.” Thus, the text rules out the negation of anything better than it, while reason rules out anything equal to it. It is therefore established that it is the best of religions.

The third point: The dispute between the two communities concerned which of their religions was better. They were not told that the two religions were equal, nor were they forbidden to regard one as superior. Rather, the grounds for the boasting, arrogance and self-delusion arising from considering one religion superior were cut off. For when a person becomes conscious of his own superiority or that of his religion, this may lead him to pride, arrogance and boasting.

All were therefore told:

“Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it.”

This applies whether his religion is superior or inferior. Evil deeds remain forbidden, and recompense for them is inevitable. Allah Most High says, “By the winds that scatter,” through to His words, “will surely come to pass.”

So, when the believers realized that they would be recompensed for their evil deeds and that the superiority of their religion would not spare them, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained to them that recompense may come

---

## Page 7499

in this world through afflictions, He then explained the falsity of the religion of the unbelievers among the polytheists and the People of the Book, saying:

“And whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or female…”

—to the end of the verse.

He explained that righteous deeds are rewarded in the Hereafter only when accompanied by faith, although a person may be rewarded for them in this world without faith. The unbelievers were thus refuted on two grounds: they would be recompensed for their evil deeds, and their good deeds would not admit them to Paradise without faith. He then explained the superiority of the upright, monotheistic religion of Islam, saying:

“And who is better in religion?”

The discourse is thus supremely precise. Similar to this in certain respects is the Prophet’s prohibition against preferring some prophets over others in a manner that disparages or belittles those considered less excellent. He said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not rank the prophets above one another.”

And he said:

“Do not prefer me over Moses.”

This makes his excellence clear, and through these two elements religion is complete. When Allah is the One worshipped, the adherent is sincerely devoted and submissive to Him, and his conduct consists of good deeds, reason recognizes that no religion can be better than this. This is unlike a religion originating with someone other than Allah, to whom its adherent submits himself; or the religion of someone who claims to worship Allah without submitting himself to Him, but instead behaves arrogantly like the Jews and associates partners with Him like the Christians; or someone who does not do good, but commits evil deeds rather than good ones. And this judgment

---

## Page 7500

is pure justice, sound reasoning, and equity. The Quran has shown people of reason how it is demonstrated. This is also true of most matters the Quran explains: it sets forth what is true and correct, together with its evidence and proofs. It does not establish these matters merely by reporting them, as many speculative theologians and philosophers may imagine—that its proofs depend on revelation and reports, and must be accepted because the one conveying them is truthful. Rather, its proofs are also rational and demonstrative. It contains the finest and most complete evidence and proofs, presented with the utmost clarity for those endowed with understanding and reason.

Thus, if someone takes these arguments from the Quran and explains them to a person who does not know that it is Allah’s speech, or does not know that the Messenger is truthful, or considers it without presupposing any obligation to accept the speaker’s word, those arguments themselves contain what establishes its truth and demonstrates its soundness.

---

## Page 7501

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Concerning the words of the Most High:

“And do not argue on behalf of those who betray themselves. Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is treacherous and sinful.”

His words, “betray themselves,” resemble His words in Sūrat al-Baqarah:

“Allah knew that you were betraying yourselves.”

Ibn Qutaybah and a group of Quran commentators said that this means, “You betray yourselves.” Some added, “You wrong yourselves.” They thus understood “yourselves” as the object of “betray,” and held that a person betrays himself—that is, wrongs himself—by stealing, as Ibn Ubayriq did, or by having intercourse with his wife on a night of fasting, as some of the Companions did.

This view, however, is open to question. Every sin a person commits involves wronging himself, whether he commits it secretly or openly. If betraying oneself means wronging oneself or committing what one has been forbidden to do, then every sinner would be betraying himself, even if he sinned openly; and the disbelief of the disbelievers would be

---

## Page 7502

a betrayal of themselves, as would their fighting against the prophets and the believers. The same would apply to highway robbery and armed aggression, and likewise to overt wrongdoing. What the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Sālih, and Shu'ayb did would also have been a betrayal of themselves.

Yet it is well known that this expression has not been used for all these meanings. Rather, it has been used for particular sins committed in secret. Ibn 'Abbās even said, concerning His words,

“You were betraying yourselves,”

that this referred to what 'Umar had done. For it is reported:

“When the Ansārī man came, he complained that he had spent that night without supper because he had fallen asleep before eating. Anyone who fell asleep before eating was forbidden to eat thereafter and would thus continue fasting. By morning, he was turning from his back onto his stomach. When he complained of his condition to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, 'Umar said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I desired my wife last night, but she said that she had already slept. I thought she had not slept, so I had intercourse with her. She then told me that she had indeed slept.’ They said: ‘Then Allah revealed concerning 'Umar: “It is lawful for you to have sexual relations with your wives on the nights of fasting.”’”

It has also been said that they were forbidden to have intercourse on the nights of fasting altogether, unlike eating, which was permitted before sleep.

It has also been reported:

“'Umar had intercourse with his wife after the night prayer but before sleeping. Afterwards, he began reproaching himself. He came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I seek Allah’s pardon for this treacherous soul of mine. I returned to my wife after praying the night prayer and noticed a pleasant fragrance. My soul enticed me, and I had intercourse with my wife.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘You were not

---

## Page 7503

one to do such a thing, O 'Umar.’” A group of the Companions also came and mentioned similar incidents, so Allah revealed this verse.

This account shows that his sinful soul enticed him and urged him to do that, and that afterwards he began reproaching it. Here, then, the soul is the treacherous wrongdoer. In secret, when no one sees him, a person’s soul urges him to do things it does not urge him to do openly. His reason forbids him from doing those things, but his soul overpowers him.

Wherever the term “betrayal” is used, it refers only to something concealed from the one betrayed. An example is someone who betrays a trust: he betrays the person who entrusted him when that person is not watching him; if he were watching, he would not betray him.

Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, do not betray Allah and the Messenger, nor knowingly betray your trusts.”

And He says:

“You will continue to discover treachery on their part, except for a few of them.”

The wife of al-'Azīz said:

“That is so he may know that I did not betray him in his absence, and that Allah does not guide the scheming of the treacherous.”

And Allah Most High says:

“He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the hearts conceal.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said when he rose:

“Was there no man among you who would rise against this man and strike off his head?”

A man said to him, “Why did you not signal to me with your eyes?”

He replied, “It is not fitting for a prophet to give treacherous signals with his eyes.”

Allah Most High says:

“Do not argue on behalf of those who betray themselves. Surely Allah does not love anyone who is treacherous and sinful.”

---

## Page 7504

“They conceal themselves from people, but they do not conceal themselves from Allah, who is with them when they plot by night words that displease Him.”

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“The signs of a hypocrite are three: when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks his promise; and when entrusted, he betrays the trust.”

Another hadith states:

“A believer may be disposed to any trait except treachery and lying.”

There are many similar examples. If this is so, how does a person betray his own soul, when he does not conceal his words and deeds from it, keeping them secret from it as he does when betraying people who do not see him? Likewise, he betrays Allah and the Messenger when out of sight, thus failing to be among those who fear Allah unseen. And why are these particular acts, rather than others, described as betrayal of one’s own soul?

The more likely interpretation—and Allah knows best—is that His words:

“You were betraying yourselves”

are like His words:

“Except one who makes a fool of himself.”

In constructions of this kind, the Basran grammarians say that the noun is in the accusative as an accusative of purpose. They take the verb safiha (“to be foolish”) away from its meaning in the language, for it is an intransitive verb; they therefore have to make it transitive without evidence.

As for the Kufan grammarians—such as al-Farrā’ and others, together with those who followed them—they hold that this noun is accusative as a specifying complement. In their view, a specifying complement may be definite just as it may be indefinite. They cite many examples from Arab usage, such as the expression: “So-and-so felt pain

---

## Page 7505

in his head,” “he felt pain in his belly,” and “he was rightly guided in his affairs.” The underlying constructions were “his soul was foolish” and “his affairs followed the right course.”

Other examples include “his judgment was weak” and “his soul grew insolent.” Allah’s words:

“Grew insolent in its livelihood”

belong to this category: it is the livelihood itself that is described as insolent. Thus, when the verb … (1), He made the noun accusative as a specifying complement. Allah Most High says:

“And do not be like those who came out of their homes in insolence and to show off before people.”

Thus, His words “makes a fool of himself” mean “except one whose soul is foolish”—that is, whose soul is characterized by foolishness. When He attributed the verb to the person, He made “his soul” accusative as a specifying complement, as in His words:

“And my head has flared with grey hair,”

and similar expressions. This is the interpretation preferred by Ibn Qutayba and others. However, “grey hair” is indefinite, whereas “his soul” is definite. This explanation given by the Kufan grammarians is more sound in both language and meaning, for it is the person himself who is foolish, as Allah Most High says:

“The foolish among the people will say,”

and:

“And do not give to the foolish.”

Likewise, His words “You betray yourselves” mean “your souls commit betrayal”: it is the souls that have committed betrayal, just as it is they that are foolish.

He used ikhtānat rather than khānat, because the augmented form ifti‘āl conveys an additional degree of action beyond simple betrayal. ‘Ikrima said: “What is meant by ‘those who betray themselves’ is Ibn Ubayriq, who stole food and cloth. He and his people began saying, ‘It was so-and-so who stole it,’ referring to another man.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 7506

So these people strove to conceal the thief’s theft and accuse someone else of it, as Allah Most High says:

“They conceal themselves from people, but do not conceal themselves from Allah, who is with them when they plot by night words that He does not approve.”

They thus betrayed their companion and the Messenger, and actively engaged in betrayal.

Likewise, those who had intercourse at night strove to keep their conduct from becoming known while they were doing it, even if they disclosed it later when repenting. At the time of the act, however, they needed greater concealment and secrecy than a lone betrayer would need. Alternatively, His words:

“You betray yourselves”

may mean that you betray one another, as in His words:

“So kill one another,”

and:

“Yet here you are, killing one another,”

and:

“Why, when you heard it, did the believing men and women not think well of one another?”

For the thief and others betrayed their fellow believers. Similarly, if the man had intercourse with his wife while she did not know that it was forbidden, he betrayed her. The first interpretation, however, is more plausible.

Fasting rests on trustworthiness: a fasting person can break his fast without anyone knowing. If he breaks it secretly, he has betrayed his trust. Breaking the fast through concealed intercourse is betrayal, just as secretly taking wealth and telling the Messenger and the wronged person that the guilty party is innocent and the innocent party guilty is betrayal. All of this is betrayal, and it is the soul that has betrayed, for it loves sensual pleasure, wealth, and leadership. The relationship between khāna and ikhtāna, both meaning “to betray,” is like that between kasaba and iktasaba, both meaning “to earn.” Thus He described the person as committing betrayal.

---

## Page 7507

Then He explained that it is the person’s soul that commits betrayal, just as it is the soul that causes harm. For this originates in its desires, not in what reason and sound judgment command. Foolishness likewise originates in the soul because of its fickleness and impetuosity. A person’s soul secretly urges him to do things that reason and religion forbid, thus betraying and overpowering him. This occurs frequently in matters of sexual intercourse and wealth. Hence, most people cannot be trusted in these matters; rather, one seeks to entrust them to someone whose soul does not urge him to betray that trust.

Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib said:

“Were I entrusted with a treasury, I would faithfully discharge that trust. But were I entrusted with a black woman, I would fear failing to discharge my trust concerning her.”

Likewise, wealth cannot be entrusted to those whose souls are eager to acquire it by whatever means. All this demonstrates that the soul betrays its trust. Even if a man initially has no intention of betrayal, his soul drives him to it without his bidding and overcomes his judgment. This is why a person blames and censures his soul for it, saying, “It is this soul that has done it all!” For it is the soul that has committed betrayal.

Section

His words:

“And do not argue on behalf of those who betray themselves”

indicate that it is not permissible to argue on behalf of a betrayer, nor may a person argue in defence of his own soul if it is

---

## Page 7508

treacherous: it harbors secret desires and inward deeds hidden from others, so it is not permissible to argue in its defense. Allah Most High says:

“He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the hearts conceal.”

And He says:

“Abandon sin, both outward and inward.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether openly committed or concealed.’”

He also says:

“Rather, man is a witness against himself, even though he offers his excuses.”

For a person offers excuses for his soul and argues in its defense, even though he sees that its true state contradicts those excuses. Allah Most High says:

“Today, your own soul is sufficient to call you to account.”

And He says:

“Among people is one whose speech about the life of this world impresses you, and who calls Allah to witness what is in his heart, yet he is the fiercest of opponents.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The most hateful of men to Allah is the fiercely contentious disputant.”

Such a person argues falsely in his own defense, displaying obstinacy—that is, deviation and crookedness away from the truth. This takes two forms. The first is arguing and defending himself before other people. The “second” concerns his relationship with his Lord: he makes excuses for his soul, believing it to be in the right and its intentions good, when in fact it is treacherous and unjust. It has hidden desires that it has concealed, so that the person remains unaware of them until he looks closely and examines himself. Shaddād ibn Aws said, “What I fear most for you is hidden desire.” Abū Dāwūd said, “It is the love of leadership.”

Such is the nature of the soul that even on the Day of Resurrection a person will seek to defend himself and dispute with Allah using falsehood. Allah Most High says:

“On the Day Allah resurrects them all, they will swear to Him as they swear to you, thinking they have something to stand on. Indeed, it is they who are the liars.”

---

## Page 7509

“Satan has gained mastery over them and made them forget the remembrance of Allah. They are the party of Satan. Indeed, it is the party of Satan who are the losers.”

Allah Most High also says:

“On the Day We gather them all together, We will say to those who associated partners with Us, ‘Where are those partners you used to claim?’”

“Then their only excuse will be to say, ‘By Allah, our Lord, we never associated partners with Him.’”

“See how they lie against themselves, and how whatever they used to invent has deserted them.”

Hadiths relate that a person will deny his deeds on the Day of Resurrection until his hearing, sight, and limbs testify against him. Allah Most High says:

“You did not conceal yourselves for fear that your hearing, your sight, or your skins would testify against you; rather, you thought that Allah did not know much of what you did.”

It is the habit of the hypocrites to argue in their own defense with lies and false oaths. Allah has described them in this way in several places.

“In the account of Tabūk, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, returned, the hypocrites came to offer him their excuses. He accepted their outward statements and left their inner secrets to Allah. When Kaʿb came, he said:

‘By Allah, O Messenger of Allah, had I sat before any king on earth, I could have escaped his displeasure, for I have been gifted with eloquence in argument. But I fear that if I tell you a lie that makes you pleased with me, Allah will soon make you displeased with me. Yet if I tell you the truth and thereby incur your displeasure, I hope for Allah’s pardon because of it. No, by Allah, I had no excuse. By Allah, I had never been stronger or better off than when I stayed behind instead of accompanying you.’

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then said:

---

## Page 7510

‘As for this man, he has told the truth’—meaning that the others were not to be believed. He then shunned him for a time, after which Allah accepted his repentance through the blessing of his truthfulness.”

Making false excuses for oneself and arguing in one’s own defense with falsehood is not permissible. Rather, if someone commits a sin privately, between himself and Allah, he should acknowledge his sin before his Lord, submit to Him in his heart, ask His forgiveness, and turn to Him in repentance. For He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful, ever accepting of repentance.

If the misdeed was public, he should repent publicly. If he outwardly displayed good while concealing evil, he should inwardly repent of that evil. Whoever does wrong in secret should do good in secret, and whoever does wrong openly should do good openly.

“Indeed, good deeds do away with misdeeds. That is a reminder for those who remember.”

---

## Page 7511

Surah al-Mā’idah (The Table Spread)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Surah al-Mā’idah is the most comprehensive surah in the Quran concerning the detailed prescriptions of divine law: what is lawful and unlawful, commands and prohibitions. Thus, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is the last of the Quran to be revealed, so regard as lawful what it declares lawful and as unlawful what it declares unlawful.”

This is also why it opens with His words:

“Fulfil your contracts.”

Contracts here are covenants. This surah contains declarations of what is lawful, unlawful, and obligatory that are not found elsewhere. Its verses are interconnected, as in His words:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors.”

It is widely reported in works of Quranic exegesis that this verse was revealed concerning those who wished to

---

## Page 7512

adopt celibacy, among them Companions such as ‘Uthmān ibn Maz‘ūn and those who gathered with him. The two Sahīh collections contain the hadith of Anas concerning the four men, one of whom said, “As for me, I will fast without a break.” Another said, “As for me, I will spend the night in prayer and never sleep.” Another said, “As for me, I will not marry women.” The fourth said, “As for me, I will not eat meat.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“But I fast and break my fast, marry women, and eat meat. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me.”

It therefore seems—and Allah knows best—that His words,

“Do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you,”

refer to someone who forbids himself something lawful, either verbally or by resolving to abstain from it, like the one who said, “I will not marry women or eat meat.” This is innovated monasticism, for a monk neither marries nor slaughters animals.

His words,

“And do not transgress,”

refer to those who said, “I will spend the night in prayer and never sleep,” and, “I will fast without a break.” Transgression means exceeding the bounds, and such a person exceeds the bounds in prescribed worship. This resembles transgression in supplication, as in His words:

“Call upon your Lord humbly and in secret. Indeed, He does not love transgressors.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There will be people who transgress in supplication and purification.”

Thus, there is transgression in “worship and scrupulousness,” as with those who scrupulously abstained from things in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, availed himself of concessions; and in “asceticism,” as with those who forbade good things. Both of these categories involve abstention. His words, “And do not transgress,” may therefore refer specifically to acts of worship, or

---

## Page 7513

transgression may encompass both transgression in worship and in declaring things forbidden. These are the two kinds for which Allah censured the polytheists in several places: they practised worship He had not authorized and declared things forbidden without His authorization. Thus, His words,

“Do not forbid,”

and,

“Do not transgress,”

cover both categories.

Transgression here is like transgression in His words:

“And do not cooperate in sin and transgression.”

It may be broader than sin, or it may be a distinct category. Alternatively, transgression may consist in exceeding the limits of prescribed acts, whether obligatory or recommended, and exceeding the bounds of what is permissible; or it may also include exceeding the bounds of prohibition. For there are three categories: what is commanded, what is forbidden, and what is permissible.

After this, He mentioned His words:

“Allah will not hold you accountable for your inadvertent oaths, but He will hold you accountable for oaths you deliberately bind yourselves by. Their expiation is…”

—to the end of the verse. He mentioned this after prohibiting the forbidding of lawful things, to explain the way out when someone has sworn by Allah, or taken an oath of another kind, to forbid himself something lawful. This is used as evidence that declaring something lawful forbidden constitutes an oath.

He then mentioned what He had forbidden of intoxicants, gambling, sacrificial altars, and divining arrows, thereby clarifying what He had forbidden. For some permissivists fall into denying the prohibitions laid down by divine law, just as some of these people fall into forbidding what is lawful. During their strenuous devotional efforts and spiritual discipline, they adopt a prohibitionist stance; then, once they have attained their goal—or so they claim—they become permissivists. And these two

---

## Page 7514

are afflictions that frequently befall devout worshippers and Sufis. The ruling on oaths is placed between the two, for both concern the mouth—what enters it and what comes out of it—just as jurists place the Book of Oaths alongside the Book of Foods.

This also provides a concession allowing expiation for oaths without restriction, contrary to the stringent position adopted by some jurists who hold that certain oaths admit of no expiation. Such stringency is akin to declaring things forbidden, since it prevents a person from doing what is obligatory or permissible.

All of this is a mercy from Allah toward us, unlike His treatment of other nations, for whom He forbade certain things as a punishment, whose oaths admitted of no expiation, and whom He did not purify of defilement as He has purified us. Reflect on this, for it is beneficial.

---

## Page 7515

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

His saying:

“They are avid listeners to falsehood, listening to other people who have not come to you.”

It has been said that the preposition lām indicates purpose: they listen in order to lie, and they listen in order to relay information to other people who have not come to you. Thus, they are liars, talebearers, and spies. The correct interpretation, however, is that the lām connects the verb to its object, as in the saying:

“Allah hears whoever praises Him.”

Here, listening carries the meaning of acceptance: they accept falsehood, listen to other people who have not come to you, and obey them. This therefore condemns them for accepting false reports and for obeying others besides him from among the unbelievers and hypocrites. This resembles His saying:

“They would have hurried about among you, seeking to sow discord among you; and among you are those who listen to them.”

That is, they seek to lead you into discord, and among you are those who listen to them. Thus, He condemns them for following falsehood in both kinds of speech: declarative and non-declarative. Falsehood in declarative speech is lying, while falsehood in non-declarative speech is obedience to those other than the messengers. This is far-fetched.

Then He said:

“Avid listeners to falsehood, devourers of unlawful gain.”

He thus stated that they, in

---

## Page 7516

nourishing both body and heart, feed on what is unlawful, unlike those who eat what is lawful and accept only the truth. This condemns anyone who is taken in by lies and accepts them, or prefers them because they accord with his desires. This includes accepting corrupt doctrines, for they are falsehoods—especially when one also accepts them for compensation, whether it comes from someone in authority, an endowment, futūh, a gift, a wage, or anything else. This resembles His saying:

“Indeed, many of the rabbis and monks consume people’s wealth unjustly and turn people away from the path of Allah.”

. . . (1)

The people of religious innovation and wickedness who believe the lies fabricated about Allah, His Messenger, and His rulings, and those who obey created beings in disobedience to the Creator.

Similar to this is His saying:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar. They pass on what they hear, and most of them are liars.”

They descend only upon a wicked liar, bringing what has been heard, in which a thousand lies are mixed with a single word of truth. He thus becomes an avid listener to falsehood from those who eavesdrop by stealth.

Then He said in the surah:

“Why do the godly scholars and rabbis not forbid them from sinful speech and consuming unlawful gain?”

Sinful speech, listening to falsehood, and consuming unlawful gain are practices that ordinarily go hand in hand. Judges have a particular share in these practices. For if a judge

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 7517

accepts a bribe, he listens to false testimony and fraudulent claims, thus becoming an avid listener to falsehood, a devourer of unlawful gain, and a speaker of sinful words. For this reason, He gave His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the choice of judging between them or declining to do so, because their aim is not to hear and accept the truth unconditionally. Rather, they listen to whatever accords with their desires, even if it is false. The same applies to scholars who invent false reports.

---

## Page 7518

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved so difficult that, in a number of Quranic commentaries, only erroneous explanations of them can be found.

Among them are His words:

“and worshipped false gods.”

The correct interpretation is that this is coordinated with His words:

“those whom Allah has cursed.”

It is a past-tense verb coordinated with the preceding past-tense verbs. In the preceding verbs, however, the subject is Allah, either explicitly named or represented by a pronoun. The subject of this verb denotes the one who worshipped false gods: it is the pronoun contained in ‘abada (“he worshipped”). The particle man (“those who”) is not repeated because these actions concern a single group, namely the Jews.

---

## Page 7519

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Allah Most High said:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors.”

“And eat of what Allah has provided for you, lawful and good”—to the end of the verse.

A well-known explanation in Quranic commentary is that this was revealed concerning a group of Companions who had resolved to adopt a monastic way of life. The two Sahīh collections report from Anas:

“Some men asked the Prophet’s wives about his worship in private, and they considered it little.”

He then related the rest of the hadith.

The two Sahīh collections also report that Sa‘d said:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—refused ‘Uthmān ibn Maz‘ūn permission to remain celibate. Had he permitted him, we would have had ourselves castrated.”

It is also reported from ‘Ikrima that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, Ibn Mas‘ūd, ‘Uthmān ibn Maz‘ūn, al-Miqdād and Sālim

---

## Page 7520

the freedman of Abū Hudhayfa, together with some of their companions, withdrew into religious seclusion. They stayed in their homes, kept away from women, wore sackcloth, and forbade themselves good food and clothing, except what the wandering ascetics among the Children of Israel ate and wore. They contemplated having themselves castrated and resolved to spend their nights in prayer and their days fasting. Then this verse was revealed. The other Quranic commentators likewise related accounts conveying a similar meaning.

Allah has condemned those who neglected prayer and followed their desires. He has condemned those who follow their desires and those who want you to go far astray and wish the believers to go far astray. He has also condemned those who pursued the luxuries they had been given, and those who enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat.

Most of those who neglected prayer and followed their desires are wine-drinkers, as Allah Most High said:

“Satan only seeks to stir up enmity and hatred among you through wine and gambling, and to turn you away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer.”

Thus, they combined forbidden pleasure with abandoning the remembrance of Allah and neglecting prayer. The same applies to others who follow their desires.

Then He, glorified be He, forbade declaring unlawful the good things He has made lawful, and transgressing in their enjoyment—that is, exceeding the bounds. Transgression in asceticism and worship has also been explained as declaring lawful things forbidden and engaging in acts of worship that harm them, thereby overstepping

---

## Page 7521

the bounds and gone to excess.

It has also been said: Do not let eating good things lead you to extravagance and unlawfully taking people’s wealth, for anyone who indulges excessively in good foods and pleasures will inevitably fall into what is unlawful because of that excess.

The purpose of asceticism is to abandon whatever harms a person in the Hereafter, and the purpose of worship is to do whatever benefits him in the Hereafter. If a person abandons what benefits his religion and his Hereafter, and performs acts of worship that cause harm, he has transgressed and gone to excess, even if he considers this beneficial asceticism and beneficial worship.

Ibn ‘Abbās, Mujāhid, Qatāda and al-Nakha‘ī explained “Do not transgress” as meaning: “Do not castrate yourselves.” ‘Ikrima said: “Do not follow a way other than that of the Muslims by abstaining from women, fasting continually and spending every night in prayer.”

Muqātil said: “Do not declare lawful things forbidden.” Al-Hasan is reported to have said: “Do not do what Allah has forbidden.” What is meant by this is: do not declare lawful things forbidden, nor do what is unlawful. This would mean that He forbade both kinds of conduct. However, the occasion of the verse’s revelation and its context support the majority view. It may also be said that this resembles His saying:

“Eat and drink, but do not be excessive,”

and His saying in the remainder of the passage:

“And eat of what Allah has provided for you, lawful and good,”

to the end of the verse.

The same applies to the authentic hadiths, such as the statement of one of them: “I will not marry women”

---

## Page 7522

and another’s statement, “I will not eat meat,” as in the previously mentioned hadith of Anas. This is among the indications that perpetual fasting is disliked, as is continually spending the whole night in prayer.

Section

What the law of Islam has brought is the straight path, through which a person’s religious life is set right. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The most balanced fasting is the fasting of David: he would fast one day and not fast the next.”

An authentic version reads “the best” instead. What is best is what is most balanced and upright.

This Quran guides to the most upright way, which lies midway between these two groups: the people of religious innovation and the people of wickedness—those given to excess and those given to excessive austerity. This is why the early generations warned against both groups.

Al-Hasan said: “It is the innovator in his religion and the wicked man in his worldly life.” They used to say: “Beware of the worldly man whom his worldly life has led astray, and the follower of desires who follows his own desires.” They would enjoin keeping away from the people of religious innovation and wickedness. Thus, the “first category” consists of the people of wickedness

---

## Page 7523

—those who live in luxury and ease, whose circumstances have led them into wickedness. The “second category” consists of those who practise monastic austerity, whose excesses and severity have led them into religious innovations. The former have enjoyed their allotted share, while the latter have engaged in vain discourse as those before them did.

Those who pursue forbidden desires, or overindulge in permissible things while neglecting the prescribed prayers and acts of worship, fall under the sway of Satan and their own desires. This causes them to forget Allah and the Hereafter and corrupts their condition, as is frequently observed among them.

Others forbid themselves the good things Allah has made lawful—even though they say, “Allah has not forbidden this.” Rather, they undertake to abstain from them, either through a vow or an oath, as many worshippers and ascetics have forbidden themselves various things. One of them says, “I vow to Allah never to eat any food during the daytime.” Another pledges not to eat food that appeals to his appetite, binding himself to this through his intention and resolve, even without swearing an oath or making a vow.

One undertakes not to drink water; another, not to eat bread; another, not to drink the barley beverage; another, never to speak at all. One emasculates himself, while another undertakes neither to marry nor to slaughter animals. Such practices are forms of the monasticism they have invented as a means of striving against the self and subduing desires and appetites.

There is no doubt that striving against the self is commanded, as is subduing desires and appetites. As is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he said:

“The one who truly strives is he who strives against his own self for Allah’s sake, and the wise person is he who calls himself to account and works for what comes after

---

## Page 7524

death, while the weak-willed person is he who lets his soul follow its desires and entertains vain hopes concerning Allah.”

But the Muslim who follows the law of Islam regards as forbidden what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. He neither forbids what is lawful nor indulges in it excessively. Rather, he takes what he needs of food, clothing, or marriage, exercising moderation in these matters and in worship, without burdening himself beyond his capacity. You will find that such a person achieves, through striving against his soul and subduing his desires, what benefits him more than that arduous, innovated path of little benefit. Most who have followed that path have retraced their steps, broken their pledge, and failed to observe it as it should be observed.

Such a person receives a reward for this that he would not receive for following that other path. Through it, his soul is purified and journeys towards his Lord, and he experiences an increase in faith that the followers of that path do not experience. Their souls will inevitably urge them towards forbidden desires, for there is no child of Adam who has not sinned or contemplated a sin, except John son of Zechariah. Allah Most High has said:

“And mankind was created weak.”

Tāwūs said that this concerns women and man’s limited ability to remain patient without them, as mentioned previously. Attraction to women is thus a natural inclination common to all the children of Adam. Many of them may also be tried by an attraction to males, as has been reported of them. A man may thus be tried by an attraction to beardless youths; even if he does not commit the gravest act of sexual immorality, he is tried by lesser acts, such as physical contact and looking. Hardly any of them escapes sexual immorality, either inwardly or

---

## Page 7525

with the beardless youth. The effects this has upon the soul are well known. People have related accounts of passionate lovers too lengthy to recount here. If a Muslim is afflicted with any such thing, he must strive against his soul for Allah’s sake. He is commanded to undertake this striving; it is not a matter of obligations and prohibitions he has imposed upon himself, whereby he would be obeying his own self and desires. Rather, it concerns something Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, with no way around that prohibition. Through striving against his soul, he thus obeys Allah and His Messenger.

In a hadith narrated by Abū Yahyā al-Qattāt, from Mujāhid, from Ibn ‘Abbās, and traced back to the Prophet:

“Whoever falls passionately in love, remains chaste, conceals it, and endures patiently, then dies, is a martyr.”

Abū Yahyā’s transmission is open to question. However, the meaning expressed in this report is supported by the Quran and Sunnah. Allah has commanded mindfulness of Him and patience. Mindfulness of Allah includes refraining from everything He has forbidden, whether a glance of the eye, a word of the tongue, or a movement of the hand or foot. Patience includes refraining from complaining of one’s suffering to anyone other than Allah, Mighty and Majestic. This is beautiful patience.

As for concealment, two things are meant by it:

“The first”: to conceal one’s anguish and pain, without complaining to anyone other than Allah. Whenever one complains to anyone other than Allah, one’s patience is diminished. This is the higher of the two forms of concealment. Yet not everyone is capable of it; indeed, many people complain of their suffering, and this may be done in

---

## Page 7526

two ways:

If he confides his suffering to a healer versed in spiritual medicine and in what harms or benefits the soul, so that he may treat his soul with the remedy of faith, he is like someone seeking a religious ruling, and this is good. If he confides in someone who helps him do what is forbidden, this is unlawful. If he confides in someone else for the relief that complaining brings—just as an afflicted person tells others of his misfortune—without intending to learn what would benefit him or to seek help in his affliction, this diminishes his patience. Yet he does not thereby incur sin unless something forbidden accompanies it, as when an afflicted person expresses resentment.

And “the second” is to conceal it and not discuss it with people, because doing so publicizes evil and indecency. When souls hear such things, they are stirred, feel desire and longing, and become enslaved by passion. Whenever a person sees, hears of, or imagines someone doing what he himself desires, this prompts him to do likewise and imitate that person. When women see male animals mounting females, they feel drawn to sexual intimacy and intercourse. When a man hears of someone engaging in such acts with beardless youths and women, sees it, or imagines it, this prompts him to do the same. When food is mentioned to someone, he craves it and feels drawn to it. If something he desires is described to him—clothing, a woman, a dwelling, or anything else—his soul inclines towards it. A person far from his homeland longs for it whenever he is reminded of it. Whenever a person pictures anything he loves, there is a stirring of

---

## Page 7527

love and longing for that beloved object, whether through its description or the sight of it. Both produce a mental image. Such an image may also arise from hearing, seeing, or thinking about something connected with it. When the soul pictures these related things, it turns towards what it has imagined, and the impulse of love is stirred, whether that love is praiseworthy or blameworthy.

This is why people feel drawn to Hajj when the Hijaz is mentioned, when the season of Hajj arrives, or when they see members of their household, relatives, friends, or others setting out for it. Had a person neither heard nor seen these things, he would not have been stirred, nor would the impulse to direct his energies towards it have arisen. Thus, mention of al-Abraq, al-Ajraʿ, al-ʿAlī, and similar places stirs his soul, because he saw those places while journeying towards what he loved; mentioning them therefore reminds him of his beloved object.

Likewise, when merchants and people of wealth hear of opportunities for profit, their desire for them is stirred. Those fond of recreation and outings are similarly drawn to them when they see others setting out for such pursuits. All these impulses are embedded in human souls, and man is ever unjust and ignorant.

Likewise, mentioning the traces of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brings him to mind and stirs love for him. Thus, when someone afflicted with sexual immorality and passionate infatuation tells another person of his condition, that person’s soul is stirred towards the same kind of thing, for souls are naturally disposed to love beautiful forms.

---

## Page 7528

so when they picture a particular kind, the beloved object draws near to them. This is why Allah Most High forbade the spreading of sexual immorality. He likewise commanded that such sins be concealed, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever falls into any of these filthy sins should conceal himself beneath Allah’s concealment, for whoever exposes his wrongdoing to us, we shall enforce Allah’s prescribed judgment upon him.”

He also said:

“All of my community will be pardoned except those who publicize their sins. One form of publicizing sin is for a man to spend the night committing a sin that Allah has concealed, then speak of it in the morning.”

As long as a sin remains concealed, its punishment falls upon its perpetrator alone. But when it becomes public and is not condemned, its harm becomes widespread. How much worse, then, when making it public encourages others to commit it!

This is why Imam Ahmad and others disapproved of reciting tender love poetry: it stirs souls towards sexual immorality. Thus, whoever is afflicted with passionate infatuation is instructed to remain chaste, conceal it, and be patient. He will then be among those of whom Allah said:

“Indeed, whoever fears Allah and remains patient—Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

The point is that he is rewarded for this struggle, and the true striver is the one who struggles against his own soul for Allah’s sake.

As for those who introduce innovations into asceticism and worship, following the path of monks, they may renounce marriage, surplus food and wealth, and similar things. This is commendable; nevertheless, most of them inevitably fall into sins of this kind. Thus, we find many of them afflicted with keeping company with young men and attending to women, and so they become afflicted with an inclination

---

## Page 7529

towards the physical beauty of women and boys in forbidden ways, to a degree not experienced by the people of the Sunnah who follow the law brought by Muhammad. The accounts of their conduct in this regard are too numerous to recount in detail in a book. Among them are hidden and open indecencies not found among others. Even the best of them incline towards young men, singing, and devotional listening, because of the comfort their souls find therein. Had they followed the Sunnah, they would have found relief from all this.

When Satan said to Abū Sa‘īd al-Kharrāz in a dream, “I have two subtle snares among you: devotional listening and keeping company with young men,” Abū Sa‘īd replied, “Few of our companions escape them.”

This has gone so far that [indeed, they] have such an intense love in their souls [for it] (1) that it has become interwoven with their path to Allah. When one of them gazes upon the object of his contemplation, he finds within himself a desire for his customary worship and asceticism that he does not experience without it. When listening to poems, he finds within himself a longing, eagerness, and vigour that he does not find when listening to the Quran. Thus they have fallen into misconception and desire. Satan was not content merely to make them fall into forbidden practices that lead them astray; he went so far as to make them regard these as worship, like those of whom Allah said:

“When they commit an indecency, they say, ‘We found our forefathers doing this, and Allah has commanded us to do it.’”

The verse continues. These are the ones who have neglected prayer and followed their desires. When they engage in devotional listening, they do so with longing, intense eagerness, and wholehearted love

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-al-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Shamela digital library.

---

## Page 7530

and devote themselves and their wealth to it. They may even offer their wives and sons, tolerating sexual dishonour within their families to achieve their aims. One of them brings his son and gives him to the shaykh, allowing him and his followers to do whatever they wish with him; they call him a hiwār. If he is good-looking, the shaykh keeps him exclusively for himself. His family considers this a blessing bestowed upon him by the shaykh, and all modesty between the boy’s mother and father and the Sufi devotees disappears.

When they pray, they pray like the hypocrites, rising reluctantly to prayer,

“showing off before people and remembering Allah only a little.”

They have thus neglected prayer and followed their desires. Yet they may abstain from some of the wholesome things Allah has permitted them and strive diligently in worship and remembrance, while combining these with religious innovation and the unlawful practices mentioned earlier. It is this innovation that has led them to follow their desires and neglect their prayers. For the revealed law is like Noah’s Ark: whoever boards it is saved, and whoever stays behind drowns. These people have stayed behind and drowned through their love. Yet Allah accepts the repentance of those who repent.

When those who follow the law brought by Muhammad fall into sin, repentance does not impose burdens and shackles upon them; rather, it belongs to the accommodating way of pure monotheism. For the people of religious innovation, however, repentance may become burdens and shackles, as it was for the monks before us. When one of them falls into sin, he cannot escape its evil except through severe affliction, because he has departed from the Sunnah.

---

## Page 7531

Some of these people may think that they cannot follow the path to Allah Most High except through a religious innovation. Likewise, self-indulgent sinners may think that they cannot fulfil their obligations or refrain from forbidden things except through the sins they commit. This happens to a great many people.

Some say that they cannot perform the prayers and avoid forbidden speech—backbiting and the like—except by consuming hashish. Another says that consuming it helps him derive knowledge and clear his mind. Some even call it “the wellspring of thought and remembrance” and “the awakener of dormant resolve.” All this is self-deception and Satan’s scheming against these people and others. In truth, it blinds the mind, and whoever consumes it becomes inarticulate and deranged, unaware of what he is saying.

Likewise, some of these people say that their love for Allah, eagerness to worship, fervour, ecstasy, longing, and other such feelings cannot be fully realised except by listening to poems, keeping company with a “witness”—whether a boy or someone else—and listening to voices and melodies. They claim that hearing these sounds and seeing these stirring forms awakens within them impulses towards asceticism and worship that would not otherwise arise, and that without this they may abandon

---

## Page 7532

the prayers and commit grave sins such as highway robbery and murder. They believe that this disciplines their souls and gives them a pleasure that keeps them from committing forbidden acts and major sins, and moves them to pray, fast, and perform Hajj.

This is the justification used by many shaykhs who invite people to their path through innovated listening practices in their various forms. Some invite people through frame drums and dancing; others add flutes. Some conduct these sessions with women and boys, others with frame drums and handclapping, and others with gatherings for remembrance, glorification of Allah, standing, recitation of poetry, and the other forms these practices take. Sometimes they add to this the company of women and beardless youths, and similar things.

They say, “These people whom we have brought to repentance used neither to pray, perform Hajj, nor fast. Rather, they committed highway robbery, murder, and fornication.
