We brought them to repentance from all this through these listening sessions.” They maintain that none of them could have brought these people to repentance by any other means.

They may acknowledge that what they have done is a religious innovation that is prohibited or unlawful. Yet they say, “We had no other means available. If we do not engage in this small measure of forbidden conduct, the result will be involvement in things even more strictly forbidden, and the neglect of obligations—a greater sin than this forbidden practice, which is slight compared with the many forbidden acts they used to commit.”

---

## Page 7533

They also say:

“A person finds within himself energy and strength for many acts of obedience when he obtains what he loves, even if it is reprehensible and unlawful. Without it, however, he finds no such energy and does not perform those acts. He also refrains from forbidden things when he is compensated with something he loves, even if it is reprehensible; otherwise, he does not refrain.”

Many people fall prey to this specious argument. The answer rests on three points:

The first is that prohibited things fall into two categories. The first category comprises things that are known with certainty never to have been permitted by divine law, whether out of necessity or otherwise: associating partners with Allah, shameful deeds, speaking about Allah without knowledge, and outright injustice. These are the four mentioned in the words of the Most High:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether committed openly or secretly, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

These things are forbidden under every revealed law. Allah sent all the messengers to prohibit them, and He has never permitted any of them under any circumstances. That is why these prohibitions were revealed in this Meccan surah, and why other things were declared not to be prohibited. For what He prohibited subsequently—such as blood, carrion, and pork—He prohibited in some circumstances but not others; their prohibition is not absolute.

---

## Page 7534

Likewise, “intoxicating drink” is permitted by agreement to dislodge something stuck in the throat. According to one of the two scholarly views, it is also permitted to relieve thirst. Those who do not permit it say that it does not relieve thirst; this is the basis of Ahmad’s position. The ruling thus depends on whether it relieves thirst. If it is known to do so, it is undoubtedly permitted, just as pork is permitted to avert starvation. The necessity arising from thirst that a person believes will kill him is greater than that arising from hunger. Hence, drinking ritually impure liquids to relieve thirst is permitted without dispute—provided that the thirst is relieved; otherwise, none of this is permitted.

Likewise, regarding “gambling,” the Lawgiver has permitted competing for stakes, which falls within its meaning, because of the need to serve the interests of jihad. Some have said that this does not constitute gambling. This is the position of those who never permit both sides to contribute stakes unless a third competitor participates without contributing a stake. Undoubtedly, gambling is less serious than intoxicating drink; if intoxicating drink is permitted because of need, gambling is all the more deserving of such permission.

Gambling itself was prohibited only because it distracts from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer, and causes enmity and hatred. Thus, when it involves cooperation in archery—which belongs to the same category as prayer—and in jihad, which involves mutual assistance and unites hearts in its pursuit, this harmful consequence ceases to exist. Likewise, sales involving uncertainty belong to the same category as gambling, and some forms are permitted when there is a need and the benefit outweighs the harm.

---

## Page 7535

Likewise, “usury” was prohibited because of the injustice it involves, and it was required that a commodity be exchanged only for an equal amount. Yet exchanging it for goods of its own kind on the basis of an estimate was permitted when there was a need. Unlike the acts prohibited unconditionally, other prohibited things are forbidden in some circumstances but not others. This is why—and Allah knows best—He denied that anything besides those acts was prohibited, meaning an absolute, universal prohibition. What is negated is of the same kind as what is affirmed: having affirmed their absolute, universal prohibition, He denied such a prohibition of anything else.

The second point is to distinguish between what one does in relation to a person, commands, or permits, and what one refrains from forbidding another person to do or declaring unlawful for him. If forbidding a prohibited act would lead to something even more strictly prohibited, one should neither forbid it nor permit it.

For this reason, it is impermissible to oppose a wrong by means of something even more wrongful. This is also why armed rebellion against those in authority is prohibited, even for the purpose of enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong: the prohibited acts committed and obligations neglected as a result are graver than the wrongs and sins committed by those rulers.

Likewise, if people engage in religious innovation or wickedness, and forbidding them would cause an evil greater than their present conduct, while they cannot be prevented from committing that greater evil and no overriding benefit would result from forbidding them, they should not be forbidden from it.

This differs from Allah’s command to the prophets and their followers to call people to Him, for their call brings about benefits that outweigh its harms, as in the call of Moses

---

## Page 7536

to Pharaoh and of Noah to his people. Moses engaged in striving and obedience to Allah, while his people exercised patience and sought Allah’s help, thereby attaining a praiseworthy outcome. The drowning of Pharaoh and his people also brought about immense benefit. Likewise, what Noah experienced resulted in his descendants alone remaining, while Allah destroyed all his people; their destruction was thus beneficial.

If forbidding an evil causes its harm to increase, yet brings about an overriding benefit, then forbidding it is good. But if its harm increases and grows greater, with no good to offset and outweigh it, forbidding it is not prescribed—unless it is offset by a greater benefit. If doing so leads to an even greater evil, it is not prescribed. For example, someone who enjoins what is right may lack patience: when subjected to harm, he becomes so distraught that he falls into sin, diminishing his faith and religious commitment. This brings no good either to him or to those people. It is otherwise if he remains patient, fears Allah, strives, and does not transgress Allah’s limits, but instead exercises piety and patience: his outcome will be praiseworthy.

Those people may repent, and Allah may accept their repentance through the blessing of his efforts; or He may destroy them for their transgression, and that may be beneficial, as the Most High says:

“So the people who did wrong were utterly destroyed. And all praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

---

## Page 7537

As for a person’s own conduct, he may not do what he knows to be forbidden on the assumption that it will help him obey Allah. Such an act can only be wholly harmful, or its harm outweighs its benefit; indeed, that act of obedience may itself become harmful. For the Lawgiver is wise: if He knew that this was beneficial, He would not have forbidden it. A person may, however, commit a forbidden act and then repent, the benefit lying in his repentance and the humility, tenderness of heart, and turning to Allah Most High that it brings him. Sins may thus bring benefit when accompanied by repentance. A person’s freedom from sins may give rise to arrogance, conceit, and hardness of heart; then, if he falls into a sin, it humbles him, breaks his heart, and softens it through the repentance that follows.

For this reason, Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr said:

“A servant may do a good deed through which he enters the Fire, and commit an evil deed through which he enters Paradise.”

This is the wisdom behind the trials of those prophets and righteous people who were tested with sins. Without repentance, however, the harm of a forbidden act necessarily outweighs its benefit. A person may not regard as lawful what he knows with certainty Allah has forbidden, and he is certainly not permitted to do it. If his own soul and Satan overcome him and he falls into it, he must repent of it. If he repents and becomes better through repentance than he was before, this is part of Allah’s mercy towards him in accepting his repentance. Otherwise, had he not repented, his condition would have deteriorated because of the sin.

He may not say, “I will do it and then repent,” nor does the Lawgiver permit him to do so. He is like someone who says, “I will eat what makes me ill and then seek treatment,” or, “I will take poison and then drink the antidote.”

---

## Page 7538

And the Lawgiver is wise: a person does not know whether he will be able to repent, whether an antidote or another remedy will bring about a cure, or whether he will even be able to drink it. But if this happens and he ultimately repents sincerely, Allah has shown him kindness by granting him repentance and pardoning his past sins. Such a person may become upright only through sinning and then repenting. Had he not done this—had he not sinned and repented—he would have been worse off.

But this is a matter concerning Allah’s creation, decree, and wisdom. No one can command a person to do this, because he does not know that it will be better for him. It does not follow that whatever Allah brings into being, in accordance with His knowledge and wisdom, is something that the messengers and His servants may do or command.

The episode of al-Khidr and Moses did not contravene Allah’s revealed Law or His command. Nor did al-Khidr act as he did merely because it had been decreed, as some people suppose. Rather, what al-Khidr did is commanded by the revealed Law, provided that one knows what al-Khidr knew of the benefit involved. He did not do anything that is forbidden unconditionally. Rather, he made a hole in the ship, killed the boy, and set the wall upright. Destroying part of some property to preserve the greater part is always lawful. Likewise, killing an aggressor to protect another person’s religion is lawful, as is enduring hunger while doing good to others.

This incident thus shows that some actions may appear harmful and therefore be deemed forbidden by someone who does not know the wisdom for which they were done, even though they are permissible under the revealed Law

---

## Page 7539

both inwardly and outwardly, for anyone who knows the wisdom that makes them good and permissible.

This does not apply to the four categories: associating partners with Allah, speaking about Allah without knowledge, immoral deeds—whether committed openly or secretly—and injustice. These contain no benefit whatsoever. Taking a life is permitted in some circumstances but not others, so it does not fall within these four categories. Likewise, destroying property is permitted in some circumstances but not others, as is enduring hunger.

For this reason, Allah says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of worship, and call upon Him, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.’”

Devoting one’s religion sincerely to Him and acting justly are therefore obligatory without qualification, in every circumstance and under every revealed law. The servant must worship Allah and call upon Him with sincere devotion to Him alone. This obligation is never waived under any circumstances. None will enter Paradise except those who worship Allah alone—those who affirm, “There is no god worthy of worship except Allah.”

This is Allah’s right over every one of His servants, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections in the hadith of Mu‘ādh:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to me, ‘O Mu‘ādh, do you know what Allah’s right over His servants is?’ I replied, ‘Allah and His Messenger know best.’ He said, ‘His right over them is that they worship Him without associating anything with Him.’”

The hadith continues. Thus, none will escape Allah’s punishment except those who devote their religion and worship sincerely to Allah and call upon Him

---

## Page 7540

with sincere devotion to Him alone. Whoever neither associates partners with Him nor worships Him refrains from worshipping both Him and anyone else, like Pharaoh and those like him. Such a person is in a worse condition than one who associates partners with Allah. Worshipping Allah alone is therefore indispensable. It is obligatory upon everyone and is never waived for anyone. This is Islam in its universal sense, besides which Allah accepts no religion.

Yet Allah does not punish anyone until He sends a messenger to him. Just as He does not punish him before that, none will enter Paradise except a believing Muslim soul. Neither one who associates partners with Allah nor one who arrogantly refuses to worship his Lord will enter it. Whoever was not reached by the call in this world will be tested in the Hereafter. None will enter the Fire except those who follow Satan; whoever has no sin will not enter it. Allah does not punish anyone with the Fire until He has sent a messenger to him. Thus, anyone whom a messenger’s call has not reached—such as a young child, a person deprived of reason, or someone who died in an interval wholly without a prophetic message—will be tested in the Hereafter, as the transmitted reports state.

With regard to obligations and prohibitions, a distinction must therefore be made between what is binding upon everyone in every circumstance—namely, justice concerning the rights of Allah and the rights of His servants, by worshipping Allah with sincere devotion to Him alone and not wronging people in any way—and what is forbidden to everyone in every circumstance, with no instance of it being permissible—namely, immoral deeds, injustice, associating partners with Allah, and speaking about Allah without knowledge—and all other matters.

Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Come, I will recite what your Lord has forbidden to you: do not associate

---

## Page 7541

anything with Him.’” This is prohibited without qualification; no instance of it is permissible.

“And be good to parents.” This is subject to qualification. If a father calls his child to associate partners with Allah, the child may not obey him; rather, he may enjoin right upon him and forbid him wrong. Such enjoining and forbidding is itself an act of kindness towards the father. If the father is a polytheist, the child may kill him, though scholars disagree over whether doing so is disliked.

His saying, “Do not kill your children because of poverty,” is a prohibition with a specific scope.

“Do not approach immoral deeds, whether openly or in secret.” This is unqualified.

“Do not approach the orphan’s property except in the best manner, until he reaches maturity.” This is qualified, since the property of orphans among the polytheist ahl al-harb (people of war) may be taken as war booty. Yet it might be said that taking and approaching it in this way is itself “in the best manner,” if “best” is understood as what Allah and His Messenger command.

“Give full measure and weight with justice.” This applies to those entitled to it.

“When you speak, be just.” This is unqualified.

“And fulfil Allah’s covenant.” Fulfilling it is obligatory, but a distinction must be drawn between Allah’s covenant and other covenants. A distinction must also be drawn between what a person remains silent about and what he says, does, and commands; and between something decreed by Allah through which good results and something a servant is commanded to do through which good results.

---

## Page 7542

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Allah’s words—exalted is He, far above all imperfection—

“Take care of yourselves. Those who go astray will not harm you if you are rightly guided”

do not entail abandoning the enjoining of right and forbidding of wrong: they neither prohibit these duties nor permit their neglect. As related in the well-known hadith in the Sunan collections, Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him, addressed the people from the pulpit of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying:

“O people, you recite this verse but apply it incorrectly. I heard Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: ‘When people see wrongdoing and do not change it, Allah may soon encompass them all with a punishment from Him.’”

Likewise, in the hadith of Abū Tha‘labah al-Khushanī, the Prophet’s explanation of this verse is reported as follows:

“When you see greed holding sway, desires being followed, and everyone admiring his own opinion, then attend to your own affairs.”

This is explained by the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd in Sahīh Muslim:

“Whoever among you sees a wrong, let him change it with his hand. If he cannot, then with his tongue. If he cannot, then with his heart—and that is the weakest level of faith.”

Thus, when evildoers become so powerful that they no longer listen to

---

## Page 7543

righteousness, but instead harm those who forbid them because greed, desire, and self-admiration hold sway, the obligation to change wrongdoing with the tongue falls away in these circumstances, while rejection of it in the heart remains obligatory.

“Greed” is an intense craving that gives rise to miserliness and injustice; it involves withholding good and disliking it. “Desire that is followed” concerns willing evil and loving it, while “admiration for one’s own opinion” concerns reason and knowledge. Thus, he mentioned the corruption of the three faculties: knowledge, love, and hatred.

As stated in another hadith:

“Three things lead to ruin: greed that is obeyed, desire that is followed, and a person’s admiration for himself. Set against them are three things that bring salvation: fearing Allah in private and in public, moderation in poverty and wealth, and speaking the truth in anger and contentment.”

These are the things the Prophet asked for in another hadith:

“O Allah, I ask You to grant me fear of You in private and in public. I ask You to grant me truthful speech in anger and contentment, and I ask You to grant me moderation in poverty and wealth.”

Fear of Allah counters following desire, for such fear prevents it, as He said:

“But as for whoever feared standing before his Lord and restrained himself from desire”.

Moderation in poverty and wealth counters greed that is obeyed, while speaking the truth in anger and contentment counters a person’s admiration for himself.

What al-Siddīq stated is clear, for Allah said:

“Take care of yourselves”.

That is, attend closely to yourselves and direct your attention to your own souls. Among the things that benefit the soul is carrying out its duty to enjoin right and forbid wrong.

He also said:

“Those who go astray will not harm you if you are rightly guided”.

Right guidance is only complete when Allah is obeyed and the duties of enjoining right and forbidding wrong, along with other obligations, are fulfilled. Yet the verse contains great benefits:

---

## Page 7544

First: The believer should not fear the disbelievers and hypocrites, for they will not harm him if he is rightly guided.

Second: He should neither grieve over them nor be distressed on their account, for their sins will not harm him if he is rightly guided. Grieving over what cannot harm one is futile. Both these meanings are mentioned in His words:

“Be patient, for your patience is only through Allah. Do not grieve over them, nor be distressed by their plots.”

Third: He should not incline towards them or look longingly at the power, wealth, and pleasures they have been given, as He says:

“Do not look longingly at what We have given some of them to enjoy, nor grieve over them.”

Thus, in one verse He forbade grieving over them and desiring what they possess, while in another He forbade grieving over them and fearing them. A person may suffer distress both on their account and because of them, whether through desire or fear.

Fourth: He should not transgress against those who commit sins by exceeding the prescribed bounds in hating them, censuring them, forbidding their wrongdoing, shunning them, or punishing them. Rather, anyone who transgresses against them should be told: “Take care of yourself; those who go astray will not harm you if you are rightly guided.” As He said:

“Do not let hatred of a people lead you…”—to the end of the verse.

He also said:

“Fight in the way of Allah those who fight you, but do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors.”

And He said:

“But if they cease, then let there be no hostility except against the wrongdoers.”

For many of those who enjoin right and forbid wrong may overstep

---

## Page 7545

the limits set by Allah, whether through ignorance or injustice. This is a matter that requires careful judgment, whether one is denouncing the conduct of disbelievers, hypocrites, the defiantly disobedient, or sinners.

Fifth: He should enjoin right and forbid wrong in the prescribed manner: with knowledge, gentleness, patience, sound intentions, and moderation. All this falls within His words:

“Take care of yourselves,”

and His words:

“If you are rightly guided.”

These are five lessons that those commanded to enjoin right and forbid wrong may derive from this verse. It also conveys another meaning: that a person should attend to his own welfare in knowledge and action, and turn away from what does not concern him, as the Prophet said:

“Part of a person’s being a good Muslim is leaving alone what does not concern him.”

This applies particularly to excessive discussion of other people’s religious and worldly affairs when one has no need to concern oneself with them, especially when such speech is motivated by envy or a desire for authority. The same applies to action. Whoever engages in such conduct is either an unjust aggressor or a fool occupied with frivolities. How often Satan presents this in the guise of enjoining right, forbidding wrong, and striving in Allah’s cause, when it is actually injustice and aggression!

Reflect, then, on this verse in relation to these matters; this is among the most beneficial things a person can do. And when you reflect on the disagreements that arise within this community—among its scholars, worshippers, and rulers

---

## Page 7546

and its leaders—you will find that most of it is of this kind: wrongdoing, whether based on an interpretation or not. Thus the Jahmiyyah wronged the adherents of the Sunnah during the ordeal concerning the divine attributes and the Quran—the ordeal of Ahmad and others. Likewise, the Rāfidah repeatedly wronged the adherents of the Sunnah, and the Nāsibah wronged Ali and his household. Those who liken Allah to His creation may likewise wrong those who affirm His transcendence. Some adherents of the Sunnah may also wrong one another, or a particular group of religious innovators, by going beyond what Allah has commanded. This is the excess mentioned in their supplication:

“Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our affairs.”

Opposite this aggression stands the failure of others to uphold the truth they have been commanded to uphold, or to carry out their duty to enjoin right and forbid wrong in all these matters. How apt are the words of one of the early generations:

“Whenever Allah commands something, Satan seeks to obstruct it in one of two ways—he cares not which succeeds: excess or neglect.”

Thus, opposite the one who assists in sin and aggression stands the one who fails to assist in righteousness and piety. Opposite the one who does what is commanded but adds something forbidden stands the one who avoids what is forbidden but also neglects some of what is commanded. May Allah guide us to the straight path. There is no power nor strength except through Allah.

---

## Page 7547

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

The meaning indicated by the Quran in Sūrat al-Mā’idah, in the verse concerning testimony, is as follows. In His words:

“Then, if you have doubts, they shall both swear by Allah, ‘We will not exchange it for any gain.’”

“It” refers to their statement. In “even if he is a close relative,” the subject pronoun is omitted because its referent is clear: even if the person in whose favour testimony is given is a close relative. This is like His words:

“When you speak, be just, even if it concerns a close relative.”

Likewise, He says:

“Stand firmly for justice as witnesses for Allah,”

through to His words:

“Whether he is rich or poor,”

meaning the person against whom testimony is given, and similar expressions. For false testimony is ordinarily given in return for something; no one gives false testimony without some recompense—even if only praise—or without placing someone under an obligation.

Testimony is corrupted either by distortion or by evasion: lying or concealment. Thus the two swear, “We will not exchange our statement for any gain”—meaning that we will not lie—“nor will we conceal testimony owed to Allah.” Alternatively, it means, “We will not exchange Allah’s covenant for any gain,” because the two had been entrusted with property and were therefore bound by a covenant to deliver it to its rightful recipient; a bequest is itself a covenant.

His subsequent words:

“But if it is discovered that the two have incurred sin,”

are not limited to wrongdoing committed

---

## Page 7548

in giving testimony or in discharging a trust. The circumstances of the verse’s revelation indicate that it concerned a breach of trust, for the two had been called upon to testify and entrusted with property. Entrusting them with property, however, does not depart from legal analogy; its ruling is clear and required no specific revelation, unlike accepting their testimony. What disclosed their wrongdoing was the discovery of some of the bequeathed property in the possession of someone who had purchased it from them, after it had been found listed in the will and they had been questioned about it and denied knowing of it.

His words:

“From among those against whom the two incurred guilt”

may incorporate the meaning “wronged them,” and thus take the preposition “against,” as one says concerning usurpation, “You have wrongfully seized my property.” Hence it is said:

“Our testimony is more truthful than theirs, and we have not transgressed,”

meaning: as they transgressed.

Then He says:

“That is more likely to make them give testimony properly, or fear that their oaths will be countered by other oaths.”

The hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās in al-Bukhārī explicitly states that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, ruled in accordance with the Quran: he transferred the oath to the two claimants, after having required the defendants to swear, once it was discovered that the two had incurred sin. This discovery came through the purchasers’ report that they had bought the “vessel” from them, after the two had said, “We have not seen it.” The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, then required the two claimants with the foremost entitlement to swear, took the “vessel” from the purchaser, and handed it over to the claimant; the sale was thereby invalidated. This could not have been the case had the two admitted selling the vessel, for there would have been no need for the two claimants’ oath had they acknowledged that it was the testator’s vessel and that they

---

## Page 7549

had wrongfully seized and sold it. Rather, they continued to deny having received it despite having sold it, or else claimed that he had bequeathed it to them; this latter possibility is unlikely.

The apparent meaning of the verse is that when a defendant accused of a breach of trust or something similar—as in this case—is shown to have lied and betrayed his trust, this constitutes incriminating circumstantial evidence that gives greater weight to the claimant’s case. The claimant therefore swears an oath and recovers what he claims, exactly as we have said concerning homicide. The rationale is the same in both cases. Since killing ordinarily takes place secretly rather than openly, establishing proof is difficult. Yet the claimant’s statement cannot be accepted unconditionally, so the statement of the party whose case carries greater weight is accepted. In the absence of incriminating circumstantial evidence, the defendant’s position is stronger. When, however, a killing is accompanied by such evidence, the claimant’s position becomes stronger, and he swears the oath.

Likewise, establishing proof of a breach of trust or theft is ordinarily difficult. Someone who allows himself to steal may have no scruples about lying. Where there is no incriminating circumstantial evidence, the presumption is freedom from liability. But if such evidence emerges—for example, some of the stolen property is found in the defendant’s possession—the claimant swears an oath and recovers what he claims. The same applies if the defendant initially swears an oath, but some of the stolen property is subsequently found in the possession of someone who purchased it from him, received it from him as a gift, or otherwise obtained it from him. Such circumstantial evidence gives still stronger grounds for a preponderant judgment. In a homicide case, however, it may be certain that a killing occurred while the killer’s identity remains uncertain; the claim thus concerns only identifying the killer.

As for property:

At times, both the loss of the property and its amount are known with certainty—for example, when it was

---

## Page 7550

known to be in a particular place. At other times, the loss of some property is certain, but not its amount: one knows that property was there and has disappeared. Sometimes, it is certain that the secure storage place was breached, but it is not known whether anything was taken. This concerns claims of theft. In claims of breach of trust, however, the breach itself is not known. If some of the property concerned is found with the defendant or with someone who received it from him, incriminating circumstantial evidence emerges, giving greater weight to the claimant’s case. Requiring the defendant to swear an oath at that point would be difficult to justify.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If people were granted whatever they claimed, some would claim the blood and property of others. Rather, the oath rests upon the defendant.”

Here he mentioned blood and property together. Just as a claimant in a homicide case swears an oath when supported by incriminating circumstantial evidence, so too does a claimant in a property dispute, just as we require him to swear an oath alongside his witness’s testimony. Anything that makes his truthfulness more likely serves the same function as his witness. Thus, in homicide cases, we have treated testimony tainted by a deficiency in the required number or qualifications of witnesses as incriminating circumstantial evidence. Likewise, in property cases, a witness is accepted together with an oath. Thus the tainted witness is accompanied by incriminating circumstantial evidence, and he is … (1)

However, consideration should be given here to the truthfulness or dishonesty of both claimant and defendant. Theft and breach of trust are committed only by a sinful person. If he is given to such conduct, he would not be … (2) if he were entirely upright.

The claimant, too, may lie. That uprightness and sinfulness should be considered here is indicated by the Ansārī’s statement: “How can we accept the oaths of people who are disbelievers?” It follows that, if the accused is wicked, the claimant may refuse to accept his oath, since someone who has no scruples about stealing will have none about swearing an oath.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 7551

Surah al-An‘ām

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about the words of the Most High:

“Then He decreed a term, and an appointed term is with Him.”

And His words:

“No one is granted a long life, nor is anything diminished from his lifespan, except that it is in a Record.”

And His words:

“Allah erases what He wills and confirms, and with Him is the Mother of the Book.”

Do this erasure and confirmation occur in the Preserved Tablet and in the book mentioned in the authentic hadith:

“Allah Most High wrote a book, which is with Him upon His Throne”—to the end of the hadith?

It has also been reported:

“The pen has dried.”

What does this mean in relation to erasure and confirmation?

Is it prescribed to say in supplication: “O Allah, if You have recorded me as such-and-such, erase that and record me as such-and-such instead, for You have said: ‘Allah erases what He wills and confirms’”?

Is it authentically reported that ‘Umar used to offer a supplication like this? Is the correct view, in your judgment, that maintaining ties of kinship increases one’s lifespan, as stated in the hadith? Give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds,

---

## Page 7552

As for His words—glory be to Him:

“Then He decreed a term, and an appointed term is with Him,”

the first term is the term of each servant, at which his life ends. The appointed term with Him is the time of the general Resurrection. This is why He says:

“Appointed with Him.”

For neither an angel brought near to Allah nor a prophet sent by Him knows the time of the Hour, as He says:

“They ask you about the Hour: when will it come? Say, ‘Knowledge of it rests only with my Lord. None but He will reveal it at its appointed time.’”

This differs from His saying merely “appointed,” as in His words:

“When you contract a debt for an appointed term.”

Since He does not qualify this by saying that it is appointed with Him, His servants may know it. As for the appointed time of death, this is known to the angels who record a servant’s provision, lifespan, deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.

As reported in the two Sahīhs, Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the truthful one whose truthfulness is confirmed—told us: ‘Each of you is formed in his mother’s womb for forty days as a drop of sperm, then becomes a clinging clot for a similar period, then a lump of flesh for a similar period. Then the angel is sent to him and instructed to record four matters. He is told: “Write down his provision, his lifespan, his deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.” Then the soul is breathed into him.’”

Thus, Allah may make this term—the appointed time of death—known to whichever of His servants He wills. But the appointed time of the Resurrection, which is with Him, is known to Him alone.

---

## Page 7553

As for His words:

“No one is granted a long life, nor is anything diminished of his lifespan,”

it has been said that the reference is to humankind in general: no human lifespan is prolonged, nor is any human lifespan shortened. Prolonging and shortening life may then have two meanings.

The first is that one person’s life is long and another’s is short, so the latter’s shorter life represents a reduction relative to someone else’s. Similarly, a long-lived person has a longer life, while this other person has a shorter life; his shorter life is thus a reduction relative to someone else’s, just as prolonging life is an increase relative to another’s. A reduction may also mean a reduction in the recorded lifespan, just as an increase may mean an increase in the recorded lifespan.

In the two Sahīhs, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is reported to have said:

“Whoever wishes his provision to be increased and his lifespan to be extended, let him uphold his ties of kinship.”

Some people have said that this means blessing in one’s lifespan, whereby a person accomplishes in a short time what another could accomplish only over a long period. They argue that provision and lifespan are both decreed and recorded. They should be told: that blessing—namely, the increase in deeds and benefit—is also decreed and recorded, and this applies to all things.

The correct answer is that Allah records an appointed term for His servant in the angels’ records.

---

## Page 7554

then, when he upholds his ties of kinship, He increases the lifespan recorded there. If he does something that entails a reduction, He reduces that recorded lifespan. A similar example is the report in al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When Adam asked Allah to show him the forms of the prophets among his descendants, Allah showed them to him. Among them he saw a man with a gleam of light and asked, ‘Who is this, my Lord?’ He replied, ‘Your son David.’ Adam asked, ‘How long will he live?’ He replied, ‘Forty years.’ Adam asked, ‘And how long will I live?’ He replied, ‘A thousand years.’ Adam said, ‘Then I give him sixty years of my lifespan.’ This was put in writing, and the angels bore witness to it. When death came to him, he said, ‘I still have sixty years of my life remaining.’ They said, ‘You gave them to your son David.’ He denied this, so they produced the document. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Adam forgot, and his descendants forgot; Adam denied, and his descendants denied.’”

It is also reported that He granted both Adam and David their full lifespans.

Thus David’s recorded lifespan was forty years, then He made it sixty. This is the meaning of what is reported from ʿUmar, who said:

“O Allah, if You have recorded me as wretched, erase that and record me as blessed, for You erase whatever You will and confirm.”

Allah, glorified is He, knows what has happened, what will happen, and what has not happened—how it would have happened had it taken place. He therefore knows what He has recorded for His servant and what He will subsequently add to it. The angels know only what Allah has taught them, whereas Allah knows things before and after they come into being;

---

## Page 7555

for this reason, the scholars have said:

Erasure and confirmation occur in the records of the angels. Allah’s knowledge, however, does not change, nor does anything become known to Him that He did not already know; thus, there is neither erasure nor confirmation in His knowledge. As for the Preserved Tablet, there are two views on whether erasure and confirmation occur in it. Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best.

---

## Page 7556

He also said:

Section:

Allah mentions that He raises in rank whomever He wills, both in the account of Abraham’s debate and in the account of Joseph’s stratagem. For this reason, the early generations said: “Through knowledge.” The context of the verses indicates this.

The account of Abraham concerns knowledge of argumentation and debate to protect religion from an opponent’s harm, while the account of Joseph concerns knowledge of practical management and planning to obtain the desired benefit. The former concerns knowledge of how to avert harm to religion, and the latter knowledge of how to secure benefits. Or one may say: the former is knowledge that protects religion from harm and secures its benefit, while the latter is knowledge of how to protect worldly affairs from harm and secure their benefit.

Or one may say: the account of Abraham concerns knowledge of beneficial words when they are needed, while the account of Joseph concerns knowledge of beneficial actions when they are needed. Thus, to secure benefit and avert harm, what is needed may be speech, or it may be . . . (1).

This is why those deficient in knowledge of arguments and proofs, and in knowledge of practical management

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original. The Harf Company’s electronic edition reads: “The need may be for speech, or it may be for action.”

---

## Page 7557

and governance are beholden to these two groups. Sometimes they need them when an enemy attacks, undermining religion through disputation or worldly affairs through oppression; sometimes they need them when these same threats assail them from within; and sometimes they need them to deliver some of their number from the harm others inflict in religious and worldly matters. At other times, they live under their protection, somewhere no proponent of religious innovation can domineer over them and no governor oppresses them. This is only because there are scholars versed in arguments that confound the people of innovation and in governance that wards off oppression.

Hence the saying:

“Two groups, when upright, set the people right: scholars and rulers.”

Just as benefit comes through these two groups, so too does harm come from them, for religious innovation and oppression arise only among these two: those who hold scholarly authority and those who hold temporal authority. Thus, a number of the early generations, including al-Thawrī, Ibn ʿUyaynah and others, said words to this effect: whoever escapes the trial of religious innovation and the trial of rulers has escaped all evil. I have discussed this at length in al-Sirāt al-Mustaqīm (The Straight Path), when discussing His words:

“So they enjoyed their share, and you have enjoyed your share just as those before you enjoyed their share, and you have indulged in vain discourse as they did.”

---

## Page 7558

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

This is an explanation of verses that have proved difficult to understand, so much so that some works of Quranic commentary offer only erroneous explanations of them.

Among these are His words:

“And what will make you realize that, when it comes, they will not believe?”

And the verse that follows it. The reading annahā, with an initial a-vowel, has puzzled many because they assumed that the following verse begins an independent sentence. This is not so; rather, it falls within the predicate governed by anna (“that”).

The meaning is: If you do not realize that, when it comes, they will not believe, and that I shall do this to them, you cannot regard their oath as truthful; rather, it may be false. This is the plain, familiar sense of the wording: anna functions as a nominalizing particle.

Had “And We turn,” and so forth, been an independent statement, it would follow that everyone to whom a sign came would have his heart turned away. Yet this is not so; many of them may indeed believe.

---

## Page 7559

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Allah Most High said:

“And the word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and justice. None can alter His words, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

This comes after His words:

“And thus We have appointed for every prophet enemies—devils from humankind and jinn—who whisper to one another embellished words to deceive. Had your Lord willed, they would not have done so. So leave them and what they fabricate.”

“And so that the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter may incline toward it, be pleased with it, and commit whatever they commit.”

“Then should I seek a judge other than Allah, when it is He who has sent down to you the Book, explained in detail? Those to whom We gave the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth. So never be among the doubters.”

Then He said:

“And the word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and justice. None can alter His words, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

Allah Most High also said:

“And recite what has been revealed to you of the Book of your Lord. None can alter His words, and you will never find any refuge besides Him.”

In these two verses, He states that none can alter the words of Allah, and in the first, that they have been fulfilled in truth and justice. It has been established through mass transmission “from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

---

## Page 7560

that he used to seek refuge, and instruct others to seek refuge, in Allah’s perfect words; some hadiths add that neither the righteous nor the wicked can overstep them.”

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, the allies of Allah will have no fear, nor will they grieve—those who believed and were mindful of Him. For them are glad tidings in this worldly life and in the Hereafter. There is no alteration to Allah’s words. That is the supreme triumph.”

Allah Most High also said:

“Messengers before you were indeed rejected, but they patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them. None can alter Allah’s words. And some accounts of the messengers have already reached you.”

In this verse too, He states that none can alter Allah’s words, immediately after saying:

“But they patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them.”

This makes clear that the promise Allah made to His messengers is among His words that none can alter. When He said concerning His allies:

“For them are glad tidings in this worldly life and in the Hereafter. There is no alteration to Allah’s words,”

He mentioned that they would have no fear, nor would they grieve, and that they would have glad tidings in this worldly life and in the Hereafter. Thus, He promised them freedom from fear and grief, and glad tidings in both abodes.

He then said:

“None can alter Allah’s words.”

This confirms the certainty of Allah’s words conveying His promise, as He said:

“So never think that Allah will fail to keep His promise to His messengers.”

He also said:

“This is Allah’s promise. Allah never breaks His promise, but most people do not know.”

And the believers said:

“Our Lord, grant us what You promised us through Your messengers, and do not disgrace us on the Day of Resurrection. Indeed, You never break Your promise.”

Thus, breaking His promise would be an alteration

---

## Page 7561

of His words—yet none can alter His words, glorified be He. This is made clear by His saying, Most High:

“Do not dispute before Me, for I had already given you warning. My word cannot be changed, and I am never unjust to My servants.”

He, glorified be He, thus states that He had given them prior warning, and says:

“My word cannot be changed.”

This implies that He is also truthful in His threats of punishment, and that His threats cannot be altered. This is among the arguments advanced by those who hold that sinful Muslims will not emerge from the Fire. We have addressed their arguments elsewhere. However, this verse weakens the response of those who say that it is permissible not to fulfil a threat. For His saying:

“My word cannot be changed,”

after saying:

“I had already given you warning,”

is evidence that His threats cannot be altered, just as His promises cannot be altered. The correct approach, however, is to reconcile the texts concerning promises and threats, interpreting them in light of one another without altering any of them, just as the texts containing commands and prohibitions are reconciled without altering any of them.

He, Most High, also said:

“Those who stayed behind will say, when you set out to take the spoils, ‘Let us follow you.’ They wish to change Allah’s words.”

And Allah knows best.
